### [1] [Unveiling Public Opinion: A Study of Sentiment Analysis Using LSTM and Traditional Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07772)
*Atiq Ur Rehman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper evaluates multiple ML and DL models for Twitter sentiment analysis, finding that LSTM networks outperform traditional classifiers (logistic regression, random forest, naïve bayes, gradient boosting), achieving 90.98% training accuracy, 80.00% testing accuracy, and a 0.92 micro-average ROC-AUC score on a preprocessed Kaggle dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The massive influx of real-time user-generated content on social media platforms like Twitter has created an urgent need for effective sentiment analysis tools. Traditional machine learning models struggle with contextual and sequential nuances in short, informal text, motivating the exploration of deep learning approaches that can better capture these linguistic subtleties for improved opinion interpretation and trend forecasting.

Method: The authors employ a Kaggle Twitter dataset preprocessed through tokenization, lemmatization, and stopword elimination. They systematically evaluate and compare five algorithms: logistic regression, random forest, naïve bayes, gradient boosting (traditional ML), and LSTM networks (deep learning). Performance is assessed using training accuracy, testing accuracy, and micro-average ROC-AUC score.

Result: LSTM achieved the best performance with 90.98% training accuracy, 80.00% testing accuracy, and a 0.92 micro-average ROC-AUC score, surpassing all conventional machine learning baselines in capturing contextual and sequential textual patterns. A notable generalization gap (≈11%) between training and testing accuracy is observed.

Conclusion: LSTM networks are the optimal choice for Twitter sentiment analysis among the evaluated models, demonstrating superior capability in modeling the sequential and contextual nature of tweet text. The authors recommend LSTM-based architectures for real-world sentiment classification tasks on social media data.

Abstract: In this age of social media, sites like Twitter have become meeting places for people to share their views and feelings on a wide range of issues and current events as they unfold in real time. Sentiment analysis, a critical application of NLP, has become indispensable due to the massive influx of user-generated content, enabling the extraction of meaningful insights from the opinions and emotions expressed in textual data. Sentiment analysis on Twitter employs sophisticated computational techniques to categorize tweets into positive, negative, or neutral sentiments. This method not only examines individual expressions but also analyzes vast databases related to specific subjects or events. By spotting these emotions, machine learning models help improve public opinion interpretation and trend forecasting. This paper examines the effectiveness of various machine learning and deep learning approaches. Designed for this use, the system evaluates logistic regression, random forest, naïve bayes, gradient boosting, and LSTM networks, among other algorithms applied in sentiment classification. This work identifies the optimal sentiment analysis model using a Kaggle Twitter dataset that has been preprocessed through tokenization, lemmatization, and stopword elimination. Emphasizing the better performance of the LSTM approach, the model attained a training accuracy of 90.98%, a testing accuracy of 80.00%, and a micro-average ROC- AUC score of 0.92. These results show that the model outperforms conventional machine learning techniques in capturing contextual and sequential textual aspects.

</details>


### [2] [From Solvers to Research: Large Language Model-Driven Formal Mathematics at the Research Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07779)
*Eric Jiang,Xiao Liang,Yikai Zhang,Yingjia Wan,Mengting Li,Haikang Deng,Alexander K. Taylor,Justin Baker,Rushil Raghavan,Junyi Zhang,Ying Nian Wu,Andrea L. Bertozzi,Kai-Wei Chang,Raghu Meka,Matthew Sottile,Nanyun Peng,Amit Sahai,Terence Tao,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This position paper argues that AI4Math must evolve from solving predefined formal proof problems to developing autonomous research agents capable of tackling open-ended frontier mathematics, and provides a systematic review of current datasets, auto-formalization, and proof synthesis alongside a strategic roadmap addressing key limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-driven theorem provers excel at well-defined formal proof problems but fail on frontier research mathematics characterized by open-endedness, under-specification, and multi-layered abstraction. A paradigm shift is needed from problem-solvers to genuine mathematical research agents.

Method: The paper conducts a systematic review of AI4Math covering three pillars — datasets, auto-formalization, and proof synthesis. It then identifies core limitations across five dimensions: datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystem, and human-AI collaboration, using these to construct a strategic roadmap.

Result: The analysis reveals that existing systems suffer from bottlenecks in dataset diversity and scale, inability to capture mathematical relational structure, lack of exploration-guided reasoning, fragmented tool ecosystems, and insufficient human-AI collaborative frameworks — all of which impede progress toward autonomous mathematical discovery.

Conclusion: Achieving the next leap in AI4Math demands a decisive shift toward research agents equipped with rigorous formal reasoning, and the proposed roadmap charts a path through addressing identified limitations to enable theorem discovery and conjecture resolution at the frontier of mathematics.

Abstract: Recent developments in AI for Mathematics (AI4Math), especially Large Language Model (LLM)-driven theorem provers, has achieved remarkable success in formal proof generation for well-defined mathematical problems through Interactive Theorem Proving (ITP) languages. However, current systems remain fundamentally limited in tackling frontier research mathematics, such as discovering new theorems or resolving open conjectures, which are often open-ended, under-specified, and involve multiple layers of abstraction. We argue that the next leap in AI4Math systems requires a decisive shift from predefined problem-solvers to research agents that can address frontier mathematical challenges with rigorous formal mathematical reasoning. In this position paper, we provide a systematic review of the field, covering datasets, auto-formalization, and proof synthesis. More importantly, we identify core limitations of existing systems in serving as mathematical research agents, examining issues across datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystem, and human-AI collaboration, outlining a strategic road-map for the future of AI4Math.

</details>


### [3] [DeepSearch-World: Self-Distillation for Deep Search Agents in a Verifiable Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07820)
*Xinyu Geng,Xuanhua He,Sixiang Chen,Yanjing Xiao,Fan Zhang,Shijue Huang,Haitao Mi,Zhenwen Liang,Tianqing Fang,Yi R. Fung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DeepSearch-Evolve is a self-distillation framework that trains web agents through iterative trajectory generation, filtering, and fine-tuning within a deterministic verifiable environment (DeepSearch-World). The resulting 9B model achieves competitive results on BrowseComp (31.2%), GAIA (61.5%), and HotpotQA (93.4%) without distillation from stronger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised fine-tuning for tool-use agents relies on static teacher demonstrations, while sparse-reward RL provides insufficient supervision for long-horizon tasks. The authors aim to enable scalable self-evolution by providing a verifiable environment where agents can learn from their own filtered experiences.

Method: DeepSearch-World provides a deterministic environment with reproducible search/page-reading tools and 420K multi-hop QA tasks built via entity-level random walks. It supports cognitive behaviors like progress verification, grounded reflection, and failure recovery. DeepSearch-Evolve iteratively generates trajectories, filters them, mixes data across iterations, and fine-tunes the agent, enabling self-improvement without external teacher models.

Result: DeepSearch-World-9B achieves 31.2% on BrowseComp, 61.5% on GAIA, and 93.4% on HotpotQA, performing competitively against open-source agents without distillation from stronger models.

Conclusion: Verifiable, deterministic environments like DeepSearch-World enable scalable self-evolution for long-horizon web agents, offering a viable alternative to teacher distillation and sparse-reward RL for training increasingly capable tool-use agents.

Abstract: Training tool-use agents to improve from their own experience remains challenging, as supervised fine-tuning relies on fixed teacher-distilled trajectories, while sparse-reward reinforcement learning provides weak supervision for long-horizon interactions. We present DeepSearch-Evolve, a self-distillation framework for web agents built on DeepSearch-World, a deterministic and verifiable environment with reproducible search and page-reading tools. DeepSearch-World contains 420K multi-hop QA tasks constructed from entity-level random walks and supports key agentic cognitive behaviors useful for self-evolving, including progress verification, grounded reflection, and failure recovery. DeepSearch-Evolve iteratively performs trajectory generation, filtering, data mixing, and fine-tuning to train stronger agents. Without distillation from more capable models, DeepSearch-World-9B achieves competitive performance compared with open-source agents, reaching 31.2% on BrowseComp, 61.5% on GAIA, and 93.4% on HotpotQA, showing that verifiable environments enable scalable self-evolution for long-horizon web agents. We will release the environment, 420K training pool, validation set, model, and code to facilitate future research on self-improving deep search agents.

</details>


### [4] [WebSwarm: Recursive Multi-Agent Orchestration for Deep-and-Wide Web Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08662)
*Xiaoshuai Song,Liancheng Zhang,Kangzhi Zhao,Yutao Zhu,Zhongyuan Wang,Guanting Dong,Jinghan Yang,Han Li,Kun Gai,Ji-Rong Wen,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WebSwarm is a progressive recursive delegation framework for LLM-based web search agents that dynamically constructs task decomposition, recursive expansion, and agent collaboration. It instantiates agentic search nodes that can solve tasks locally or delegate to child nodes, with evidence flowing upward for further expansion. WebSwarm outperforms single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved search tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single ReAct-style agents are limited by one long trajectory and restricted context, making them inadequate for complex search tasks requiring both depth (recursive drilling down) and coverage (broad exploration). Existing multi-agent systems improve coverage via parallel execution but fall short in recursive depth, collaboration adaptability, and evidence-grounded expansion.

Method: WebSwarm uses a progressive recursive delegation framework where agentic search nodes are dynamically instantiated during inference. Each node couples a local objective with a search mode dictating how search and collaboration are organized. Nodes can solve objectives locally or recursively delegate to child nodes. After solving, evidence flows upward, enabling parent nodes to expand, revise, or aggregate. The framework first probes how task-relevant information is organized on the web to ground node expansion and reuses process-level experience across homogeneous sibling nodes.

Result: Experiments on BrowseComp-Plus, WideSearch, DeepWideSearch, and GISA benchmarks show WebSwarm consistently outperforms both single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved deep-and-wide tasks.

Conclusion: WebSwarm effectively addresses the limitations of existing search agents by combining recursive depth with parallel coverage through dynamic delegation and evidence-grounded expansion. Ablation studies, task difficulty analysis, web tool efficiency, and model generalization results further validate its design and offer insights for future multi-agent search systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based web search agents are transforming information seeking from simple factoid question answering into complex, deep-and-wide search and research-oriented tasks. A single ReAct-style agent is constrained by one long trajectory and limited context, making it difficult to handle depth and coverage simultaneously. Existing multi-agent systems improve search coverage through parallel execution and aggregation, but still exhibit clear limitations in recursive depth, collaboration adaptability, and evidence-grounded expansion. We propose WebSwarm, a progressive recursive delegation framework that jointly constructs task decomposition, recursive expansion, and agent collaboration during inference. WebSwarm dynamically instantiates agentic search nodes, each coupling a local objective with a search mode that specifies how the node should organize search and collaboration. Each node can either solve its objective itself or further delegate child nodes; after solving, it returns evidence and results upward, enabling parent nodes to further expand, revise, or aggregate the search process. To guide this process, WebSwarm first probes how task-relevant information is organized on the web to ground subsequent node expansion, and reuses process-level experience across homogeneous sibling nodes. Experiments on BrowseComp-Plus, WideSearch, DeepWideSearch, and GISA show that WebSwarm consistently outperforms single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved deep-and-wide tasks. Further analyses of ablation, task difficulty, web tool efficiency, and model generalization explain WebSwarm's effectiveness and provide insights for multi-agent search systems.

</details>


### [5] [How Do I Know What to Say Next? Barenholtz's Autogenerative Theory as an Enrichment of Harrisean Integrationism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07891)
*J. Mark Bishop,Stephen J. Cowley*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a synthesis between Roy Harris's Integrationist linguistics and Elan Barenholtz's autogenerative theory, arguing that the latter fills key explanatory gaps in the former — namely, the structural mechanism of prospective openness, the continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic semiosis, and the nature of the semiotic archive — while preserving Integrationism's core commitments and offering NLP/LLM researchers a principled account of what statistical language structure is and what it cannot provide.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrationist linguistics offers a powerful critique of referentialism in computational language models but suffers from three explanatory gaps: (1) no structural mechanism for how signs sustain prospective openness, (2) an undertheorised account of semiotic continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic activity, and (3) no detailed theory of the accumulated archive of past integrations. The paper aims to fill these gaps through synthesis with autogenerative theory.

Method: The paper employs a theoretical synthesis approach, mapping Barenholtz's autogenerative theory of language — developed from observations of LLM behaviour — onto Harris's Integrationist framework. It identifies precise points of conceptual correspondence and supplementation, showing how autogenerative constructs (structural mechanism for prospective openness, computational correlates of semiotic continuity, and a theory of the archive) address Integrationism's lacunae without contradicting its foundational commitments.

Result: The synthesis demonstrates that autogenerative theory provides: (1) a structural mechanism for the prospective openness central to bipartite communication; (2) a computational correlate for semiotic continuity between language and other sign-making; and (3) a theory of the archive — what accumulated residues of past integrations look like and how new participants draw upon them. The integration preserves Harris's ontological primacy of the situated integrative act while adding explanatory content Integrationism itself lacks.

Conclusion: The synthesis enriches Integrationism without undermining its core commitments. For NLP and LLM research, it provides a principled account of what the statistical structure exploited by LLMs actually represents and, crucially, what it cannot by its nature supply — namely, the situated, prospective, bipartite integrative act that constitutes genuine linguistic activity in Harris's sense.

Abstract: Roy Harris's Integrationist linguistics offers a compelling critique of the referentialist tradition embedded deep at the heart of computational approaches to language, arguing that language is not a code that maps onto a pre-given world but a situated, bipartite activity oriented toward prospective joint action. Yet Integrationism leaves certain explanatory gaps: it does not fully account for the structural mechanism by which signs sustain prospective openness, it undertheorises the continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic semiotic activity, and it offers no detailed account of the structural properties of the accumulated archive of past integrations. This paper argues that Elan Barenholtz's autogenerative theory of language, developed in response to the behaviour of Large Language Models (LLMs), can fill precisely these gaps, enriching Integrationism without undermining any of its core commitments. Specifically, the autogenerative account provides: a structural mechanism for the prospective openness that Harris identifies as central to bipartite communication; a computational correlate for Harris's thesis of semiotic continuity between language and other sign-making activity; and a theory of the archive: what the accumulated residue of past integrations looks like and how new participants draw upon it. The synthesis preserves Harris's ontological primacy of the situated integrative act while adding explanatory content that Integrationism itself does not supply. For practitioners and researchers in natural language processing and large language model design, the argument offers a principled account of what the statistical structure that LLMs so effectively exploit actually is, and of what it cannot, by its nature, provide.

</details>


### [6] [Scalable and Culturally Specific Stereotype Dataset Construction via Human-LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07895)
*Weicheng Ma,John Guerrerio,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a cost-efficient human-LLM collaborative framework to build EspanStereo, a Spanish-language stereotype dataset spanning multiple Spanish-speaking countries, and evaluates LLMs to reveal significant cross-country variation in stereotypical behavior, advocating for culturally grounded multilingual bias assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stereotype research in LLMs is overwhelmingly English-centric due to a lack of datasets in other languages and the prohibitive cost of manual annotation in underrepresented cultures. This creates a blind spot for biases affecting Spanish-speaking populations across Europe and Latin America.

Method: A human-LLM collaborative annotation framework where LLMs generate candidate stereotypes, which are then validated by in-culture human annotators from different Spanish-speaking countries. This pipeline is applied to construct EspanStereo, capturing both known stereotypes and culturally specific biases. The dataset is then used to benchmark stereotype behavior in Spanish-supporting LLMs.

Result: The framework effectively identifies nuanced, region-specific biases. Evaluation using EspanStereo reveals significant variation in stereotypical behavior across different Spanish-speaking countries, underscoring the inadequacy of monolithic language-level bias assessments.

Conclusion: Culturally grounded stereotype evaluation is essential for fair LLM assessment. The proposed framework is adaptable to other languages and regions, offering a scalable path toward comprehensive multilingual and cross-cultural stereotype benchmarks.

Abstract: Research on stereotypes in large language models (LLMs) has largely focused on English-speaking contexts, due to the lack of datasets in other languages and the high cost of manual annotation in underrepresented cultures. To address this gap, we introduce a cost-efficient human-LLM collaborative annotation framework and apply it to construct EspanStereo, a Spanish-language stereotype dataset spanning multiple Spanish-speaking countries across Europe and Latin America. EspanStereo captures both well-documented stereotypes from prior literature and culturally specific biases absent from English-centric resources. Using LLMs to generate candidate stereotypes and in-culture annotators to validate them, we demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in identifying nuanced, region-specific biases. Our evaluation of Spanish-supporting LLMs using EspanStereo reveals significant variation in stereotypical behavior across countries, highlighting the need for more culturally grounded assessments. Beyond Spanish, our framework is adaptable to other languages and regions, offering a scalable path toward multilingual stereotype benchmarks. This work broadens the scope of stereotype analysis in LLMs and lays the groundwork for comprehensive cross-cultural bias evaluation.

</details>


### [7] [When Debiasing Backfires: Counterintuitive Side Effects of Preprocessing-Based Stereotype Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07937)
*Yahan Zheng,John Guerrerio,Soroush Vosoughi,Weicheng Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Preprocessing-based stereotype mitigation methods often produce unintended side effects—shifting stereotyping or counter-stereotyping toward other demographic groups, including unrelated ones. These effects persist across model types, mitigation strategies, and training scales, yet standard benchmarks fail to detect them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preprocessing-based debiasing methods (e.g., removing stereotypical sentences, group mentions, or swapping group references) are widely adopted for stereotype mitigation, but their broader impacts on non-targeted demographics remain poorly understood. The authors aim to systematically investigate whether these methods inadvertently increase stereotyping or counter-stereotyping for other groups.

Method: The authors conduct controlled experiments across two model families (encoder-only and decoder-only), three preprocessing strategies (removing stereotypical sentences, removing group mentions, swapping group references), and both pre-training and post-training phases at varying data scales on Wikipedia. They measure shifts in stereotyping for non-targeted demographics and use attention-rollout analysis to explore mechanistic explanations.

Result: All tested preprocessing methods induce side effects—stereotyping or counter-stereotyping increases relative to neutral baselines for other demographics, including across unrelated demographic categories. These shifts are missed by standard benchmarks. Attention-rollout analysis reveals that side effects are not accompanied by large changes in attention flow, complicating mechanistic interpretation.

Conclusion: Side effects from preprocessing-based debiasing are pervasive and cross-category, yet go undetected by standard evaluation. The authors call for side-effect-aware, transparent mitigation practices and provide actionable diagnostics for practitioners.

Abstract: Preprocessing-based methods for stereotype mitigation, such as pre-/post-training on debiased corpora, are widely used in NLP. While these approaches reduce measurable stereotypes for targeted groups, we find they often induce unintended shifts-side effects, where stereotyping or counter-stereotyping can increase relative to neutral baselines for other demographics, including across unrelated demographic categories. We demonstrate these side effects across two model families (encoder-only and decoder-only), multiple preprocessing strategies (removing stereotypical sentences, removing group mentions, and swapping group references), and both pre- and post-training at different data scales on Wikipedia. Standard benchmarks frequently miss these shifts. Using attention-rollout analysis, we observe that such side effects are not accompanied by large changes in attention flow, complicating mechanistic explanations. We discuss implications for evaluation, provide actionable diagnostics, and argue for side-effect-aware, transparent mitigation practices.

</details>


### [8] [A Multi-cluster Boundary Learning Method for Out-of-Scope Intent Detection via MiniLM Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07974)
*Yihong Xu,Mingyu Kang,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a multi-cluster boundary learning method using MiniLM embeddings in a one-class classification framework for out-of-scope intent detection, achieving SOTA results on CLINC150, StackOverflow, and Banking77 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multi-class OOS detection degrades as known intent classes increase; LLM-embedding methods are parameter-heavy and hard to deploy. A lightweight, scalable alternative is needed.

Method: A one-class classification workflow where MiniLM (all-MiniLM-L6-v2) generates utterance embeddings, then multi-cluster boundaries are learned from training utterances. Out-of-domain utterances falling outside these boundaries are rejected as OOS intents.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art OOS intent detection performance on CLINC150, StackOverflow, and Banking77 datasets compared to baselines. Ablation studies confirm MiniLM's suitability for the embedding requirements.

Conclusion: The multi-cluster boundary learning approach with MiniLM embeddings effectively addresses OOS intent detection with lightweight parameters, strong performance, and practical deployability.

Abstract: Intent detection is a critical task that bridges human intents and system actions in human-machine interaction systems. However, there still exist challenges for detecting out-of-scope (OOS) intents. (i) The traditional methods view the OOS intent detection as a multi-class classification, then the detection accuracy decreases as the class number of the known intents increases; (ii) LLM-embedding methods require large parameters, that makes them difficult to train and practically deploy. Thus, this work proposes a multi-cluster boundary learning method to detect OOS intents via MiniLM embedding (i.e., all-MiniLM-L6-v2) in an one-class classification workflow. The method learns the boundaries of multi-cluster embeddings generated by MiniLM from the training utterances, and then rejects the out-of-domain utterances as OOS intents. Experiments are conducted on public CLINC150, StackOverflow and Banking77 datasets. The results show that the method achieves the state-of-the-art OOS intent detection performance compared the other baselines. Ablation studies are also conducted and the results show that the used MiniLM can better adapt to the workflow and utterance embedding requirements. The code is available at supplementary materials.

</details>


### [9] [When Implausible Tokens Get Reinforced: Tail-Aware Credit Calibration for LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07976)
*Xiuyi Lou,Zicheng Xu,Yu-Neng Chuang,Hoang Anh Duy Le,Zhaozhuo Xu,Guanchu Wang,Vladimir Braverman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Positive-Credit Contamination" as a failure mode in critic-free RL for LLMs, where erroneous tail tokens receive the same positive credit as plausible ones. The authors propose TACO (Tail-Aware Credit CalibratiOn), which computes a tail-risk score to selectively dampen positive credit for risky tokens, improving RL training stability and performance across multiple LLMs and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critic-free RL methods like GRPO use uniform credit assignment, broadcasting the same advantage to all tokens in a trajectory. The authors identify that this causes "Positive-Credit Contamination": low-probability, contextually erroneous tail tokens get reinforced identically to plausible tokens, leading to indiscriminate reinforcement of flawed reasoning. This motivates the need for token-level credit calibration that distinguishes between incidental noise and useful exploration.

Method: TACO (Tail-Aware Credit CalibratiOn) operates in two stages: (1) It computes a tail-risk score for each token using local generation context, assessing the probability that a token falls into the unreliable tail of the distribution — distinguishing between unexpected rarity (risky) and uncertainty-driven exploration (potentially useful). (2) It uses this score to calibrate positive credit assignment, dampening (but not zeroing out) gradients for high-risk tokens. This allows genuinely useful rare patterns to accumulate reinforcement over time while progressively suppressing incidental noise.

Result: Experiments across three LLMs and eight benchmarks demonstrate that TACO consistently outperforms GRPO-style baselines. TACO notably improves training stability and enables sustained performance gains in long-horizon RL settings.

Conclusion: Uniform credit assignment in critic-free RL suffers from Positive-Credit Contamination. TACO effectively addresses this via context-aware tail-risk scoring and calibrated credit dampening, yielding better performance and training stability. The method is open-sourced.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved remarkable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, widely used critic-free RL methods rely on uniform credit assignment, broadcasting the same advantage to all tokens regardless of their differences. We identify a critical failure mode of this design, which we refer to as Positive-Credit Contamination: low-probability tail tokens that are contextually erroneous receive identical positive credit to plausible ones within the same trajectory, resulting in the indiscriminate reinforcement of flawed reasoning behavior. To mitigate this issue, we propose Tail-Aware Credit calibratiOn (TACO), a method that calibrates uniform credit assignment to suppress undesirable positive updates. TACO first computes a tail-risk score that incorporates the local generation context to assess each token's risk of falling into the unreliable tail, distinguishing unexpected rarity from uncertainty-driven exploration. TACO then uses this score to tune positive credit for risky tokens without removing their gradients entirely, so that recurring useful rare patterns can accumulate reinforcement while incidental noise is progressively dampened. Experimental results across three LLMs and eight benchmarks show that TACO consistently outperforms GRPO-style baselines. Notably, TACO improves training stability, supporting sustained performance gains in long-horizon RL. The source code is available at: https://github.com/xiuyilou/TACO.

</details>


### [10] [ICDAR 2026 HIPE-OCRepair Competition on LLM-Assisted OCR Post-Correction for Historical Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08143)
*Maud Ehrmann,Emanuela Boros,Juri Opitz,Andrianos Michail,Florian Wagner,Simon Clematide*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HIPE-OCRepair-2026 is an ICDAR competition evaluating LLM-assisted OCR post-correction on historical documents (English, French, German, 17th–20th c.), using a retrieval-oriented evaluation framework and a harmonized multilingual dataset. Four participating teams explored strategies from zero-shot to fine-tuning, demonstrating that LLMs significantly improve OCR quality, though performance varies by language, noise level, and dataset, with over-correction on low-noise inputs remaining a key challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OCR post-correction is a persistent challenge in digital heritage: large-scale digitized collections suffer from legacy OCR errors, and re-digitization at scale is impractical. While LLMs offer a promising new avenue, their effectiveness across languages, document types, noise conditions, and their hallucination tendencies remain poorly understood. This motivates a systematic competition and benchmark.

Method: The competition provides the HIPE-OCRepair-2026 dataset, a harmonized multilingual corpus of historical newspapers and printed works. Participants correct noisy OCR transcripts at the paragraph/article level without access to source images. Evaluation uses a retrieval-oriented scoring approach rather than diplomatic transcription metrics. Four teams submitted systems employing strategies from zero-shot prompting to continued pre-training and fine-tuning of LLMs.

Result: Modern LLM-assisted systems substantially improve OCR quality, but performance is uneven across datasets, languages, and noise levels. A recurring issue is over-correction on low-noise inputs, underscoring the limitations of character-error-rate-centric evaluation alone.

Conclusion: LLM-based OCR post-correction holds significant promise for digital heritage collections, but robust evaluation must account for language-specific behavior, noise-level sensitivity, and the risk of over-correction. The publicly released dataset, scorer, and pipeline aim to facilitate reproducible future research.

Abstract: We present the results of HIPE-OCRepair-2026, an ICDAR competition on LLM-assisted OCR post-correction of historical documents. OCR post-correction remains a long-standing challenge in digital heritage: large-scale collections of digitized documents are affected by legacy OCR errors, while re-digitization at scale remains impractical. Large language models (LLMs) offers a major opportunity to revisit this challenge, yet their effectiveness across languages, document types, and noise conditions - and their tendency to hallucinate - remains insufficiently understood. HIPE-OCRepair-2026 pursues two objectives: (i) to evaluate the capabilities of modern OCR post-correction systems, and (ii) to provide a reproducible evaluation framework anchored in the HIPE-OCRepair-2026 dataset, a harmonized multilingual resource consolidating existing and newly curated historical datasets. Participants were tasked with correcting noisy OCR transcripts from historical newspapers and printed works in English, French, and German (17th-20th century), working at the level of coherent transcription units (paragraphs or articles) without access to source images. The evaluation adopts a retrieval-oriented rather than diplomatic scoring approach, reflecting the practical use case of search and access over digitized collections. Four teams submitted systems ranging from zero-shot prompting to continued pre-training and fine-tuning, offering insights into the merits of different adaptation strategies. Results show that modern LLM-assisted systems can significantly improve OCR quality, but performance varies across datasets, languages, and noise levels. Over-correction on low-noise inputs emerges as a recurring challenge, highlighting the importance of evaluation beyond character error reduction. The dataset, scorer, and evaluation pipeline are publicly released to support future research.

</details>


### [11] [A Reliability Assessment of LALM Audio Judges for Full-Duplex Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07985)
*A. Sayyad,J. Emmons,S. Jones,T. Lin,H. Krishnan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates Gemini models as "listening audio language models" (LALMs) that judge full-duplex agent conversations from raw stereo audio. Using Gemini 2.5 Flash validated against three human raters on 209 sessions across 8 dimensions, the study finds the LALM matches human reliability on most dimensions, transfers rank-ordering ability across Gemini family models, and costs ~100x less than human rating — establishing an empirical basis for LALMs as substitutes or supplementary raters in production audio evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual human rating of full-duplex (simultaneous two-way) spoken agent conversations is expensive, slow, and difficult to scale. The authors seek to determine whether Gemini-family LALMs can serve as reliable, cost-effective audio judges that score directly from raw stereo waveforms, potentially replacing or augmenting human raters in production evaluation pipelines.

Method: The study tests three Gemini models (2.5 Flash, 3.5 Flash, 3.1 Pro) as audio judges. The primary validation uses Gemini 2.5 Flash as ground-truth, evaluated against three calibrated human raters on 209 stereo sessions scored across 8 production dimensions. The dataset comprises 152 full-duplex conversations spanning 13 accent-and-condition strata, plus 57 adversarial defect-injected clips. Three statistical tests are applied: (i) Spearman rank correlation (LALM-human vs. human-human) with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals, (ii) agreement within 1 point of the three-rater human mean, and (iii) sensitivity analysis using Newcombe-Wilson 95% confidence intervals across 48 defect-dimension cells.

Result: For Gemini 2.5 Flash: (i) On 5/8 dimensions, LALM-human Spearman ρ departs from human-human ρ by ≤0.07; 7/8 dimensions have overlapping 95% bootstrap CIs. (ii) LALM agrees within 1 point of the human mean on 60–92% of sessions for 6/8 dimensions. (iii) On 45/48 defect-dimension cells, LALM sensitivity matches or exceeds humans. For model transfer: Gemini 3.5 Flash achieves simple agreement on all 8 dimensions; Gemini 3.1 Pro shows comparable rank correlation but rates several dimensions markedly lower than humans. Human rating costs approximately two orders of magnitude more than LALM inference.

Conclusion: Gemini 2.5 Flash provides defensible empirical evidence for deployment as a substitute or fourth rater on most evaluated dimensions. However, model substitution (e.g., upgrading to 3.5 Flash or 3.1 Pro) requires re-validation on calibration specifically, not merely relying on rank-correlation transfer. Four deployment risk areas are identified, and the ~100x cost advantage makes LALMs compelling for production evaluation cadences.

Abstract: We report the empirical reliability of Gemini models as audio judges that score full-duplex agent conversations directly from the raw stereo waveform, tested across three models in the Gemini family: 2.5 Flash, 3.5 Flash, and 3.1 Pro. Our primary evidence base uses Gemini 2.5 Flash as the ground-truth model, validated against three calibrated human raters on 209 stereo sessions, scored on 8 production dimensions: 152 full-duplex conversations across 13 accent-and-condition strata, together with 57 adversarial defect-injected clips. The evidence for Gemini 2.5 Flash is consistent across three tests. (i) On 5 of 8 dimensions the LALM-human Spearman rho departs from the pairwise human-human rho by at most 0.07, and on 7 of 8 dimensions the two quantities 95 percent bootstrap confidence intervals overlap. (ii) The LALM agrees with the three-rater human mean within 1 point on 60 to 92 percent of sessions on 6 of 8 dimensions. (iii) On 45 of 48 (defect, dimension) cells the LALM is as sensitive as humans or better under Newcombe-Wilson 95 percent confidence intervals, though most of these are underpowered nulls rather than demonstrated parity. Rank-ordering ability transfers across the Gemini family: 3.5 Flash improves simple agreement to 8 of 8 dimensions, while 3.1 Pro rates several dimensions markedly lower than humans despite comparable rank correlation. A model swap should be re-validated on calibration specifically, not assumed from rank-correlation alone. We identify four areas where deployment requires care, and we estimate that human rating alone for our current evaluation cadence costs roughly two orders of magnitude more than the equivalent LALM workload. The data presented here provides a defensible empirical basis for deploying the LALM as a substitute or fourth rater on the dimensions where the evidence supports it.

</details>


### [12] [LEXIC: Lightweight Eye-tracking eXtension via Injected Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08152)
*Sumin Lee,Kyeonghun Kim,Subeen Lee,Jiwon Yang,Tien Nguyen,Ken Ying-Kai Liao,Nam-Joon Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A gaze-only model for reading comprehension prediction is enhanced by injecting three word-level difficulty signals (GPT-2 surprisal, word frequency, word length) via concatenation or residual conditioning, yielding statistically consistent AUROC gains of +1.8–2.2 pp on unseen text, with concatenation also improving unseen reader generalization (+2.9 pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On EyeBench, text-aware models achieve 56–63% AUROC for reading comprehension prediction while gaze-only models perform at chance. The authors investigate how far a purely gaze-based model can be pushed without pretrained language models, using only lightweight word-level difficulty signals.

Method: Building on the EyeBench AhnCNN baseline (LEXIC-Base), two conditioning mechanisms are proposed: LEXIC-Concat (direct concatenation of three word-level signals — GPT-2 surprisal, word frequency, word length — to per-fixation input) and LEXIC-Res (a small prediction head estimates typical-reader gaze response; the encoder conditioned on the deviation). Evaluated with K=5 seed-ensemble training across ten folds on the OneStop reading comprehension task.

Result: Both LEXIC-Concat and LEXIC-Res yield statistically consistent AUROC gains on Unseen Text of +1.8 to +2.2 pp (Wilcoxon p ≤ 0.065). LEXIC-Concat additionally improves Unseen Reader by +2.9 pp (p = 0.010). LEXIC-Res shows a weaker +1.8 pp gain on Unseen Reader (p = 0.19), traced to the prediction head being calibrated to training readers and transferring poorly to out-of-distribution readers.

Conclusion: Lightweight, language-model-free lexical conditioning can meaningfully improve gaze-only reading comprehension prediction, but architectural choices matter: residual deviation-based conditioning suffers from reader-specific calibration, limiting out-of-distribution generalization compared to direct concatenation.

Abstract: On the recent EyeBench benchmark, predicting reading comprehension from eye movements exposes a stark gap: text-aware models using pretrained language models reach 56--63% AUROC, while gaze-only models operate at chance. We ask how far a gaze-only model can be pushed by lightweight, language-model-free conditioning. Building on the EyeBench AhnCNN baseline, LEXIC-Base, we propose two mechanisms to inject three precomputed word-level difficulty signals, GPT-2 surprisal, word frequency, and word length, into the per-fixation input: direct concatenation, LEXIC-Concat, and a residual mechanism, LEXIC-Res, where a small head predicts typical-reader gaze response and the encoder is conditioned on the deviation. On the OneStop reading comprehension task, with K=5 seed-ensemble training across ten folds, both mechanisms produce statistically consistent AUROC gains on Unseen Text, +1.8 to +2.2 percentage points, Wilcoxon p <= 0.065. LEXIC-Concat additionally lifts Unseen Reader by +2.9 percentage points, p = 0.010. We trace an architectural boundary in LEXIC-Res on Unseen Reader, +1.8 percentage points, p = 0.19, to the prediction head being calibrated to training readers, transferring imperfectly to out-of-distribution readers.

</details>


### [13] [Hallucination Self-Play: Bootstrapping Reinforced Detector via Evolved Generator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07993)
*Shiping Yang,Shining Liang,Weihao Liu,Wenbiao Ding,Linjun Shou,Lu Cheng,Angel X. Chang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hallucination Self-Play (HSP) is a framework where a detector and a generator—initialized from the same base model—iteratively bootstrap each other: the detector provides reward signals to train the generator via RLAIF to produce harder hallucinations, and the generator's outputs are used to further fine-tune the detector via rule-based RL, progressively improving faithfulness detection without external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for detecting faithfulness hallucinations rely on static LLM-based data synthesis, which limits iterative improvement. The authors aim to break this limitation by allowing the detector and generator to co-evolve.

Method: HSP initializes both a detector and a generator from the same base LLM. The detector is first fine-tuned on human-labeled data, then used as a reward model to train the generator via RLAIF to produce increasingly hard-to-detect hallucinations. The evolved generator then synthesizes new hallucination data, which is used to further optimize the detector through rule-based reinforcement learning. This self-play loop repeats, progressively strengthening both components.

Result: Experiments on the RAGTruth benchmark across two model families show that HSP progressively enhances a small LLM to match or surpass the performance of advanced LLMs, all without external supervision beyond the initial human-labeled seed data.

Conclusion: HSP demonstrates that iterative self-play between a generator and detector can effectively bootstrap hallucination detection capabilities, enabling smaller models to rival or exceed much larger, more advanced LLMs in faithfulness assessment.

Abstract: Identifying faithfulness hallucinations in LLM-generated outputs remains challenging due to the scarcity of high-quality annotated data. Recent work relies on advanced LLMs to synthesize training data, including rationales, labels, and hallucinated claims. However, these methods treat the generator as a static component, limiting iterative improvement of the detector. To address this limitation, we introduce Hallucination Self-Play (HSP), a novel framework that enables the detector to bootstrap with an evolved generator. HSP involves two roles initialized from the same base model, a detector that assesses the faithfulness of model outputs, and a generator that produces increasingly hard-to-detect hallucinated responses. Specifically, the detector is first fine-tuned on human-labeled data and then employed as a reward model to train the generator via reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF). In turn, the evolved generator synthesizes hallucination data to further optimize the detector through rule-based reinforcement learning. Experiments on RAGTruth benchmark and two model families demonstrate that the proposed framework can progressively enhance a small LLM to match or even outperform advanced LLMs without external supervision. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Hallucination-Self-Play-50B5 .

</details>


### [14] [From Execution to Education: A Bloom-Aligned Framework for Measuring Educational Control in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08009)
*Yi Zhang,Julia Rayz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A Bloom-aligned framework measures LLMs' ability to shift cognitive demand in programming tasks. Comparing general (Qwen3-Next-80B) and code-specialized (Qwen3-Coder-Next) models on 2,520 tasks, both reliably increase but struggle to lower cognitive demand. Probing reveals the general model shows clearer middle-layer separability, while the coder model's separability peaks deeper, demonstrating that execution performance does not imply educational control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the gap between LLMs' ability to solve programming tasks and their ability to adapt those tasks for learners at specified cognitive levels. The authors introduce educational control as a distinct capability — preserving instructional intent while shifting cognitive demand toward learning objectives — and argue that strong code generation does not guarantee this pedagogical skill.

Method: The authors define a Bloom-aligned framework using revised Bloom's Taxonomy as an operational scale of cognitive demand. They evaluate two intervention settings: (1) general difficulty control (harder/easier) and (2) Bloom's control (targeting higher/lower Bloom's levels). A matched model pair — Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct vs. Qwen3-Coder-Next — is evaluated across 2,520 tasks from three benchmarks. Analysis includes semantic-delta clustering to characterize output changes and layer-wise Fisher's Discriminant Ratio probing to examine internal representations.

Result: Both models exhibit a robust directional asymmetry: they reliably increase cognitive demand but struggle to lower it. The general instruction model shows clearer middle-layer separability for both general difficulty and Bloom-control contrasts. The coder model shows weaker separability for general difficulty and a deeper peak for Bloom-control contrasts, suggesting different internal processing strategies.

Conclusion: Strong execution performance does not automatically entail Bloom-aligned educational control. The directional asymmetry and representational differences between general and code-specialized models highlight that pedagogical task adaptation is a distinct capability requiring attention beyond raw coding proficiency.

Abstract: We introduce a Bloom-aligned framework for measuring educational control in Large Language Models (LLMs): the ability to preserve a task's instructional intent while shifting its cognitive demand toward specified learning objectives. We apply this framework to programming tasks in computer science education to study the gap between solving tasks and adapting them for learners. Using revised Bloom's Taxonomy as an operational scale of cognitive demand, we evaluate two intervention settings: general difficulty control, where models are asked to make tasks harder or easier, and Bloom's control, where models are asked to target higher or lower Bloom's levels. We evaluate a matched Qwen3-Next model pair, comparing Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct with Qwen3-Coder-Next across 2,520 tasks from three benchmarks. The framework reveals a robust directional asymmetry: both models reliably increase cognitive demand, but struggle to lower it. We further characterize these outcomes with semantic-delta clustering and layer-wise Fisher's Discriminant Ratio probing. Within this controlled comparison, the general model shows clearer middle-layer separability for both general difficulty and Bloom-control contrasts, whereas the coder model shows weaker separability for general difficulty and a deeper peak for Bloom-control contrasts. These results show that strong execution performance does not automatically entail Bloom-aligned educational control.

</details>


### [15] [Tool-Making and Self-Evolving LLM Agents in Low-Latency Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08010)
*Kalle Kujanpää,Ning Liu,Shahnawaz Alam,Yeshwanth Reddy Sura,Tianyu Yang,Kristina Klinkner,Shervin Malmasi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Production LLM agents waste resources regenerating code for repeated procedural steps. The authors replace this with a tool-making pipeline that compiles standard operating procedures (SOPs) into validated, versioned tools before deployment, then use them at runtime with fallback code generation. Deployed in a fulfillment center alarm-triage system, tool calls reduced p50 latency by 42%, cut error rates by up to 53%, and enabled a simpler architecture with a further 62% latency reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production LLM agents repeatedly regenerate code for identical procedural SOP steps on every request, wasting latency, compute, and introducing run-to-run variance that harms reliability. The authors seek to eliminate this redundant inference-time coding loop by pre-compiling repeated procedural knowledge into reusable, validated tools.

Method: The proposed agentic tool-making pipeline operates before deployment: it collects execution traces from the live environment, observes backend schemas and values, generates candidate tools, and repairs them against labeled test cases. The resulting tools are validated, versioned, and stored. At runtime, the production agent calls these tools directly (bypassing code generation) and falls back to traditional code generation only when no suitable tool exists. The approach is evaluated in a Fulfillment Center alarm-triage system with a 44-node SOP spanning heterogeneous metric backends.

Result: In production, tool calls reduced p50 latency by 42%. On 1,500 historical alarms, end-to-end error rate dropped by up to 53% by suppressing run-to-run variance. Compact structured verdicts from tools enabled a direct-call architecture that further reduced p50 latency by 62% in a controlled ablation. Versioned tools also improved auditability and exposed specification gaps and upstream data drift.

Conclusion: Self-evolving agents that pre-compile SOP steps into validated tools can make industrial LLM systems faster, more reliable, and easier to operate, demonstrating that shifting inference-time code generation to pre-deployment tool synthesis is a viable strategy for production LLM agent systems.

Abstract: Production LLM agents often waste latency and reliability by regenerating code for the same procedural steps on every request. We replace this inference-time coding loop with an agentic tool-making pipeline that compiles repeated SOP steps into validated, versioned tools before deployment. The tool-maker grounds synthesis in the live environment as it collects execution traces, observes backend schemas and values, generates candidate tools, and repairs them against labeled cases. At runtime, the production agent calls these tools directly and falls back to code generation only when needed. We deploy the approach in a Fulfillment Center alarm-triage system, where an agent diagnoses alarms against a 44-node SOP over heterogeneous metric backends. In production, tool calls reduce p50 latency by 42%. On 1,500 historical alarms, they reduce end-to-end error rate by up to 53% by suppressing run-to-run variance in repeated steps. Because tools return compact structured verdicts, they also enable a simpler direct-call architecture, reducing p50 latency by a further 62% in a controlled ablation. Versioned tools also improve auditability and expose specification gaps and upstream data drift. Our results show that self-evolving agents can make industrial LLM systems faster, more reliable, and easier to operate.

</details>


### [16] [Large-Language-Models-as-a-Judge in Theory-Agnostic Adaptive Metric-Alignment for Prototypical Networks in Personality Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08374)
*Jing Jie Tan,Ban-Hoe Kwan,Danny Wee-Kiat Ng,Yan-Chai Hum,Shih-Yu Lo,Po-An Chen,Noriyuki Kawarazaki,Kosuke Takano,Anissa Mokraoui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: JAM is a theory-agnostic framework for personality recognition that learns unified latent pseudo-facets from text without relying on predefined taxonomies, using an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network with Cross-Theory Harmonization and an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism to improve generalization across personality theories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional personality recognition is constrained by theory-dependent formulations that fit predefined psychological taxonomies (e.g., Big Five, MBTI), limiting cross-theory generalization. Personality traits are fundamentally theory-invariant, yet existing annotations reflect partial, inconsistent views of the same latent constructs. The authors aim to shift from theory-specific labeling toward discovering shared underlying psychological structure.

Method: The JAM framework comprises three core components: (1) an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network that learns structured psychological representations through clustering in embedding space; (2) a Cross-Theory Harmonization (CTH) approach integrating Human-Guided Linkage and Machine-Induced Consensus to unify heterogeneous datasets without predefined labels; and (3) an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism operating in two configurations — LLM-before-the-loop (pre-filtering ambiguous samples) and LLM-in-the-loop (guiding adaptive metric learning during training).

Result: Experiments demonstrate that JAM improves cross-framework generalization and overall performance compared to theory-dependent baselines. The framework successfully supports low-resource personality theories and establishes a strong step toward theory-agnostic personality inference.

Conclusion: JAM demonstrates that learning theory-agnostic psychological representations is feasible and beneficial, enabling personality inference directly from text without theory-specific labels. The released code, model weights, and artifacts support reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Personality recognition has traditionally been constrained by theory-dependent formulations, where models are trained to fit predefined psychological taxonomies rather than uncovering shared underlying behavioral structure. This limits generalization, as personality itself is better understood as theory-invariant, while existing annotations reflect only partial and sometimes inconsistent views of the same latent traits. In this work, we introduce JAM ((J)udge for (A)daptive (M)etric-Alignment), a theory-agnostic framework that shifts learning from adapting to predefined personality theories toward discovering unified latent pseudo-facets that capture shared psychological structure. Rather than constraining the model to any personality taxonomy during training or inference, the framework learns generalizable psychological representations and can infer an individual's latent psychological profile directly from the textual samples, without requiring theory-specific labels. JAM achieves this through an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network that learns structured representations via clustering in embedding space, together with a Cross-Theory Harmonization (CTH) approach that integrates (i) Human-Guided Linkage and (ii) Machine-Induced Consensus to unify heterogeneous datasets without relying on predefined labels. To further improve robustness and data quality, we incorporate an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism operating in two configurations, (i) LLM-before-the-loop and (ii) LLM-in-the-loop which identifies ambiguous samples to guide adaptive metric learning. Experiments show that JAM improves cross-framework generalization and performance, establishing a strong step toward theory-agnostic personality inference and supporting low-resource personality theories. The related code repository, model weights, and artifacts are available at https://research.jingjietan.com/JAM

</details>


### [17] [Can We Trust LLM's Logic? Quantifying Uncertainty, Coherence, and Robustness via a Graph-Based Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08017)
*Riccardo Revalor,Jalees Rehman,Debjit Pal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GRAPHEVAL introduces a graph-based framework for evaluating LLM reasoning faithfulness, proposing the Graph Reasoning Coherence Score (GRCS) as a novel uncertainty metric and Graph Self-Consistency (GSC) as a medoid-based decoding strategy that prioritizes reasoning fidelity over mere final-answer agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often produce flawed intermediate reasoning that Self-Consistency fails to detect, since SC only checks final-answer agreement without evaluating logical validity. This motivates the need for holistic, structure-aware uncertainty quantification that captures semantic and causal coherence across reasoning paths.

Method: GRAPHEVAL reframes UQ as a reasoning fidelity problem using graph-based representations. The GRCS metric quantifies semantic-structural consensus across the reasoning space, detecting mode collapse and confident hallucinations. GSC is a medoid-based decoding strategy that selects the most structurally central reasoning path. Adversarial medoid ablation tests the robustness of selected reasoning topology.

Result: GRCS is the only metric consistently negatively correlated with reasoning faithfulness across both large and small models. GSC reveals that SC accuracy is inflated by unfaithful lucky guesses in smaller models while preserving or improving accuracy in more capable ones. Adversarial ablation shows the GSC-selected path is a "load-bearing path" whose removal degrades faithfulness and, in targeted cases, accuracy.

Conclusion: Reasoning fidelity requires evaluating structural and semantic consensus, not just final answers. GRCS provides a principled UQ metric, while GSC offers a decoding alternative that trades nominal accuracy for faithfulness, exposing brittleness in smaller models and reinforcing robustness in larger ones.

Abstract: Large-Language Models (LLMs) can be prone to flawed and unfaithful reasoning that decoding strategies like Self-Consistency (SC) fail to detect as they evaluate only final-answer agreement while ignoring the logical validity of intermediate steps. This raises three fundamental questions: How can we reliably quantify uncertainty in LLM reasoning? Can semantic, structural, and causal awareness select more faithful reasoning compared to naïve majority voting? and How robust is reasoning topology under adversarial conditions? To address these questions, we introduce GRAPHEVAL, a graph-based reasoning framework that re-frames uncertainty quantification (UQ) as a holistic reasoning fidelity problem. We propose a novel UQ metric, Graph Reasoning Coherence Score (GRCS), that quantifies semantic-structural consensus of the reasoning space and captures pathological mode collapse and confident hallucinations. We find that GRCS is the only metric that is consistently negatively correlated with reasoning faithfulness across both more capable and smaller models. Additionally, we introduce Graph Self-Consistency (GSC), a medoid-based decoding strategy that trades nominal accuracy for reasoning fidelity, exposing the degree to which SC is inflated by unfaithful lucky guesses in smaller models, while preserving or improving accuracy in more capable ones. Finally, through adversarial medoid ablation, we demonstrate that the GSC-selected path acts as a "load-bearing path" and forcing models away from it degrades reasoning faithfulness and, in targeted cases, causes drops in accuracy.

</details>


### [18] [Structured Pruning of Large Language Models via Power Transformation and Sign-Preserving Score Aggregation with Adaptive Feature Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08027)
*Ryota Kobayashi,Tsubasa Hirakawa,Takayoshi Yamashita,Hironobu Fujiyoshi,Yasunori Ishii,Tomoyuki Okuno,Kazuki Kozuka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes an improved structured pruning method for LLMs that adapts AFR (an unstructured pruning technique) by solving three key issues: heterogeneous score distribution mismatch, sign information loss, and outlier influence — using power transformation, sign-preserving aggregation, and percentile-based outlier removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive Feature Retention (AFR) is effective for unstructured pruning but cannot be directly applied to structured pruning due to three fundamental problems: (1) distribution mismatch between heterogeneous pruning scores across different components, (2) loss of sign information that indicates optimization direction consistency, and (3) distortion caused by outliers. Structured pruning is more desirable for practical inference speedup, motivating a principled adaptation.

Method: A unified three-component approach: (1) power transformation for nonlinear distribution alignment of heterogeneous pruning scores, (2) sign-preserving score aggregation to retain optimization direction consistency information, and (3) percentile-based outlier removal to mitigate outlier distortion. Applied to structured pruning of Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B, and LLaVA-v1.5-13B.

Result: The proposed method achieves accuracy comparable to unstructured AFR pruning while delivering practical inference speedup through structured pruning. Demonstrated across three model families: Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B, and the multimodal LLaVA-v1.5-13B.

Conclusion: The unified approach successfully bridges the gap between unstructured and structured pruning of LLMs, enabling practical deployment benefits (actual inference speedup) without sacrificing the accuracy retention capabilities that make AFR effective in the unstructured setting.

Abstract: This paper proposes an improved structured pruning method for large language models (LLMs) that addresses key challenges in adapting Adaptive Feature Retention (AFR), an unstructured pruning technique, to structured pruning. When applying AFR to structured pruning, three major problems arise: distribution mismatch between heterogeneous pruning scores, loss of sign information indicating optimization direction consistency, and influence of outliers. To address these issues, we propose a unified approach combining power transformation for nonlinear distribution alignment, sign-preserving score aggregation, and percentile-based outlier removal. Experiments on Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B, and LLaVA-v1.5-13B demonstrate that our method maintains accuracy comparable to unstructured pruning while achieving practical inference speedup through structured pruning.

</details>


### [19] [PLURAL: A Global Dataset for Value Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08034)
*Dhruv Agarwal,Anya Shukla,Tanya Goyal,Aditya Vashistha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PLURAL is a large-scale, value-focused preference dataset (~500K triplets across 20 countries) built from the Integrated Values Survey (92 countries). It enables pluralistic alignment of LLMs, reducing value-prediction error by up to 27.7% and passing human evaluation in India, Brazil, and Japan.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs disproportionately reflect Western values, limiting their capacity to represent diverse global value systems. There is a scarcity of large-scale, culturally grounded preference data that captures both cross-national value differences and within-country diversity for pluralistic alignment.

Method: A two-stage generation pipeline: (1) mapping nationally representative IVS survey responses from 92 countries into structured value signals, and (2) transforming those signals into synthetic preference triplets (scenario + preferred/rejected completions) that preserve normative value orientations while producing realistic, context-grounded comparisons. The initial release covers 20 countries with ~500K triplets.

Result: Three evaluations: (i) dataset-level validation confirms preservation of cross-country value differences and within-country diversity; (ii) automated evaluation shows training on PLURAL reduces mean absolute error in cultural profile alignment by up to 27.7% vs. strong baselines; (iii) blind human evaluation with 176 judges from India, Brazil, and Japan finds PLURAL-aligned responses significantly more representative of their national values.

Conclusion: PLURAL provides a learnable, scalable signal for value steering in LLMs, enabling pluralistic alignment that better reflects the normative diversity of non-Western populations. It offers a practical resource for culturally attuned model fine-tuning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are used worldwide, yet disproportionately reflect Western values, limiting their ability to represent diverse value systems. We introduce PLURAL, a large-scale, value-focused preference dataset grounded in the Integrated Values Survey (IVS), a nationally representative survey spanning 92 countries. Using a two-stage generation pipeline, we transform survey responses into synthetic preference triplets that preserve normative value signals while producing realistic scenarios. We release an initial version of PLURAL containing ~500,000 preference triplets representing people in 20 diverse countries. We evaluate PLURAL in three ways: (i) dataset-level validation showing that it preserves both cross-country value differences and within-country diversity from the original survey; (ii) automated evaluation showing that training on PLURAL improves alignment with target countries' cultural profiles, reducing mean absolute error by up to 27.7% relative to strong baselines; and (iii) blind human evaluation with 176 evaluators in India, Brazil, and Japan, who judge PLURAL-aligned responses as more representative of their national values. Together, these results show that PLURAL contains learnable signal for value steering, offering a scalable resource for pluralistic alignment. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/agdhruv/plural-alignment

</details>


### [20] [What LLM Forecasters Know but Don't Say: Probing Internal Representations for Calibration and Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08046)
*Raphaël Sarfati,Pratyush Ranjan Tiwari,Siddharth Boppana,Christopher J. Earls,Srikar Varadaraj,Eric Ho*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LM forecasters are poorly calibrated and their CoT reasoning is unfaithful. Probing internal representations yields better calibration, detects when reasoning ignores evidence changes (functioning as a "lie detector"), and reveals that forecasts are largely fixed before reasoning — enabling 30–47% token savings via pre-reasoning triage without accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuned LMs for forecasting suffer from two key problems: (1) they are often poorly calibrated despite accuracy, and (2) their chain-of-thought reasoning may not faithfully reflect the actual evidence driving their forecasts. The authors hypothesize that internal representations may provide a more direct and reliable window into both calibration and reasoning faithfulness than surface-level CoT traces.

Method: Using Eternis-Forecaster 8B on the OpenForesight dataset (with validation on GLM-4.7-Flash and GLM-4.5-Air), the authors: (1) train representation-pooling probes on intermediate activations to assess calibration; (2) test CoT faithfulness via evidence ablation (removing influential sources) and diversionary injection, comparing probe-predicted changes against reasoning trace changes; (3) evaluate probes as "lie detectors" by tracking whether activations reflect behavioral shifts when CoT does not; and (4) use forced answering with a single pre-reasoning pass to recover committed answers and confidence, then route questions based on answer distribution spread to save tokens.

Result: Representation-pooling probes achieve substantially better calibration than the model's own output. Probes function as effective lie detectors: their activations track behavioral shifts after evidence perturbations far better than CoT traces, predicting the direction of change in 84% of cases even when CoT conceals the perturbation. Pre-reasoning forced answering reveals forecasts are largely fixed before reasoning; routing by spread of the pre-set answer distribution saves 30–47% of generated tokens with no accuracy loss.

Conclusion: Probing internal representations is a practical and effective tool for calibrating, auditing, and triaging LM-based forecasters and reasoning models. The finding that forecasts are substantially pre-determined before explicit reasoning challenges assumptions about CoT's causal role and opens avenues for efficient inference via early-exit or selective reasoning strategies.

Abstract: Large language models fine-tuned for forecasting can be accurate yet poorly calibrated, and their chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning may not faithfully reflect the evidence behind a forecast. We ask whether internal representations offer a more direct window into both. Working with Eternis-Forecaster 8B on OpenForesight, we train representation-pooling probes on intermediate activations and find they achieve substantially better calibration; a result that also holds for GLM-4.7-Flash and GLM-4.5-Air. We then assess CoT faithfulness through evidence ablation and diversionary injection: removing an influential source in the prompt often changes the model's forecast while leaving the reasoning trace untouched. The same probes function as lie detectors: their activations track behavioral shifts far better than the reasoning trace does, and they also predict the direction of change in 84% of cases, including when the CoT conceals the perturbation's influence. Finally, forced answering reveals that forecasts are largely fixed before reasoning begins: a single pre-reasoning pass recovers the committed answer and confidence, and routing questions by the spread of this pre-set answer distribution saves 30-47% of generated tokens, with no loss of accuracy. Together, these results establish probing internal representations as a practical tool for calibrating, auditing, and triaging language model forecasters and reasoning models more broadly.

</details>


### [21] [Holographic Neural PCFG for Unsupervised Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08063)
*Ryosuke Yamaki,Daichi Mochihashi,Nobutaka Shimada,Tadahiro Taniguchi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hol-PCFG introduces holographic embeddings with circular correlation to give PCFG rule probabilities a closed-form algebraic structure, achieving state-of-the-art unsupervised constituency parsing across six languages while reducing rule-scoring parameters by 99.94% and enabling character-level Japanese parsing without morphological segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Neural PCFGs rely on opaque, high-capacity black-box networks for rule scoring, leaving rule probabilities without interpretable mathematical form. The authors seek to replace these with a transparent algebraic formulation that preserves or improves parsing performance.

Method: Hol-PCFG recasts PCFG rule scoring as algebraic relation modeling using Holographic Embeddings (Nickel et al., 2016). Specifically, it models left-child, right-child, and lexical-emission relations via circular correlation over grammar-symbol embeddings constrained to a torus. This yields a closed-form expression for every rule probability that structurally encodes the compositional nature of grammar rules by construction.

Result: Hol-PCFG achieves state-of-the-art unsupervised constituency parsing performance on six languages, reduces rule-scoring parameter count by 99.94% compared to the baseline Neural PCFG, and exhibits more stable training. It further demonstrates the ability to parse Japanese directly from raw characters without morphological segmentation, matching morpheme-level performance.

Conclusion: A structurally grounded, low-parameter algebraic approach to PCFG rule scoring can match or exceed black-box neural alternatives in unsupervised constituency parsing, offering both interpretability and practical benefits such as training stability and morphological-segmentation-free parsing.

Abstract: Unsupervised constituency parsing aims to accurately induce latent tree structures from raw text alone. Recent neural parameterizations of PCFGs achieve strong performance in both supervised and unsupervised parsing, yet rely on high-capacity black-box networks for rule scoring -- as exemplified by the Neural PCFG family -- leaving rule probabilities without an interpretable mathematical form. In this paper, we propose Holographic Neural PCFG (Hol-PCFG), which recasts PCFG rule scoring as algebraic relation modeling among grammar-symbol embeddings. Hol-PCFG adapts Holographic Embeddings (Nickel et al., 2016), which scores knowledge-graph triples via circular correlation, to the left-child, right-child, and lexical-emission relations over torus-constrained embeddings, giving every rule probability a closed form that carries the intrinsic structure of grammar rules by construction. Hol-PCFG achieves state-of-the-art parsing performance in six languages while cutting rule-scoring parameters by 99.94% relative to the baseline model and training more stably. Additionally, we demonstrate that Hol-PCFG can parse Japanese directly from characters without any morphological segmentation, retaining nearly the same morpheme-level performance.

</details>


### [22] [COBART: Controlled, Optimized, Bidirectional and Auto-Regressive Transformer for Ad Headline Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08071)
*Yashal Shakti Kanungo,Gyanendra Das,Pooja A,Sumit Negi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A prefix-controlled BART fine-tuning method for generating ad headlines that achieves superior CTR and allows customizable headline length for different ad formats, outperforming baselines by 25.82% in ROUGE-L and 5.82% in estimated CTR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ad headline generation methods struggle to adapt to evolving ad formats and changing creative requirements. There is a need for a flexible method that can generate optimized headlines with controllable length to fit diverse ad formats while maintaining high CTR and quality.

Method: The authors propose using prefix control tokens in conjunction with BART fine-tuning. The prefix tokens encode desired constraints (e.g., headline length), allowing the model to generate headlines tailored to specific ad formats. The approach is architecture-agnostic and adaptable to different optimization criteria.

Result: The proposed method achieves a 25.82% improvement in ROUGE-L score and a 5.82% improvement in estimated CTR compared to a previously published strong ad headline generation baseline.

Conclusion: Prefix-controlled BART fine-tuning is an effective, flexible approach for generating optimized ad headlines that meet diverse format requirements, and it can be readily extended to other generative tasks with different constraints and optimization objectives.

Abstract: Online ads are essential to all businesses and ad headlines are one of their core creative component. Existing methods can generate headlines automatically and also optimize their click-through-rate (CTR) and quality. However, evolving ad formats and changing creative requirements make it difficult to generate optimized & customized headlines. We propose a novel method that uses prefix control tokens along with BART fine-tuning. It yields the highest CTR and also allows users to control the length of generated headlines for use across different ad formats. The method is also flexible and can easily be adapted to other architectures, creative requirements and optimization criteria. Our experiments demonstrate a 25.82% increment in Rouge-L and a 5.82% increment in estimated CTR over previously published strong ad headline generation baseline.

</details>


### [23] [MASTE: A Multi-Agent Pipeline for Zero-Shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08080)
*Ao Hong,Lehang Wang,Zhirun Yue,Mingxin Wang,Zihan Wang,Houde Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MASTE proposes a multi-agent pipeline that decomposes zero-shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction into four sequential stages, with specialized agents handling distinct subtasks.
