This training-free approach substantially outperforms existing zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines across four benchmarks, narrowing the gap to supervised methods without using any labeled triplets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs struggle with zero-shot ASTE because single-pass generation forces the model to simultaneously determine span boundaries, opinion grouping, and sentiment polarity in one decoding step. Few-shot ICL and chain-of-thought prompting offer only marginal gains and depend on in-domain demonstrations or hand-crafted reasoning prompts, neither of which is available in true zero-shot settings.

Method: MASTE is a multi-agent pipeline that decomposes ASTE into four sequential stages: (1) aspect term extraction, (2) opinion term extraction, (3) aspect-opinion pairing/grouping, and (4) sentiment polarity classification. Each stage is handled by a specialized agent with explicit conditioning on outputs from prior stages, enabling fully training-free, zero-shot operation across different LLM backbones.

Result: Extensive experiments on four ASTE benchmarks demonstrate that MASTE substantially outperforms zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines using the same backbone models. The approach narrows the performance gap to fully supervised methods without requiring any labeled triplet data.

Conclusion: Decomposing complex structured prediction tasks like ASTE into sequential subtasks handled by specialized agents is an effective strategy for zero-shot settings. The MASTE pipeline is backbone-agnostic, training-free, and achieves significant improvements over existing zero-shot approaches, with code publicly released.

Abstract: Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction (ASTE) requires jointly identifying (aspect, opinion, sentiment) triples from a given review sentence. While large language models (LLMs) achieve strong zero-shot performance on many NLP benchmarks, their effectiveness on ASTE remains limited, as single-pass generation forces the model to determine span boundaries, opinion grouping, and sentiment polarity in a single decoding step. Common remedies, such as few-shot in-context learning and chain-of-thought prompting, offer only marginal improvements and rely heavily on either in-domain demonstrations sampled from labeled training data or carefully engineered reasoning prompts, neither of which is broadly available in zero-shot deployment. Inspired by the classical agent paradigm, we propose MASTE, a multi-agent pipeline for zero-shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction. MASTE decomposes ASTE into four sequential stages, where specialized agents handle different compositional subtasks with explicit conditioning on prior outputs. This design enables entirely training-free zero-shot ASTE and generalizes across different backbones and datasets. Extensive experiments on four ASTE benchmarks show that MASTE substantially outperforms zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines under the same backbone, narrowing the gap to fully supervised methods without using any labeled triplets. Code is available at https://github.com/Hankerlove/MASTE.

</details>


### [24] [COALA: Robust Contextualized Speech-augmented Language Modeling for ASR via Contrastive Regularizer and Biasing Score Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08117)
*Jhih-Rong Guo,Bi-Cheng Yan,Tien-Hong Lo,Berlin Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: COALA is a contextual biasing framework for speech-augmented language models that maps SLM latent representations into a discriminative space to match audio segments with candidate entities, addressing training collapse in multi-entity scenarios and achieving strong performance on LibriSpeech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLMs have inherent context-window limitations, making it challenging to identify relevant target entities from large-scale biasing lists, especially in multi-target utterances where multiple rare words co-occur — a scenario that causes training collapse in prior methods.

Method: COALA maps SLM latent representations into a specialized discriminative space to quantify matching intensity between audio segments and candidate entities. This scoring-based approach enables effective identification of relevant entities from large biasing lists while handling complex multi-entity utterances.

Result: COALA consistently achieves superior contextual biasing performance across various biasing list scales on the LibriSpeech benchmark.

Conclusion: COALA provides a robust solution for integrating external knowledge into SLM-based ASR systems, effectively overcoming context-window limitations and multi-target training instability to improve domain-specific entity recognition.

Abstract: Contextual biasing seeks to integrate external knowledge into automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems to accurately recognize domain-specific entities. In this paper, we propose COALA (Contextualized ASR Leveraging Biasing Scoring), a robust framework designed to enhance speech-augmented language models (SLMs) in complex multi-entity scenarios. Considering the inherent context-window limitations of SLMs, identifying relevant target entities from a large-scale biasing list is crucial for effective recognition. To this end, COALA maps SLM latent representations into a specialized discriminative space to quantify the matching intensity between audio segments and candidate entities. Furthermore, we address the training collapse in prior study when handling multi-target utterances-where multiple rare words co-occur. Experimental results on the LibriSpeech benchmark demonstrate that COALA consistently achieves superior contextual biasing performance across various biasing list scales.

</details>


### [25] [SQuaD-SQL: Efficient Text-to-SQL with Small Language Models via LLM-Guided Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08161)
*Wangyu Wu,Xiaojian Lin,Rong Fu,Zaiyang Yu,Xuhang Chen,Wenjun Yu,Zhenhong Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SQuaD-SQL is a knowledge distillation and synthetic data generation framework that enables small language models (SLMs) to achieve near-LLM performance on the Text-to-SQL task (86.9% execution accuracy on WikiSQL) while offering faster inference and lower memory consumption, making them viable for resource-constrained deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models perform well on Text-to-SQL but are computationally prohibitive for deployment in resource-limited environments. There is a need to bridge the performance gap so that smaller, more efficient models can serve as practical alternatives.

Method: The approach consists of three components: (1) LLM-based synthetic data generation using carefully designed prompts to extract structured knowledge; (2) parameter-efficient fine-tuning that enables full training on a single consumer-grade GPU; and (3) domain-adaptive fine-tuning using domain-specific synthetic data to boost targeted-domain performance.

Result: On the WikiSQL test set, SQuaD-SQL achieves 86.9% execution accuracy, approaching LLM-level performance while providing faster inference and lower memory usage.

Conclusion: With appropriate training strategies — specifically knowledge distillation and synthetic data generation — small language models can serve as practical, efficient alternatives to LLMs for Text-to-SQL applications in resource-limited settings.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL is a fundamental task in natural language processing that enables users to interact with structured databases using natural language. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance on this task, their substantial computational requirements hinder deployment in resource-constrained settings. In this paper, we introduce SQuaD-SQL (Small-Qualified and Distilled for SQL), a novel approach that empowers small language models (SLMs) to approach the performance of LLMs on the Text-to-SQL task while significantly improving efficiency through knowledge distillation and synthetic data generation. Our method comprises three key components: (1) LLM-based synthetic data generation, where structured knowledge is extracted from LLMs via carefully designed prompting strategies; (2) parameter-efficient fine-tuning, enabling full model training on a single consumer-grade GPU; and (3) domain-adaptive fine-tuning, where domain-specific synthetic data further enhances performance in targeted domains. Experiments on the WikiSQL dataset demonstrate that SQuaD-SQL achieves an execution accuracy of 86.9% on the test set, approaching the performance of LLMs while offering faster inference and lower memory usage. These results suggest that, with proper training strategies, SLMs can serve as practical and efficient alternatives for Text-to-SQL applications in resource-limited environments.

</details>


### [26] [Hidden Decoding at Scale: Latent Computation Scaling for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08186)
*Aiwei Liu,Cheng Shi,Chuhan Wu,Ci Lei,Di Lu,Donald He,Fan Zhang,Fanhao Kong,Feifei Zhang,Guan Wang,Haicheng Wang,Haoyu Liu,Houjin Yu,Jiachen Ding,Jiayi Feng,Jie Zhou,Jijun Chi,Jindi Shi,Jing Lei,Junjie Zhang,Laiyi Li,Le Tian,Linhao Zhang,Miao Fan,Sijun Zhang,Wei Jia,Weiwei Shi,Wenhan Li,Wentao Zhao,Wenteng Liang,Xiao Zhou,Xiaojin Zhou,Xihuai Wang,Xinyu Gao,Xuanliang Wang,Xuyang Ao,Yang Yu,Yangxiu You,Yinuo Zhao,Yufei Kuang,Yufei Wang,Yuan Liu,Yuan Liu,Yuwen Chen,Zhencong Tian,Zhongyin Zhao,Zilin Yu,Zitao Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hidden Decoding expands each token into multiple streams along the sequence-length dimension during continued pretraining, enabling fixed-backbone LLM scaling without adding layers. Stream-Factorized Attention keeps costs manageable, and experiments at 80B and 617B MoE scales demonstrate the first practical sequence-length scaling method for frontier LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling LLMs typically requires enlarging the Transformer backbone with costly full pretraining. Depth-recurrent (looped) Transformers add per-token computation but are incompatible with standard pipeline parallelism. The authors seek a fixed-backbone scaling method that adds per-token computation in a way that integrates with existing large-model training infrastructure.

Method: Hidden Decoding operates during continued pretraining (CPT) by expanding each input token into n parallel streams, each with independent embedding tables. Intermediate streams' key-value caches serve as context, enabling richer internal computation without adding or widening layers. To control the quadratic attention cost, Stream-Factorized Attention restricts cross-stream attention to only a few layers — most layers attend within-stream — reducing complexity from O(n²) to approximately linear in n.

Result: At frontier scale, WeLM-HD4-80B and WeLM-HD4-617B (both at n=4) outperform matched non-HD baselines. This represents the first demonstrated sequence-length scaling method at the 100B+ MoE scale. Gains increase monotonically with expansion factor n, validating sequence-length expansion as a practical fixed-backbone scaling path.

Conclusion: Sequence-length scaling via Hidden Decoding provides a viable and practical alternative to backbone enlargement for improving already-strong LLMs, especially at frontier scales where full retraining is prohibitively expensive.

Abstract: Scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) has been driven mainly by enlarging the Transformer backbone, but for an already-strong model this requires another round of costly pretraining. We study whether an existing backbone can keep improving by allocating more computation to each token while leaving the Transformer backbone fixed. Depth-recurrent (looped) Transformers pursue this goal but are hard to scale, because looped computation does not fit naturally with the pipeline parallelism used to train the largest models. We add computation along the sequence-length dimension, where the extra computation is simply a longer input and stays compatible with standard large-model training. We propose Hidden Decoding, a sequence-length scaling method applied during continued pretraining (CPT). It expands each token into n streams with independent embedding tables and keeps the intermediate streams' key-value cache as context, so each token performs more internal computation without adding or widening Transformer layers. To keep this affordable at scale, we introduce Stream-Factorized Attention, in which most layers attend only within each stream and only a few layers mix across streams, reducing the attention cost from quadratic to roughly linear in n. Experiments support two scaling results. At frontier scale, we train WeLM-HD4-80B and WeLM-HD4-617B at n=4 and improve their matched non-HD baselines, making Hidden Decoding the first demonstrated sequence-length scaling method at the 100B+ MoE scale. Across expansion factors, the gains grow as n increases, showing that sequence-length expansion is a practical fixed-backbone scaling path for frontier-scale LLMs.

</details>


### [27] [Diarization-Guided Qwen-ASR Adaptation for Multilingual Two-Speaker Conversational Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08208)
*Hao Wu,RongQi Han,Zhen Wang,Wei Liang,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A modular speaker diarization front end combined with an adapted Qwen3-ASR-1.7B model achieves a tcpMER of 23.70 (dev) and 17.97 (eval) on the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge Task 1, with supervised fine-tuning, LoRA on synthetic speech, and GRPO reinforcement learning yielding cumulative gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge Task 1 on multilingual two-speaker conversational speech, requiring robust speaker-attributed ASR in code-switching, multi-language scenarios.

Method: A pipeline architecture: (1) speaker diarization front end with VAD, CAMPPlus embeddings, two-speaker spectral clustering, and RTTM-based segmentation; (2) Qwen3-ASR-1.7B adapted via supervised full fine-tuning, LoRA fine-tuning on TTS-generated synthetic speech, and GRPO reinforcement learning with WER/CER rewards plus hallucination, repetition, and length-deviation penalties.

Result: Dev set tcpMER of 23.70 (6.83 absolute points improvement over baseline Qwen-ASR-1.7B); eval set tcpMER of 17.97. Supervised fine-tuning contributed the largest gain; LoRA and RL further improved robustness.

Conclusion: The modular diarization-plus-adapted-ASR pipeline is effective for multilingual conversational speech. Combining supervised fine-tuning, synthetic data augmentation via TTS, and GRPO-based reinforcement learning yields substantial and complementary error reductions.

Abstract: This paper describes our self-designed system for Task 1 of the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge for multilingual two-speaker conversational speech. The system combines a modular speaker diarization front end with a challenge-adapted Qwen3-ASR-1.7B recognizer. The diarization front end performs voice activity detection, subsegment generation, CAMPPlus speaker embedding extraction, two-speaker spectral clustering, and RTTM-based audio segmentation. The resulting speaker-attributed segments are grouped by language or region and decoded by the adapted ASR model. For ASR adaptation, we first perform supervised full fine-tuning on the official training data, then apply LoRA fine-tuning with synthetic speech generated by a three-pipeline TTS-based synthetic speech augmentation framework, and finally refine the model using GRPO reinforcement learning with rewards based on WER/CER and penalties for hallucination, repetition, and length deviation. On the official development set, the full system achieves an average tcpMER of 23.70, reducing the error rate by 6.83 absolute points relative to the released Qwen-ASR-1.7B performance. On the final evaluation set, the system achieves an average tcpMER of 17.97. Ablation results show that supervised fine-tuning provides the largest gain, while synthetic-speech LoRA adaptation and reinforcement learning further improve robustness.

</details>


### [28] [Best-of-$N$ TTS Evaluation is Confounded by ASR Family Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08256)
*Taehyung Yu,Seongjae Kang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Best-of-N inference with ASR verifiers for TTS suffers from evaluator-verifier coupling bias. The authors propose cross-family rank ensembles that mitigate this confound, achieving state-of-the-art WER reductions without degrading MOS metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify and address a methodological confound in BoN inference for TTS: verifier quality rankings are not absolute but depend strongly on which ASR family is used as the evaluator, undermining the reliability of single-evaluator benchmarking.

Method: The authors systematically measure verifier-evaluator coupling across Whisper, wav2vec 2.0, and HuBERT families using F5-TTS on LibriSpeech-PC test-clean. They quantify oracle headroom recovery and linear CKA similarity between representations. They then propose two cross-family rank ensemble strategies — rank-averaging and conjunctive max-rank — that aggregate rankings from diverse ASR verifier families to select BoN candidates.

Result: Same-family verifier-evaluator pairs recover 2–3× more oracle headroom than cross-family pairs despite high representational similarity (linear CKA 0.978). Cross-family rank ensembles achieve the lowest mean WER across three independent evaluators (1.61% at N=10, −12% relative to F5-TTS) with no degradation under SIM-o/UTMOS. The best single verifier reduces WER from 2.06% to 1.72% (−16.5%) under the official F5-TTS evaluator.

Conclusion: Cross-evaluator triangulation should become default reporting practice for BoN TTS inference, as single-evaluator results are unreliable due to systematic verifier-evaluator coupling that persists even with near-identical representations.

Abstract: Best-of-$N$ (BoN) inference improves content consistency in zero-shot text-to-speech by selecting from $N$ candidates with an automatic speech recognition (ASR) verifier. We identify an underexplored evaluation confound: a verifier's apparent quality depends strongly on which ASR family judges it. On LibriSpeech-PC test-clean~\citep{librispeechpc} with F5-TTS~\citep{f5tts}, verifier rankings reverse across Whisper, wav2vec~2.0, and HuBERT evaluators, and same-family verifier-evaluator pairs recover 2-3$\times$ more oracle headroom than cross-family pairs despite near-identical representations (linear CKA $0.978$) -- a pattern consistent with identity- or lineage-level coupling rather than representational overlap. We propose two \textbf{cross-family rank ensembles} (rank-averaging and conjunctive max-rank) that attain the lowest mean WER across three independent evaluators -- $1.61\%$ at $N{=}10$ ($-12\%$ relative to F5-TTS) -- with no measurable degradation under automatic SIM-o/UTMOS metrics; the best single verifier drives WER from $2.06\%$ to $1.72\%$ ($-16.5\%$) under the official F5-TTS evaluator. We recommend cross-evaluator triangulation as default reporting practice.

</details>


### [29] [XALPHA: A Memory-Driven AI Quant Researcher for Hypothesis-to-Code Alpha Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08332)
*Fengyuan Liu,Yuchen Fu,Yuqi Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: XAlpha is a memory-driven AI quant researcher that transforms alpha discovery from isolated factor generation into a continuous, closed-loop process of reading, hypothesizing, implementing, validating, reflecting, and evolving, achieving superior performance on CSI300.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial markets are noisy, non-stationary, and high-dimensional, making predictive signal discovery difficult. Existing approaches automate isolated steps (factor design, search, evaluation) rather than functioning as end-to-end quant researchers capable of absorbing external knowledge, closing the hypothesis-to-code validation loop, and learning from accumulated feedback.

Method: XAlpha features: (1) a multi-source research memory system integrating report-grounded financial knowledge with discovery feedback; (2) a Macro Brain that plans research themes and selects Archetypes; (3) a Micro Brain that transforms hypotheses into executable factor code and verifies ex-ante tri-alignment among hypothesis idea, code logic, and financial plausibility; (4) a Cross Brain that consolidates empirical outcomes into generation-level feedback, cycle-level summaries, and archetype-level research cues for future exploration.

Result: Experiments on CSI300 demonstrate that XAlpha achieves stronger overall alpha discovery performance compared to representative baselines.

Conclusion: XAlpha establishes a closed-loop, memory-driven paradigm for alpha discovery, enabling continuous learning and evolution that surpasses existing isolated-step automation methods.

Abstract: Financial markets are noisy, non-stationary, and high-dimensional, making it difficult to discover predictive and robust trading signals. Alpha discovery has evolved from manual factor design to machine learning, evolutionary search, and recent LLM-based frameworks, improving the efficiency of factor generation, search, and evaluation. However, existing methods still mostly automate isolated steps, rather than functioning as end-to-end quant researchers that can absorb external knowledge, close the hypothesis-to-code validation loop, and learn from accumulated discovery feedback. To fill this gap, we introduce XAlpha, a memory-driven AI Quant Researcher for continuous hypothesis-to-code alpha discovery. XAlpha maintains a multi-source research memory system that integrates report-grounded financial knowledge with discovery feedback from prior generations and research cycles. Guided by this memory system, a Macro Brain plans research themes and selects suitable Archetypes; a Micro Brain transforms the planned hypothesis pool into executable factor code and verifies ex-ante tri-alignment among the hypothesis idea, code logic, and financial plausibility; and a Cross Brain consolidates empirical outcomes into generation-level feedback, cycle-level summaries, and archetype-level research cues for future exploration. In this way, XAlpha turns alpha mining from isolated factor generation into a closed-loop research process that continuously reads, hypothesizes, implements, validates, reflects, and evolves. Experiments on CSI300 show that XAlpha achieves stronger overall alpha discovery performance than representative baselines.

</details>


### [30] [TypeProbe: Recovering Type Representations from Hidden States of Pre-trained Code Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08339)
*Giuliano Gorgone,Fausto Carcassi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether pretrained code models internally encode type information in their residual streams, finding that cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped code, and that hidden states linearly encode result types from typed function application, with robustness to lexical and syntactic perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the strong performance of state-of-the-art code models, there is limited understanding of how (or whether) these models internally represent formal type semantics. Prior interpretability work on code models has not directly targeted type representations, particularly in a cross-lingual setting.

Method: The authors probe the residual streams of pretrained code models using a parallel dataset of Java (typed) and Python (untyped/dynamically typed) code examples. They train linear probes on hidden states from one language to infer argument and result types in the other language, testing for cross-lingual type representations and robustness to lexical perturbations and syntactic variations.

Result: Cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped code (Python). Hidden states linearly encode the result type implied by typed function application, enabling probes trained on one language to infer types in the other. This structure is partly robust to lexical perturbations and cross-language syntactic variations.

Conclusion: Pretrained code models develop internal, cross-lingual representations of type semantics, suggesting that type information is implicitly learned even without explicit type annotations. This opens new directions for interpretability in code models and has implications for type inference and code understanding tasks.

Abstract: State-of-the-art code models achieve impressive performance, yet the extent to which they internally encode type information remains poorly understood. We probe the residual streams of pretrained code models for internal type representations using a parallel dataset of Java and Python code examples. Our results show that cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped code. Moreover, we test whether hidden states linearly encode the result type implied by typed function application by training probes on one language to infer argument and result types in the other. Finally, we find that this structure is partly robust to lexical perturbations and cross-language syntactic variations. To the best of our knowledge, prior work on interpretability of code models has not directly targeted formal type semantics or cross-lingual type representations. We release our code and datasets.

</details>


### [31] [Grounded Event Extraction from SEC 8-K Filings with a Fine-Grained Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08346)
*Rian Dolphin,Joe Dursun,Jarrett Blankenship,Katie Adams,Quinton Pike*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A two-stage LLM system tags SEC 8-K filings with 119 fine-grained event types, anchoring each tag to verbatim quotes and assigning quality scores. Precision ranges from 12% to 96% depending on score thresholds, and the taxonomy separates economically distinct events that share the same coarse SEC item code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SEC 8-K filings use coarse item codes that lump together materially different events—from routine administrative changes to CEO departures—and many market-moving disclosures fall into a generic catch-all category. This coarseness impedes economic analysis, but fine-grained manual labeling at corpus scale is infeasible. The authors aim to make automated fine-grained labeling reliable by ensuring every tag is grounded in source text and comes with a validated quality measure.

Method: A two-stage system: Stage 1 uses an LLM constrained to output only valid entries from a three-tier, 119-category taxonomy, anchoring each tag to a verbatim quote from the 8-K via fuzzy n-gram validation. Stage 2 re-grades each quoted passage against the category definition using a separate LLM pass, producing a calibrated quality score. Applied to 292,984 filings (2022–2026), yielding 601,088 grounded tags. Validation uses an LLM judge over 5,125 stratified tags, plus an event study on unsigned abnormal returns.

Result: Precision rises monotonically with the quality score: from 12% at the lowest scores to 96% at the highest. Unsupported (hallucinated) tags drop from ~8% to near zero. Ablation confirms the quality score is only well-calibrated when computed in a dedicated second pass (not in a single pass). The event study with unsigned abnormal returns shows the taxonomy separates economically distinct events sharing the same SEC item code, validated without any language model.

Conclusion: LLM-based fine-grained labeling of regulatory filings is viable at corpus scale when grounded in source quotes and coupled with a calibrated quality-scoring mechanism. The released dataset of 601,088 grounded tags enables richer economic analysis of corporate disclosures than coarse SEC item codes alone permit.

Abstract: Form 8-K filings are the primary channel through which U.S. public companies disclose material events, but the SEC item codes attached to them are coarse: a single item spans routine administrative changes and chief executive departures, and many of the most market-moving disclosures fall into a catch-all item. Large language models make fine-grained labelling feasible at corpus scale, but only if the labels can be traced to the source text and shown to be reliable. We present a two-stage system that tags 8-K disclosures against a three-tier taxonomy of 119 event types. The first stage constrains output to valid taxonomy entries and anchors every tag to a verbatim quote via fuzzy n-gram validation; the second re-grades each cited quote against the category definition to produce a quality score. Applying the system to 292,984 filings from 2022 to 2026 yields 601,088 grounded event tags, which we release. Over 5,125 stratified tags, an LLM judge finds precision rises monotonically with the quality score, from 12% to 96%, while unsupported tags fall from 8% to near zero. Ablation shows the score is calibrated only when assigned in a dedicated second pass. An event study on unsigned abnormal returns confirms, without any language model, that the taxonomy separates economically distinct events sharing an item code.

</details>


### [32] [Echoes Across Vietnam's Highlands, Delta, and Coast: A Multilingual Corpus for Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08362)
*Anh Trac Duc Dinh,Khang Nhat Hoang Vo,Vinh Cong Doan,Tai Tien Ta,Khoa Duc Anh Lam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CKTN introduces the first NLP corpus and benchmark for Vietnamese ethnic minority languages (Cham, Khmer, Tay-Nung). The authors show that standard multilingual adaptation fails due to script differences and propose a script-aware adaptation recipe (vocabulary augmentation + calibrated replaced-token pretraining) that reduces fragmentation and improves classification, while revealing that lexical-overlap retrieval is a misleading evaluation signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vietnamese ethnic minority languages (Cham, Khmer, Tay-Nung) are severely underrepresented in NLP. Standard multilingual adaptation methods perform poorly because these languages differ sharply in script, degree of Vietnamese contact, and standardization level — conditions where models can learn spurious signals (e.g., trivial script mismatches) rather than genuine linguistic competence.

Method: The authors construct CKTN, a 44,367-document (24M subword tokens) corpus and benchmark covering continued pretraining, category classification, and summary-document retrieval. They then propose a script-aware adaptation recipe combining two techniques: (1) vocabulary augmentation to better handle diverse scripts, and (2) calibrated replaced-token pretraining designed to prevent the discriminator from exploiting trivial script mismatches, forcing the model to learn semantic rather than orthographic signals.

Result: Existing multilingual encoders exhibit severe fragmentation on Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung. Common adaptation metrics prove misleading: models can achieve low language-modeling loss or excel at lexical-overlap retrieval while still failing at semantic generalization across documents. The proposed script-aware adaptation recipe yields an encoder with substantially reduced fragmentation and the strongest classification performance among all evaluated models.

Conclusion: Lexical-overlap retrieval is an unreliable evaluation signal for these languages; script-aware adaptation that explicitly prevents exploitation of orthographic shortcuts is essential for building semantically competent encoders for fragmented, low-resource languages with diverse scripts. CKTN provides a foundation for future NLP work on Vietnamese minority languages.

Abstract: Vietnam's ethnic minority languages are almost absent from the field of Natural Language Processing (NLP), and the challenge goes beyond data scarcity: Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung differ sharply in script, Vietnamese contact, and standardization, conditions under which standard multilingual adaptation can learn the wrong signals. We introduce CKTN, the first corpus and benchmark for these languages (44,367 documents, 24M subword tokens), spanning continued pretraining, category classification, and summary-document retrieval. We show that existing multilingual encoders severely fragment these languages, and that common adaptation metrics can mislead: models may lower language-modeling loss or excel at lexical-overlap retrieval while still failing at semantic generalization across documents. We address this with a script-aware adaptation recipe - vocabulary augmentation combined with calibrated replaced-token pretraining - that prevents the discriminator from exploiting trivial script mismatches. The result is an encoder with substantially less fragmentation and the strongest classification performance among evaluated models, exposing the limits of lexical-overlap retrieval as an evaluation signal.

</details>


### [33] [Prompt Compression via Activation Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08399)
*Thibaud Ardoin,Semira Einsele,Evis Bregu,Gerhard Wunder*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM instruction prompts can be compressed into a single activation vector (via learned weighted sum of mid-layer activations) and re-injected at an early layer, replacing the original token sequence with under 2% accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether task-relevant information in instruction prompts — typically processed through dozens of transformer layers — can be distilled into a single activation vector, thereby reducing per-query computation for fixed instructions without reprocessing the full token sequence.

Method: Extract activations at an intermediate layer of the target LLM and learn a weighted sum to produce a compressed vector; this vector is then injected at an early layer, bypassing the need to process the original prompt tokens through all preceding layers.

Result: The compressed vector preserves task-relevant information with under 2% accuracy degradation compared to full prompt processing. Analysis further reveals: (i) mid-layer representations transfer meaningfully to early layers (cross-layer compatibility); (ii) a single activation vector encodes quantifiable semantic information; (iii) weighted-sum activation pooling is a robust compression mechanism.

Conclusion: Prompt compression into a single activation vector is viable and efficient, offering practical compute savings for fixed-instruction scenarios. The findings also illuminate structural properties of LLM activation spaces, including cross-layer representational compatibility and the information density of mid-layer embeddings.

Abstract: Large language models process prompts by propagating activations through dozens of layers before generating a response. We ask whether the task-relevant information contained in an instruction prompt can be compressed into a single activation vector and re-injected into the model, replacing the original token sequence? We show this is achievable using a learned weighted sum of activations extracted at an intermediate layer and injected at an early layer of the target LLM. The compressed vector preserves task-relevant information, incurring an accuracy drop of under $2\%$ relative to full prompt processing. Beyond its practical implications, including reducing per-query computation for fixed instruction prompts without reprocessing the original token sequence, our analysis reveals structure in the activation space of LLMs: (i) mid-layer representations transfer meaningfully to early layers, suggesting a degree of cross-layer compatibility in how information is encoded; (ii) a single activation vector encodes a quantifiable and recoverable amount of semantic information; (iii) a weighted sum of activations is a robust representation compressor.

</details>


### [34] [When Synthetic Speech Is All You Have: Better Call GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08409)
*Shashi Kumar,Yanis Labrak,Hasindri Watawana,Sergio Burdisso,Esaú Villatoro-Tello,Kadri Hacioğlu,Petr Motlicek,Andreas Stolcke*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GRPO, a critic-free reinforcement learning method, substantially outperforms supervised fine-tuning for adapting LLM-based ASR to regulated domains using synthetic speech, achieving 40-45% relative WER reduction by improving stopping calibration and attention alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting LLM-based ASR to privacy-regulated domains like banking is constrained by limited access to real speech data. Synthetic TTS data offers a workaround, but suffers from acoustic mismatch with real recordings. Prior work addressing this gap has been confined to supervised fine-tuning, leaving potential gains from reinforcement learning unexplored.

Method: The authors apply Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a critic-free RL method, to adapt an LLM-based ASR model using only synthetic TTS speech. GRPO rewards low-WER hypotheses. Two adaptation protocols are tested: GRPO-only adaptation on synthetic data, and an SFT-then-GRPO combination. Analysis traces improvements to behavioral changes — specifically insertion error reduction via better stopping calibration and improved speech-to-text alignment through attention anchoring to audio — rather than representational changes in early layers.

Result: Synthetic-only GRPO adaptation reduces WER by 40% relative to SFT (36.71% → 22.09%). An SFT-then-GRPO pipeline pushes this to 45% relative reduction. Gains stem from behavioral improvements: reduced insertion errors, better stopping calibration, and attention better anchored to audio. Early-layer representations remain largely unchanged.

Conclusion: When synthetic speech is the primary adaptation resource for LLM-based ASR, reinforcement learning — specifically GRPO — should be preferred over supervised fine-tuning, as it extracts substantially more value from the same synthetic data through behavioral rather than representational improvements.

Abstract: LLM-based ASR adapted to regulated domains such as banking is bottlenecked by privacy: real speech is costly and legally constrained to collect, making synthetic text-to-speech (TTS) an attractive substitute. Yet synthetic speech stays acoustically mismatched with real recordings, and work on this gap has stayed within supervised fine-tuning (SFT). We instead turn to reinforcement learning, and show that Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) extracts far more from the same synthetic speech than SFT. Synthetic-only adaptation of the model with GRPO, a critic-free method rewarding low-WER hypotheses, reduces WER by 40\% relative to SFT (36.71\%$\to$22.09\%), and an SFT-then-GRPO combination pushes this further to 45\%. We trace the gain to behavior rather than representation: GRPO reduces insertion errors by improving stopping calibration and speech-to-text alignment by better anchoring attention to audio, leaving early-layer representations intact. When synthetic speech is the main resource, reinforcement learning should be preferred over supervised fine-tuning.

</details>


### [35] [Detecting Ladder Logic Bombs in IEC 61131-3 PLC Programs using ESBMC-PLC+: A Formal Verification Approach with Trigger Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08417)
*Pierre Dantas,Lucas Cordeiro,Waldir Junior*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ESBMC-LLB detects ladder logic bombs in PLC programs by exposing function-block logic to formal verification, using k-induction for unbounded proofs and bounded model checking for trigger recovery. It achieves 100% detection on the Iacobelli 2024 dataset (30/30) and 99% on PLC-Defuser's SWaT corpus (149/150), and identifies where semantic model checking and CFG-triage each excel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ladder-diagram verifiers drop function-block bodies from their intermediate representation (IR), rendering malicious logic hidden inside function blocks invisible to provers. This creates a blind spot where Ladder Logic Bombs (LLBs) can evade detection. Furthermore, current detectors (signature, anomaly, CFG-triage) lack guarantees about trigger recovery and cannot handle adaptive triggers (computed, opaque-arithmetic, multi-scan).

Method: ESBMC-LLB builds on the ESBMC-PLC+ verification engine and adds a modeling layer that exposes function-block logic. It recasts bomb detection as formal verification: a scan-watchdog mechanism exposes non-termination payloads, and output wiring exposes actuator-forgery payloads as safety violations. The approach employs k-induction for unbounded proofs of bomb absence across all scans, and bounded model checking to return counterexamples that serve as recovered triggers. An analog extension is added to make the PLC-Defuser SWaT corpus parseable.

Result: On the Iacobelli 2024 dataset: 30/30 bombs detected (100%) with full trigger recovery, including adaptive triggers that evade CFG-triage. On PLC-Defuser's SWaT corpus v1.0.0: 149/150 bombs detected (99%) with zero false positives and full trigger recovery. On a later SWaT version with nonlinear non-termination bombs: detection drops to 49% due to SMT solver timeouts.

Conclusion: Semantic model checking and CFG-triage are complementary: semantic model checking provides unbounded proofs, robustness to adaptive triggers, and handles Boolean/integer and linear analog logic effectively; CFG-triage handles nonlinear analog non-termination better. The authors delineate where each approach wins, advocating for combined use.

Abstract: A Ladder Logic Bomb (LLB) is malicious control logic in a Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) program that lies dormant until a trigger activates a payload to manipulate actuators, forge sensor readings, or deny operator control. We observe that real malicious logic hides inside function-block bodies, which existing ladder-diagram verifiers drop from their intermediate representation (IR), making bombs invisible to provers. We present ESBMC-LLB, which uses ESBMC-PLC+ as its verification engine and adds a modeling layer that exposes function-block logic and recasts bomb detection as a formal verification problem: a scan-watchdog exposes non-termination payloads, and output wiring exposes actuator-forgery payloads as safety violations. k-induction gives an unbounded proof of bomb-absence across all scans, and the bounded model checker returns a counterexample that is the trigger - guarantees that signature, anomaly, and CFG-triage detectors lack. On the public Iacobelli 2024 dataset, ESBMC-LLB detects all 30 bombs and recovers every trigger; it also detects adaptive triggers (computed, opaque-arithmetic, multi-scan) that evade CFG-triage. We also report the first semantic model-checker evaluation on PLC-Defuser's SWaT corpus: our analog extension makes the full corpus parseable; on v1.0.0, it detects 149/150 bombs (99%) with zero false positives, recovering each trigger; on a later version with nonlinear non-termination bombs, detection drops to 49% as the SMT solver times out. We conclude that semantic model checking and CFG-triage are complementary - the former gives unbounded proofs, adaptive-trigger robustness, and handles Boolean/integer and linear analog logic; the latter leads to nonlinear analog non-termination, and we delineate where each wins.

</details>


### [36] [Two Axes of LLM Abstention: Answer Correctness and Question Answerability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08456)
*Benedikt J. Wagner*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs have a systematic blind spot: answer-confidence metrics track correctness but fail to detect whether a question is answerable. However, linear probes on hidden states can reliably detect answerability. By combining these two separate axes into a dual-threshold policy, the authors achieve calibrated refusal that separately certifies budgets for wrong answers and unanswerable-question answers, dramatically outperforming single-threshold approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing refusal mechanisms rely on a single confidence threshold, which conflates two distinct failure modes: (1) answering incorrectly when the model doesn't know the answer, and (2) attempting to answer unanswerable or false-premise questions. These require different detection strategies, but current approaches cannot disentangle them.

Method: The authors evaluate five instruction-tuned models (2B–14B, three families) on answerability vs. correctness axes. They compare: (a) answer-confidence (token probabilities, P(IK), P(True)), (b) linear probes trained on hidden states to predict answerability, and (c) direct prompting to check premises. They then construct a dual-threshold policy using separate answerability and correctness scores, each with its own certified guarantee.

Result: Answer-confidence is nearly blind to answerability (near chance on CREPE false-premise questions). Hidden-state probes reach 0.69–0.77 AUROC for answerability detection. Instructing models to check premises backfires (57% false challenge rate). The dual-threshold policy certifies both error budgets at 0.75 coverage of correct answers, vs. 0.31 for single-threshold; at 14B it is the only policy that certifies at all.

Conclusion: Answerability and correctness are separate, dissociable axes in LLM representations. A calibrated dual-threshold policy exploiting this separation provides the first certification framework that simultaneously bounds both wrong-answer and unanswerable-answer rates, with guarantees that tighten with model scale.

Abstract: A model should refuse two different things: answers it would get wrong, and questions it should not answer at all, such as unanswerable ones or ones resting on a false premise. The usual recipe thresholds a single confidence score, which cannot tell these apart. Across five instruction-tuned models from three families (2B to 14B), we find they are separate axes. Ordinary answer-confidence tracks whether an answer is right but is nearly blind to whether the question is answerable; a linear probe on hidden states does the reverse. The blind spot does not shrink with scale. It is worst on naturally occurring false-premise questions (CREPE). There, answer-confidence, P(IK), P(True), and even asking the model outright whether a premise is false all stay near chance, while a hidden-state probe reaches 0.69 to 0.77 AUROC: the model represents a problem it will not report. This turns out to be fixable. Instructing a model to check premises backfires, because it then disputes sound and false premises alike (57% false challenges), unable to tell them apart; routing the same instruction with the probe roughly triples challenge precision. We turn the two axes into a calibrated policy that answers only when an answerability score and a correctness score each clear a separately certifies behave differently: the unanswerable-answer rate is controllable at every scale, while the wrong-answer rate is capped by model accuracy, so the guarantee tightens as threshold policy certifies both budgets at 0.75 coverage of correct answers, against 0.31 for a single threshold; at 14B it is the only policy that certifies at all.

</details>


### [37] [Cross-seed explainability using Procrustes-conditioned Joint End-to-end Top-K Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08499)
*Bendegúz Váradi,Zoltán Kmetty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Procrustes-conditioned Joint End-to-end Top-K Sparse Autoencoder that aligns independently trained BERT models' activation spaces before joint SAE training, producing highly universal cross-seed features (Pearson r ≥ 0.70) that outperform post-hoc alignment baselines across three benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-seed feature universality is a fundamental challenge in mechanistic interpretability: independently trained neural networks learn misaligned feature spaces due to the non-convex nature of dictionary learning, meaning apparently identical features may diverge solely due to random initialization. Existing post-hoc alignment methods are insufficient for achieving truly universal features.

Method: The method computes an orthogonal Procrustes rotation between the activation spaces of independently trained BERT models before performing joint sparse autoencoder training. The SAE incorporates three key components: (1) Top-K sparsity constraint, (2) end-to-end downstream optimization, and (3) an auxiliary dead-feature revival loss. Evaluation is conducted on five independent seed pairs (ten BERT models total) across three datasets: SST-2, Stanford Politeness, and TweetEval Emotion.

Result: The full Procrustes-conditioned joint training pipeline yields cross-seed universal features with Pearson r ≥ 0.70, outperforming post-hoc alignment baselines on all three benchmark datasets. Qualitative analysis confirms that high-universality features encode interpretable sociolinguistic patterns.

Conclusion: Pre-aligning activation spaces via orthogonal Procrustes rotation before joint SAE training effectively addresses the feature misalignment problem in independently trained models, enabling the extraction of genuinely universal, interpretable features across different random initializations.

Abstract: We present a Procrustes-conditioned Joint End-to-end Top-K Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) for extracting cross-seed universal features from independently trained BERT models. Cross-seed feature universality is a fundamental challenge in mechanistic interpretability: because dictionary learning is non-convex, independently trained networks learn misaligned feature spaces, so apparently identical features may differ by random initialization. We address this by computing an orthogonal Procrustes rotation between seeds' activation spaces before joint SAE training, combining Top-K sparsity, end-to-end downstream optimization, and an auxiliary dead-feature revival loss based on previous SAE literature. Evaluating on five independent seed pairs (ten BERT models) across three benchmark datasets (SST-2, Stanford Politeness, TweetEval Emotion), our full pipeline produces more universal features (Pearson r $\geq$ 0.70 across seeds) than post-hoc alignment baselines on all three datasets. A minimal qualitative analysis confirms that high-universality features encode interpretable sociolinguistic patterns.

</details>


### [38] [When the Judge Changes, So Does the Measurement: Auditing LLM-as-Judge Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08535)
*Zongyou Yang,Yinghan Hou,Xiaokun Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM-as-judge scores shift when the evaluator model is upgraded, even with fixed candidate responses — a validity problem. Comparing Qwen3 dense scaling (1.7B→32B) and MiniMax API releases, the authors find upgrades are not interchangeable, biases persist, and recommend reporting standards including bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and audit trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge is widely used for evaluation, but changing the judge model (upgrading) introduces ambiguity: scores change without any change in candidate quality. This threatens measurement validity, yet no systematic framework exists to assess whether judge upgrades are interchangeable or what biases persist across upgrades.

Method: The authors treat evaluator-replacement ambiguity as a measurement-validity problem. Across four judgment datasets, they compare two real-world upgrade paths: (1) scaling Qwen3 dense judges from 1.7B to 32B parameters, and (2) moving across MiniMax M2→M2.7 released APIs. They analyze score shifts, position bias, verbosity bias, repeated-sample jury reliability (error dependence), and structured debate protocols with parser/fallback logging.

Result: Key findings: (1) Only Qwen3 1.7B→4B yields a robust adjacent gain; MiniMax adjacent releases are not interchangeable. (2) Stronger judges reduce but do not eliminate position and verbosity bias. (3) Repeated-sample juries add little value when judge errors are correlated. (4) Structured debate shifts decisions substantially but cannot be attributed to deliberation without parser and fallback logs.

Conclusion: LLM-as-judge evaluations are sensitive to judge model replacement in non-interchangeable ways. The authors recommend standardized reporting: dataset slices, bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and full protocol audit trails (including parser/fallback logs) to improve validity and reproducibility.

Abstract: An LLM-as-judge score can move even when the candidate responses stay fixed, simply because the evaluator has changed. We treat this evaluator-replacement ambiguity as a measurement-validity problem. Across four judgment datasets, we compare two upgrade paths available in practice: scaling Qwen3 dense judges from 1.7B to 32B parameters and moving across MiniMax M2-M2.7 released APIs. The main pattern is that judge upgrades are not interchangeable: only Qwen3 1.7B to 4B gives a robust adjacent gain, while MiniMax adjacent releases do not. Stronger judges reduce but do not remove position and verbosity bias. Repeated-sample juries add little when errors are correlated. Structured debate can move decisions substantially, but without parser and fallback logs those shifts cannot be attributed to deliberation. We argue that LLM-as-judge reports should include dataset slices, bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and protocol audit trails.

</details>


### [39] [It Takes a MAESTRO To Prune Bad Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08601)
*Palaash Goel,Ayush Maheshwari,Tanmoy Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MAESTRO is a structured pruning method for MoE language models that models expert activation trajectories as Ergodic Markov chains, using stationary distributions to derive globally-aware importance scores. It outperforms baselines by up to 10.61% under 50% compression while showing lower cross-task variance across Safety, Bias, and Ethics domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE models achieve inference efficiency by sparsely activating experts, but the entire expert bank must reside in memory, creating a deployment bottleneck. Existing pruning methods use local heuristics that ignore the interdependent, cross-layer nature of MoE routing, leading to suboptimal expert removal decisions.

Method: MAESTRO models autoregressive expert activation trajectories across layers as Ergodic Markov chains. The stationary distribution of this chain captures cross-layer routing dependencies, providing a globally-aware importance heuristic for selecting which experts to prune. This contrasts with prior locally-derived heuristics that are blind to inter-layer routing structure.

Result: Under a strict 50% compression regime across five diverse domains (Safety, Bias, Ethics, and others), MAESTRO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 10.61% in average performance retention and exhibits substantially lower cross-task variance, indicating more consistent generalization.

Conclusion: Global, routing-congruent pruning via Markov chain modeling of expert trajectories yields MoE models that retain performance better and generalize more consistently across heterogeneous tasks compared to local importance heuristics.

Abstract: Sparsely-activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models achieve remarkable inference efficiency by activating only a small fraction of parameters per token, yet their full expert banks reside in memory at all times, creating a prohibitive deployment bottleneck. Existing structured pruning methods, largely designed for dense transformers, assess expert importance using locally derived heuristics that are blind to the interdependent nature of MoE routing. We introduce MAESTRO (Markov-chain Approximated Expert Sparsification via Transition-based ROuting), a structured pruning framework designed for MoE architectures that models autoregressive expert activation trajectories as Ergodic Markov chains whose stationary distributions encode cross-layer dependencies, yielding a globally aware importance heuristic. Evaluated across five diverse domains including Safety, Bias, and Ethics, MAESTRO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 10.61% in average performance retention under a strict 50% compression regime, while exhibiting substantially lower cross-task variance, indicating that global, routing-congruent pruning produces models that generalize more consistently across heterogeneous tasks.

</details>


### [40] [DominoTree: Conditional Tree-Structured Drafting with Domino for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08642)
*Saw S. Lin,Jyh-Shing Roger Jang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DominoTree combines Domino's GRU-based path-dependent drafter with a best-first tree search, using a top-M restricted per-node correction for tractability, achieving state-of-the-art acceptance lengths (up to 10.7 tokens/round) and throughput gains across temperatures on Qwen models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding drafters face a structural mismatch: block-diffusion drafters like DFlash produce only per-position marginals, while best-first tree methods like DDTree use factorized formulations that cannot represent path-dependent distributions. Domino's GRU-based causal correction makes each draft token distribution dependent on the full path, but this non-factorized structure is incompatible with DDTree. The authors aim to bridge this gap without retraining.

Method: DominoTree is a training-free best-first draft tree algorithm scored using Domino's conditional, non-factorized correction along each root-to-node path. Tractability is achieved by restricting the per-node correction to a candidate top-M set. A GPU-native CUDA-graph builder ensures bit-identical results to a reference Python implementation while keeping per-round tree construction cheap.

Result: On Qwen3-4B across 8 benchmarks: up to 6.6× speedup over autoregressive, highest mean accept length (up to 10.7 tokens/round) at all tested temperatures, 9–10% overall throughput gain over released Domino, up to +22% on Alpaca. On Qwen3-8B: highest accepted length at all temperatures, +24% throughput over DDTree at T=0, with persistent aggregate wins over DFlash and Domino at higher temperatures.

Conclusion: DominoTree successfully integrates Domino's path-dependent drafting into a best-first tree framework, achieving superior acceptance lengths and throughput across temperatures and model sizes, demonstrating that non-factorized, conditional draft distributions can be practically exploited for speculative decoding trees.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by drafting several tokens and verifying them in parallel. Block-diffusion drafters such as DFlash produce
  a draft block in one pass but model only per-position marginals; best-first tree methods such as DDTree expand candidate trees from those marginals. The
  released Domino drafter adds a GRU-based causal correction that makes each draft token's distribution path-dependent, a structure DDTree's factorized
  formulation cannot represent. We introduce DominoTree, a training-free best-first draft tree scored by Domino's conditional, non-factorized correction
  along each root-to-node path, made practical by restricting the per-node correction to a candidate top-M. On Qwen3-4B across eight benchmarks, DominoTree
  reaches up to 6.6x speedup over autoregressive decoding and the highest mean accept length of any evaluated method, up to 10.7 tokens per round, at every
  temperature we test. DominoTree constructs its tree with a GPU-native, CUDA-graph builder that is bit-identical to a reference Python implementation, so
  acceptance is unchanged, while keeping per-round tree construction cheap. With this builder as default, DominoTree wins throughput over the released
  Domino decoder at every temperature, 9-10% overall on Qwen3-4B and up to +22% on Alpaca, and over DDTree/CaDDTree at every temperature we test. On Qwen3-
  8B, DominoTree keeps the highest accepted length at every temperature and adds a decisive throughput win at T=0, +24% over DDTree; at higher temperature
  that edge over DDTree/CaDDTree narrows to a tie and a small loss, while its Overall aggregate wins over DFlash and Domino persist.

</details>


### [41] [UltraX: Refining Pre-Training Data at Scale with Adaptive Programmatic Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08646)
*Xinlong Zhao,Dongsheng Liu,Hengyu Zhao,Zixuan Fu,Zheng Wang,Jie Cai,Jie Zhou,Qiang Ma,Xuanhe Zhou,Xu Han,Yudong Wang,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UltraX is a function-calling refinement framework for large-scale pre-training data that introduces insertion alongside deletion and modification operations, using a program-supervision generation pipeline with dataset-adaptive prompts, structured mapping, and reliability mechanisms to achieve state-of-the-art data efficiency and refinement reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As training data approaches physical limits, gains from Scaling Laws diminish, shifting focus from data quantity to data quality. Existing refinement methods fall short: rule-based approaches lack instance-level adaptability, while LLM-based methods are inefficient and unreliable at large scale. UltraX addresses this gap by enabling fine-grained, efficient, and reliable instance-level data editing.

Method: UltraX proposes a function-calling framework with three key components: (1) an expanded editing function space (insertion, deletion, modification); (2) a program-supervision generation pipeline using dataset-adaptive prompt optimization, Line Alignment Mapping, and Dynamic Context Replacement to convert original-refined text pairs into structured supervision; (3) reliability mechanisms including low-confidence example filtering, ratio-controlled sampling by operation combination, sliding-window prediction, global operation aggregation, and systematic post-processing for stable large-scale execution.

Result: UltraX achieves the highest average performance across all evaluated corpora and matches or surpasses baselines while using fewer training tokens, demonstrating superior data efficiency and refinement reliability.

Conclusion: UltraX effectively addresses the quality-efficiency-reliability trilemma in large-scale pre-training data refinement, establishing a new state-of-the-art approach for improving LLMs through better data utilization rather than mere data expansion.

Abstract: As available training data approaches its physical limit, gains from Scaling Laws have begun to diminish. Consequently, improving Large Language Models (LLMs) now depends less on data expansion and more on higher-quality data utilization. However, in the context of large-scale corpora, existing refinement methodologies face significant limitations in quality, efficiency, and reliability: Rule-based approaches are constrained by fixed heuristics and struggle with instance-level variations; LLM-based approaches improve quality but fail to meet the efficiency and reliability requirements of large-scale data processing. To address these challenges, we propose UltraX, a function-calling refinement framework for large-scale pre-training data that completes the editing function space by introducing insertion in addition to deletion and modification, enabling fine-grained instance-level editing. Specifically, UltraX builds a reliable program-supervision generation pipeline. In this pipeline, dataset-adaptive prompt optimization first guides an expert LLM to produce high-quality end-to-end refined texts, and Line Alignment Mapping and Dynamic Context Replacement then convert original-refined text pairs into structured program supervision. Meanwhile, UltraX improves supervision quality and stabilizes the training distribution with low-confidence example filtering and ratio-controlled sampling by operation combination. During inference and execution, it normalizes and validates model outputs through sliding-window prediction, global operation aggregation, and systematic post-processing, improving the stability and reliability of large-scale execution. Experiments show that UltraX achieves the highest average performance across all corpora and also matches or surpasses baselines with fewer training tokens, demonstrating stronger data efficiency and refinement reliability.

</details>


### [42] [Do You Need a Frontier Model as a Citation Verifier? Benchmarking Rubric LLMs for Deep-Research Source Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08700)
*Ethan Leung,Elias Lumer,Corey Feld,Austin Huber,Vamse Kumar Subbiah,Kevin Paul*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper examines how reliably LLM judges can evaluate citation quality (source relevance and factual support) for use as reward signals in reinforcement learning. Testing 8 off-the-shelf judges on 1,248 human-reviewed rubric decisions, they find cheaper models like GPT-5-mini are competitive with more expensive ones, but that similar F1 scores mask significant differences in directional bias (pass-rate drift, false positives/negatives) — exactly what downstream RL would amplify. Calibrating the judge is essential before using citation rubrics as rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Before trusting an LLM judge's scores as a reward model in RL training, we must understand how capable and how biased that judge is. The paper addresses this calibration question specifically for citation quality in deep-research systems, where generated claims must be supported by cited sources — a structured task requiring nuanced judgment across two dimensions.

Method: The authors construct an adversarial long-form benchmark with gold labels from human review. They evaluate 8 off-the-shelf LLM judges across 3 model families on 1,248 rubric decisions, with 378 hard cases adjudicated from judge disagreements. Judges are scored on two dimensions — source relevance and factual support — using metrics including F1, Cohen's κ, pass-rate drift, false positive rate, and false negative rate.

Result: Cheaper judges remain competitive: GPT-5-mini achieves the highest source-relevance pass-class F1 (0.908, κ=0.636). On factual support, all judges are statistically indistinguishable (overlapping confidence intervals). Critically, at comparable F1 scores, judges exhibit substantial differences in directional bias metrics (pass-rate drift, FPR, FNR) — a finding that scalar F1 alone obscures but which downstream RL would reinforce.

Conclusion: Calibrating the LLM judge is a prerequisite for safely using citation rubrics as reward signals in RL, and this calibration does not require the most expensive model. The choice of judge must account for directional bias, not just aggregate accuracy.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning increasingly relies on an LLM judge to score each rubric criterion, and that judge acts as the reward model during training. Before such a signal can be trusted, we need to know how capable the judge must be and how biased it is. We study this calibration question for citation quality in deep-research systems, where a search-grounded LLM must support each claim it writes with a cited source. Citation quality is a structured rubric task in which each attribution-citation pair is judged along two dimensions that require an LLM, source relevance and factual support. On an adversarial long-form benchmark, we score 8 off-the-shelf LLM judges from 3 model families against gold labels over 1,248 rubric decisions, all of which were human-reviewed and 378 of which were hard cases adjudicated from judge disagreements. Cheaper judges remain competitive across both dimensions, with GPT-5-mini attaining the strongest source-relevance pass-class F1 at 0.908 ($κ$=0.636), while on factual support the judges are statistically indistinguishable (overlapping confidence intervals), so no single model dominates. At comparable F1, the judges still differ substantially in pass-rate drift, false positive rate, and false negative rate. Scalar F1 obscures this directional bias, yet it is exactly what a downstream reinforcement learning loop would reinforce. Calibrating the judge is therefore a prerequisite for using citation rubrics as reward signals, and our results show that this calibration does not require the most expensive available model.

</details>


### [43] [Validity of LLMs as data annotators: AMALIA on authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08731)
*Manuel Pita*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Portugal's national LLM AMALIA achieves competitive agreement with human coders on moral foundations, but a "recovery gap" test reveals it relies on surface shortcuts rather than the construct's underlying theory. A calibrated English instrument fails to transfer to AMALIA, though an open multilingual model succeeds on the same Portuguese corpus, suggesting the model — not the corpus or language — is the locus of failure. The paper argues that benchmark batteries for sovereign LLMs must test evidential warrant, not just coder agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: National language models are increasingly positioned as instruments for measuring public discourse. While AMALIA-9B, Portugal's publicly funded LLM for European Portuguese, shows strong inter-coder agreement (reliability), the authors argue that agreement alone does not establish validity. The core concern is whether the model genuinely follows the theoretical logic of the construct being measured (here, the moral foundation of authority) or merely exploits correlated surface features to arrive at the correct code — a distinction with serious implications for using such models in social measurement.

Method: The paper introduces the **recovery gap** methodology: holistic prompting (asking the model to code for "authority" directly) is compared against a decomposed prompt that breaks the codebook into atomic clauses, has the model answer each separately, and then mechanically recombines responses via the theory's explicit aggregation rule. The performance drop between holistic and decomposed conditions constitutes the recovery gap. If the gap closes under calibration, portability across models and languages is plausible; if not, the model is likely relying on construct-irrelevant shortcuts. The study tests whether a calibrated English-language instrument transfers to AMALIA-9B on European Portuguese text, and benchmarks against an open multilingual LLM as a control.

Result: AMALIA-9B achieves holistic agreement with trained human coders within six F1 points of models 8–13× its size. However, decomposition recovers only about half of AMALIA's holistic performance, indicating a large recovery gap. Error analysis implicates reliance on surface correlates — particularly moral outrage language near authority figures — rather than genuine construct-relevant reasoning. Critically, the open multilingual LLM closes the recovery gap on the same Portuguese corpus under identical instructions, ruling out corpus or language artifacts as the primary explanation and pointing to AMALIA itself as the locus of failure.

Conclusion: AMALIA can serve usefully for screening and pre-coding at scale but cannot yet measure the moral foundation of authority with sufficient validity to stand alone. The study presents a single counterexample, not a general condemnation of national models. The key methodological recommendation is that sovereign-LLM evaluation batteries should incorporate tests like the recovery gap that assess evidential warrant — how the model arrives at its judgments — rather than relying solely on agreement metrics with human coders.

Abstract: A national language model offers a linguistic community its own instrument for measuring what its citizens say and value. Portugal's AMALIA, a publicly funded 9B-parameter model for European Portuguese, appears competitive on agreement alone: asked to code the moral foundation of authority, it agrees with trained human coders to within six F1 points of open models eight to thirteen times its size. Yet agreement is reliability, not validity. For theoretical constructs that must be inferred rather than read from surface features, the question is whether the model follows the construct's theory or reaches the right code by correlated shortcuts. We test this with the recovery gap: the loss in performance when a holistic prompt is decomposed into the codebook's atomic clauses and recombined by the theory's explicit rule. If calibration closes that gap, some portability should survive across models and languages; where it does not, the construct-model instrument is the likely locus of failure. We ask whether a calibrated English instrument transfers to AMALIA-9B and to European Portuguese. For one construct and one corpus, it does not. Decomposition recovers only about half of AMALIA's holistic performance, and error analysis suggests reliance on surface correlates, especially moral outrage near authority figures. An open multilingual LLM closes the gap on the same Portuguese corpus under the same instructions, pointing away from the corpus as the main explanation. AMALIA can still screen and pre-code at scale, but it cannot yet measure this construct well enough to stand alone. The study is a single counterexample, not a verdict on national models; it argues that sovereign-LLM benchmark batteries should test not only agreement with human coders, but the evidential route by which that agreement is warranted.

</details>


### [44] [UniClawBench: A Universal Benchmark for Proactive Agents on Real-World Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08768)
*Zhekai Chen,Chengqi Duan,Kaiyue Sun,Bohao Li,Yuqing Wang,Manyuan Zhang,Xihui Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UniClawBench is a capability-driven benchmark for evaluating proactive agents in real-world settings, using 400 bilingual tasks across five foundational capabilities, evaluated via live Docker containers with a closed-loop multi-agent evaluation strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks fail to adequately evaluate proactive agents because they rely on sandboxed environments, single-turn evaluation, and scenario-based taxonomies that conflate multiple capabilities, obscuring the root causes of agent failures.

Method: The benchmark is structured around five core capabilities (Skill Usage, Exploration, Long-Context Reasoning, Multimodal Understanding, Cross-Platform Coordination) with 400 bilingual real-world tasks. Evaluation occurs in live Docker containers using step-by-step completion checkpoints. A closed-loop evaluation strategy employs three agents — executor, hidden supervisor, and user agent — to simulate realistic multi-turn human feedback without leaking grading criteria. Multiple agent frameworks are tested to disentangle base model capabilities from framework design choices.

Result: Comprehensive comparisons across both state-of-the-art models and agent frameworks reveal how base model capabilities and agent framework designs jointly shape real-world performance, with capability-driven analysis enabling precise identification of failure sources.

Conclusion: UniClawBench provides a more rigorous and diagnostic evaluation paradigm for proactive agents, and the benchmark plus code are publicly released to facilitate future research.

Abstract: The rapid development of large language models and multimodal large language models has accelerated the emergence of proactive agents capable of operating everyday tools and assisting users in real-world environments. However, existing benchmarks struggle to evaluate such agents effectively, as they often rely on sandboxed environments and single-turn evaluation paradigms. Moreover, their scenario-based task taxonomies mix multiple model capabilities within the same task category, making it difficult to identify the root causes of agent failures. To address these limitations, we introduce UniClawBench, the first capability-driven benchmark designed to evaluate proactive agents in dynamic, real-world settings. UniClawBench is built around five foundational model capabilities: Skill Usage, Exploration, Long-Context Reasoning, Multimodal Understanding, and Cross-Platform Coordination. Based on these capabilities, we design 400 bilingual real-world tasks. Unlike previous benchmarks that rely on static, pre-recorded answers, our benchmark evaluates agents in live Docker containers using fine-grained, step-by-step completion checkpoints. Furthermore, we design a closed-loop evaluation strategy comprising an executor agent, a hidden supervisor agent, and a user agent to simulate realistic multi-turn human feedback without leaking grading criteria. To disentangle base model capabilities from framework-level design choices, we evaluate state-of-the-art models under multiple agent frameworks. Through comprehensive comparisons across both models and frameworks, we show how base model capabilities and agent framework designs jointly shape performance in real-world environments. To facilitate future research, we make our benchmark and code publicly available at https://github.com/HKU-MMLab/UniClawBench.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [45] [REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738)
*Nicolas Koller,Andreas u. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Current LLM binary analysis benchmarks ignore alignment reliability between binaries and source code. Reforge introduces a provenance-tracked pipeline with an eight-gate confidence funnel to quantify alignment uncertainty, revealing that unpaired comparisons suffer from survivorship bias and that optimization severely reduces evaluable functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary reverse engineering treat function-level ground truth as trivial to obtain and fail to report how many functions are actually evaluable. The authors argue that binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization—not model capability—is the real bottleneck for fair evaluation, and that current practices obscure this through undisclosed survivorship bias.

Method: Reforge builds function-level ground truth through a provenance-tracked pipeline spanning C compilation, DWARF debug info and syntactic extraction, binary-to-source alignment, and decompilation. It operationalizes alignment uncertainty via an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification (high/medium/low confidence), enabling uncertainty-aware benchmarking.

Result: On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield drops from 87.2% (O0) to 65.9% at higher optimization levels. Unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay due to survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven LLMs on function naming validates the framework.

Conclusion: Reliable LLM benchmarking for binary analysis requires uncertainty-aware evaluation that accounts for alignment confidence. Reforge provides the substrate for such practice, and the community should adopt provenance-tracked, confidence-stratified ground truth to avoid survivorship bias in future evaluations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. Claims about their capability, however, outpace our ability to measure it.
