Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved pre-processing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. This paper presents Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and that operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the substrate and motivates uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice

</details>


### [46] [PERFOPT-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents on Software Performance Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07744)
*Yingyun Cui,Yi Xie,Piaohong Wang,Jiawei Ma,Bo Liu,Liangliang Cao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PERFOPT-Bench evaluates LLM-based agents on end-to-end performance optimization tasks, requiring profiling, bottleneck diagnosis, safe code editing, and verified speedup measurement. Evaluation of 7 agent stacks on 7 tasks shows performance is workload-dependent, no single stack dominates, and raw speedup alone is an unsafe metric due to shortcut exploitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding-agent benchmarks focus on functional correctness (producing patches that pass tests), but production software engineering also requires measurable performance improvements. Performance optimization is a distinct, under-benchmarked agentic task involving profiling, cross-layer bottleneck diagnosis, correctness-preserving edits, and artifact-free measurement verification.

Method: The authors introduce PERFOPT-Bench with 7 long-horizon optimization tasks, each providing a correct but suboptimal codebase targeting a specific performance metric. Scoring combines hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level auditing. Seven agent stacks (combinations of LLMs and agent frameworks) are evaluated, including an exploratory relay pilot where agents restart from externalized optimization summaries.

Result: Optimization performance is workload-dependent rather than determined solely by model identity; no single agent stack dominates across tasks. Changing the agent framework can materially alter the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. Raw speedup is an unsafe benchmark score because large gains can arise from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation. The relay pilot shows that restarting from an externalized summary can unlock additional performance headroom after an initial session plateaus.

Conclusion: Performance optimization is a distinct agentic capability requiring holistic evaluation beyond functional correctness. Future benchmarks should incorporate verified measurements, guard against shortcut exploitation, and consider multi-session optimization workflows to better reflect real-world engineering practice.

Abstract: Coding-agent benchmarks have largely measured whether agents can produce functionally correct patches, but production software also demands measurable speedups on real execution targets. Performance optimization is a distinct agentic task: agents must profile executions, diagnose cross-layer bottlenecks, edit code without breaking correctness, and verify that gains are reproducible rather than measurement artifacts. We introduce PERFOPT-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this full performance-engineering loop. Each task provides a correct but deliberately suboptimal codebase and asks the agent to improve a target performance metric; scoring requires hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. We evaluate 7 agent stacks with different LLMs and agent frameworks on 7 long-horizon optimization tasks. The results show that optimization performance is workload-dependent rather than determined by model identity alone: no single stack dominates, and changing the agent framework can materially change the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. We further find that raw speedup is unsafe as a benchmark score, since some large gains arise from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation; an exploratory relay pilot suggests that restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional headroom after an initial session stops. The benchmark and our evaluation are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Dataset-D3CC.

</details>


### [47] [Functional and Secure Code Generation with Task Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07881)
*Felix Wang,Anudeep Das,Mei Nagappan,N. Asokan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SecVecCoder uses task-vector arithmetic on LLM weights to jointly align code generation for both functionality and security, achieving significant gains in trustworthy code completions (up to 36 pp) with negligible decoding latency overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding LLMs struggle to generate code that is simultaneously functional and secure. Prior work treats functionality and security separately, uses disjoint datasets, or attempts post-generation vulnerability fixes. Meanwhile, alignment techniques from text generation—particularly task-vector arithmetic—offer a lightweight way to shape model behavior at the weight level without decoding overhead, but remain unexplored for joint secure-and-functional code generation.

Method: SecVecCoder applies task-vector arithmetic directly on LLM weights by computing and combining task vectors derived from fine-tuning on separate functionality and security objectives. This linear weight-space manipulation allows the model to acquire both qualities simultaneously without requiring specialized decoding strategies or post-generation repair.

Result: Evaluated on six coding LLMs from three families using the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves trustworthy code completion rates by 2.1–36.0 percentage points over base models. On unseen CWE types, improvements reach up to 39.1 percentage points. Decoding latency remains within 0.6% of the base model on average.

Conclusion: Task-vector arithmetic is an effective and computationally efficient method for jointly aligning coding LLMs toward secure and functional code generation, outperforming post-hoc approaches and requiring no inference-time modifications.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for code generation, but they struggle to generate functional code free of security vulnerabilities. Prior work to improve the secure code generation abilities of such coding LLMs has largely focused on evaluating code functionality and security separately using different datasets, or focused on finding vulnerabilities post-generation. At the same time, the text-generation domain has seen significant work on alignment techniques, where models are tuned such that their outputs exhibit certain qualities (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness). Of particular interest is task-vector arithmetic, where linear operations on LLM weights can be used to arbitrarily enhance alignment while incurring only minimal computational overhead. We develop a novel method, SecVecCoder, leveraging task vectors to produce trustworthy code that is simultaneously functional and secure without the need for post-generation adjustment. Across six coding LLMs from three families on the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves the rate of trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over the base model, with improvements on unseen CWE types reaching up to 39.1 percentage points. Since the effectiveness of the coding LLM relies only on changing the model weights, SecVecCoder requires no method-specific decoding and hence achieves a decoding latency within 0.6% of the base model's, on average.

</details>


### [48] [Bug Report Specification Refinement with Trajectory Guidance for Automated Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07882)
*S M Farah Al Fahim,Md Nakhla Rafi,Md Ahasanuzzaman,Zeyang Ma,Dong Jae Kim,Shaowei Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TrajSpec refines bug reports for repository-level automated program repair by collecting execution trajectories, extracting three-level hierarchical evidence (interpretation, diagnostics, observations), and applying repository-based review. Refined reports boost repair Pass@1 significantly across multiple agents and models on SWE-Bench Lite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug reports for repository-level APR often lack repair-relevant information such as failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirements, and implementation scope. This causes repair agents to inspect irrelevant code, infer incorrect requirements, or generate superficial patches that fix symptoms without restoring intended behavior.

Method: TrajSpec operates in four stages: (1) a trajectory-collection agent runs on the pre-fix repository to gather unverified execution traces as specification evidence; (2) evidence is organized into a three-level representation: high-level issue interpretation, diagnostic findings supporting the interpretation, and concrete repository observations; (3) a draft refined report is generated; (4) repository-based review removes unsupported claims, revises uncertain claims, and adds repository-supported details to produce the final refined specification.

Result: On SWE-Bench Lite (300 instances), TrajSpec improved Pass@1 from 41.00%→59.67% (GPT-5-mini) and 54.67%→64.33% (MiniMax M2.5) with Mini-SWE-Agent V2. On a 100-instance stratified sample: 41.00%→71.00% (Agentless) and 47.00%→72.00% (AutoCodeRover). Ablations show removing repository-based review drops Pass@1 to 48.00% and removing hierarchical evidence drops it to 47.67%.

Conclusion: TrajSpec demonstrates that trajectory-guided, repository-supported bug report refinement provides actionable context that consistently and substantially improves automated program repair performance across diverse repair agents and models.

Abstract: Bug reports serve as task specifications for repository-level automated program repair (APR) agents, but they often describe only the observed failure and omit repair-relevant information such as the failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, and implementation scope. As a result, a repair agent may inspect irrelevant code, infer an incorrect requirement, or generate a patch that addresses the reported symptom without restoring the intended repository behavior. We present TrajSpec, a trajectory-guided approach for repository-supported bug report specification refinement. Given an original report and a pre-fix repository, TrajSpec runs a trajectory-collection agent and uses the resulting unverified trajectory as a source of trajectory-derived specification evidence. It organizes this evidence into a three-level representation consisting of a high-level interpretation of the issue, diagnostic findings supporting that interpretation, and concrete repository observations. TrajSpec then generates a draft refined report and applies repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain claims, and add repository-supported details. We evaluate TrajSpec on all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances using Mini-SWE-Agent V2. TrajSpec's refined reports improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini and from 54.67% to 64.33% with MiniMax M2.5. On a stratified sample of 100 instances, TrajSpec's refined reports also improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 71.00% with Agentless and from 47.00% to 72.00% with AutoCodeRover. Ablation results show that removing repository-based review or the hierarchical evidence representation reduces Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%, respectively. Overall, TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance.

</details>


### [49] [The Memory Wall of Green Software: Empirical Energy Evaluation of Memento Design Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07944)
*Imane Jriri,Tarik Houichime,Younes El Amrani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper empirically compares the energy consumption of two Memento design pattern implementations (Classic full-snapshot vs. Differential delta-encoding) against an unabstracted baseline. The Differential strategy achieves up to 65.8% energy savings for mid-scale states but catastrophically fails at 200 MB due to GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes, revealing a critical architectural trade-off between memory traffic optimization and saturation behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy efficiency has become a mission-critical non-functional requirement in Green Software Engineering, yet the "metabolic cost" imposed by design pattern abstraction layers remains largely unquantified during design. The authors seek to empirically expose the hidden energy dynamics of the Memento pattern to help architects make informed trade-offs between structural integrity and sustainability.

Method: Empirical measurement using RAPL (Running Average Power Limit) interface for hardware-level energy telemetry. Three implementations are compared: a direct unabstracted baseline, a Classic full-snapshot Memento, and a Differential delta-encoding Memento. Energy dissipation is measured across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB, capturing CPU package and DRAM energy.

Result: The Differential Memento reduces memory traffic and achieves up to 65.8% energy reduction for mid-scale state volumes. However, at 200 MB, it hits a "memory wall" — algorithmic optimizations are neutralized by severe GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes, causing catastrophic energy performance collapse.

Conclusion: Architects must weigh the memory-traffic efficiency of delta-encoding against its saturation-point fragility. The paper synthesizes findings into evidence-based heuristics to guide the selection of Memento strategies under Green IT constraints, ensuring structural design quality does not come at an unsustainable energy cost.

Abstract: As Green Software Engineering matures, energy efficiency has transitioned into a mission-critical non-functional requirement. While software design patterns ensure structural integrity, their inherent abstraction layers impose an implicit "metabolic cost" that often remains obscured during the design phase. This paper empirically investigates the energy dynamics of the Memento design pattern, contrasting a direct, unabstracted baseline against Classic full-snapshot and Differential delta-encoding strategies. Leveraging the RAPL interface for high-fidelity hardware telemetry, we quantify energy dissipation across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB. Our empirical results expose a critical architectural trade-off: the Differential strategy minimizes memory traffic, yielding a maximum energy reduction of 65.8% for mid-scale states, but collides with a catastrophic "memory wall" at 200 MB. At this saturation point, algorithmic optimizations are completely neutralized by severe GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes. We synthesize these findings into evidence-based heuristics, providing architects with a robust framework to reconcile structural design quality with sustainable Green IT imperatives.

</details>


### [50] [DeepSWE: Measuring Frontier Coding Agents on Original, Long-Horizon Engineering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07946)
*Wenqi Huang,Charley Lee,Leonard Tng,Serena Ge*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DeepSWE is a new benchmark of 113 hand-crafted software engineering tasks that avoids the data contamination and grading flaws of SWE-bench by using original tasks never contributed upstream and custom verifiers instead of inherited tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding agent benchmarks like SWE-bench suffer from two critical problems: (1) tasks are mined from merged GitHub fixes that likely appeared in pretraining data, conflating recall with genuine problem-solving; (2) grading relies on tests written for a specific fix, which can reject correct alternative implementations or pass incomplete solutions. DeepSWE addresses both issues.

Method: DeepSWE comprises 113 original tasks written from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories and 5 languages. Tasks are never contributed back upstream, keeping reference solutions out of public training data. Each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks requested functionality and accepts any correct implementation, rather than relying on fix-specific tests. An independent LLM judge validates grading reliability.

Result: The LLM judge disagrees with DeepSWE's verifiers only 1.4% of the time, compared to 32.4% disagreement with SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests. DeepSWE's prompts are about half the length of SWE-Bench Pro's yet describe tasks whose reference solutions touch 5.5x more code. The benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than existing leaderboards where agents otherwise cluster.

Conclusion: DeepSWE provides a more reliable, contamination-free, and discriminative benchmark for evaluating coding agents, released alongside its verifiers and full evaluation trajectories.

Abstract: DeepSWE is a benchmark of 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks for evaluating coding agents. Most public agentic coding benchmarks follow SWE-bench in mining merged fixes from public GitHub repositories, which creates two problems: the fixes and their discussion were likely seen during pretraining, so a high score can reflect recall rather than problem-solving; and each task is graded by the tests that shipped with its merged fix, which were written to confirm one specific fix rather than grade an arbitrary solution, so they can fail a correct alternative or pass an incomplete one. DeepSWE avoids both. Its tasks are written from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories and five languages and are never contributed back upstream, so their reference solutions stay out of the public record that model training scrapes; and each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks the requested functionality and accepts any implementation that provides it. When an independent LLM judge re-reviews graded runs, it disagrees with DeepSWE's verifier about an order of magnitude less often than with SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests (1.4% versus 32.4%). Despite being about half the length of SWE-Bench Pro's prompts, DeepSWE's prompts describe tasks whose reference solutions touch 5.5x more code, and the benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than the leaderboards on which they otherwise cluster. We release the benchmark, its verifiers, and the full record of evaluation trajectories.

</details>


### [51] [3100 Opinions on Code Review in an AI World: Building Causal Theory from Practitioner Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07980)
*Shyam Agarwal,Courtney Miller,Christian Kästner,Bogdan Vasilescu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A study examining how AI coding agents affect code review practices. The authors first show that GitHub metrics yield contradictory trends depending on analysis choices. They then synthesize 38,709 grey-literature documents into a causal theory with 26 constructs and 67 relationships, arguing that code review is the control point determining whether AI's effect on software is positive or negative, with the outcome depending on team expertise and review process design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing repository-mining studies capture surface-level trends about AI-authored code in review but fail to explain the underlying mechanisms, and the trends themselves are unstable (direction flips under different analysis choices). There is sharp practitioner disagreement about whether human review remains necessary, whether it becomes a bottleneck, and whether it erodes understanding. The authors seek to move beyond observing what is changing to explaining why.

Method: A two-phase approach: (1) A motivating observational study of public GitHub activity comparing agent-authored vs. human-authored pull requests on review frequency, merge speed, and discussion volume, demonstrating that analysis choices (e.g., bot-filtering thresholds) can reverse trend directions. (2) A grey-literature theory-building method: collecting 38,709 documents (engineering blogs, Reddit threads), filtering for substantive code-review content, and coding a stratified random sample of 3,100 using an LLM-assisted pipeline to construct a causal model with 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested).

Result: The GitHub observational analysis showed agent-authored PRs are reviewed less often, merged faster, and discussed less — but these trends reverse under alternative analysis choices. The synthesized causal theory identifies code review as the central control point mediating AI's effect on software quality, with team expertise and review process structure as key moderators. The theory formalizes competing practitioner positions into falsifiable propositions.

Conclusion: AI does not deterministically fix the sign of its effect on software — the team sets it, through human expertise and how review processes are structured. The paper contributes both an explanatory causal theory of AI-in-code-review and a scalable LLM-assisted grey-literature theory-building method for software engineering research.

Abstract: Coding agents now author entire pull requests, and practitioners sharply disagree about what this does to code review: whether it becomes the bottleneck, whether human review is still necessary, and whether it quietly erodes the understanding that it once built. Repository-mining studies measure surface trends but seldom explain the mechanisms beneath them, and the trends themselves prove unstable. A motivating observational analysis of public GITHUB activity finds that agent-authored pull requests are reviewed less often, merged several times faster, and discussed less than human-authored ones, yet the direction of these trends flips under different but equally defensible analysis choices, so the traces establish what is changing without explaining why. To recover the mechanisms, we synthesize practitioner discourse at scale into an explanatory theory: we collect 38,709 grey-literature documents (engineering blogs and Reddit threads), filter to those substantively about code review, and code a stratified random sample of 3,100 with an LLM-assisted pipeline, from which we build a causal model of 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested). Its organizing claim is that review is the control point through which a coding agent's effect on software is decided, and that AI does not fix the sign of that effect: the team sets it, through the expertise its humans bring and how it structures the review process. The theory makes the competing positions explicit and turns "AI is changing code review" into falsifiable propositions with named constructs and moderators. As a secondary contribution, we offer the underlying LLM-assisted, grey-literature theory-building method as a scalable template for software-engineering research, with a public implementation.

</details>


### [52] [On the Correctness of Software Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07987)
*Akira Mori,Masatomo Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new structural merge tool grounded in formal correctness criteria: the merge result must be both parsable (syntactically valid) and universal (incorporating all and only the edits from each branch, with common edits applied once, formalized via category-theoretic pushouts). Large-scale experiments on 43,774 merge scenarios from 76 Java projects show existing tools like Git's merge produce incorrect results, while the proposed tool does not. Comparisons with developer-resolved merges and refactoring scenarios highlight the strengths and limitations of structural merging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing three-way merge tools lack rigorous correctness criteria; their results are defined intuitively and accepted without scrutiny, leading to undetected incorrect merges. There is a need for principled, formally defined merge correctness to improve reliability.

Method: The authors propose syntactic correctness criteria for merge results: (1) parsability — the result must be syntactically valid per the language grammar; (2) universality — the result must include all and only the edits from each branch, with shared edits applied exactly once, formalized using the notion of pushouts from category theory. They implement a structural merge tool based on these criteria and evaluate it through large-scale experiments on 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, comparing against Git's merge tool, 2,582 developer-resolved merges, and 2,459 merge scenarios across 21 refactoring types.

Result: In 43,774 file merge scenarios, the Git companion merge tool produced a number of incorrect results, whereas the proposed structural merge tool reported none. The tool was further validated against 2,582 developer-resolved merges and 2,459 refactoring-involved merge scenarios, revealing both strengths and current limitations of the structural merge approach.

Conclusion: The proposed syntactic criteria (parsability and universality via pushouts) provide a solid foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools. The experimental results underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria in merge tool design and demonstrate that structural merging can outperform traditional approaches in correctness.

Abstract: Three-way merge tools play crucial roles in modern software development, where a developer forks a branch to make local modifications and requests it to be merged into the main branch via a "pull request." Despite its importance, the task has traditionally been defined in an intuitive manner, and the results of merge tools are often accepted without scrutiny. In this paper, we present a new structural merge tool in comparison with existing tools based on the syntactic criteria we propose for evaluating the merge results. We require the merge result to be both parsable and universal. Being parsable means that the result is syntactically valid according to the grammar of the programming language. Being universal means that the result incorporates all and only the edit operations occurring in each branch while ensuring that edits common to both branches are applied only once. This requirement can be precisely defined using the notion of pushouts in category theory. In a large-scale experiment involving 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, we found a number of incorrect results reported by existing tools such as the Git companion merge tool, whereas our tool reports none. We further compared d3j's results with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and with 2,459 merge scenarios involving 21 refactoring types. These experiments revealed both the strengths and current limitations of structural merge, and underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria. We expect that the proposed criterion will provide a foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools.

</details>


### [53] [Aleena: Alignment Agent for Research Software Engineering Collaborations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08043)
*Kshitij Dani,Cordero Core,Landung Setiawan,Carlos Garcia Jurado Suarez,Anshul Tambay,Vani Mandava,Anant Mittal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Aleena is an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that transforms multi-modal stakeholder interactions on GitHub into structured project records to surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity in research software engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research software collaborations across meetings, chats, pull requests, and issues cause decisions to lose their original rationale. This leads domain researchers and research software engineers to develop divergent mental models regarding project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions. Alignment is framed as a continuous lifecycle problem requiring systematic tracking rather than one-off communication.

Method: The authors present Aleena, an agentic AI system that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface. It ingests multi-modal stakeholder interactions (Slack threads, meeting notes, pull requests, issues) and transforms them into structured project records. The system is grounded in experiences from university-based research software engineering centers.

Result: The paper presents the motivating problem, the system design of Aleena, a working prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios demonstrating how the agent surfaces risks, tracks open questions, and preserves decision continuity across research software projects.

Conclusion: Agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking in research software engineering without replacing human decision-making. Aleena demonstrates a viable approach to addressing the alignment problem by maintaining structured, persistent records of project rationale and decisions.

Abstract: Research software collaborations span meetings, informal chats, pull requests, and GitHub issues. A decision surfaced in a Slack thread, refined in a meeting, and implemented in a pull request can lose its original rationale across these artifacts, leaving domain researchers and research software engineers with divergent mental models of project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions. We argue that alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and that agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human decision-making. We present Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface, transforming multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records that surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity. Grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences, this paper presents the motivating problem, system design, prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios for Aleena.

</details>


### [54] [ProjAgent: Procedural Similarity Retrieval for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08691)
*QiHong Chen,Aaron Imani,Iftekhar Ahmed*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ProjAgent introduces procedural similarity as a new retrieval signal for repository-level code generation, using an agentic workflow to match intermediate reasoning steps with similar repository functions, combined with static-analysis feedback for iterative repair, achieving 41.14% Pass@1 on REPOCOD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval methods for repository-level code generation rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity but overlook functions that implement similar procedural logic under different identifiers or domains. This gap motivates explicitly capturing procedural similarity to improve cross-file context retrieval.

Method: ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps, then uses an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions with similar procedural behavior at each step. The retrieved procedural context is fused with conventional semantic retrieval results. A conservative static-analysis feedback loop iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis diagnostics.

Result: ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1 on the REPOCOD benchmark, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines.

Conclusion: Procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation, and combining it with static-analysis-based iterative repair yields substantial performance gains.

Abstract: Repository-level code generation requires implementing target functions while accounting for complex cross-file dependencies and project-specific conventions. Existing retrieval methods predominantly rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, often overlooking repository functions that implement similar procedural logic despite differing in identifiers or application domains. We propose ProjAgent, a repository-level code generation system that introduces procedural similarity as an explicit retrieval signal. ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps and employs an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions that exhibit similar procedural behavior at each step. The retrieved procedural context is integrated with conventional semantic retrieval to construct a richer repository context for code generation. ProjAgent further incorporates a conservative static-analysis feedback loop that iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback. Evaluated on REPOCOD, ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines. These results demonstrate that procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation.

</details>


### [55] [TTHE: Test-Time Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08124)
*Jun Nie,Yonggang Zhang,Jun Song,Qianshu Cai,Dahai Yu,Yike Guo,Xinmei Tian,Bo Han*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TTHE optimizes LLM agent harnesses (executable programs) at test time using only unlabeled execution traces, evolving candidate workflows via an agentic proposer and judge—without weight updates, gold labels, or separate models—yielding persistent improvements across diverse coding and agent tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed agent harnesses optimized pre-deployment fail to adapt when test distributions, failure modes, or tool interactions diverge from development conditions. The authors seek a method for online, unsupervised harness refinement at evaluation time.

Method: TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses during evaluation. An agentic proposer analyzes execution traces from test inputs to suggest improved harnesses; a judge selects which harness to commit using proxy signals derived from execution (e.g., success indicators), without gold labels. The solver, proposer, and judge are all roles played by the same frozen LLM, so adaptation occurs solely through program-level changes.

Result: Across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, TTHE consistently improves over fixed ReAct-style baselines, yielding persistent, inspectable gains rather than per-query retries or pre-searched workflows.

Conclusion: Test-time adaptation for LLM agents can be recast as evolution over executable control programs. Execution-derived proxy reliability emerges as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

Abstract: The behavior of an LLM agent is determined not only by the underlying model, but also by its harness: the executable program that constructs context, invokes tools, verifies intermediate results, and recovers from failures. Existing approaches optimize such harnesses before deployment, searching training or development data for a fixed agent workflow that is then frozen at test time. This limits adaptation when the test distribution, failure modes, or tool interactions differ from those seen during development. We ask whether the harness can instead be optimized during evaluation itself, using only the unlabeled execution traces the agent produces on the test inputs. We introduce Test-Time Harness Evolution (TTHE), which treats the executable harness as the state of test-time adaptation. During evaluation, TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses and refines them through an agentic proposer that reasons over their execution traces, without gold labels or task-specific supervision; a judge then commits an improved harness from execution-derived proxy signals, and the selected program persists to govern subsequent inputs. Crucially, TTHE does not update model weights, require gold labels, or train a separate adaptation model: solver, proposers, and judge are different roles and harnesses around the same frozen LLM, so all adaptation occurs through changes to the surrounding program. Across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses, yielding persistent, inspectable improvements rather than a pre-searched workflow or per-query retries. These results recast test-time adaptation for LLM agents as evolution over executable control programs and identify execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

</details>

### [56] [fog: Expressing Motion and Emotion through Function Composition of AI-Generated Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07952)
*Vivian Liu,Lydia Chilton*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Fog is a function composition framework for creating and combining motion functions to express motion and emotion in animations, evaluated through perceptual studies showing 68% recognition accuracy and improved user iteration in a mixed-methods study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion and emotion are fundamental to intelligent, expressive behavior, but existing tools lack flexible, composable frameworks for building an open-ended motion vocabulary that captures semantic meaning such as verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions.

Method: The authors introduce fog, a function composition framework that enables users to implement and compose motion functions. It includes a code generation system for creating functions representing verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions, paired with an animation editor supporting direct manipulation and dynamically generated UI for refinement.

Result: In a perceptual evaluation of 452 fog-generated animations, participants recognized semantic meaning with 68% accuracy (2.68× over chance baseline). A mixed-methods user study with professionals and novices demonstrated that fog supports more rapid iteration, exploration, and control.

Conclusion: Fog successfully enables the composition of expressive motion functions, with its generated animations achieving significantly above-chance recognition. The framework in interface form enhances user workflows for both novices and professionals, validating its utility for expressive animation authoring.

Abstract: Motion and emotion are core parts of intelligent, expressive behavior. In this paper, we introduce fog, a function composition framework for implementing and compose motion functions. We demonstrate how fog can be used to express motion and emotion in Heider-Simmel style animations. This code generation framework can help users generate functions for verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions to create an open-ended motion vocabulary. It is complemented by an animation editor that helps users refine motion through direct manipulation and dynamically generated UI. We evaluate our approach with a perceptual evaluation, where we test 452 fog-generated animations to see if people can recognize the semantic meaning of the motion. We find that fog's motion functions can be recognized at 68% accuracy, a 2.68x improvement over a chance baseline. In a mixed-methods user study with professionals and novices, we show that fog in interface form can support users with more rapid iteration, exploration, and control.

</details>


### [57] [The Behavioural Reflection Test: A time-efficient measure of reflective reasoning in morally and epistemically charged decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07961)
*Sion Weatherhead,Flora Salim,Aaron Belbasis,Ben R. Newell*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT) and a bespoke CRT (bCRT) to measure reflective reasoning in morally/epistemically charged scenarios, addressing familiarity confounds plaguing the classic CRT. In 473 adults, bCRT predicted evidence-sensitive, ethical decision-making and distinctive linguistic markers, outperforming the familiarity-adjusted CRT. The BRT is open-ended yet time-efficient (median ~12 min).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classic Cognitive Reflection Test (CRT) suffers from widespread exposure effects that erode its validity. Moreover, the CRT leaves open how reflective tendencies manifest in broader decision styles and linguistic expression. A new measure is needed that is resistant to familiarity confounds and captures reflection in morally and epistemically rich, real-world contexts.

Method: The authors developed two instruments: (1) a four-item bespoke CRT (bCRT) as a low-exposure anchor for numeracy-based reflection, and (2) the Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT), an open-ended measure presenting morally and epistemically charged scenarios. Both were administered to 473 online adults. Predictive validity was assessed against evidence-sensitivity, ethical decision-making, source-quality reliance, and linguistic features (emotional engagement, risk-attentiveness, economical expression). The bCRT was compared against a familiarity-adjusted version of the classic CRT.

Result: Higher bCRT scores significantly predicted evidence-sensitive, ethically driven decisions and reliance on high-quality information sources. bCRT performance was also associated with more emotionally engaged, risk-attentive, and economical language. These associations were not recovered by the familiarity-adjusted classic CRT. The bCRT demonstrated convergent validity and contributed item-level information beyond mean ability. The BRT proved time-efficient (median completion 11.8 minutes) despite its open-ended format.

Conclusion: The bCRT and BRT together provide a valid, familiarity-resistant, and ecologically broader measurement framework for cognitive reflection. The BRT extends assessment into morally and epistemically charged reasoning, capturing individual differences in reflective decision style that the classic CRT—even when familiarity-adjusted—fails to detect.

Abstract: How readily people override intuitive conclusions through reflection shapes how they navigate dense information environments with reliable and misleading sources; yet the effectiveness of a prominent measure, the Cognitive Reflection Test (CRT), is eroded by widespread exposure to classic items and leaves open how such tendencies manifest more generally in decision style and linguistic expression. The Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT) addresses these issues with a brief open-ended measure of reasoning in morally and epistemically charged scenarios, alongside a four-item bespoke CRT (bCRT) as a low-exposure anchor. Among 473 online adults, higher bCRT predicted more evidence-sensitive, ethically driven decisions and reliance on high-quality sources, marked by more emotionally engaged, risk-attentive, economical language; associations the familiarity-adjusted CRT did not recover. The bCRT showed convergent validity, added item information above mean ability. Though open-ended, the BRT remained a time-efficient (median 11.8 minutes) behavioural assay of reflection with scope to extend across domains.

</details>


### [58] [HeadRoom: Lightweight, Edge-deployable Pipeline for Adaptive Notification Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08083)
*Dinithi Dissanayake,Prasanth Sasikumar,Suranga Nanayakkara*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: HeadRoom is an edge-deployable system that estimates real-time availability of visual and auditory channels from egocentric video/audio, enabling adaptive notification routing. A user study (N=25) shows routing notifications to the more available channel under high perceptual load reduces response time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emerging multi-sensory wearables (e.g., smart glasses) can deliver notifications through different sensory channels, but there is limited understanding of how to dynamically select the optimal channel based on the user's real-time perceptual state.

Method: The authors propose HeadRoom, a lightweight pipeline deployable on edge devices that processes egocentric video and audio to estimate real-time availability of both visual and auditory channels. A controlled user study with 25 participants was conducted, manipulating perceptual load and comparing response times when notifications were routed to the more available versus less available channel.

Result: Under conditions of high perceptual load, routing notifications to the more available sensory channel significantly reduced response time compared to routing them to the less available channel.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates the feasibility and benefit of adaptive, real-time sensory channel selection for notification delivery in wearable and immersive systems, opening a new direction for context-aware multimodal interfaces.

Abstract: Emerging wearables, such as smart glasses, can deliver notifications through multiple sensory channels, but there is still a limited understanding of how to choose the right channel at the right moment. We propose HeadRoom, a lightweight, edge-deployable pipeline that estimates the availability of visual and auditory channels in real time from egocentric video and audio. Our controlled user study (N=25) shows that, under high perceptual load, routing notifications to the more available channel reduces response time relative to routing them to the less available channel. This work opens up a new possibility for adaptive routing of notifications in wearable and immersive systems.

</details>


### [59] [How Analysts Use AI in High-Stakes Crime Linkage: An Industrial Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08274)
*Jessica Woodhams,Amy Burrell,Wanyin Li,Fahim Ahmed,Matthew Tonkin,Jan Lemeire,Arkady Konovalov,Steven Frisson,Mark Webb,Sarah Galambos,Vesna Nowack,Dalal Alrajeh*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: An industrial evaluation of an AI-enabled crime-linkage tool co-developed with UK law enforcement reveals that analysts selectively use AI predictions, frequently cross-validate them against behavioural evidence, and value model explanations — highlighting the need for better integration of AI with traditional analytical workflows in high-stakes settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crime linkage analysis — identifying series of offences committed by the same individual — is manually performed by specialist analysts searching across large databases. This process is time-consuming, cognitively demanding, and involves repeated exposure to disturbing material. An AI-enabled decision-support tool was developed to assist, but its real-world usability and trustworthiness in operational settings remained unevaluated.

Method: A mixed-methods usability study was conducted in an industrial (operational) setting, combining direct observation, eye-tracking, mouse-tracking, and surveys. This multi-modal approach captured how analysts engaged with AI predictions and with model features presented as explanations during their workflow.

Result: Analysts used AI predictions selectively and frequently validated them against behavioural (non-AI) evidence, indicating partial trust and continued reliance on established analytical practices. They attended to the presented model features and valued their availability, but identified opportunities to improve how explanations are presented and integrated into the workflow.

Conclusion: AI-enabled decision-support tools must better integrate explanations with traditional analytical methods. In-situ evaluation is essential for engineering usable and trustworthy AI systems in high-stakes domains like law enforcement.

Abstract: Crime linkage analysis is used in many countries to identify series of offences that may have been committed by the same individual. In practice, specialist analysts manually search for behavioural and situational connections across large crime databases, an effort that is time-consuming, cognitively demanding, and can involve repeated exposure to disturbing material. To support this work, an Artificial Intelligence (AI)-enabled decision-support tool was co-developed with a UK law enforcement agency to assist analysts in identifying likely crime linkages.
  This paper reports an industrial evaluation of the crime-linkage tool. We conducted a mixed-methods usability study combining direct observation, eye-tracking, mouse-tracking, and surveys to examine how analysts engage with AI predictions and with the model features presented as explanations. Our findings show that analysts used the AI predictions selectively and frequently validated them against behavioural (non-AI) evidence, reflecting partial trust and an ongoing reliance on established analytical practices. We also found that analysts attended to the presented model features and valued their availability, while identifying opportunities to improve how explanations are presented and integrated into the workflow. Overall, our results highlight the need for AI-enabled decision-support tools to better integrate explanations and traditional analytical methods, and demonstrate the importance of in-situ evaluation for engineering usable and trustworthy AI in high-stakes settings.

</details>


### [60] [ImputeViz: A Visual Analytics Dashboard for Diagnosing Missing Data and Comparing Imputation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08579)
*Aitik Dandapat,Lalith Punepalle Raveendrareddy,Mithilesh Kumar Singh,Klaus Mueller*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ImputeViz is an interactive visual analytics dashboard for diagnosing missing data patterns, configuring and comparing multiple imputation methods (MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, kNN, and a novel geographically informed gKNN), and evaluating imputation results through coordinated views and cross-method comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Missing data is pervasive across scientific and social science research, introducing bias and placing heavy accountability on analysts for handling missing values. Existing imputation tools lack integrated visual diagnostics that make missingness patterns explicit and support systematic cross-method comparison, particularly for geospatial data where spatial context matters.

Method: The authors develop ImputeViz, an integrated visual analytics system featuring: (1) coordinated views including heatmaps, co-missingness summaries, and distributional diagnostics to help analysts reason about MCAR, MAR, and MNAR patterns; (2) a method-agnostic comparison framework supporting MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, and kNN; (3) gKNN, a geographically informed kNN variant that blends socioeconomic and spatial distances and exposes donor contributions for provenance-based accountability; (4) a Method Comparison Summary reporting MAE, RMSE, Delta RMSE, and runtime; (5) cached per-method results and locked axis scales to reduce cognitive overhead during method switching.

Result: The system enables analysts to identify where imputation methods disagree, surface which variables are sensitive to imputation choices, and assess how those choices affect downstream summaries. Case studies demonstrate the dashboard's effectiveness in helping analysts select strategies, evaluate model robustness, and achieve provenance-based visual accountability through gKNN's donor contribution exposure.

Conclusion: ImputeViz contributes a method-agnostic visual analytics environment that elevates cross-method comparison to a first-class visual task, integrating the novel gKNN alongside standard imputation methods to support both statistical rigor and geospatial reasoning in missing data workflows.

Abstract: Missing data is a persistent obstacle in scientific, social science, and public health research, often biasing analyses and placing accountability on analysts for how they handle missing values. We introduce ImputeViz, an integrated visual analytics dashboard that supports diagnosing missingness, configuring imputation models, and evaluating results. The system brings together widely used methods, including MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, and kNN, within an interactive environment that makes missingness patterns explicit. To support geospatial reasoning, we introduce gKNN, a geographically informed kNN variant that blends socioeconomic and spatial distances and exposes donor contributions, enabling provenance-based visual accountability by showing which regions drive each estimate. Our primary contribution is a method-agnostic visual analytics environment that makes cross-method comparison a first-class visual task and integrates gKNN alongside standard methods. Coordinated views reveal missingness structure through heatmaps, co-missingness summaries, and distributional diagnostics that help analysts reason about missingness patterns (MCAR/MAR) and cases where missingness may be non-random (MNAR). Users can compare and tune models and interrogate results via distributional overlays, a Method Comparison Summary reporting MAE, RMSE, Delta RMSE, and runtime for each algorithm on the current target and mask, along with variable-level discrepancy views. Cached per-method results and locked axis scales reduce cognitive overhead from shifting ranges during method switching. These comparisons highlight where methods disagree, which variables are sensitive, and how imputation choices affect downstream summaries. Case studies demonstrate how ImputeViz helps analysts select effective strategies, surface sensitive variables, and assess model robustness.

</details>


### [61] [How YouTube Frames ChatGPT Use in Education: An Epistemic Network Analysis with Supporting Multimodal Metadata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08698)
*Shayla Sharmin,Mohammad Al-Ratrout,Mohammad Fahim Abrar,Roghayeh Leila Barmaki*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Analysis of 52 educational YouTube videos reveals three distinct discourse groups in how ChatGPT is framed to learners: as a thinking scaffold (G1), a retrieval/skill-building tool (G2), and an output generator (G3). Epistemic Network Analysis confirmed significant differences across groups, mirrored in multimodal metadata. Output-oriented content (G3) achieved visibility comparable to learning-oriented content (G2) despite weaker pedagogical depth, revealing a structural tension where shallow engagement content competes for reach against deeper AI literacy framing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Little is known about how large language models like ChatGPT are presented to learners in informal, creator-driven public discourse on platforms such as YouTube. The study addresses this gap by investigating how ChatGPT is framed across different creator groups and how those framings relate to audience response and platform reach.

Method: Following PRISMA guidelines, 52 educational YouTube videos about ChatGPT were selected. The study employed Epistemic Network Analysis (ENA) to identify and compare discourse patterns across creator groups. Multimodal metadata — transcripts, titles, thumbnails, and viewer comments — were systematically analyzed to triangulate framing differences and their relationship to audience reception and platform metrics.

Result: Three structurally distinct discourse groups emerged: G1 framed ChatGPT as a conceptual scaffold for thinking; G2 oriented toward retrieval practice and skill-building; G3 framed ChatGPT as a tool for output generation. ENA revealed statistically significant group differences with large effect sizes, consistently reflected across transcript discourse, titles, and thumbnails. Viewers of learning-oriented content described ChatGPT as a thinking partner or tutor; viewers of output-oriented content raised concerns about over-reliance, surface-level learning, and cognitive offloading. Critically, G3 achieved comparable platform reach to G2 despite substantially weaker learning-oriented framing.

Conclusion: A structural tension exists in self-directed AI learning: content prioritizing quick outputs reaches far more learners than content promoting deep engagement. This gap raises critical questions about whose vision of AI literacy scales and what learners are ultimately left with, highlighting potential risks in how informal, creator-driven discourse shapes public understanding of LLMs.

Abstract: We examine educational YouTube videos through multimodal metadata, such as transcripts, titles, thumbnails, and viewer comments, to investigate how ChatGPT is framed across creator groups and how those framings relate to audience response and platform reach. Little is known about how large language models are presented to learners in informal, creator-driven public discourse. Following PRISMA, we selected 52 videos for analysis. We identified three structurally distinct discourse groups: (G1) videos that positioned ChatGPT as a conceptual scaffold for thinking, (G2) videos oriented toward retrieval practice and skill-building, and (G3) videos that framed ChatGPT as a tool for output generation. Epistemic Network Analysis revealed statistically significant group differences with large effect sizes. Multimodal metadata consistently reflected these distinctions across transcript discourse, titles, and thumbnails. Viewers of learning-oriented content described ChatGPT as a thinking partner or tutor, whereas viewers of output-oriented content raised concerns about over-reliance, surface-level learning, and cognitive offloading. G3 achieved comparable platform reach to G2, yet with substantially weaker learning-oriented framing. This may suggest that output-oriented content competes for visibility despite lower pedagogical depth. These findings reveal a structural tension in self-directed AI learning: content that prioritizes quick outputs reaches far more learners than content that promotes deep engagement. This gap raises critical questions about whose vision of AI literacy scales and what learners are actually left with.

</details>


### [62] [Sculptable Mesh Structures for Room-Scale Form-Finding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08736)
*Jesse T. Gonzalez,Yanzhen Zhang,Dian Zhu,Alice Yu,Sapna Tayal,Nazm Furniturewala,Ziying Qi,Somin Ella Moon,Leyi Han,Alexandra Ion,Scott E. Hudson*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper presents a room-scale, shape-aware mesh structure with sensor-equipped edges that allows designers to physically sculpt coarse forms by adjusting edge lengths, with the resulting geometry automatically captured and integrated into a CAD design pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical prototyping with low-fidelity materials (paper, clay, foam) is essential for understanding the interplay between real-world objects and design intent, but the process is time-consuming—especially at large scales—and translating the resulting physical geometry into a CAD environment for further refinement is difficult.

Method: The authors developed a user-adjustable, room-scale mesh structure whose edges are equipped with resistive length sensors. Users physically manipulate the mesh by lengthening or shortening edges to sculpt coarse forms. Edge configurations are transmitted to a central computer in real time, enabling digital reconstruction of the physical form.

Result: The system enables direct, intuitive physical manipulation of a large-scale mesh while simultaneously capturing its geometry digitally, bridging the gap between low-fidelity physical prototyping and computational design workflows.

Conclusion: The shape-aware mesh structure successfully connects physical model-making with digital design pipelines, allowing designers to leverage the immediacy of tangible prototyping without losing the benefits of CAD integration and further refinement.

Abstract: It can be hard to design a physical structure entirely within the confines of a computer monitor. To better capture the interplay between real-world objects and a designer's work-in-progress, practitioners will often go through a sequence of low-fidelity prototypes (paper, clay, foam) before arriving at a form that satisfies both functional and aesthetic concerns. While necessary, this model-making process can be quite time-consuming, particularly at larger scales, and the resulting geometry can be difficult to translate into a CAD environment, where it will be further refined.
  This paper introduces a user-adjustable, room-scale, "shape-aware" mesh structure for low-fidelity prototyping. A user physically manipulates the mesh by lengthening and shortening the edges, altering the overall curvature and sculpting coarse forms. The edges are equipped with resistive length sensors, and transmit their configuration to a central computer. The structure can later be reproduced in software, connecting this prototyping stage to the larger computational design pipeline.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [63] [Context Graphs for Proactive Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07721)
*Avinash Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a proactive enterprise AI system using a Context Graph that continuously monitors entity state changes, ranks insights by urgency/relevance, and surfaces notifications via LLM — achieving significant latency reduction over reactive RAG systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG and agentic AI systems are fundamentally reactive, waiting for user queries before acting. The authors argue that genuine enterprise productivity gains require proactive systems that anticipate information needs and surface actionable insights before workers explicitly ask.

Method: Introduces three core components: (1) Context Graph — a live relational data structure modeling enterprise entities, relationships, and state transitions; (2) Delta Detection Engine — continuously monitors state changes in the graph; (3) Proactivity Scorer — ranks candidate insights using urgency, relevance, and persona-fit signals; and (4) an LLM-powered Surfacing Layer that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. A unified Proactivity Score function formalizes the ranking, and a full Python implementation using NetworkX and Claude API demonstrates feasibility.

Result: Across three enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, sales pipeline hygiene), the system achieves Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and reduces mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 seconds.

Conclusion: Context-graph-driven proactive agents represent a meaningful paradigm shift from reactive to anticipatory enterprise AI, enabling significant productivity improvements through timely, persona-aware information surfacing with substantial latency reductions.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic frameworks have advanced enterprise AI considerably, yet agents remain fundamentally reactive: they wait for a human query before acting. This paper argues that genuine enterprise productivity gains require proactive agents: systems that surface relevant, actionable information to workers before they ask. We propose the Context Graph, a live relational data structure that models enterprise entities, their relationships, and state transitions over time. Built on this graph, we define a Delta Detection Engine that continuously monitors state changes, a Proactivity Scorer that ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit, and a Surfacing Layer powered by an LLM that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. We formalize each component, derive a unified Proactivity Score function, and provide a complete end-to-end Python implementation using NetworkX and the Anthropic Claude API. Evaluation across three generic enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, and sales pipeline hygiene) demonstrates that context-graph-driven proactivity achieves Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and reduces mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 second.

</details>


### [64] [ASMR: Agentic Schema Generation for Ship Maintenance Report Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08177)
*Sohrab Namazi Nia,Amogh Dalal,Ning Sa,Peter Ly,Marti Zentmaier,Tomek Strzalkowski,Jay Miller,Rishi Singh,Senjuti Basu Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ASMR, a two-agent framework that automatically generates compact, informative schemas from historical ship maintenance reports. A Field Generation Agent extracts concepts via multi-granularity clustering, and a Structural Optimizer Agent uses reinforcement learning to prune redundancy and optimize schema quality. The approach aims to improve report completeness and consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual schema design for diverse ship maintenance and operational reports is labor-intensive and inconsistent across form categories. An automatic method is needed to discover the essential information requirements embedded in historical narratives, enabling schemas that guide authors toward more complete, consistent, and actionable reports.

Method: ASMR is a modular agentic framework with two components: (1) a Field Generation Agent that extracts semantic concepts from historical report narratives and generates candidate schema fields via adaptive multi-granularity clustering; (2) a Structural Optimizer Agent that applies reinforcement learning to select compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations from the candidates.

Result: Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the ASMR approach, though the paper explicitly notes that open research challenges remain at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Conclusion: The ASMR framework shows potential for automating schema discovery from unstructured operational reports, but the work is still in early stages. The authors identify open challenges spanning data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI, suggesting future work is needed to fully realize the approach.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the automatic schema generation problem: given a collection of historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, automatically discover compact and informative schemas that capture the essential information requirements of each report type. To address this challenge, we propose ASMR, a modular agentic framework consisting of two specialized agents. A Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from historical narratives and generates candidate schema fields through adaptive multi-granularity clustering, while a Structural Optimizer Agent employs reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations. The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports. Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight several open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

</details>


### [65] [AI-integrated models for assessing agricultural resilience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07759)
*Joshua R. Waite,Dana Golden,Brett Indelicato,Kevin Camp,Mojdeh Saadati,Shannon Regan,Patrick Schnable,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian,Carlos Messina,Suzanne Thornsbury,Soumik Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an AI-powered tool integrating economic (GTAP) and biophysical (APSIM) models to analyze agricultural supply chain disruptions via natural language queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural supply chains face vulnerabilities from interconnected biophysical (e.g., climate, crop yields) and economic (e.g., trade, prices) shocks. Existing analytical tools are siloed within disciplines, hindering comprehensive cross-domain assessment by policymakers and market participants.

Method: Development of an AI-powered tool that couples the GTAP computable general equilibrium model with the APSIM crop simulation model. The system translates natural language queries into model inputs, executes cross-disciplinary simulations, and returns synthesized results in accessible language.

Result: The integrated tool enables users to query and receive responses about cross-disciplinary supply chain impacts, bridging biophysical and economic modeling domains without requiring expertise in either modeling framework.

Conclusion: AI-driven integration of heterogeneous models can democratize access to complex supply chain analysis, empowering stakeholders to assess disruptions across linked biophysical-economic systems through intuitive natural language interfaces.

Abstract: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions through linked biophysical and economic systems. We develop an AI-powered tool that integrates economic models (GTAP) with biophysical models (APSIM) to analyze supply chain shocks, enabling policymakers and market participants to assess cross-disciplinary impacts through queries and responses written in natural language.

</details>


### [66] [Adversarial Social Epistemology for Assemblies of Humans and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07760)
*Mihnea C. Moldoveanu,Joel A. C. Baum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an "adversarial social epistemology" (ASE) framework to analyze how agents strategically exploit the trust mechanisms underlying scaffolded public communication—going beyond concepts like echo chambers and misinformation—and offers tools for auditing and redressing such trust breaches using epistemic networks and inferentialist semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing accounts of epistemic dysfunction—such as epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, and misinformation diffusion—fail to adequately capture how communicative agents in densely interactive landscapes exploit the very commitments and entitlements that render scaffolded assertions trustworthy. The authors seek to explain strategic manipulation (distortion, omission, fabrication, under-specification) driven by private, reputational, rhetorical, or material incentives.

Method: The authors develop a conceptual framework called "adversarial social epistemology" (ASE), which analyzes scaffolded public communication (chains of testimony, inference, institutional certification, tacit trust). They enrich this with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions, model communicative landscapes as epistemic networks, and outline mechanisms for auditing inferential chains and redressing trust breaches.

Result: The paper provides language and conceptual machinery to identify mechanisms that subvert trust in scaffolded public communications, explains how agents exploit normative commitments and entitlements, and offers a framework for detecting when the auditability of inferential chains has been undermined.

Conclusion: A robust analysis of contemporary communicative dysfunction requires moving beyond bubble/chamber metaphors toward an adversarial framework that accounts for strategic exploitation of epistemic norms. ASE, combined with epistemic network analysis and inferentialist semantics, provides the necessary tools for both diagnosing and redressing such trust breaches.

Abstract: We outline an adversarial social epistemology (ASE) for densely interactive communicative landscapes in which public assertions are scaffolded by chains of testimony, inference, institutional certification, and tacit trust. In such landscapes, agents have incentives and affordances to distort, color, omit, fabricate, or strategically under-specify information for private, reputational, rhetorical, or material gains. We argue that these phenomena are not adequately captured by familiar descriptions of epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, or misinformation diffusion. What requires explanation is how communicative agents exploit the commitments and entitlements that normally make scaffolded assertions trustworthy. We provide language that delivers the requisite analysis, outline mechanisms that subvert trust in scaffolded public communications, and outline machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches arising from subverting the auditability of inferential chains, drawing on epistemic networks, enriched with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions.

</details>


### [67] [Aligning Clinical Needs and AI Capabilities: A Survey on LLMs for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07761)
*Qi Peng,Jiatong Li,Sirui Huang,Yiyang Jiang,Kaisong Gong,Ronger Ding,Shijie Ye,Changmeng Zheng,Yi Cai,Xiaobo Yang,Jin Huang,Xiao-Yong Wei,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey examines medical LLMs through a dual-view framework: a clinical competency hierarchy based on Miller's Pyramid (five levels from knowledge recall to dynamic case management) and computational reasoning patterns (deductive, inductive, abductive). The authors benchmark 18 models across five reasoning levels, finding specialist models excel at diagnosis while general models lead in decision support and dialogue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical LLMs are increasingly deployed in healthcare, yet there lacks a systematic framework connecting clinical competency requirements with computational reasoning methods. Understanding model capabilities across different reasoning levels is essential for developing safer, workflow-ready systems.

Method: The authors propose a dual-view taxonomy: (1) a clinical axis organizing competency into five levels following Miller's Pyramid — knowledge recall, comprehension, application in simulated settings, real-world performance, and dynamic case management; (2) a computational axis mapping deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to medical tasks. They construct a benchmark dataset covering all five levels and evaluate 18 state-of-the-art models (both general and medical-specialist LLMs).

Result: Medical specialist LLMs outperform general models on diagnosis-centric tasks, while general-purpose models demonstrate superior performance in decision support and dialogue-oriented scenarios. Performance varies systematically across the five competency levels.

Conclusion: The dual-view framework provides a structured approach to evaluating and developing medical LLMs. Key open challenges include data scarcity, hallucination, and grounding in clinical reality. Future work should target safer, more reliable systems suitable for real clinical workflows.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as important tools in healthcare, showing growing potential for clinical reasoning and patient care. This survey examines recent progress in medical LLMs, focusing on reasoning applications and requirements. We present a dual-view approach that connects clinical practice with computational methods. On the clinical side, we establish a five-level competency scheme following Miller's Pyramid, progressing from knowledge recall to dynamic case management. On the computational side, we link deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning patterns to common medical goals and tasks. We also introduce a benchmark dataset spanning five levels of medical reasoning capability and report results on 18 state-of-the-art models, revealing that medical specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks while general models lead in decision support and dialogue. We conclude by discussing current progress and open challenges, including data limitations, hallucination, and grounding issues, and outline directions toward safer, more reliable, and workflow-ready systems.

</details>


### [68] [Alignment Plausibility: A New Standard for Assuring AI in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07766)
*Gwydion Williams,Sara Zannone,Bilal A Mateen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs increasingly provide mental health support but are driven by engagement maximization rather than therapeutic friction. Current safety efforts are reactive and miss subtler long-term risks like dependency and boundary erosion. The authors propose a three-level structural alignment framework mirroring clinical practice — value specification, embedded training, and deployment oversight — yielding "alignment plausibility," a regulatory construct analogous to biological plausibility for demonstrating that AI systems are safe and beneficial for health outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs deployed in mental health contexts are products of the attention economy, incentivized toward sustained user engagement rather than the therapeutic friction that effective psychological support requires. Current developer safety responses are reactive, targeting only the most visible acute harms, while subtler longitudinal risks — dependency, boundary erosion, amplification of distorted beliefs — remain under-addressed. There is no structured regulatory framework for demonstrating that an LLM is safe for health applications.

Method: The authors propose a three-level alignment framework that mirrors how society assures safety in human clinical practice: (1) explicit value specification grounded in codified normative commitments of clinical practice; (2) training that embeds those values into the model; and (3) deployment oversight that detects drift and long-term harm, analogous to clinical supervision. These three levels together yield the construct of "alignment plausibility," which the authors position as a regulatory construct by direct analogy to biological plausibility in medicine — a principled, structured argument for or against trust that a system is aligned to positive health outcomes.

Result: The paper introduces the concept of alignment plausibility as a regulatory framework. It does not present empirical results but provides a theoretical scaffold: by organizing alignment across value specification, training, and oversight, developers and regulators can make structured plausibility arguments about safety, non-maleficence, and patient benefit — analogous to how biological plausibility supports causal inference in epidemiology and drug approval.

Conclusion: Making LLMs structurally safe for mental health requires moving beyond reactive harm mitigation toward proactive, three-level alignment that mirrors clinical governance. Alignment plausibility offers a principled regulatory construct for evaluating whether AI systems are trustworthy for health contexts — ensuring they cause no harm when capable of doing so and ultimately lead to patient benefit.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become significant providers of mental health support, yet they remain products of an attention economy whose operational and commercial targets favour sustained engagement over the friction that effective psychological support often requires.
