Developers' safety responses have been largely reactive, addressing the most visible and acute harms while subtler, longer-term patterns of risk (e.g., dependency, boundary erosion, the amplification of distorted beliefs) receive less attention. We contend that making LLMs structurally safe requires alignment organised at three levels that mirror how society assures the safety of human clinical practice: 1) explicit value specification grounded in the codified normative commitments of clinical practice; 2) training that embeds those values in the model; and 3) oversight that detects drift and longer-term harm during deployment, much as clinical supervision does for human practice. Organising alignment in this way yields a construct we call alignment plausibility - a structured demonstration that a system's values, training regime, and oversight mechanisms are together consistent with safe and positive outcomes. We propose alignment plausibility as a regulatory construct (by drawing analogy to the established construct of biological plausibility) for AI in health: a principled way to argue for, or against, trust that systems are aligned to positive health outcomes, will cause no harm even where capable of doing so, and will ultimately lead to patient benefit.

</details>


### [69] [Idiobionics: The Unification of Privacy and Intelligent Robotic Prostheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07775)
*Kwesi Afari Darfoor,Patrick M. Pilarski,Bailey Kacsmar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of "idiobionics" — a new research area examining privacy threats at the intersection of intelligent bionic limbs and adversarial exploitation. The authors ground this concept in literature, present preliminary evidence of potential attacks on bionic limb designs, and propose open research questions for the wearable robotics community.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As robotic prostheses (bionic limbs) become increasingly integrated with advanced sensors and AI-based control, they also introduce threat vectors that malicious entities could exploit to violate user privacy. The authors argue that understanding and addressing these privacy risks is essential for user adoption and to fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs.

Method: The authors define the new research domain of "idiobionics," ground it in related literature across privacy and intelligent bionics, provide preliminary evidence demonstrating potential adversarial attacks on intelligent bionic limb designs, and curate a set of open research questions for the field.

Result: The paper establishes idiobionics as a formal line of inquiry, demonstrates plausible adversarial attack vectors that could target intelligent bionic limbs, and delivers a curated list of open research questions relevant to wearable robotics and human-facing autonomous systems researchers.

Conclusion: Idiobionics research is expected to help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices by proactively addressing privacy concerns that could otherwise hinder adoption and capability.

Abstract: The human body is at the center of a growing family of technologies designed to tightly and persistently couple biological and digital systems. Robotic prostheses are a representative example of this tight coupling. Also referred to as bionic limbs, robotic prostheses are devices that support people who have lost limbs in pursuing daily life activities such as walking and grasping objects. Bionic limbs are now perceptive and responsive owing to their integration with advanced sensors and artificial intelligence-based control approaches. Consequently, such robotic prostheses can now be viewed as semiautonomous wearable robotic systems that can co-adapt with their users. However, the same sensing and control advancements that increase the capability of robotic prostheses also introduce threat vectors that could be exploited by malicious entities to violate the privacy of users. To fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, we maintain it is important to directly understand and address these privacy risks and the barriers they might present to user adoption. This paper therefore introduces a new line of inquiry we term idiobionics to holistically investigate issues at the intersection of privacy and intelligent bionic limbs. As the main contribution of this paper, we define idiobionics, ground it in related literature, and provide preliminary evidence showing and discussing potential adversarial attacks that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs. We then contribute a curated list of open research questions within idiobionics that are relevant to researchers in wearable robotics and other human-facing autonomous systems. We expect that idiobionics research will help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices.

</details>


### [70] [Infinity-Parser2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07836)
*Zuming Huang,Jun Huang,Kexuan Ren,Baode Wang,Weizhen Li,Jianming Feng,Yu Wang,Yichen Yao,Shijun Lin,Yige Tang,Cheng Peng,Weidi Xu,Wei Chu,Yinghui Xu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Infinity-Parser2 is a large multimodal model for document parsing that combines a controllable data synthesis pipeline (producing a 5M-sample bilingual corpus) with multi-task joint reinforcement learning across eight objectives. It achieves SOTA results on olmOCR-Bench (87.6%) and ParseBench (74.3%), with two variants released for low-latency (Flash) and precision-critical (Pro) settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated document parsing corpora limits progress in end-to-end document parsing. Existing datasets lack coverage across diverse document types, canonical content forms, and reading-order annotations, motivating a scalable synthetic data engine combined with multi-task RL to unify perception, structure, and reasoning.

Method: (1) A controllable data-synthesis pipeline with iterative refinement that generates Infinity-Doc2-5M, a 5M-sample bilingual corpus annotated with bounding boxes, canonical forms (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, structured charts), and reading order. (2) A verifiable multi-task reward system enabling Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives: document parsing, layout analysis, table/math/chart/chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding. (3) Two model variants under a shared architecture: Flash (low-latency, 3.68× throughput gain) and Pro (precision-optimized).

Result: Infinity-Parser2-Pro achieves state-of-the-art performance: 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5. Strong generalization is demonstrated on charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA. The Flash variant achieves a 3.68× throughput improvement over Infinity-Parser-7B.

Conclusion: The combination of scalable controllable data synthesis and multi-task joint RL yields a highly capable, generalizable document parsing system. The open-sourcing of Infinity-Doc2-5M and the release of both efficiency- and precision-oriented variants provide practical value for diverse deployment scenarios.

Abstract: We present Infinity-Parser2, a large multimodal model that couples a controllable data-synthesis pipeline with multi-task reinforcement learning for end-to-end document parsing, addressing the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing corpora. Our contributions are threefold. First, we build a scalable synthesis engine, pairing a controllable rendering framework with an iterative refinement loop, and use it to construct and open-source Infinity-Doc2-5M: a 5-million-sample bilingual (Chinese/English) corpus spanning diverse document types, annotated with element bounding boxes, canonical content forms (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, structured charts), and full-page reading order. Second, we introduce a verifiable, multi-task reward system that enables Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table parsing, math formula parsing, chart parsing, chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding), unifying perception, structure, and reasoning in a single optimization signal. Third, we release two variants under a shared architecture: Infinity-Parser2-Flash, optimized for low-latency inference with a $3.68\times$ throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B, and Infinity-Parser2-Pro, engineered for precision-critical settings. Infinity-Parser2-Pro reaches state-of-the-art 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

</details>


### [71] [VectorizationLLM: Smart Vectorization Based AI Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07846)
*Ryan Duke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VectorizationLLM is a RAG-augmented LLM based on Google open-weight models, built to tutor students in MATLAB-based signal processing and mathematical analysis without giving direct answers, using course-specific materials from CTEC 247 at NYIT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide students with an instructive, non-answer-giving assistant that supports learning complex topics — smart vectorization, Fourier analysis, piecewise functions, and differential equations — within a specific MATLAB-focused engineering course, ensuring explanations are grounded in authentic course materials.

Method: Built on Google open-weight LLMs, VectorizationLLM employs a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) architecture with a curated knowledge base drawn from in-class notes. A structured system prompt constrains the model to offer detailed conceptual explanations with code, text, and image examples while explicitly preventing it from providing direct answers to problem sets.

Result: The model delivers multi-modal, context-rich responses (code, text, images) grounded in the CTEC 247 curriculum, functioning as a domain-specific pedagogical assistant that reinforces learning through guided explanation rather than solution disclosure.

Conclusion: VectorizationLLM demonstrates a targeted application of RAG-based LLMs for higher education in electrical and computer engineering, offering a controlled instructional tool that aligns with academic integrity by scaffolding student understanding without circumventing assessment.

Abstract: VectorizationLLM is a specialized Large Language Model based on Google open-weight LLMs. The model is designed to assist students to learn smart vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB. The course application is CTEC 247: Applied Computational Analysis II by the Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering Technology at New York Institute of Technology Old Westbury. The LLM model is designed to be an instructive assistant, providing detailed explanations of concepts with examples from in-class notes without providing direct answers to questions. The model is designed with a RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) knowledge base and system prompt architecture. Examples in both code, text, and images are provided in the LLM responses.

</details>


### [72] [Feedback Manipulation Regularization: Enabling Offline Agent Alignment for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07859)
*Benjamin Poole,Minwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FMR is an algorithm-agnostic method that uses evaluative feedback to correct imitation learning policies, achieving up to 98% reduction in misalignment on adapted Safety Gymnasium benchmarks, even with limited or noisy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most alignment research combines human demonstrations and feedback through multi-stage pipelines for contextual bandit settings (e.g., language generation), neglecting how these complementary signals can be jointly leveraged for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments.

Method: Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR) — an algorithm-agnostic approach that integrates evaluative feedback as a corrective signal into imitation learning. Evaluated on adapted Safety Gymnasium environments serving as alignment testbeds.

Result: FMR demonstrates improved alignment aptitude and up to 98% reduction in misalignment across various imitation learning algorithms. Remains robust under limited data regimes with scarce aligned demonstrations and uninformative noisy data.

Conclusion: Evaluative feedback can serve as an effective corrective signal for single-stage offline alignment in sequential decision-making, and FMR provides a robust, algorithm-agnostic mechanism to achieve this.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) research has increasingly shifted focus towards alignment, ensuring agents learn behaviors adhering to human values. While human demonstrations and feedback have proven crucial for alignment, existing approaches predominantly combine these signals using multi-stage pipelines designed for the contextual bandit framing of language generation. Yet little work explores how these complementary inputs can serve as a richer, interconnected signal for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments. We propose Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR), an algorithm-agnostic method that harnesses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve the alignment of imitation learning policies. We adapt Safety Gymnasium environments to be a principled testbed for alignment evaluation, demonstrating improved aptitude and up to a 98\% reduction in misalignment across a range of imitation learning algorithms. FMR remains robust in limited data regimes, even when learning from scarce aligned and uninformative noisy demonstrations.

</details>


### [73] [A Graph Neural Network Model for Real-Time Gesture Recognition Based on sEMG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07850)
*Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan,Kamal Premaratne,Manohar Murthi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel graph network-based representation of sEMG signals enables real-time hand gesture recognition with 99% accuracy, outperforming state-of-the-art methods while maintaining low latency (48ms) suitable for prosthetics and AR applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate and immediate hand gesture recognition is essential for seamless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality. Current sEMG-based approaches lack representations that fully capture spatial muscle activation patterns across the forearm.

Method: The authors propose representing sEMG signals as graph networks that encode muscle activation patterns in the forearm. A graph neural network (GNN) is then trained for real-time hand gesture classification. Data was collected using a MyoBand (8 electrodes) from 8 healthy subjects.

Result: The proposed method achieved average classification accuracy of 99%, surpassing state-of-the-art techniques. Total latency (graph construction + prediction) averaged 48ms on an M1 Pro CPU, meeting real-time requirements.

Conclusion: Graph network-based sEMG representation combined with GNNs provides a highly accurate and computationally efficient solution for real-time hand gesture recognition, well-suited for prosthetics and augmented reality applications.

Abstract: For seemless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality, accurate and immediate hand gestures recognition is essential. Surface electromyography (sEMG) signals obtained from the forearm are commonly employed for this purpose. In this paper, we present a novel approach for sEMG representation that utilizes graph networks which contain information about muscle activation patterns in the forearm. Based on these graph networks, we have developed a machine learning algorithm capable of real-time hand gesture recognition using a graph neural network. The algorithm's performance was evaluated using sEMG signals acquired from myoband, which has 8 electrodes placed around the forearm, involving 8 healthy subjects. The proposed method demonstrated an average classification accuracy of 99\%, surpassing the performance of state-of-the-art techniques. The average time for both graph construction and prediction stood at 48ms utilizing a M1 pro CPU, rendering the approach well-suited for real-time applications.

</details>


### [74] [Agentic AI and Retrieval-Augmented Models in Straight-Through Underwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07858)
*Robert Richardson,Josh Meyers,Brian Hartman,David Sandberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores AI architectures for actuarial work, comparing three underwriting pipelines (single-LLM, naive RAG, and multi-agent Agentic RAG) for small commercial Business Owner Policies. The agentic system, which combines targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and multi-step rule evaluation, outperforms the others, especially in complex scenarios involving multiple steps or missing information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Actuarial practice is being reshaped by AI, creating a design space spanning rule-based automation to LLMs, RAG, and agentic systems. Actuaries need architectures that support transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance, particularly for straight-through decision processes. The paper aims to provide concrete guidance on how these emerging architectures can meet actuarial priorities.

Method: The authors construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). They compare three underwriting pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent "Agentic RAG" pipeline that integrates targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation.

Result: The agentic system achieves the best overall performance. The largest gains occur in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection mechanisms help the model avoid making unsupported straight-through decisions.

Conclusion: Multi-agent agentic architectures that combine retrieval, external data verification, and reflective rule evaluation offer superior reliability for actuarial straight-through decision processes compared to single-LLM or naive RAG approaches, particularly under conditions of complexity and incomplete data.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is beginning to reshape actuarial practice, particularly in domains that require reasoning over unstructured documents, heterogeneous data sources, and regulated decision workflows. Actuaries now face a design space that ranges from traditional rule-based automation to large language models (LLMs), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and multi-agent ``agentic'' systems that plan, retrieve, call tools, and reflect. This paper examines how these emerging architectures can support actuarial priorities such as transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance, with a focus on straight-through decision processes. To make these ideas concrete, we develop and analyze an agentic AI framework for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). We construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment and compare three underwriting pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent ``Agentic RAG'' pipeline that combines targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation. The agentic system performs best overall, with the largest gains in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection help the model avoid unsupported straight-through decisions.

</details>


### [75] [AutoPersonas: A Multi-Timescale Loop Engine for Open-Ended Persona Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08252)
*Mengchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper identifies "self-locking" as a failure mode in long-term persona agents where life trajectories collapse toward repetitive, stagnant patterns. The authors introduce AutoPersonas, an OSO (Occurrences-Observations-State) loop engine that separates divergent event generation from evidence-governed absorption, and demonstrate through simulations that this approach reduces thematic repetition while preserving identity continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term persona agents suffer from self-locking: a runtime failure where locally plausible events accumulate but the generated life trajectory collapses into familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. Existing systems lack mechanisms to balance identity continuity with adaptive divergence, leading to behavioral convergence toward high-probability channels.

Method: AutoPersonas introduces a multi-timescale life-environment engine with an OSO (Occurrences → Observations → State) loop architecture. The system separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Key design: admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. Evaluated via: (1) a three-year compressed simulation, (2) an eight-model 40-day stress test generating 1,600 events, (3) a 40-day A/B test with context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting, and (4) a juvenile-goblin fictional-world run.

Result: Three-year simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. 40-day stress test: mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition reached 95.2%–97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%–88.0% macro-theme repetition in direct-loop runs. A/B test: context-slice masking plus divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. The fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions.

Conclusion: The bounded claim is supported: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity. This offers a principled approach to maintaining long-term persona agency without collapse into stereotyped behavioral loops.

Abstract: Long-term persona agents must remain identifiable while adapting to new events, relationships, evidence, and social conditions. We identify self-locking as a runtime failure mode in continuing persona-life loops: locally plausible events keep appearing while the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. We trace this failure to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity from State, memory, history, and environment summaries. We introduce AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine for bounded persona-level recursive self-evolution. It separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. A three-year compressed simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. An eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events and found mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition across all direct-loop runs. In a same-runtime 40-day A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions. These results support a bounded claim: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

</details>


### [76] [Nigeria Machinery: A Low-Resource Industrial Dataset with a Domain-Grounded Reasoning Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07883)
*Gospel Bassey,Vincent Fakiyesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset (89 records, 28 indicators, 2006–2025) and a method for generating chain-of-thought reasoning examples from sparse numeric data. It also addresses a quality problem where LLM-generated prompts match numbers superficially without genuine domain grounding, and demonstrates a fix that raises domain-grounded prompts from 1/78 to 94/94.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a scarcity of public, model-ready industrial machinery data for African economies, which hampers quantitative analysis and training of language models on numeric tasks grounded in that context. The authors aim to fill this gap with a curated dataset and a reasoning-layer construction method.

Method: The authors curate 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators from public sources covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil & gas sectors (2006–2025), each with a codebook and source provenance. They then build a chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning layer yielding 94 prompt–completion–reasoning-trace rows, where each prompt explicitly names the real indicator, subsector, year, and source. They also identify and fix a common problem in LLM-built datasets: prompts that match numeric values but lack actual domain content, and validate the fix by comparing the share of domain-grounded prompts before and after.

Result: The fix raised domain-grounded prompt share from 1 out of 78 to 94 out of 94. All retrieval answers (84 out of 84) now match their source values. The dataset comprises 89 records with 28 indicators (17 having only one observation), making it a reference/seed dataset rather than a large training set. Most reasoning rows are retrieval-based rather than multi-step computation.

Conclusion: The released dataset and reasoning layer provide a starting point for quantitative work on Nigerian industrial machinery. The paper also highlights and addresses a subtle quality failure mode in LLM-assisted dataset construction — surface-level numeric matching without domain grounding — and shows it can be systematically corrected. The authors explicitly note the dataset's limitations as a small, sparse reference set.

Abstract: There is relatively little, public, and model-ready data on industrial machinery for African economies. This makes it hard to do quantitative analysis or to train language models on numeric tasks grounded in that setting. We release two things to help with part of this problem. The first is the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset: 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators, covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil and gas sectors from 2006 to 2025. Every record names a public source and is decoded by a codebook. The second is a method for building chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples from these sparse numeric values. The result is 94 prompt, completion, and reasoning-trace rows. In every row, the prompt names the real indicator, subsector, year, and source of the record it comes from. The data adaptation work was carried out by Adaption Labs. Along the way we describe a problem that is common when language models are used to build datasets. The prompts can match the real numbers while saying nothing about the real domain. We show that fixing this raises the share of domain-grounded prompts from 1 out of 78 in an earlier release to 94 out of 94, and that every retrieval answer now matches its source value (84 out of 84). We release the data, the reasoning layer, and a per-row provenance file under CC-BY-4.0. We are clear about the limits. With 89 records and 17 indicators that have only one observation, this is a reference and seed dataset, not a large training set. Most reasoning rows are retrieval rather than multi-step computation.

</details>


### [77] [From Prompts to Contracts: Harness Engineering for Auditable Enterprise LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08028)
*Joongho Ahn,Moonsoo Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a harness-engineering methodology for transitioning LLM prototypes into production-grade, auditable applications. The approach moves deterministic behavior into code, manifests, and validation artifacts while preserving source-grounded claims as the authoritative runtime answers. Evaluated on Korean corporate data, the harness enforces source-grounding, entity-routing, traceability, and output contracts reliably across model substitutions, and its code-owned guarantees outperform prompt-only enforcement and external guardrails in preserving both safety and utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise LLM applications start as prototypes where behavior is driven by prompts and retrieval context. Productionization introduces stricter requirements: source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces. The core motivation is to bridge the gap between exploratory prototypes and auditable, production-ready systems without sacrificing the flexibility of LLM-driven components.

Method: A harness-engineering approach that reconstructs the LLM application pattern into a traceable, auditable agent architecture. Deterministic behaviors — routing, contract enforcement, validation, and trace generation — are extracted into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts wrapped around a replaceable composition boundary. Source-backed claims remain authoritative at runtime. The approach is instantiated on public data from five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) and evaluated through three research questions with fault-injection controls and ablation studies (prompt-only enforcement vs. harness vs. bolt-on guardrails).

Result: (1) The harness preserved all contracts (source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, recommendation-language) across fixed validation scenarios; fault-injection confirmed validators flag broken contracts. (2) Across three hosted models on 270 composition-boundary runs, harness-enforced checks passed universally; model-side failures were caught and recorded. (3) Prompt-only enforcement allowed recommendation-language and internal-trace-leakage violations to surface; the harness blocked them entirely. A bolt-on guardrail also blocked violations but over-refused, dropping utility to 88/120 versus the harness's 120/120. Only code-owned enforcement preserved both safety and utility.

Conclusion: The harness-engineering pattern provides a reusable, versioned methodology for converting exploratory LLM prototypes into auditable, production-grade applications. Code-owned enforcement of contracts around a replaceable model composition boundary proves essential: it maintains full utility while reliably blocking violations — a result that prompt-only approaches and external guardrails fail to achieve simultaneously.

Abstract: Enterprise large language model (LLM) applications often begin as prototypes whose behavior is carried by prompts and retrieval context. Productization adds requirements for source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces. We present a harness-engineering approach that reconstructs this pattern into a traceable, auditable LLM-agent architecture: deterministic behavior moves into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary, while source-backed claims remain the authority for runtime answers. We instantiate it on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) and evaluate three research questions. (1) The harness preserves its source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, and recommendation-language contracts across the fixed validation scenarios; a fault-injection control confirms the validators flag deliberately broken contracts. (2) The checks the harness enforces held under model substitution: across three hosted models, they passed on all 270 composition-boundary runs; failures were confined to the model-composed side and were caught and recorded. (3) The code-owned guarantees are load-bearing, not reproducible by prompting alone: holding the model fixed and varying only the enforcement layer, prompt instructions alone let recommendation-language and internal-trace-leakage violations reach the reader, which the harness blocks entirely. A bolt-on external guardrail prevents such violations too but over-refuses, dropping utility to 88/120 where the harness preserves full utility (120/120); in this ablation, only code-owned enforcement preserves both safety and utility. The result is a reusable engineering pattern for turning exploratory prototypes into auditable applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts.

</details>


### [78] [Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916)
*Luke Baines,Anton Gonzalvez Hawthorne,Mariia Koroliuk,Irakli Shalibashvili,Clément Dumas,Konstantinos Voudouris,David Demitri Africa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method to control LLM personas by treating them as positions in the OCEAN personality trait space. Using low-rank adapters trained to amplify or suppress individual traits, the authors demonstrate monotonic trait scaling, additive composition for mixed personas, and preservation of capability benchmarks. They also show downstream safety effects and introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline for discovering behavioral factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit recurring behavioral patterns (personas) that affect generalization and safety, yet there is a lack of reliable tools to decompose, measure, and control these personas systematically.

Method: The authors treat personas as positions in the OCEAN trait space (Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, Neuroticism). They train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress each trait, then evaluate effects using: (1) an LLM-judge calibrated against a human-validated panel, (2) trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and (3) standard capability evaluations. They also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four interpretable behavioral factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts.

Result: Across six models from three families (4B–32B), each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with scale. Adapters combine approximately additively to construct mixed personas, and capability benchmarks are preserved at moderate scales. Trait axes affect safety-relevant behavior: neuroticism influences frustration, and agreeableness influences sycophancy. The unsupervised pipeline recovers interpretable behavioral factors distinct from OCEAN.

Conclusion: Persona control can be framed as learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, providing a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety. The approach offers a principled mechanism for steering LLM behavior through trait decomposition and recomposition.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit recurring behavioural patterns -- personas -- that shape generalisation and safety, but we lack reliable tools for decomposing, measuring, and controlling them. Our central insight is to treat personas as positions in a space of behavioural traits, using the OCEAN framework to describe model personas in terms of Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism. We train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress individual traits, and evaluate their effects using an LLM-judge calibrated against a human-validated panel, trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and standard capability evaluations. Across six models from three families (4B-32B), we find that each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with scale, combines approximately additively with other adapters to construct mixed personas, and preserves performance on capability benchmarks at moderate scales. We further show that the induced trait axes affect safety-relevant behaviour in downstream evaluations: for example, moving along neuroticism and agreeableness axes affects frustration and sycophancy respectively. We also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four interpretable behavioural factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts. Persona control can then be considered in terms of learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, providing a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

</details>


### [79] [Evaluating the Effect of Frame Rate in Sequence-Based Classification of Autism-Related Self-Stimulatory Hand Idiosyncrasies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07957)
*Raunak Mondal,Peter Washington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study optimizes automated video-based detection of autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors by comparing LSTM and GRU architectures at various temporal sampling rates (peak accuracy ~98-99% at 15-frame intervals), evaluating ten data augmentation strategies (horizontal flip best standalone; upsampling most critical), and demonstrating a personalized per-subject modeling approach, providing practical guidance for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over 75 million individuals worldwide are affected by ASD, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening are lacking. Prior CNN-based approaches achieved only 62-76% accuracy on the SSBD dataset, motivating the search for better architectures, optimal temporal sampling rates, and effective augmentation strategies for small behavioral datasets.

Method: (1) LSTM and GRU models were trained on pose-derived features from the SSBD dataset at six frame sampling intervals (1, 5, 15, 30, 45, 90 frames). (2) Ten data augmentation strategies were evaluated within an I3D transfer learning pipeline via ablation study. (3) A personalized per-subject modeling approach was tested using temporally split video segments with per-subject train/test splits.

Result: Both LSTM (97.5%) and GRU (98.75%) substantially outperformed prior CNN baselines (62-76%), with optimal sampling at every 15 frames. Horizontal flip achieved the highest standalone augmentation accuracy (48.78%); removing upsampling caused the largest performance drop, indicating its necessity. The personalized per-subject approach yielded consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79).

Conclusion: Sequence-based architectures (LSTM/GRU) with appropriate temporal sampling dramatically improve over CNNs for ASD-related behavior classification. Upsampling is essential for augmenting small behavioral video datasets. The findings offer practitioners concrete, evidence-based guidance on architecture selection, sampling rate, and augmentation strategy in data-scarce clinical domains.

Abstract: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) affects over 75 million individuals worldwide, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening remain limited. This study addresses two complementary challenges in automated detection of autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors from video: (1) identifying the optimal sequence-based neural network architecture and temporal sampling rate, and (2) characterizing data augmentation strategies for training on small behavioral datasets. For the first objective, long short-term memory (LSTM) and gated recurrent unit (GRU) models were trained on pose-derived features from the Self-Stimulatory Behavior Diagnosis (SSBD) dataset at frame sampling intervals of 1, 5, 15, 30, 45, and 90 frames. Both architectures exceeded prior convolutional neural network (CNN) baselines (62-76% accuracy), with peak accuracies of 97.5% (LSTM) and 98.75% (GRU) at a sampling interval of every 15 frames. For the second objective, ten data augmentation strategies were applied to an I3D transfer learning pipeline, with an ablation study quantifying the marginal contribution of each technique. Horizontal flip achieved the highest standalone accuracy (48.78%), while exclusion of upsampling from the augmentation pipeline produced the largest performance degradation, indicating its necessity for complex behavioral video augmentation. A personalized machine learning approach, in which per-subject models were trained and tested on temporally split segments of each video, produced consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79). These results provide practitioners with concrete guidance on architecture selection, sampling rate, and augmentation strategy for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical domains.

</details>


### [80] [Workflow as Knowledge: Semantic Persistence for LLM-Mediated Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08740)
*Emanuele Quinto,Carlo Andrea Rozzi,Francesco Zanitti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a conceptual model for LLM workflows where all artifacts (definitions, instances, traces, snapshots) are persistent knowledge objects in a shared substrate, with a key semantic distinction between deterministic "derive" and LLM-mediated "infer," enabling inspectable, resumable, and reviewable workflow representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM applications increasingly rely on explicit workflows for tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, and human approval. While existing workflow systems handle execution, there is no coherent conceptual model that treats workflow artifacts as first-class persistent knowledge objects with clear semantics distinguishing deterministic computation from LLM-mediated judgment.

Method: Uses a Lisp-inspired but language-independent conceptual framework based on three explanatory lenses: (1) symbolic forms, (2) object identity, and (3) live-image thinking. In this model, five categories of artifacts — workflow definitions, workflow instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations — are represented as persistent knowledge objects within a shared knowledge substrate. The central semantic contribution is the derive/infer distinction: "derive" is deterministic computation over available state; "infer" is mediated LLM judgment under declared context with executor-controlled capability policy.

Result: A preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence: workflows can themselves be represented as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects, rather than merely producing knowledge and leaving traces.

Conclusion: The proposed model establishes a foundation for semantic persistence in LLM workflows, though formal transition semantics remain future work.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly use explicit workflows for tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, and human approval. Existing workflow systems already address many execution concerns. This paper proposes a Lisp-inspired but language-independent conceptual model: symbolic forms, object identity, and live-image thinking are used as explanatory lenses, not implementation commitments. In this model, workflow definitions, workflow instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations are represented as persistent knowledge objects in a shared knowledge substrate. Its central semantic distinction is between derive and infer: derive is deterministic computation over available state; infer is mediated LLM judgment under declared context and executor-controlled capability policy.
  The result is a preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence: workflows do not merely produce knowledge and leave traces, but can themselves be represented as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects, while formal transition semantics remain future work.

</details>


### [81] [Agentic Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07984)
*Seokhoon Jeong,Mijung Kim,Taehwan Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentNAS combines LLM-generated seed architectures with slotted decomposition to automatically define task-specific NAS search spaces, achieving SOTA on 11 of 17 diverse tasks. The LLM and NAS components are complementary: LLM seeds outperform baselines, and NAS adds further gains via combinatorial slot recombination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NAS methods are constrained by manually engineered, task-specific search spaces requiring domain expertise. LLMs can generate architectures open-endedly, but no existing work optimally divides labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search. The paper aims to bridge these paradigms by letting an LLM automatically define the search space for NAS, eliminating manual engineering.

Method: A three-phase pipeline called AgentNAS: (1) an LLM generates a high-quality seed architecture; (2) the seed is decomposed into a "slotted architecture" — a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that defines a bounded, task-specific NAS search space; (3) conventional NAS explores this automatically derived space via combinatorial recombination across slots. The method is evaluated across three LLMs of varying capability.

Result: AgentNAS achieves new SOTA on 11 of 17 tasks across classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS). The LLM-generated seed alone surpasses published baselines on most tasks. NAS delivers additional gains through slot-level combinatorial search, which independent LLM sampling cannot replicate. Results are robust across three LLMs.

Conclusion: LLM-driven architecture generation and NAS-driven search are broadly complementary. The slotted architecture mechanism successfully bridges the two paradigms, automating search space construction and yielding strong empirical performance across diverse tasks and modalities without task-specific manual design.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) methods have grown increasingly efficient, yet they remain bounded by manually engineered search spaces that require substantial domain expertise and must be rebuilt for every new task. Large language models (LLMs) can generate architectures in an open-ended space, but how to optimally divide the labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search remains unexplored. We propose a mechanism that bridges these two paradigms: an LLM produces a high-quality seed architecture, then decomposes it into a "slotted architecture", a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space for conventional NAS to explore, without manual engineering. We instantiate this mechanism in AgentNAS, a modular three-phase pipeline in which each component's contribution can be measured independently. On 17 tasks spanning classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging across diverse modalities (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS), AgentNAS establishes a new state of the art on 11 tasks, outperforming published baselines including task-specific expert designs. Ablation studies show that the two search mechanisms are broadly complementary: the LLM-generated seed already surpasses published baselines on the majority of tasks, and NAS delivers additional gains in most cases through combinatorial recombination across slots, a mode of search that independent LLM samples cannot replicate. These patterns hold across three LLMs of different capability levels, confirming that the division of labor is robust. Our code is available at https://github.com/alroimfebruary/AgentNAS.

</details>


### [82] [Using AI-based Learning Assistants in Higher Education: A Large-Scale Descriptive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08748)
*Kristina Schaaff,Quintus Stierstorfer,Valerie Heckel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large-scale analysis of 77,543 students reveals that AI learning assistant (Syntea) usage in higher education varies significantly across gender, age, study cluster, degree, and study mode, providing empirical evidence where prior research relied on small samples and self-reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior research on educational chatbots has been limited by small sample sizes and self-reported survey data, leaving a gap in large-scale, objective evidence on how students actually use AI-based learning assistants in real educational settings.

Method: Descriptive analysis of objective log data from 77,543 distance-learning students using the Syntea AI learning assistant, examining usage patterns across five demographic and structural dimensions: gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode.

Result: Syntea is already integrated into many learners' study routines, but meaningful usage differences exist across demographic groups and structural contexts — indicating that adoption and engagement are not uniform.

Conclusion: The study delivers a large-scale empirical foundation for refining AI-based learning support in higher education and demonstrates the value of analyzing actual usage behavior rather than relying solely on self-reported data.

Abstract: In this study, we present a large-scale descriptive analysis of the use of an AI-based learning assistant (Syntea) in higher education. Based on objective log data from 77,543 students enrolled in distance studies, we examine usage patterns across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode. To date, existing research on educational chatbots has largely relied on comparatively small samples and self-reported survey data, while large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior remains limited. Our findings show that Syntea is already embedded in the study routines of many learners, but that usage differs across demographic and structural contexts. By identifying these patterns, our study provides an empirical basis for the further development of AI-based learning support and contributes a large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

</details>


### [83] [Concretized Proposition Prompting Resolves Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08018)
*Changhun Lee,Minguk Jeon,Jongkyung Shin,Chiehyeon Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that resolves the Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy by explicitly concretizing relevant propositions, significantly boosting reasoning in knowledge-intensive domains like medicine while remaining competitive in deductive reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face a fundamental tension—the Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy—where they struggle to simultaneously maintain strong compositional reasoning (deductive, logical) and knowledge-grounded factual accuracy. Existing approaches tend to favor one at the expense of the other.

Method: Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP): a framework that explicitly identifies and concretizes propositions relevant to a given question, providing a factually grounded yet logically organized scaffold for reasoning.

Result: CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, especially on medical benchmarks requiring precise factual knowledge. It remains competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning is prioritized. The approach scales across various foundation models and parameter sizes.

Conclusion: CPP effectively bridges composition-based and knowledge-based reasoning paradigms, resolving the composition-knowledge dichotomy and establishing a foundational framework for logically organized, factually grounded reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: LLMs often struggle to balance compositionality with knowledgeability, a challenge we define as Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy. To address this, we propose Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that explicitly concretizes propositions relevant to questions. The results demonstrate that CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, particularly in medical benchmarks where precise knowledge is paramount, while being competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning is prioritized. Additional experiments reveal that CPP is scalable to various foundation models and parameter sizes, being a fundamental paradigm that bridges the gap between composition- and knowledge-based approaches. Consequently, CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a solid foundation for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning.

</details>


### [84] [A safety-oriented hypothetico-deductive framework for AI-assisted differential diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08038)
*Fan Ma,Mauro Giuffrè,Donald Wright,Kent McCann,Mark Iscoe,Lingfei Qian,Mingyang Jiang,Chi Wing Ng,Na Hong,Huan He,Cathy Shyr,Qingyu Chen,Lee Schwamm,Lucila Ohno-Machado,Hua Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AegisDx is a safety-oriented LLM framework for clinical diagnosis that uses hypothetico-deductive reasoning with verification gates, outperforming standalone LLMs on diagnostic accuracy across multiple benchmarks and improving physician-rated safety scores in real-world ED settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs treat diagnosis as one-shot prediction without safeguards against missed high-risk alternatives or reasoning verification. Diagnostic error remains a major patient safety threat, motivating a framework that prioritizes safety-oriented reasoning over raw predictive accuracy.

Method: AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components with role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates. It generates broad differential diagnoses, enforces screening for "must-not-miss" conditions, verifies reasoning against grounded medical evidence, and structures actionable next steps. Evaluated on NEJM/JAMA case reports, Annals of Emergency Medicine cases, and a blinded physician evaluation of 43 real-world Yale New Haven ED notes (compared against GPT-5).

Result: Top-3 accuracy: 59.9% vs 52.1% (JAMA), 62.7% vs 51.4% (NEJM), 85.7% vs 68.6% (Annals of EM). Must-not-miss capture: 78.0% vs 52.0%. Blinded physician evaluation: composite safety score improved from 4.31 to 4.55 on 5-point scale (adjusted p = 2.1×10⁻⁴), with qualitative gains in must-not-miss identification and reasoning safety.

Conclusion: Engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework rather than optimizing for raw accuracy alone provides safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful bedside decision support for acute care workflows.

Abstract: Diagnostic error is a major threat to patient safety, yet current large language model (LLM) systems often treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction task, lacking safeguards against missed high-risk alternatives or rigorous verification of their reasoning. Here, we present AegisDx, a safety-oriented framework for hypothetico-deductive clinical reasoning. AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components through role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differential diagnoses, enforce explicit screening for dangerous "must-not-miss" conditions, verify reasoning against grounded medical evidence, and structure actionable next steps. We evaluated AegisDx across three layers. On literature-derived case reports from NEJM and JAMA, with GPT-oss-120B as the shared backbone, Top-3 diagnostic accuracy was 59.9% versus 52.1% for the standalone LLM on JAMA cases and 62.7% versus 51.4% on NEJM cases. On cases from Annals of Emergency Medicine, Top-3 accuracy was 85.7% versus 68.6%; against physician-consensus must-not-miss diagnosis sets, AegisDx captured at least one such condition among its top three diagnoses in 78.0% of cases versus 52.0%. In a blinded physician evaluation of 43 real-world emergency department notes from the Yale New Haven Health System compared against GPT-5, AegisDx improved the physician-rated composite safety score from 4.31 to 4.55 on a 5-point scale (adjusted p = 2.1x10^-4), with qualitative gains in must-not-miss identification and reasoning safety. Our findings suggest that engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework, rather than optimizing raw predictive accuracy alone, can provide a safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful layer of bedside decision support for acute care workflows.

</details>


### [85] [When LLMs Agree, Are They Right? Auditing Self-Consistency and Cross-Model Agreement as Confidence Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08065)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common assumption that consistency among LLM judges (or self-consistency) reliably indicates correctness, showing through a large-scale study (265,000 samples across 53 runners) that agreement is only a weak, regime-dependent proxy for accuracy, with frontier models exhibiting particularly dangerous over-confidence where high agreement masks frequent errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge and ensemble evaluation methods are widely deployed in enterprise pipelines under the assumption that agreement among or within models signals correctness. The authors aim to empirically test whether this foundational assumption holds up, especially as these methods scale to production use.

Method: A large-scale cross-runner study: 53 runners each drew K=50 samples on overlapping cases from GPQA Diamond and AIME datasets, spanning comparisons of model tier, prompting strategies, and inference scale, yielding 265,000 total samples. Majority-correctness served as the deployment label, with hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap for statistical analysis. An exploratory cross-family check across three Claude tiers was also conducted.

Result: Agreement is a positive but weak predictor of correctness (Spearman's rho 0.20–0.59). Its utility is regime-dependent: it works best for unsaturated mid-tier models and compute allocation decisions. Critically, the most consistent frontier model showed severe over-confidence — achieving agreement ≥0.8 on 77% of GPQA cases, yet 48% of those were wrong. Cross-family checks on Claude tiers confirmed frontier over-confidence with recurring confident errors across providers.

Conclusion: Self-consistency is a conditional proxy for correctness at best, not a standalone confidence score. The assumption that agreement implies accuracy is unreliable, particularly for frontier models where high consistency can mask systematic errors. Practitioners should not treat consistency as a substitute for ground-truth evaluation.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge (Zheng et al., 2023) is increasingly the default for evaluating AI systems in enterprise pipelines, often scaled to ensembles (Verga et al., 2024) or "mixture-of-experts" (Shazeer et al., 2017) panels of judges. These systems share a key assumption: that consistency -- agreement among judges, or among a model's own samples -- indicates correctness. We show this assumption is unreliable. Agreement is not accuracy: a model can agree with itself, and different models can agree with each other, out of shared bias, a memorized heuristic, or an option-position prior rather than truth. We ask when agreement is nonetheless a usable proxy, in a large-scale cross-runner study: 53 runners drew K=50 samples for assigned overlapping cases across comparisons of model tier, prompting, and scale on GPQA Diamond and AIME -- 265,000 samples. Using majority-correctness as the deployment label and a hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap, agreement is a positive but weak predictor (rho 0.20-0.59, all positive under item-clustered resampling) whose usefulness is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and for allocating compute, and worst -- over-confident yet no more accurate -- for the most consistent frontier model (agreement >=0.8 on 77% of GPQA case-result entries, 48% of those wrong). An exploratory cross-family check on three Claude tiers shows the same frontier over-confidence, with confident errors recurring across providers above a marginal-preserving null. Self-consistency is thus a conditional proxy for correctness, not a standalone confidence score. We publicly release the de-identified per-run rows and answer distributions.

</details>


### [86] [Persuasion Attacks Can Decrease Effectiveness of CoT Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08066)
*Jennifer Za,Julija Bainiaksina,Nikita Ostrovsky,Tanush Chopra,Victoria Krakovna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Chain-of-thought monitoring can backfire in adversarial settings: adversarial agents exploit the CoT scratchpad as a persuasion channel, increasing harmful action approval by 9.5% on average. A model-diverse fact-checking framework (e.g., Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor + GPT-4.1 fact-checker) reduces policy-violating approvals by up to 45%, compared to only 6% with same-model setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While CoT monitoring is a promising safety mechanism that uses visible reasoning traces to detect misaligned behavior, recent work shows LLMs are vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks. The authors investigate whether this vulnerability extends to CoT monitors themselves — specifically, whether an adversarial agent can persuade its monitor to approve policy-violating actions through the reasoning channel.

Method: The authors design an evaluation framework comprising 40 tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions. In the adversarial setting, agents are explicitly instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. They then introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework where the monitor and fact-checker roles can be assigned to models from different families (e.g., Claude 3.7 Sonnet as monitor, GPT-4.1 as fact-checker), comparing same-model vs. cross-model pairings.

Result: In adversarial settings, monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases approval of harmful actions by 9.5% on average, as the scratchpad becomes an additional persuasion channel. The model-diverse fact-checking approach (Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor + GPT-4.1 fact-checker) reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only 6% when using the same model for both roles.

Conclusion: CoT monitoring alone is insufficient against adversarial persuasion. Model-diverse fact-checking — pairing monitors and fact-checkers from different model families — provides a robust mitigation, substantially reducing policy-violating action approvals.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising safety mechanism for AI agents, based on the premise that visible reasoning traces can surface misaligned or deceptive behavior. While effective in standard scenarios, recent work highlights that LLMs remain vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks, where natural-language arguments override model constraints. We stress-test whether this vulnerability extends to monitoring LLMs: can an adversarial agent persuade its CoT monitor to approve proposed actions that violate the monitor's policy? We design an evaluation framework with 40 tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions, where agents are instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. We find that in such adversarial settings, monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases rather than decreases approval of harmful actions on average by 9.5%, as the scratchpad provides an additional persuasion channel. To address this, we introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework. We find that a fact-checker and monitor pairing from different model families, for example a Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor paired with a GPT-4.1 fact-checker, reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only 6%, when using the same model for both fact-checking and monitoring roles. Our results demonstrate that CoT monitoring alone may be insufficient against adversarial persuasion, and that model-diverse fact-checking provides a robust mitigation.

</details>


### [87] [PARA-PV: Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented PV Prediction Based on Frozen Foundation Model and Distribution Shift Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08079)
*Hang Fan,Weican Liu,Ying Lu,Dunnan Liu,Long Cheng,Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARA-PV is a physics-aware retrieval-augmented framework for PV power forecasting that retrieves historically similar patterns, calibrates predictions with a frozen time-series foundation model, corrects distribution shifts under changing regimes, and uses a physics-constrained loss to emphasize critical operational states like peaks and ramps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PV power forecasting is challenging because generation is jointly influenced by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and physical constraints. Existing methods struggle to capture these compounded factors, and dominant regular regimes in training data suppress learning of operationally critical but rare states (peaks, ramps, night-time).

Method: PARA-PV has four stages: (1) Patch-level encoding of multivariate PV observations with physics-aware retrieval of historically similar patches and analog trajectories; (2) Residual adapter calibration against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation model to inject general temporal knowledge without overriding physics; (3) Physics-aware distribution shift correction using gated mean-shift and scale corrections conditioned on power, weather, timestamp, and day/night; (4) Physics-constrained loss that partitions samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes with adaptive reweighting.

Result: Not explicitly reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: Embedding physical knowledge throughout the entire forecasting pipeline — from retrieval and calibration to distribution shift correction and loss design — yields a more physically grounded and operationally reliable PV forecasting framework. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Accurate photovoltaic (PV) power forecasting is essential for reliable grid dispatch and renewable energy integration, yet it remains challenging because PV generation is jointly shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and strict physical constraints. We propose PARA-PV, a Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented framework that embeds physical knowledge throughout the forecasting process. The framework first encodes multivariate PV observations into patch-level representations and, through a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner, retrieves historical patches and analog trajectories that are consistent with the current window in temporal shape, power level, PV operating state, and intra-day period; this yields a physically grounded base forecast. To supplement local memory with broader temporal knowledge, the base forecast is then calibrated against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation-model prior through a lightweight residual adapter, so that general temporal regularities are adapted to PV-specific dynamics without overriding the physically grounded prediction. Because residual conditional distribution shifts persist when weather and diurnal regimes change, a physics-aware distribution shift correction module subsequently adjusts the preliminary forecast using power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions, applying gated mean-shift and scale corrections selectively. Finally, a physics-constrained loss function partitions the samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes and adaptively reweights their error contributions, preventing the dominant regular regime from suppressing learning of operationally critical states. Our code is available at https://github.com/weican1103/PARA-PV.

</details>


### [88] [CausalDS: Benchmarking Causal Reasoning in Data-Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08093)
*Andrej Leban,Yuekai Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausalDS is a benchmark that evaluates LLMs as integrated data-science agents by combining symbolic causal reasoning with realistic data analysis. It uses synthetically generated structural causal models (SCMs) with observational data, natural-language narratives, and tasks spanning all three rungs of Pearl's causal hierarchy, requiring coding, tool use, uncertainty quantification, and abstention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks suffer from a critical divide: causal reasoning benchmarks lack realistic data analysis, while data science benchmarks lack principled causal structure. Moreover, current causal datasets rely on curated examples with limited templatized variation rather than systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures, creating a "causal parrot" risk where models exploit memorized patterns.

Method: The authors introduce CausalDS, where each instance ("scene") comprises: (1) a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data, (2) a synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain, and (3) optionally, empirical grounding via distributions from real-world datasets. Tasks are derived spanning Pearl's three rungs (association, intervention, counterfactual), with data-science prediction at Rung 1. An observation model introduces imperfect observations, requiring multi-step tool use and coding. Abstention (recognizing unanswerable questions) is a scored outcome.

Result: The benchmark jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science proficiency, uncertainty quantification, appropriate abstention, and tool-use/coding capabilities in a unified, principled framework that reduces memorization risks through complete synthetic generation while retaining empirical structure.

Conclusion: CausalDS fills a gap in the evaluation landscape by providing a systematic, synthetically generated benchmark that integrates causal reasoning across Pearl's full ladder with practical data-science workflows, tool use, and calibrated abstention.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as integrated data-science agents, combining abstract reasoning with advanced tool use. Yet the relevant benchmark landscape largely divides into symbolic causal reasoning benchmarks without realistic data analysis or data analysis benchmarks without a principled causal data-generating structure. Furthermore, existing causal evaluation datasets are often restricted to curated examples from existing sources, with diversity coming from limited templatized variations rather than from systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures. We introduce CausalDS, a benchmark for evaluating causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows. Each benchmark instance is a scene consisting of a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data and an accompanying synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain. We optionally ground the composition of the benchmark components in empirical distributions obtained from real-world datasets, thus retaining empirical structure while reducing the "causal parrot" risk through completely synthetic generation. From each scene, we then derive tasks spanning all three of Pearl's rungs, with typical data-science prediction tasks appearing as Rung 1. Most tasks include a data science coding component, where the model typically needs to use several tools to arrive at the final answer due to the frequent presence of imperfect observations, which are generated by an observation model. Additionally, recognizing when a question admits no warranted answer and abstaining is treated as a first-class scored outcome. The benchmark thus jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding.

</details>


### [89] [Answer Set Programming Energised! End-to-End Neurosymbolic Reasoning and Learning with ASP and Energy Based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08136)
*Jakob Suchan,Julius Monsen,Salim Baloch,Mehul Bhatt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a neurosymbolic framework that integrates answer set programming (ASP) with energy-based models, enabling joint continuous optimization with explicit declarative constraints and non-monotonic reasoning, demonstrated on MNIST, CLEVR, and MOT benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neurosymbolic approaches lack a unified framework that tightly couples symbolic reasoning (specifically ASP's non-monotonic, constraint-rich semantics) with continuous latent-space learning. The authors aim to bridge answer set programming, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories in a single, trainable architecture suitable for dynamic domains involving perception and interaction.

Method: The methodology modularly integrates answer set programming with an energy-based model (EBM) substrate. ASP provides declarative semantics with background knowledge, constraints, and non-monotonic inference. The EBM substrate enables joint optimization in continuous latent space. This yields an ASP-centric, end-to-end trainable platform that supports robust learning and reasoning.

Result: The authors provide a practical implementation, demonstrate basic functionality on MNIST digit classification, and evaluate on two benchmarks: the CLEVR visual question-answering dataset and the MOT multi-object tracking benchmark.

Conclusion: The proposed neurosymbolic methodology successfully generalizes and unifies recent advances at the intersection of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories, offering a practical platform for robust end-to-end training in dynamic, perception-driven domains.

Abstract: We present a general neurosymbolic reasoning and learning methodology based on a modular integration of answer set programming with an energy based model substrate. Key contributions are: (1) supporting joint optimisation in the continuous latent space through explicit ASP-based declarative semantics fully incorporating background knowledge, constraints, non-monotonic inference; and (2) advancing recent works at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories by providing a generalised model and practical platform for ASP-centric robust, end-to-end training for applications in dynamic domains (e.g., involving perception and interaction). We provide a practical implementation, and demonstrate basic use and application (with MNIST), and evaluate with the visual question-answering benchmark Clevr and the multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

</details>


### [90] [Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08173)
*Jack Hopkins,Dipika Khullar,Fabien Roger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "overthinking" — amplifying reasoning task vectors (RTVs) by extrapolating beyond a reasoning-distilled model's parameters — to better surface hidden information during black-box auditing. Layer-wise attenuation strategies preserve output quality while boosting reasoning. Overthinking models reveal secrets up to 10× more often than base reasoning models across 2B–32B scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box auditing of language models can miss subtle misalignment and hidden information acquired during training. Existing methods may not adequately elicit concealed behaviors. The authors aim to develop a lightweight, inference-time technique that amplifies a model's propensity to "think out loud," thereby increasing the likelihood that hidden secrets or unintended behaviors surface during auditing.

Method: The authors define the overthinking model as a linear interpolation in weight space: θ_Oα = θ_M + α(θ_R − θ_M), with α > 1, where M is a non-reasoning instruct model and R is its reasoning-distilled counterpart. The term (θ_R − θ_M) is the reasoning task vector (RTV). To preserve coherence, they propose layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning-relevant layers rather than uniformly scaling the entire RTV. The approach is evaluated across four experimental settings on models ranging from 2B to 32B parameters.

Result: Overthinking models surface hidden information up to 10× more frequently than the original reasoning model. The mechanism by which secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation specifically along the reasoning direction, while others emerge under any sufficiently large weight perturbation. Layer-wise attenuation successfully maintains output quality and coherence while amplifying reasoning.

Conclusion: Reasoning amplification via overthinking is an effective, low-cost auditing tool that significantly improves detection of hidden information in language models. The findings highlight that different secret types respond to different perturbation strategies, suggesting nuanced mechanisms underlying concealed model behaviors.

Abstract: Black box auditing of language models is an essential pre-deployment tool, but it may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information. To better elicit hidden information during an auditing process, we introduce \emph{overthinking}: the process of using reasoning task vectors to amplify the propensity to think out loud of reasoning models. Given the parameters of a non-reasoning instruct model $M$ and reasoning-distilled model $R$, we define the \emph{overthinking model} as $\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{O}_α} = \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}} + α(\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{R}} - \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}})$, where $α> 1$ amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model $R$. Additionally, we introduce new layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning without losing quality and coherence of model outputs. We demonstrate that overthinking models are more likely to reveal hidden information across four experimental settings, across 2B-32B models. Our findings suggest that reasoning amplification may surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training up to $10\times$ more frequently than the original reasoning model. How secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation along the reasoning direction, while others yield to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

</details>


### [91] [A First-Principles Theory of Slow Thinking and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08196)
*Hongkang Yang,Zhi-Qin John Xu,Feiyu Xiong,Weinan E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a mathematical framework called "active lifting" that formally models thinking and perception by lifting probability distributions between observable and latent spaces.
