It derives slow thinking mechanisms in LLMs, positions them within representation and sampler hierarchies, and yields practical pathways for improving slow thinking models, unified encoder/generator construction, and human-like visual representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous first-principles mathematical formulation of thinking and perception, and to formally derive the mechanisms underlying slow thinking (active perception) in large language models, encompassing their design, training, and inference.

Method: The authors propose "active lifting," which operates by lifting and projecting probability distributions between observable and latent spaces. It involves sampling latent sequences driven by an intrinsic objective to reduce uncertainty at maximum rate. This yields a design space with a "static theory" subspace containing slow thinking models, organized along representation and sampler hierarchies. Inference proceeds along an internal time axis, and training follows a minimum-length coding objective akin to language invention.

Result: The active lifting framework yields: (1) a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, (2) a unified approach for constructing encoders and generative models across all data modalities, (3) a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and (4) a possible solution to policy collapse.

Conclusion: Active lifting characterizes the agency of perception and formally accounts for the emergence of slow thinking formats. The theory provides both a conceptual foundation and practical technical by-products for advancing slow thinking AI systems.

Abstract: As part of a series on first-principles modeling of cognitive functions, this paper attempts to provide a mathematical formulation of thinking and perception. It formally derives slow thinking or more generally, active perception, and encompasses the design, training and inference of slow thinking large language models. Our starting point is the lifting and projection of probability distributions on the observable and latent spaces, with the objective of representing complex data distributions by simple function families such as neural networks. A theory called "active lifting" is proposed, based on the sampling of latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate. It derives a large design space, containing the slow thinking models in a subspace that we call the static theory. These models are positioned on the representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy induced by the static theory, and can be upgraded by climbing the two hierarchies. Active lifting further derives an inference process with an internal time axis, and a training objective that resembles minimum-length coding as well as the invention of languages. Thus, it characterizes the agency of perception, including the emergence of the slow thinking formats. Technical by-products of this theory include a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models for all data modalities, a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and a possible solution to policy collapse.

</details>


### [92] [Playing ZendoWorld: Challenging AI Agents on Active Visual Concept Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08233)
*Sophia Koehler,Antonia Wüst,Inga Ibs,Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Wolfgang Stammer,Constantin Rothkopf,Kevin Ellis,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZendoWorld is a controlled interactive environment for studying how agents jointly perceive visual scenes, infer hidden logical rules, and design informative experiments. Evaluation reveals that VLM-based agents fail at active hypothesis reduction despite good label prediction, and a gap exists between human and machine inductive reasoning, especially for complex rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building intelligent systems that can jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and actively design informative experiments to test them remains a central challenge. Existing benchmarks often fail to disentangle perception from induction or to evaluate active experimentation.

Method: The authors introduce ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment where agents observe visual game scenes following a hidden logical rule, then iteratively propose new scenes, receive feedback, and refine hypotheses. They evaluate multiple agent classes: pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods, and collect human baseline data for comparison.

Result: Three key findings: (1) high label-prediction accuracy on observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction form distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. Human data reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules.

Conclusion: ZendoWorld provides a meaningful step toward evaluating intelligent agents on integrated perception, induction, and experimentation. It identifies concrete failure modes—especially in active information acquisition—and highlights directions for improvement relevant to scientific discovery and beyond.

Abstract: A central challenge in building intelligent systems is enabling agents to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them. To study this problem, we propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment in which agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine their hypotheses based on feedback from the game environment. We evaluate several agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods. Our main findings are: (1) high accuracy in predicting labels for observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; and (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. To compare these results, we collect human data on the task, which reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules. Overall, ZENDOWORLD takes an important step toward evaluating intelligent agents and identifies concrete avenues for improvement, particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

</details>


### [93] [Compete Then Collaborate: Frontier AI Teachers Build a Verifiable Curriculum to Improve a Coding Student Beyond Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08255)
*Miseong Shawn Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Four frontier LLMs compete under execution-based verification, then collaborate to build a verifiable RL environment. Imitation (SFT) degrades student performance, while RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) on the same curriculum yields significant gains, showing that AI-teacher value lies in constructing environments for learning-by-doing, not answer pooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-teacher knowledge distillation merges outputs without determining which teacher is best, and often uses LLM judges biased toward their own outputs. The authors aim to replace subjective judging with execution-based verification and test whether teacher ranking matters for student learning, while also challenging the assumption that imitating verified solutions improves student models.

Method: A compete-then-collaborate framework: (1) Four frontier models (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head on competition and standard coding problems using execution-based judges (unit tests, stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls; (2) verified solutions form a collaborative curriculum; (3) student model (Qwen2.5-Coder at 7B/32B) is trained via both SFT (imitation) and RLVR (GRPO) on the same curriculum; (4) reproduced with an on-prem pipeline on NVIDIA GB10.

Result: (1) All teachers achieve near-perfect (99-100%) on standard problems after self-correction (saturation effect); competition problems separate them: Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%. Student results are robust to teacher ranking. (2) SFT on verified solutions degrades performance: MBPP drops from 76.7% → 72.7% (32B), competition problems from 5.9% → 2.9%. (3) RLVR on the same curriculum improves competition problems from 5.9% → 8.8% (+49% relative gain), reversing SFT's negative direction.

Conclusion: The value of multi-teacher AI collaboration is not in pooling answers for imitation (which can harm competent students) but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment for reinforcement learning. Learning-by-doing with verifiable rewards outperforms supervised imitation of the same data.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as teachers generating training data for smaller students. Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, often relying on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. We introduce a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head by an execution-based judge (unit tests and stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls, and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student (Qwen2.5-Coder). We report three findings. (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly after self-correction (99-100%) due to a saturation effect, but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%); however, the robust student-side results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions does not improve, and can degrade, an already-competent student at 7B and 32B (e.g., from 76.7% to 72.7% on MBPP-test, and 5.9% to 2.9% on competition problems). (3) Using the same collaborative curriculum as a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) environment improves the student (from 5.9% to 8.8% peak on competition problems, a +49% relative gain), reversing SFT's direction. The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. We release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with framework patches for running GRPO on a bleeding-edge stack.

</details>


### [94] [MentalHospital: A Virtual Environment for Evaluating Psychiatric Clinical Encounters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08257)
*Yuming Yang,Xiao Sun,Yuanwei Zou,Zhengxiao Wu,Yun Chen,Jiang Zhong,Haoyang Zeng,Jingwang Huang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalHospital is a virtual evaluation environment that simulates complete psychiatric clinical encounters using the S.O.A.P. workflow with 1,193 EHR-derived standardized patients. It employs MentalEval, five specialized evaluators trained via SFT and DPO, to assess LLM performance. Results show the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 pp in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLMs in psychiatry focus on isolated tasks (dialogue, diagnosis, treatment planning) rather than complete clinical encounters. There is a need for holistic evaluation environments that simulate the full psychiatric workflow and can scale specialist-quality assessment.

Method: The authors build MentalHospital, a virtual environment instantiating the S.O.A.P. workflow (Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, Treatment Planning). It uses 1,193 de-identified psychiatric EHR cases spanning 76 ICD-11 disorders to construct skill-augmented standardized patients. Evaluation follows a dual-track protocol: objective comparison against EHR references and subjective assessment of clinical process quality. MentalEval — five domain-specific evaluators (communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, treatment appropriateness) — is trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Twenty-two clinicians validated the system.

Result: Clinicians rated MentalHospital's clinical fidelity at 3.88/5. MentalEval achieved an average Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) of 0.944 against expert judgments. The strongest LLM tested lagged behind clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status examination identified as the primary bottleneck.

Conclusion: MentalHospital provides a high-fidelity, scalable benchmark for evaluating LLMs on complete psychiatric encounters. Despite strong performance on isolated tasks, current LLMs exhibit a substantial gap relative to clinicians in holistic psychiatric competence, particularly in mental status assessment, highlighting critical directions for future improvement.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on isolated psychiatric tasks, including dialogue, diagnosis, and treatment planning, yet existing benchmarks rarely simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters. We introduce $\textbf{MentalHospital}$, a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters. MentalHospital instantiates the Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, and Treatment Planning (S.O.A.P.) workflow, using skill-augmented standardized patients constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric electronic health record (EHR) cases spanning all major ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders. Each encounter is assessed through a dual-track protocol that combines objective comparison against EHR-derived references with subjective assessment of clinical process quality. To scale specialist judgment, we develop $\textbf{MentalEval}$, five domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness, trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Survey responses from 22 clinicians support MentalHospital's clinical fidelity (3.88/5), while MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows that even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

</details>


### [95] [Different Teachers, Different Capabilities: Sub-1B On-Device Distillation for Structured Text Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08268)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distilling an 8B reasoning model (DeepSeek-R1) into a 0.6B student via QLoRA recovers 58% of the quality gap for structured news extraction. The reasoning teacher transfers writing quality, a managed pipeline transfers label diversity, and a same-size non-reasoning teacher yields no improvement. A per-field routing map is proposed since no single engine dominates all sub-tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-volume structured extraction is expensive when large models process every item. On-device distillation could deliver comparable output at much lower latency and cost, but it is unclear what distillation actually delivers per sub-task and which teacher characteristics matter most.

Method: An 8B reasoning teacher (DeepSeek-R1:8B) is distilled into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B) using QLoRA with three seeds. Two teacher controls are included: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. Evaluation uses a blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scoring each condition against the full article. Two non-distillation baselines are few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The test set comprises 93 news articles (22 thin-source), each mapped to a JSON object with a summary and five categorical labels.

Result: The 0.6B student runs at ~0.8 s/article vs. the teacher's 39 s. It recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating constrained decoding by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, indicating gains stem from the teacher's reasoning nature, not scale. Capabilities split by teacher lineage: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality; the managed pipeline transfers label diversity; the instruction-teacher student stays more grounded (74 vs. 55 faithful articles on the 22 thin-source subset), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. This grounding difference is a consistent ordering, not a statistically significant aggregate effect.

Conclusion: No single distillation engine wins every field. The practical deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment, matching each sub-task to the most suitable distillation lineage.

Abstract: High-volume structured extraction pays a large model's latency on every item, so distilling the task into a small on-device model is attractive: comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost. We measure what that distillation actually delivers, per sub-task. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object with a short summary and five categorical labels. We distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds), and add two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. A blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scores every arm against the full article, alongside two non-distillation baselines, few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The student runs at about 0.8 s per article against the teacher's 39 s, and recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating its primary baseline (constrained decoding) by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by a secondary +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, so the summary gain follows from the teacher's reasoning nature rather than its scale. Capabilities then split by teacher: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality and the managed pipeline transfers label diversity, while a same-size instruction teacher's students stay more grounded on the 22 short, thin-source articles in the 93-item test set (74 versus 55 faithful), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. That grounding difference is a consistent ordering rather than a significant aggregate effect, and the subgroup is small, so we report it as a direction. Because no single engine wins every field, the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment.

</details>


### [96] [PolyUQuest: Verifiable Structure-Aware Web RAG over Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08269)
*Ying Liu,Yi Ye,Quanyu Feng,Mingxi Ye,Mingtao Zhang,Haoyang Li,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyUQuest is a structure-aware web RAG framework that models web content as a heterogeneous graph (hyperlink topology, DOM hierarchy, entity-relation knowledge), uses a two-tier router for mode-specific retrieval, and ensures full answer verifiability through structural provenance. It outperforms flat-text RAG baselines on a 4,240-page university website benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems flatten HTML pages into unstructured text, discarding critical structural and semantic signals such as hyperlink topology, DOM hierarchy, and entity relationships. This leads to degraded retrieval quality, reduced answer faithfulness, and lack of verifiability. The authors aim to exploit these structural signals to improve retrieval accuracy, coverage, and answer traceability.

Method: PolyUQuest constructs a heterogeneous graph unifying three layers: (1) hyperlink topology between pages, (2) DOM hierarchy within pages, and (3) entity-relation knowledge extracted across pages. A two-tier router classifies each query and dispatches it to one of three retrieval modes: direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, or multi-hop entity reasoning. Every cited answer block is annotated with its source page, heading path, and entity links for full verifiability.

Result: Evaluated on the PolyU official website (4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, 37,680 relations) using a multi-type benchmark, PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query.

Conclusion: Structure-aware graph-based retrieval with modality-specific routing yields superior RAG performance over flat-text approaches. The framework is being deployed as a student-facing QA service at PolyU, with an interactive interface for inspecting citations, comparing retrieval traces, and exploring evidence paths.

Abstract: Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML. We present PolyUQuest, a verifiable, structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlink topology between pages, DOM hierarchy within pages, and entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router dispatches each query to one of three retrieval modes matched to its structural need, including direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning. Every answer is fully verifiable, as each cited block carries its source page, heading path, and entity links so that users can trace any claim back to its structural evidence. We evaluate on the official websites of the Hong Kong Polytechnic University (PolyU), comprising 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations, together with a multi-type evaluation benchmark. PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query. The demonstration provides an interactive interface for inspecting cited answers, comparing retrieval traces across routing modes, and exploring evidence graph paths. PolyUQuest is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

</details>


### [97] [Understanding Axes of Difficulty For Long Context Tasks Via PredicateLongBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08284)
*Siddhartha Jain,Ameya Velingker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PredicateLongBench introduces a benchmark that stress-tests LLM long-context reasoning by requiring models to find the longest contiguous subsequence satisfying given predicates. It systematically explores axes of difficulty using both synthetic and natural-text pipelines, revealing that frontier models degrade significantly as difficulty scales, despite the tasks being conceptually simple and not requiring LLM-based evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context evaluations (NIAH, multi-hop reasoning, summarization) measure only average-case performance, are often saturated or lack robustness, and critically do not systematically probe how model performance scales with increasing difficulty along multiple axes. The authors aim to fill this gap with a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning capabilities in a controlled, systematic manner.

Method: The benchmark asks models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies specified predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering). The key innovation is the systematic identification and exploration of multiple axes of difficulty. Two complementary generation pipelines are provided: (1) a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and (2) a real-world setup sampling words from natural documents while preserving distributional properties. No LLM-based generations or judges are required.

Result: Frontier models struggle to perform well as difficulty is scaled up along the benchmark's axes of difficulty, demonstrating the benchmark's utility in exposing limitations of current long-context capabilities.

Conclusion: PredicateLongBench reveals systematic weaknesses in current LLMs' long-context reasoning that prior benchmarks failed to capture. The conceptually simple yet challenging tasks, combined with systematic difficulty scaling and evaluation without LLM judges, make it a robust tool for understanding and improving long-context capabilities.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated rapidly improving long-context capabilities, prompting a wave of benchmarks designed to evaluate them. However, existing long-context evaluations - from Needle-in-a-Haystack (NIAH) tests to more recent multi-hop reasoning and summarization tasks - predominantly measure average-case performance, and many are either saturated or lack robustness. Notably absent is a systematic way to probe how models perform as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along various axes. We address this gap by proposing PredicateLongBench, a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by asking models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies given predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering), drawn from a broader predicate class. The central innovation of our benchmark is the identification and systematic exploration of multiple different axes of difficulty which test multiple aspects of long context understanding. We provide two complementary generation pipelines - a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and a real-world setup that samples words from natural documents while preserving their distributional properties. We find that frontier models struggle to perform well as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along our axes, demonstrating the utility of our benchmark in understanding the limitations of current long-context capabilities. Furthermore, the tasks in PredicateLongBench, though challenging, are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generations or judges.

</details>


### [98] [Psychological Competence as a Missing Dimension in AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08285)
*Marcos Economides,Paul M. Sacher,Samuel Salzer,Alexis Michelle Abellar,Fendi Tsim,Antoine Ferrère*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes "psychological competence" as a new, essential dimension for evaluating human-facing AI, focusing on how AI systems influence user cognition, emotions, beliefs, trust, and decision-making during interaction, beyond traditional technical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation frameworks prioritize technical metrics (accuracy, robustness, reasoning) but overlook how human-facing AI systems (advisors, coaches, tutors, companions) psychologically impact users. These systems can shape reasoning, emotional interpretation, belief formation, trust calibration, and decision-making, making the human-AI interaction—not just the model—the critical unit of evaluation.

Method: The authors define the construct of psychological competence, delineate its boundaries and core domains, and outline how it can be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. They draw on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research to build a conceptual framework, without proposing a specific quantitative benchmark.

Result: A conceptual framework for psychological competence is presented, encompassing interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. The framework identifies these as measurable dimensions that existing evaluation methods do not directly assess.

Conclusion: Psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deployers, researchers, and regulators. Incorporating it into evaluation practice would better capture the real-world effects of human-facing AI and support safer, more effective human-AI interactions.

Abstract: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus primarily on technical performance, including accuracy, robustness, reasoning ability, and policy compliance. These measures remain essential, but they are not sufficient for systems that interact directly with users through natural language. Human-facing AI systems are increasingly used as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. In these roles, their responses can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions. The relevant unit of evaluation is therefore not only the model, but the human-AI interaction. This paper introduces psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation. We define psychological competence as the capacity of a human-facing AI system to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making in ways that are appropriate to the user, context, and purpose of the interaction. This includes interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. Existing evaluation approaches capture parts of this problem but rarely assess these psychological effects directly. Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, we outline a conceptual framework for psychological competence and its core domains. Rather than proposing a specific benchmark, we define the construct, clarify its boundaries, and describe how it may be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. We argue that psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

</details>


### [99] [INTENT: An LSTM Framework for Vehicle Intention Prediction in Intersection Scenarios with Comprehensive Ablation Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08316)
*Logine M. Zaki,Catherine M. Elias*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes INTENT, an LSTM-based framework for predicting vehicle intentions (going straight, turning left/right) at intersections 2 seconds ahead, achieving 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles need human-like interpretation of driver intention for safe navigation in complex, interactive scenarios (intersections, roundabouts, emergencies). Accurate intention prediction enables timely evasive actions and can enhance trajectory prediction through intention-conditioned modeling.

Method: Proposes the INTENT framework employing an LSTM model to perform vehicle intention classification at intersections, predicting 2 seconds in advance whether a vehicle will go straight, turn left, or turn right. Model experiments and ablation studies are conducted on the InD dataset.

Result: The INTENT framework achieves 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset for the three-class intention prediction task.

Conclusion: The INTENT framework demonstrates highly accurate vehicle intention prediction at intersections, confirming the viability of LSTM-based approaches for real-time intention-aware autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: Vehicle intention prediction is a pivotal aspect in the agility and safety of autonomous vehicles in all driving scenarios; if genuine enhancement of autonomous vehicles are required, we need to make them adopt human interpretation of driver's intention especially in cases that require a lot of human interaction as well as complex driving behaviors like the ones at intersections, roundabouts and emergency cases such as sudden stops where vehicle intention prediction helps in taking the correct evasive action within a real time period where every second of action makes an impact and can prevent a catastrophe from taking place. In the worst case, it helps minimize the damage and make safety a priority. Intention prediction can also be used to enhance trajectory prediction (intention conditioned trajectory prediction). In this study, The INTENT framework is proposed using LSTM model to predict the vehicle's intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead of the event occurrence to predict whether the cars in intersections are going straight, turning left, or turning right. Various model experiments and ablation study are thoroughly tested on InD dataset achieving 99.71% accuracy.

</details>


### [100] [Blind-Spots-Bench: Evaluating Blind Spots in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08317)
*Matteo Santelmo,Xiuying Wei,Israa Fakih,Felix Bauer,Juan Garcia Giraldo,Chengkun Li,Etienne Bamas,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces blind-spots-bench, a 235-sample benchmark of human-trivial but AI-challenging tasks (sourced from students), revealing persistent model blind spots not captured by standard benchmarks. Closed-source frontier models outperform open-weight ones by ~10%, but no single model dominates all task types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI benchmarks fail to measure persistent blind spots where modern models struggle on tasks humans find trivially easy (e.g., string manipulation, drawing a five-legged dog), necessitating a diagnostic stress test that exposes concrete weaknesses.

Method: Authors collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, develop a task taxonomy for the 235-sample dataset, and build an automated grading pipeline to evaluate open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models.

Result: Closed-source frontier models outperform open-weight models by approximately 10% on blind-spots-bench, despite comparable performance on existing benchmarks. No single model excels across all task types, and certain tasks remain unsolved by every evaluated model.

Conclusion: blind-spots-bench serves as an effective diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete, persistent weaknesses in modern AI systems that standard benchmarks overlook, highlighting the value of human-trivial task evaluation.

Abstract: Modern AI models achieve strong performance on many established benchmarks, yet they still fail on tasks that humans find almost trivial, such as manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs. These examples suggest that existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current systems. We introduce $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$, a benchmark designed to expose such blind spots through tasks that appear simple for humans but remain challenging for modern AI. We collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, and propose a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting dataset of 235 samples. We further develop an automated grading pipeline to evaluate a wide range of models, including open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models. Our analysis on $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ reveals that closed-source frontier models can substantially outperform open-weight models with even $\approx10\%$ gap, even when they attain comparable performance on existing benchmarks. A more fine-grained analysis shows that no single model dominates across all task types, and that some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models. These results highlight the value of $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current modern models.

</details>


### [101] [MobiDiff: Semantic-Aware Multi-Channel Discrete Diffusion for Human Mobility Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08357)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Taichi Liu,Desheng Zhang,Yuan Tian,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MobiDiff is a discrete diffusion framework that generates synthetic human mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons (spatial, activity, temporal), avoiding costly continuous/latent trace pipelines. It achieves competitive fidelity, strong privacy preservation, and is significantly faster (5.3×) than prior methods like GeoGen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world mobility data is expensive to collect and hard to share due to privacy concerns. Existing diffusion-based generation methods rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, which cannot natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures. They also require costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine realization pipelines.

Method: MobiDiff is an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that decomposes each check-in event into three channels: spatial (region), activity, and temporal. It applies structured masking at event, group, and channel levels during the forward diffusion process to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies. The model learns to reverse this discrete noise process, directly generating clean multi-channel semantic skeletons without intermediate representations.

Result: Evaluated on three large-scale datasets (Atlanta, Boston, Seattle), MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions, remains competitive on broader mobility statistics, and achieves significant speed gains — averaging 5.3× faster inference than GeoGen. It also demonstrates strong privacy-preserving properties.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion provides an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation. MobiDiff's direct multi-channel discrete approach outperforms continuous/latent alternatives in speed while maintaining competitive fidelity and privacy.

Abstract: Human mobility data are essential for transportation optimization, urban planning, and resource allocation, yet real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. Recent diffusion-based methods have shown promise in synthesizing realistic mobility patterns, but they typically rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, limiting their ability to natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures. To address this issue, we introduce MobiDiff, an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that efficiently generates mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons, avoiding the costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine realization pipelines widely used in existing diffusion-based methods. Specifically, MobiDiff decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies. We evaluate generation fidelity, privacy-preserving, and efficiency on three large-scale real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle. Results show that MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics; it is also much faster than state-of-the-art methods, e.g., 5.3$\times$ faster than GeoGen on average during inference. These findings suggest that discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation.

</details>


### [102] [FedOPAL: One-Shot Federated Learning via Analytic Visual Prompt Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08368)
*Lingyu Qiu,Daniela Annunziata,Stefano Izzo,Fabio Giampaolo,Francesco Piccialli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedOPAL introduces visual prompts as feature rectifiers with local proximal constraints to align heterogeneous data distributions for analytical federated learning, achieving SOTA accuracy at zero server-side training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-shot federated learning reduces communication but suffers from high server costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning (AFL) offers efficient gradient-free closed-form aggregation, but its static feature assumptions break under non-IID data, causing feature manifold misalignment and performance degradation.

Method: FedOPAL adapts visual prompts as feature rectifiers that actively correct heterogeneous feature distributions into a linearly separable space. Local proximal constraints are applied to satisfy AFL's theoretical assumptions, enabling gradient-free least-squares closed-form aggregation without server-side training.

Result: FedOPAL significantly outperforms original analytical methods across multiple benchmarks and achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative one-shot FL methods, all while maintaining zero server-side training cost.

Conclusion: FedOPAL provides a new engineering paradigm for efficient large-model collaboration at the edge by bridging analytical federated learning with prompt-based feature rectification, eliminating server-side computation without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: With the widespread deployment of basic models in edge intelligence, communication bandwidth has become a core bottleneck restricting the scalability of federated learning. Although one-shot federated learning alleviates this problem by minimizing communication rounds, existing iterative fine-tuning or knowledge distillation methods still face challenges such as high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning achieves efficient gradientfree aggregation using least-squares closed-form solutions, but in environments with non-independent and identically distributed data, its static feature assumptions fail, leading to feature manifold misalignment and severely impairing model performance. To address this contradiction, this paper proposes the FedOPAL framework. This framework adapts the visual prompts as feature rectifiers, actively correcting the feature distribution of heterogeneous data to a linearly separable space by applying local proximal constraints, thereby satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning. Experimental results show that FedOPAL not only significantly outperforms the original analytical methods on several benchmarks, but also achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs, providing a new engineering paradigm for efficient collaboration of large models on the edge.

</details>


### [103] [Towards Mechanistically Understanding Why Memorized Knowledge Fails to Generalize in Large Language Model Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08393)
*Lu Dai,Ziyang Rao,Yili Wang,Hanqing Wang,Hao Liu,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuned LLMs memorize new facts quickly but struggle to use them in reasoning—a phenomenon formalized as the "Knowing–Using Gap." Using a novel "self-patching" intervention, the authors trace this to a knowledge-circuit misalignment: memorized representations exist internally but aren't routed to computation-effective layers. A simple heuristic recovers 58–75% of the lost generalization performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning LLMs with new knowledge leads to rapid memorization, yet the models often fail to apply that knowledge in downstream reasoning tasks. Understanding why this disconnect occurs—and how to fix it—is the central motivation.

Method: The authors formalize the problem as the Knowing–Using Gap (accuracy gap + temporal lag). They introduce "self-patching," an intervention technique that identifies activation locations where relocating internal representations improves failed generalization cases. This is used to monitor spatial permeation dynamics of newly acquired knowledge and to test the knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis.

Result: Self-patching reveals that memorized knowledge representations exist but are misrouted away from computation-effective layers. A heuristic strategy based on this diagnostic recovers 58–75% of the oracle headroom in generalization failures. Results are validated cross-domain.

Conclusion: The Knowing–Using Gap stems from a routing problem rather than a representation-learning failure. Interventions that redirect knowledge to the right layers can substantially close the gap between memorization and generalization.

Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs to inject new knowledge faces a critical challenge: LLMs can quickly memorize new facts, yet fail to use them for downstream reasoning tasks. We formalize this failure as the \textit{\textbf{Knowing--Using Gap}}, characterized by an accuracy gap and a temporal lag between memorization and generalization. To understand this phenomenon, we fine-tune LLMs with unseen knowledge and monitor the spatial permeation dynamics of the knowledge internally using a novel intervention technique called self-patching. Self-patching identifies activation locations where relocating representations substantially improves failed generalization cases. These results are consistent with a knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis: memorized representations can exist internally but may not be routed to computation-effective layers. To demonstrate the practicality of this diagnostic finding, we design a simple heuristic strategy which recovers 58--75\% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure. Experiments are done cross-domain for the robustness of this finding.

</details>


### [104] [Game Theory Driven Multi-Agent Framework Mitigates Language Model Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08403)
*Runzhe Liu,Biquan Bie,Zihao Wang,Yuchao Ma,Yexin Liu,Xinghai Li,Harry Yang,Wenbo Yang,Jinzhe Cao,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework (G-Frame) combining Bayesian and game-theoretic principles enables automated synthesis of high-quality reasoning data, yielding a 7B-parameter chemistry model (OmniChem) that matches GPT-4o mini on chemistry benchmarks while reducing hallucinations by 79.46%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight LLMs fail in rule-based scientific domains because they mimic surface-level linguistic patterns instead of performing genuine axiomatic reasoning, leading to frequent hallucinations. There is a need for a scalable method to inject structured, constraint-driven reasoning into smaller models without relying on proprietary large models.

Method: The authors propose G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework that integrates Bayesian inference and team game principles to create an automated closed-loop for data synthesis and model training. Structured reasoning processes force the internalization of domain constraints, generating a specialized corpus of 363,045 chain-of-thought sequences and 199,589 QA pairs. This corpus is used to train a 7B-parameter model called OmniChem.

Result: OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT-4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench. It exhibits a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations compared to its base architecture. The model also demonstrates advanced capabilities in molecular design and synthesis planning.

Conclusion: The G-Frame framework establishes a scalable paradigm using adaptive multi-agent systems to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies in lightweight LLMs, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific domains.

Abstract: The application of lightweight Large Language Models in rule-based scientific domains remains severely limited by their tendency to mimic linguistic patterns rather than reproduce axiomatic reasoning, causing frequent hallucinations. Here, we show that G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework integrating Bayesian and team game principles, establishes an automated closed-loop for high-quality data synthesis and model training. By forcing the internalization of domain constraints through structured reasoning, we synthesized a specialized corpus of 363,045 chains-of-thought and 199,589 question-answer pairs. The resulting 7B model OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT 4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench while exhibiting a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture. We further demonstrate the advanced capabilities of OmniChem in molecular design and synthesis planning. This work establishes a scalable paradigm utilizing adaptive multi-agents to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific fields.

</details>


### [105] [OmniFood-Bench: Evaluating VLMs for Nutrient Reasoning and Personalized Health Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08423)
*Qian Jiang,Zhecheng Shi,Jingpu Yang,Zirui Song,Miao Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniFood-Bench is a new benchmark that evaluates Vision-Language Models on food understanding across three progressive tiers: perception, quantitative reasoning, and safety-critical advisory. It reveals a "Semantic-Physical Gap" where models excel at dish naming but fail catastrophically at mass estimation and hallucinate unsafe dietary advice for high-risk conditions like diabetes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing food-related VLM benchmarks only assess coarse-grained classification (e.g., naming dishes), ignoring the full reasoning chain needed for real-world dietary management: identifying hidden ingredients, estimating physical mass, and generating medically safe recommendations. This "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and nutritional content remains unaddressed.

Method: The authors construct OmniFood-Bench from the MM-Food-100K dataset, structuring evaluation around three progressive capability tiers: (1) Basic Perception — ingredients and cooking methods; (2) Quantitative Reasoning — portion size estimation and nutritional profiling; (3) Safety-Critical Advisory — disease-specific dietary recommendations. Six state-of-the-art VLMs (including GPT-5.1, Gemini-3-Flash, and Qwen3-VL-8B) are benchmarked.

Result: Models achieve near-human accuracy on dish naming (Basic Perception) but exhibit catastrophic failure in mass estimation (Quantitative Reasoning). Critically, in Safety-Critical Advisory, models frequently hallucinate benign but medically inappropriate advice for high-risk diabetic profiles, exposing a dangerous "Semantic-Physical Gap."

Conclusion: The benchmark establishes a rigorous trustworthiness standard for autonomous agents in public health, demonstrating that current VLMs are unsafe for deployment in dietary management without addressing the semantic-physical reasoning gap.

Abstract: The rapid integration of Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into critical infrastructure promises to revolutionize
  personalized healthcare and dietary management. However, in the domain of food systems, autonomous agents face a
  unique and persistent challenge: the "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and intrinsic
  nutritional composition. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on coarse-grained classification tasks, such as food
  category recognition, which fail to evaluate the intricate reasoning chain required for real-world dietary management
  -- specifically, the ability to traverse from identifying hidden ingredients to estimating physical mass, and finally
  synthesizing safety-critical medical advice. In this paper, we introduce OmniFood-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark
  constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset. Unlike previous works, OmniFood-Bench evaluates VLMs across three
  progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size &
  Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). We evaluate six
  state-of-the-art VLMs, including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B. Our extensive experiments reveal a
  startling "Semantic-Physical Gap": while models achieve near-human accuracy in naming dishes, they exhibit
  catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles. This
  work establishes a rigorous standard for trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health. The code
  and datasets are available in: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OmniFood-Bench-7D0B

</details>


### [106] [Applying JEPA-Style Predictive Learning to JA4-Derived Network Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08465)
*Javier Izquierdo,Aygul Zagidullina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Explores applying JEPA-style predictive learning (matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs) to JA4 network fingerprints. A Transformer model (JA4-JEPA) trained on ~397K samples from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017 achieves 0.9899 cosine similarity and 0.9220 kNN accuracy on protocol-family classification (TLS, DNS, SSH), demonstrating that JEPA objectives can produce useful embeddings from compact fingerprint data even with incomplete view overlap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: I-JEPA and V-JEPA have proven effective for images and video by predicting in latent space rather than reconstructing raw inputs. The authors investigate whether this self-supervised predictive objective generalizes to a fundamentally different, compact domain — network fingerprinting using JA4 hashes — where data is highly structured, sparse, and lacks the spatial/temporal redundancy of visual data.

Method: JA4-JEPA is a Transformer-based architecture trained on four JA4 fingerprint subfields (JA4, JA4H, JA4S, JA4X) from combined JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017 datasets (~397K samples). The model uses a JEPA objective: a context encoder produces latent predictions that are matched against target encoder outputs, avoiding input reconstruction. Representations are evaluated via a frozen kNN probe on a 39,416-sample heldout set for protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH.

Result: On the 39,416-sample heldout test set, JA4-JEPA achieved a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and a kNN classification accuracy of 0.9220 for protocol-family classification (TLS, DNS, SSH).

Conclusion: JEPA-style predictive representation learning can be successfully adapted to compact, non-visual domains like JA4 network fingerprints, producing high-quality embeddings for downstream classification tasks even when training data has incomplete view overlap across sources.

Abstract: I-JEPA and V-JEPA learn by matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs rather than regenerating the original input, and this has worked well for images and video. We explore whether the same objective works for compact network fingerprints. We built JA4-JEPA, a Transformer-based model trained on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields drawn from JA4DB and CIC-IDS- 2017. The training data combines roughly 397K samples from both sources, though no single sample contains all four view families. We evaluated the learned representations with a frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH. On 39,416 heldout samples the model achieved a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and a kNN accuracy of 0.9220. These results indicate that JEPA-style predictive learning can produce useful embeddings from JA4-derived fingerprints, even with incomplete view overlap across sources.
  Keywords: JA4, network fingerprinting, JEPA, predictive representation learning, self-supervised learning

</details>


### [107] [Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) for Adaptive Semantic Modeling in Biomedical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08490)
*Bharathwaj Vijayakumar,Sahana K. Varadaraju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DATGR is a lightweight framework that dynamically rewires co-occurrence graph edges based on estimated semantic drift, improving biomedical link prediction (AUROC +0.066) without retraining embeddings, while maintaining precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical language evolves rapidly, causing static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs to lose semantic fidelity over time, degrading retrieval and knowledge discovery performance. Retraining embeddings per time slice is computationally expensive, motivating a lightweight alternative.

Method: The DATGR framework models concept evolution by dynamically updating co-occurrence edge weights using a logistic update rule. Instead of full retraining, it performs feedback-driven rewiring based on estimated semantic drift across time slices, enabling edge-level temporal adaptation.

Result: Evaluated on BIOMRC, DATGR achieved a mean AUROC of 0.699 vs. 0.633 for the static baseline (absolute gain ~0.066), with comparable AUPRC (0.738 vs. 0.744), demonstrating enhanced link-prediction recall without precision loss.

Conclusion: Edge-level, drift-aware adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text, remaining computationally efficient and interpretable compared to full retraining approaches.

Abstract: Biomedical language evolves rapidly as new discoveries emerge, causing traditional text models to lose semantic fidelity over time. Static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs cannot capture such evolution, leading to performance degradation in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks. This paper introduces a Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework that models concept evolution by dynamically updating co-occurrence edges based on estimated semantic drift. Instead of retraining embeddings for each time slice, DATGR performs lightweight, feedback-driven rewiring using a logistic update rule applied to edge weights. Evaluated on the Biomedical Multi-Relation Corpus (BIOMRC), the method achieved a mean Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (AUROC) improvement of approximately 0.066 absolute difference (0.699 vs. 0.633) over a static baseline. Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC) remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), showing that drift-aware adaptation enhances link-prediction recall without a loss in precision. These results demonstrate that edge-level adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

</details>


### [108] [AI-guided stimuli discovery and generation to optimize facial emotion perception studies in autism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08533)
*Kushin Mukherjee,Na Yeon Kim,Maren Wehrheim,Ralph Adolphs,Kohitij Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autistic-neurotypical differences in facial emotion perception are concentrated in a sparse subset of diagnostic expressions. The authors used neural network models to identify and synthesize stimuli that maximize or minimize group differences, demonstrating a model-guided framework for optimized behavioral phenotyping of neurodivergent perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on facial emotion perception differences between autistic and neurotypical adults yield inconsistent findings. The authors hypothesized this variability stems from image-level sparsity — that group differences are not uniform across stimuli but concentrated in specific diagnostic facial expressions. A method is needed to systematically identify and manipulate these diagnostic stimuli.

Method: Population-specific artificial neural network models were trained to predict emotion judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants separately. These models were then used to (1) select novel faces from a larger set predicted to maximize group separation, validated in an independent cohort against random images, and (2) paired with a generative adversarial network (GAN) to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement, with phenotype-matched validation comparing synthesized images to their originals.

Result: Model-selected images produced significantly larger behavioral differences between autistic and neurotypical participants than matched random images in an independent validation cohort. Conversely, GAN-synthesized images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched original diagnostic images, confirming that the models successfully identified and manipulated the features driving group differences.

Conclusion: The study establishes a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences, moving behavioral phenotyping beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized, condition-specific assays of neurodivergent perception.

Abstract: Understanding perceptual differences between autistic and neurotypical adults requires behavioral assays that are sensitive, reliable, and mechanistically informative. Facial emotion perception is a useful test case because group differences have been reported, but findings vary across studies. Here we show that this variability may reflect image-level sparsity: autistic-neurotypical differences in emotion judgments were concentrated in a small subset of diagnostic facial expressions rather than spread uniformly across stimuli. We trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants, then used these models to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences than matched random images. We then used the same models with a generative adversarial network to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement. In phenotype-matched validation, synthesized images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals. These results establish a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences. More broadly, they show how behavioral phenotyping can move beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify the conditions under which neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

</details>


### [109] [CommuniWave:A Machine Learning Model for Quantifying the Degree of Temporary Informal Behavior in Urban Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08554)
*Hongye Yang,Shien Liu,Zhihao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CommuniWave is a machine learning framework that detects and quantifies informal resident behaviors (Degree of Informal Behavior, DIB) from street videos, using YOLOv10, mmaction2-based action recognition, and random forest to generate dynamic DIB fluctuation charts for data-driven community resilience planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Top-down community planning lacks effective metrics to quantify residents' informal behaviors, causing frequent conflicts between plans and actual usage. Urban managers need dynamic monitoring tools to enhance territorial resilience under complexity and uncertainty.

Method: CommuniWave integrates three components: (1) Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2 for action recognition; (2) a self-developed YOLOv10 variant (YLX) for object detection; (3) Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest to compute the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB). Street video inputs yield DIB fluctuation charts for dynamic monitoring.

Result: The model produces DIB fluctuation charts derived from street videos, enabling dynamic monitoring of informal behaviors in urban communities and supporting refined, resilience-oriented decision-making by urban managers.

Conclusion: CommuniWave provides a quantitative, bottom-up monitoring tool that captures informal resident behaviors, bridging the gap between top-down planning and actual community usage, and ultimately contributing to enhanced territorial resilience.

Abstract: For urban managers and designers, improving the functional attributes of urban communities to enhance territorial resilience in the face of complexity and uncertainty is crucial. Currently, community planning often follows a top-down approach and lacks effective metrics to quantify informal behaviors of residents, leading to frequent conflicts with original plans. This study introduces CommuniWave, a machine learning model designed to efficiently detect and quantify the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) in urban communities. The model integrates a Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2, a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX), and a Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest. Ultimately, by generating DIB fluctuation charts from street videos, the model facilitates dynamic monitoring, supporting urban managers in making refined decisions to enhance the overall resilience of communities.

</details>


### [110] [SHAP-Weighted Cross-Modal Expert Fusion for Emotion and Sentiment Recognition: Evidence and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08573)
*Adis Alihodzic,Selma Skopljakovic Hubljar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (XGAF), a tree-based mixture-of-experts approach for multimodal emotion/sentiment recognition. It analyzes how SHAP attribution reduction methods (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs) interact with unequal feature dimensionalities across unimodal and cross-modal experts. Sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass and prevents suppression of high-dimensional cross-modal experts, enabling XGAF to match or exceed early fusion while maintaining modularity and significantly outperforming late fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early fusion is accurate but monolithic (hard to interpret, hard to add/remove modalities), while late fusion is modular but loses cross-modal interactions. XGAF aims to combine the best of both worlds using SHAP-based sample-level routing, but prior work did not address how SHAP reduction methods interact with unequal expert feature dimensionalities — a critical gap since cross-modal experts typically have higher dimensionality and can be unfairly penalized by scale-dependent reductions like mean-abs.

Method: XGAF uses a tree-based mixture of experts: unimodal experts (text, audio, video), pairwise cross-modal experts, and a trimodal expert — all trained independently. A router tree (e.g., XGBoost) is trained on all concatenated features to predict the target. TreeSHAP computes per-sample, per-feature attributions, which are aggregated per expert using different reduction schemes (mean-abs, median-abs, sum-abs). These aggregated weights form a probability distribution over experts for adaptive fusion. The paper systematically compares reduction methods on MELD (7-class emotion) and CMU-MOSEI (3-class sentiment) using weighted F1, McNemar tests, and ablation studies.

Result: Sum-abs XGAF with a Transformer face aggregator achieves 0.5983 WF1 on MELD, nearly matching early fusion (0.6018) and far exceeding late fusion (0.4598). McNemar test shows no significant difference from early fusion (p=1.000) but significant superiority over late fusion (p<0.0001). On CMU-MOSEI, sum-abs XGAF (0.6519) slightly exceeds early fusion (0.6485). Ablation reveals the main gain comes from adding cross-modal experts (especially the trimodal expert) rather than complex routing. Mean-abs and median-abs weights are nearly uniform, while sum-abs concentrates weights on the trimodal expert.

Conclusion: SHAP reduction method choice is critical when experts have unequal dimensionalities. Sum-abs reduction is necessary to preserve cross-modal expert influence. XGAF with sum-abs reduction provides a modular, interpretable alternative to early fusion with statistically comparable performance, with the cross-modal experts (particularly trimodal) driving most of the gain.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion and sentiment recognition is commonly addressed by early fusion, which concatenates modalities before classification, or late fusion, which combines independently trained unimodal predictors. Early fusion can be accurate but monolithic, while late fusion is modular but may lose cross-modal interactions. This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (\xgaf), a tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts whose sample-level weights are derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. We focus on the effect of SHAP attribution reduction when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities. In this setting, mean-abs and median-abs reductions can suppress high-dimensional cross-modal experts, whereas sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass. On MELD 7-class emotion recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} nearly matches early fusion across three face-sequence aggregators; the Transformer variant reaches 0.5983 \wf{}, compared with 0.6018 for early fusion and 0.4598 for probability-average late fusion. McNemar testing shows no significant difference between sum-abs \xgaf{} and early fusion on MELD ($p=1.000$), while \xgaf{} remains significantly better than late fusion ($p<0.0001$). On CMU-MOSEI 3-class sentiment recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} reaches 0.6519 \wf{}, slightly exceeding early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Ablation studies show that the main gain comes from adding cross-modal experts, especially the trimodal expert, rather than from complex per-sample routing. Diagnostics further show that mean-abs and median-abs weights are nearly uniform, while sum-abs weights concentrate on the trimodal expert. Thus, the main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis of how SHAP reduction, expert dimensionality, and cross-modal expert design affect modular multimodal fusion.

</details>


### [111] [Towards Precision Therapy in Hepatocellular Carcinoma: A Clinical-Reasoning LLM for Risk Stratification and Treatment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08602)
*Peng Cui,Jitao Wang,Siyan Xue,Yao Huang,Haoming Xia,Dong Li,Dengxiang Liu,Weilin Wang,Liping Liu,Leida Zhang,Yunfu Cui,Tao Peng,Daolin Ji,Haitao Zhao,Wei Zhang,Xiaojuan Wang,Weijie Ma,Zongren Ding,Jinlong Li,Yuan Ding,Jiajing Zhao,Zhiyu Chen,Chengkun Yang,Ziyue Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Fusheng Liu,Yang Zhou,Xiaojuan Wang,Zhongquan Sun,Shiyun Bao,Xiaojun Wang,Ming Yang,Guangxin Li,Bin Shu,Yong Liao,Hongxuan Li,Yao Tang,Shizhong Yang,Yongyi Zeng,Yufeng Yuan,Yinpeng Dong,Jihui Hao,Jun Zhu,Jiahong Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HCC-STAR is a large language model that analyzes EMR narratives to provide HCC risk staging, ranked treatment recommendations with rationales, and survival estimates. Trained on 30,000 SEER-derived synthetic EMR narratives using a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework with verifiable composite rewards, it outperformed clinical guidelines (BCLC/CNLC) and models like GPT-5 in a 6,668-patient multi-center Chinese cohort, demonstrating longer projected median survival (51 vs. 29-32 months) and surpassing physicians in treatment accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current HCC staging systems (e.g., BCLC, CNLC) provide coarse categorical stratification that misses within-stage heterogeneity and fails to integrate the rich clinical context embedded in EMR narratives. There is a need for a model that jointly addresses staging, treatment recommendation with evidence-based rationales, and individualized prognosis in a clinically aligned manner.

Method: The authors curated ~30,000 SEER HCC cases and expanded them into EMR-style narratives via a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. They developed HCC-STAR, an LLM trained with a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized using a step-verifiable composite reward to move beyond text memorization. The model was evaluated on a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 Chinese hospitals, benchmarked against BCLC/CNLC guidelines and models including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro, and assessed via hypothetical survival analysis and blinded clinician-centric evaluations.

Result: HCC-STAR achieved SOTA in treatment recommendation and risk stratification. Hypothetical OS analysis: median survival under HCC-STAR adherence was 51 months vs. 29 (BCLC) and 32 (CNLC) months. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and improved physician accuracy/speed when used as an assistant. Blinded specialists rated its reasoning as trustworthy.

Conclusion: HCC-STAR represents a reliable, verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC, with significant survival benefits over current guidelines and demonstrable clinical utility when assisting physicians.

Abstract: Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is a common malignancy and a leading cause of cancer-related mortality. Current guidelines and staging systems provide coarse categories, but often miss within-stage heterogeneity and the clinical context in electronic medical records (EMRs). We present HCC-STAR (Hepatocellular Carcinoma Staging, Treatment And pRognosis), a clinically aligned large language model that reads routine EMR narratives and jointly outputs risk score-based staging, ranked guideline-consistent treatments with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimates. We curated about 30,000 HCC cases from SEER and expanded them into EMR-style narrative training data using a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. On this corpus, we developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized with a step-verifiable composite reward, moving beyond text-level memorization of clinical guidelines. In a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 hospitals in China, HCC-STAR achieved state-of-the-art performance in treatment recommendation and risk stratification compared with clinical guidelines and competitive models, including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Hypothetical overall-survival analysis showed a median survival of 51 months under adherence to HCC-STAR recommendations, compared with 29 and 32 months under BCLC and CNLC. In clinician-centric evaluations, blinded hepatobiliary specialists rated HCC-STAR's reasoning and evidence-based justifications as trustworthy. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and helped physicians make more accurate decisions faster when used as an assistant. These findings support HCC-STAR as a reliable and verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC.

</details>


### [112] [The complexities of patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08625)
*João Matos,Olivia Buege,Donny Cheung,Gary S. Collins,Paula Dhiman,Nan Li,Bingyu Mao,Benjamin W. Nelson,Michail Ouroutzoglou,Paul Varghese,Jonathan Amar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Real patient-chatbot conversations exhibit diverse communication patterns and emotions. A patient simulator modeling clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style was developed. Simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones (human graders: 55% accuracy). Communication style significantly altered LLM triage outcomes across 1,164 cases, highlighting that systems designed for idealized interactions risk amplifying health disparities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health chatbot development and evaluation typically rely on cooperative, articulate simulated patients, which fails to capture the wide variability in real-world communication patterns, emotional expression, and conversational strategies. This gap risks building systems that underperform for diverse patient populations.

Method: The authors analyzed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations to characterize communication diversity. They then developed a patient simulator that separately models four dimensions: clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style. A Turing-inspired evaluation with 15 human graders assessed realism. Five patient personae were used across 1,164 clinician-graded cases to evaluate four LLMs on urgency/triage assessment.

Result: Real conversations showed wide variation in communication patterns and emotional expression. In the realism evaluation, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones (human grader accuracy: 55%, near chance). Communication style significantly altered triage outcomes across all four LLMs tested.

Conclusion: Patient-centered conversational AI must accommodate communication diversity. Systems optimized for idealized rather than realistic patient interactions risk underperforming in deployment and may amplify existing health disparities.

Abstract: Consumer-facing health chatbots powered by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for symptom assessment. However, chatbot development and evaluation often rely on cooperative, articulate, simulated patients. We analysed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations and found that communication patterns and expression of emotions vary widely across users. We developed a patient simulator that separately models clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style. In a Turing-inspired evaluation of realism with 15 human graders, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones, with human graders achieving an accuracy of 55%. We used five distinct patient personae, across 1,164 clinician-graded cases, to evaluate the performance of four LLMs in urgency assessment. We found that communication style can significantly alter triage outcomes. Patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence must accommodate communication diversity: systems designed for idealised, rather than realistic, interactions risk underperforming and amplifying health disparities when deployed in the real world.

</details>


### [113] [Formal Mechanisms for Market Stability in Self-Interested Agent Societies: A Marketplace Simulation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08652)
*Eugene Ng Yi Sheng,Bingquan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents in a simulated marketplace tend toward defection unless governance mechanisms are applied. Mediation proves the most effective mechanism, sustaining cooperation even under adversarial LLM-driven troll attacks—bent but not broken.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-interested agents in repeated social dilemmas naturally drift toward defection, collapsing cooperative gains. The authors aim to identify formal mechanisms that can maintain market stability atop unrestricted communication and to test how resilient those mechanisms are against adversarial attacks.

Method: A multi-agent marketplace simulation with 18 DeepSeek-V3 LLM agents possessing complementary production specialties trading within a constrained social network. Two experimental phases: (1) mechanism comparison across eight conditions with progressive troll injection over 200 rounds; (2) adversarial red-teaming of the best mechanism (Mediation) using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven trolls across six attack versions.

Result: Mediation was the top-performing mechanism among eight tested. The strongest adversarial attack (v6) reduced honest-agent utility by only 13.3% and could not collapse the market. Mediation enabled recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure.
