Conclusion: Mediation is robust to adversarial attack—it can be bent but not broken. The paper defines adversarial robustness as a mechanism's ability to sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimised attack, and Mediation satisfies this criterion.

Abstract: Self-interested agents, left unconstrained, tend toward defection in repeated social dilemmas, causing cooperative gains from trade to collapse. This paper investigates what formal mechanisms, layered on top of unrestricted communication, are sufficient for a society of such agents to maintain market stability, and how resilient those mechanisms are to adversarial attack. We instantiate the research question as a multi-agent marketplace simulation where 18 LLM agents (DeepSeek-V3) with complementary production specialties must trade within a constrained social network to obtain utility. We conduct two experimental phases: (1) a mechanism comparison across eight conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds, identifying Mediation as the top-performing mechanism; and (2) adversarial red-teaming of Mediation using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven trolls, finding that the best attack (v6) reduces honest-agent utility by 13.3% but cannot collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure. We define adversarial robustness as a mechanism's ability to sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimised attack, and find that Mediation is robust: it can be bent but not broken.

</details>


### [114] [SolarChain-Eval: A Physics-Constrained Benchmark for Trustworthy Economic Agents in Decentralized Energy Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08681)
*Shilin Ou,Yifan Xu,Luyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SolarChain-Eval is a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents in decentralized energy markets. It frames market governance as a Markov Decision Process and introduces an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer to constrain unsafe actions. Experiments show a utility-safety trade-off: RL agents maximize utility but exploit invalid physics when penalties are removed, and the LLM layer improves auditability but cannot fully compensate for misspecified reward functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic AI systems are deployed in cyber-physical environments like decentralized energy markets, evaluating both task performance and trustworthiness becomes critical. Autonomous agents may improve market utility but can also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and make unstable governance decisions. Existing evaluation frameworks lack integrated physics constraints and transparent intervention mechanisms.

Method: The authors propose SolarChain-Eval, a Gymnasium-compatible MDP benchmark where agents make hourly market governance decisions. It evaluates policies across six dimensions: market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. An LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer is introduced: the Planner sets episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions. All interventions are logged with trigger signals, proposed/revised actions, and audit rationales. Experiments compare static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies.

Result: Experiments reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can still produce unsafe behaviors. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks, but cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function.

Conclusion: Trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. Neither RL optimization alone nor LLM-based oversight suffices in isolation. The authors release SolarChain-Eval as open-source data and code on GitHub to support replicable research on safe and auditable autonomous agents in cyber-physical-economic systems.

Abstract: As agentic AI systems are increasingly applied to cyber-physical environments, their evaluation requires assessment of both task performance and trustworthiness. In decentralized energy markets, autonomous agents may improve market utility, but may also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions. Therefore, we propose SolarChain-Eval, a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents. It formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process, where agents make hourly decisions. SolarChain-Eval evaluates each policy across multiple dimensions, including market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. To support agentic evaluation, SolarChain-Eval incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer. The Planner defines episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions. All interventions are recorded through structured logs, including trigger signals, proposed actions, revised actions, and audit rationales. Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can still produce unsafe behavior. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks, but it cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function. These results indicate that trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. We release data and code as open access on GitHub for replicability.

</details>


### [115] [Remember When It Matters: Proactive Memory Agent for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08716)
*Yifan Wu,Lizhu Zhang,Yuhang Zhou,Mingyi Wang,Bo Peng,Serena Li,Xiangjun Fan,Zhuokai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses "behavioral state decay" in long-horizon tasks, where critical decision-relevant information gets buried or lost in expanding context windows. The authors propose an active memory agent that runs alongside an unmodified action agent, selectively injecting memory-grounded reminders from a structured memory bank. The approach is plug-and-play and yields significant pass@1 improvements on Terminal-Bench 2.0 (+8.3 pp) and τ²-Bench (+6.8 pp), with ablations confirming selective intervention outperforms passive retrieval and other baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In long-horizon tasks, decision-relevant information such as task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, and open subgoals becomes scattered across an expanding trajectory. As trajectory length grows, this information can be buried in the context window or pushed out entirely, leading to a failure mode the authors term "behavioral state decay," where critical state fails to influence decisions when needed.

Method: The authors introduce a separate memory agent that operates alongside an unmodified action agent. The memory agent updates a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and makes active decisions about whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. Key design choices include selective intervention (rather than always-on injection or passive exposure). The module is designed to be plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. As an early step toward open-weight memory policies, they also train Qwen3.5-27B using SFT and GRPO on the SETA dataset.

Result: The memory agent yields pass@1 improvements of +8.3 percentage points on Terminal-Bench 2.0 and +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench, benefiting both weaker and stronger action agents. Ablations demonstrate that selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. The trained Qwen3.5-27B model shows improved validation reward and partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

Conclusion: Memory framed as an active intervention mechanism — where a separate memory agent selectively injects reminders — effectively mitigates behavioral state decay in long-horizon tasks, offering a practical, plug-and-play solution compatible with existing agent systems. The results also represent a promising step toward trainable, open-weight memory policies.

Abstract: In long-horizon tasks, decision-relevant state is often scattered across an expanding trajectory, while the action agent must surface it and act. As trajectories grow, task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, and open subgoals can be buried in the context window or pushed beyond it, failing to influence decisions when needed. We call this failure mode "behavioral state decay". We study memory as an active intervention mechanism rather than passive retrieval. A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent, updating a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and deciding whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. Across Terminal-Bench 2.0 and $τ^2$-Bench, it improves pass@1 for both weaker and stronger action agents, with gains of +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on $τ^2$-Bench. Ablations show that selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. As an early step toward open-weight memory policies, we train Qwen3.5-27B on SETA using SFT and GRPO, improving validation reward and achieving partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

</details>


### [116] [The Illusion of Equivalency: Statistical Characterization of Quantization Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08734)
*Baha Rababah,Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora,Carson K. Leung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantization preserves task accuracy but introduces behavioral divergence from base models, measurable via a new "correctness agreement" metric. Query/key projections are most sensitive, with non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional quantization evaluation relies solely on accuracy and perplexity, which the authors show fail to capture behavioral changes. There is a need for metrics that assess whether quantized models truly approximate their base counterparts in decision-making, not just aggregate performance.

Method: The authors introduce "correctness agreement," a decision-level metric measuring overlap in correct predictions between base and quantized models. They analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights, quantifying layer-wise distortions via statistical and distributional measures across multiple models (8-bit to 2-bit).

Result: Behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. Non-linear breakpoints appear at low bit-widths. Query and key projections are consistently more sensitive to quantization than value and output projections.

Conclusion: There exists an "illusion of equivalence" between base and quantized models. Behavioral evaluation beyond accuracy/perplexity is essential, and correctness agreement offers a more faithful measure of quantization-induced degradation.

Abstract: Post-training quantization is widely used to deploy large language models in resource-constrained settings, yet its evaluation relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity. We show that these metrics fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization. We introduce correctness agreement, a decision-level metric that measures overlap in correct predictions between a base model and its quantized variants, independent of absolute accuracy. Across multiple models and quantization schemes from 8-bit to 2-bit, we find that behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. To explain this effect, we analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights and quantify layer-wise distortions using statistical and distributional measures. Our results reveal non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths and show that query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections. These findings expose an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models and motivate behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics.

</details>


### [117] [AUTOPILOT VQA: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Incident-Centric Dashcam Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08745)
*Siddharth Damodharan,Radhika Gupta,Ali Alshami,Ryan Rabinowitz,Jugal Kalita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUTOPILOT-VQA is an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding that evaluates vision-language models on safety-critical reasoning about real-world driving incidents and near-incidents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision-language and multimodal models have advanced autonomous driving tasks, but there is no standardized way to evaluate whether they can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents. This gap necessitates a benchmark focused on safety-aware, temporally grounded reasoning beyond mere object recognition.

Method: The authors construct AUTOPILOT-VQA, a benchmark dataset featuring structured questions grounded in dashcam videos of real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The questions span diverse safety-relevant categories: weather/lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability reasoning. The benchmark is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition.

Result: The paper presents the benchmark itself rather than model evaluation results. The dataset enables systematic assessment of autonomous driving systems' reasoning capabilities across multiple safety-critical dimensions and provides a standardized evaluation framework for the competition.

Conclusion: AUTOPILOT-VQA provides a standardized benchmark that moves autonomous driving evaluation beyond perception toward safety-aware, interpretable, and temporally grounded reasoning about incidents, supporting the development of more robust vision-language systems for real-world driving.

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models have improved autonomous driving tasks such as scene understanding, decision making, trajectory prediction, and visual question answering. However, evaluating whether these models can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents remains challenging. To address this gap, we present AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding. The dataset evaluates different systems through structured questions designed around real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The benchmark covers diverse safety-relevant categories, including weather and lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability-related reasoning. By requiring models to answer grounded questions about both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details, AUTOPILOT-VQA moves beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition and provides a standardized benchmark for assessing the reliability of autonomous driving systems in different scenarios. Our benchmark support developments for more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving.

</details>


### [118] [Ideas Have Genomes: Benchmarking Scientific Lineage Reasoning and Lineage-Grounded Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08758)
*Yifan Zhou,Qihao Yang,Yan Li,Donggang Li,Xiru Hu,Hokin Deng,Ziyang Gong,Xuanyi Zhou,Huacan Wang,Xiangchao Yan,Wanghan Xu,Wenlong Zhang,Shaofeng Zhang,Yue Zhou,Yifan Yang,Zhihang Zhong,Xue Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench) is a benchmark for evaluating AI systems on scientific lineage reasoning and lineage-grounded idea generation. It models papers as "Idea Genome" objects with evolutionary dynamics (inheritance, mutation, loss, etc.), contains ~1,961 lineage traces across 10 domains, and exposes a compositional bottleneck where the best LLM achieves only 27.3% accuracy on lineage reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific ideas evolve through inheritance, recombination, and repair of prior work — akin to biological genomes — yet current AI benchmarks fail to assess whether models can reason about or generate ideas following this lineage structure. The authors aim to fill this gap by operationalizing scientific lineage as a structured, testable framework.

Method: The paper introduces the IdeaGene framework: each paper is decomposed into minimal, typed, evidence-grounded "Idea Genome" objects. A "GenomeDiff" records evolutionary operations (inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, novel insertion) across six dynamics. IG-Bench is built with 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 Idea Genome objects, and 920 GenomeDiff records across 10 scientific domains. Two evaluation protocols are defined: (1) IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) for closed-form lineage reasoning (abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, verification); (2) IG-Arena, which uses a Population-Evolution Score (PES) to assess whether generated proposals can coherently insert into a lineage by inheriting correct genome objects, varying meaningfully, and offering selection value.

Result: Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientist systems reveal a severe compositional bottleneck: the strongest system achieves only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning tasks. Additionally, providing structured lineage context does not uniformly help all systems — it reshuffles rankings, indicating that current models lack robust lineage-aware reasoning capabilities.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based scientist systems exhibit a fundamental weakness in scientific lineage reasoning, achieving poor exact-match accuracy even with structured lineage context. The benchmark establishes that lineage-grounded idea generation remains a significant open challenge, and the non-uniform effect of lineage context suggests that better architectural or training innovations are needed to make models genuinely lineage-aware.

Abstract: Scientific ideas rarely start from a blank page. They inherit mechanisms, repair known limitations, and recombine pieces of earlier work, much like biological genomes. Current benchmarks still say little about whether AI systems can follow this inheritance structure. We present IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench), a benchmark for scientific lineage reasoning and lineage-grounded idea generation. IG-Bench is organized around the IdeaGene framework: each paper or proposal is represented as a set of minimal, typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects, and a GenomeDiff aligns these objects to record inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, and novel insertion under six operational evolutionary dynamics. The benchmark contains 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 curated Idea Genome objects, and 920 pairwise GenomeDiff records across 10 scientific domains. It supports two evaluations. IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) tests closed-form lineage reasoning across Idea Genome abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, and lineage verification. IG-Arena evaluates generation with a lineage-conditioned Population-Evolution Score(PES), asking whether a proposal can be inserted as a coherent descendant of a given lineage population: it should inherit the right Idea Genome objects, vary meaningfully from nearby work, and offer selection value for future research. Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientists expose a compositional bottleneck. The strongest system reaches only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning, and structured lineage context reshuffles system rankings rather than helping every participant uniformly.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [119] [Towards the Explainability of Temporal Graph Networks via Memory Backtracking and Topological Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07716)
*Yazheng Liu,Xi Zhang,Sihong Xie,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a TGN explanation method that attributes predictions via topology attribution trees and memory backtracking trees, applying LRP to ensure contribution conservation and using optimization-based selection to identify faithful important events, outperforming baselines across diverse temporal graph tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods for Temporal Graph Networks (TGNs) overlook the memory module — the core component that records and updates node histories — leaving the influence of past historical events on model predictions unexplored. This gap motivates the need for explanations that account for how memory vectors are shaped by prior events.

Method: The method constructs two structures: (1) a topology attribution tree that captures the influence of neighbors and their memory vectors, and (2) a memory backtracking tree that quantifies how historical events shape node memory vectors. Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) is applied to TGNs, ensuring the total contribution of events equals the model logits. Additionally, recognizing that top-k selection from logits to probabilities is a nonlinear mapping and may be unfaithful, the authors design optimization objectives to identify the truly important events.

Result: Experiments on nine temporal graph datasets spanning node property prediction, link prediction, and graph classification tasks demonstrate that the proposed method provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: The work establishes that incorporating memory module attribution via backtracking trees combined with LRP-based conservation and optimized event selection yields faithful, effective explanations for TGN predictions, filling a critical gap in temporal graph explainability.

Abstract: Temporal graphs are ubiquitous in real-world applications and Temporal Graph Networks (TGNs) have achieved superior predictive accuracy. Understanding which historical events drive model predictions can enhance trustworthiness of TGNs. Existing explanation methods overlook the memory module, the core component that records and updates node histories, leaving the influence of past events unexplored. To address this, we attribute TGNs predictions through the topology attribution tree and memory backtracking tree. The topology attribution tree captures the influence of neighbors and their memory vectors, then the memory backtracking tree quantifies how historical events shape node memory vectors. We apply the LRP in TGNs, ensuring that the total contribution of events equals the logits of model. Finally, top-k selection may be unfaithful due to the nonlinear mapping from logits to probabilities, we design optimization objectives to identify the important events. Experiments on nine temporal graph datasets, spanning node property prediction, link prediction tasks and graph classification tasks, show that our method provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/yazhengliu/MemExplainer

</details>


### [120] [Who Gets Missed in the Tail? Thresholded Subgroup Underdiagnosis in Long-Tailed Chest X-ray Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07717)
*Ha-Hieu Pham,Hai-Dang Nguyen,Dang P. M. Cao,Thanh-Huy Nguyen,Min Xu,Trung-Nghia Le,Ulas Bagci,Huy-Hieu Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Long-tailed CXR classification exhibits fairness gaps: rare-positive patients within subgroups are disproportionately missed post-thresholding. Combining group-tail weighting with tail-aware threshold selection dramatically reduces worst-group false negative rates on VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, but residual misses remain high, showing that fairness depends jointly on finding, subgroup, and threshold—not just label frequency or ranking metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even when CXR classifiers achieve acceptable aggregate ranking performance, rare-positive patients—especially those in disadvantaged subgroups (sex, age, race, insurance)—may still fall below the decision threshold. The authors frame this as a pre-deployment audit question: after score-to-decision conversion, who is systematically missed?

Method: A diagnostic ladder with four interventions is applied: (1) class-level long-tail loss separation, (2) subgroup-aware weighting, (3) group robustness via GroupDRO, and (4) tail-aware threshold selection. Experiments span VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT benchmarks. Paired bootstrap contrasts statistically validate thresholded FNR reductions, and GroupDRO baselines test whether group robustness alone suffices.

Result: On VinDr-CXR: group-tail weighting + tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR (0.665→0.269), sex worst-group FNR (0.705→0.157), age worst-group FNR (0.822→0.133), while macro-mAP improves (0.611→0.635). On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT: tail FNR drops 0.866→0.741 with similar subgroup improvements, though residual missed-positive rates stay high. GroupDRO alone fails to eliminate rare subgroup misses. Bootstrap contrasts confirm statistical significance of FNR reductions on VinDr.

Conclusion: Rare-label fairness in CXR is a joint function of the specific finding, the subgroup, and the operating threshold—not label frequency or ranking metrics alone. Tail-aware thresholding is essential; group robustness training alone is insufficient for this setting.

Abstract: In chest X-ray (CXR) classification, acceptable ranking performance can still leave rare-positive patients below threshold, especially within subgroups. We study this pre-deployment fairness problem as an audit question: after a long-tailed multi-label CXR model is converted from scores into decisions, who is missed? Across VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, we use a diagnostic ladder to separate class-level long-tail losses, subgroup-aware weighting, group robustness, and threshold selection. On VinDr-CXR, group-tail weighting followed by tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR from 0.665 to 0.269, sex worst-group FNR from 0.705 to 0.157, and age worst-group FNR from 0.822 to 0.133, while macro-mAP increases from 0.611 to 0.635. On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, the same score-to-threshold comparison reduces tail FNR from 0.866 to 0.741 and lowers worst-group FNR across sex, age, race, and insurance; residual missed-positive rates nevertheless remain high. Paired bootstrap contrasts on VinDr support the thresholded FNR reductions, and GroupDRO reference runs indicate that aggregate group robustness alone does not remove rare subgroup misses in this setting. The study supports a narrow audit claim: rare-label fairness in CXR depends jointly on the finding, subgroup, and operating threshold, not on label frequency or ranking metrics alone.

</details>


### [121] [LLT: Local Linear Transformer for PDE Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07718)
*Oded Ovadia,Eli Turkel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLT is a transformer-based neural operator for PDEs that combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing to overcome quadratic scaling and lack of local inductive bias in standard attention. It achieves competitive accuracy with 1.8–2.5× faster training than Transolver and scales to 3D unstructured meshes with 32k+ nodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention in transformer-based neural operators suffers from two key limitations: (1) quadratic computational complexity with respect to the number of mesh/nodes, making it expensive for large-scale problems, and (2) no explicit bias toward local interactions, which are fundamental to PDE dynamics. The goal is to design an efficient attention mechanism that addresses both issues while maintaining competitive accuracy across diverse PDE problems, discretizations, and mesh types.

Method: The Local Linear Transformer (LLT) architecture combines: (1) linear global attention that reduces complexity from O(N²) to O(N) by using a kernelized formulation, (2) local spatial mixing layers that inject an explicit locality bias by aggregating information from neighboring nodes, and (3) coordinate/geometry embeddings that encode positional and geometric structure. The model is evaluated on structured and unstructured meshes across finite-element, finite-volume, and finite-difference discretizations.

Result: LLT achieves competitive or lower relative L₂ error compared to other neural operators and transformer baselines across five PDE problems (elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, Darcy flow). On matched structured discretizations, training wall-clock time is reduced by 1.8× to 2.5× relative to Transolver. The method also scales successfully to a 3D car aerodynamics dataset with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample.

Conclusion: LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient neural operator for learning PDE solution maps. It generalizes well across different discretization schemes, mesh types, and problem settings, and scales effectively to large 3D unstructured meshes, making it a strong candidate for accelerating physics simulations.

Abstract: Neural operators have become a common approach for learning PDE solution maps and accelerating numerical simulations. Transformer-based neural operators are of particular interest, since attention can learn long-range dependencies in the computational domain. However, standard attention has two major limitations when applied to PDEs: it scales quadratically with the number of computational nodes, and it lacks an explicit bias toward local interactions. To address these issues, we introduce Local Linear Transformer (LLT) for PDE operator learning. The architecture combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing, and incorporates coordinate and geometry information. We evaluate LLT on several PDE problems, including elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, and Darcy flow. The reference data for these problems span finite-element, finite-volume, and finite-difference discretizations on structured and unstructured meshes. Compared with other neural-operator and transformer baselines from prior studies, LLT achieves competitive or lower relative $L_2$ error across these problems. On matched structured discretizations, wall-clock time per training iteration is reduced by factors of 1.8 to 2.5 relative to Transolver. We also scale the approach and apply it to a three-dimensional car aerodynamics dataset with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample. Together, these results indicate that LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient operator for PDE problems across discretizations, mesh types, and problem settings.

</details>


### [122] [ReCoLoRA: Spectrum-Aware Recursive Consolidation for Continual LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07719)
*Wentao Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoLoRA is a continual fine-tuning framework that recursively consolidates low-rank adapters using spectrum-aware SVD-based initialization, enabling sequential task learning without catastrophic forgetting by re-decomposing the effective weight before each new task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In continual fine-tuning across a sequence of tasks, standard LoRA-style methods stack low-rank updates on frozen pretrained weights, causing each new task to overwrite previous ones (catastrophic forgetting). The paper aims to address this by enabling parameter-efficient continual learning where each new task builds upon a model that has already absorbed prior tasks.

Method: ReCoLoRA uses three key techniques: (1) adapters are initialized from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight matrix; (2) per-layer effective ranks are selected via an elbow criterion on the singular value spectrum; (3) before each new task, the current effective weight (not the original pretrained weight) is re-decomposed into a frozen residual component, a slowly-updated principal component, and a fresh trainable adapter. This recursive consolidation ensures each task starts from a model that has integrated all predecessors, while the principal subspace is adapted before residual capacity is opened.

Result: On a six-task continual GLUE sequence across four 7-8B parameter backbones, ReCoLoRA achieved the best final average score on three out of four backbones compared to rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA baselines, while training fewer parameters. An oracle-routed task-bank variant provided an upper bound under full task isolation.

Conclusion: ReCoLoRA effectively mitigates catastrophic forgetting in continual fine-tuning by recursively consolidating low-rank adapters, outperforming existing PEFT methods on sequential GLUE tasks while maintaining parameter efficiency. The recursive SVD-based consolidation strategy proves to be a principled approach for continual learning with large language models.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts a large language model to one task cheaply, but across a task sequence LoRA-style methods keep stacking low-rank updates on the same frozen weight, so each new task tends to overwrite the previous ones. We present ReCoLoRA (Recursive Consolidation of Low-Rank Adapters), a spectrum-aware framework for continual fine-tuning: adapters are initialized from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight, per-layer effective ranks are selected by an elbow criterion, and the principal subspace is adapted before residual capacity is opened. Before each new task, ReCoLoRA re-decomposes the current effective weight, rather than the original one, into a frozen residual, a slowly updated principal component, and a fresh adapter (recursive consolidation), so every task starts from the model that has already absorbed its predecessors. On a six-task continual GLUE sequence over four 7-8B backbones, ReCoLoRA attains the best final average score on three of the four backbones against rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA baselines while training fewer parameters; an oracle-routed task-bank variant serves as an upper bound under full task isolation. Code: https://github.com/bhqy666/ReCoLoRA.

</details>


### [123] [Omni-Sleep: A Sleep Foundation Model via Hierarchical Contrastive Learning of CNS--ANS Dynamic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07720)
*Zhoujie Hou,Song Wang,Kexin Lou,Mo Wang,Chen Wei,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Omni-Sleep is a sleep foundation model that incorporates CNS/ANS physiological hierarchy as a prior, using three learning objectives (intra-system consistency, inter-system synchronization, latent-space masked temporal modeling) on 100K+ hours of multimodal PSG data, achieving state-of-the-art performance on sleep staging and disease classification with strong generalization and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sleep foundation models fuse multimodal biosignals (EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, respiration) in a topology-agnostic manner, ignoring the physiological organization of the CNS and ANS. This overlooks the structured, coordinated dynamics between neural and cardio-respiratory subsystems that underlie sleep physiology.

Method: Omni-Sleep uses the CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained representation learning with three objectives: (1) intra-system consistency — captures shared subsystem-level factors within CNS and ANS signal groups; (2) inter-system synchronization — aligns subsystem trajectory representations to model brain–body dynamics; (3) latent-space masked temporal modeling — captures long-horizon sleep dynamics. The model is pre-trained on over 100,000 hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data.

Result: Omni-Sleep outperforms strong foundation-model baselines across multiple datasets and modality-ablation settings on sleep staging and multi-disease classification. It demonstrates improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities.

Conclusion: Incorporating physiological hierarchy (CNS/ANS topology) as a modeling prior significantly improves the generalizability and robustness of sleep representation learning, as demonstrated by Omni-Sleep's comprehensive empirical results.

Abstract: Sleep physiology arises from the coordinated dynamics of the central nervous system (CNS) and autonomic nervous system (ANS), as reflected by multimodal polysomnography signals including EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, and respiration. However, existing sleep foundation models often fuse heterogeneous biosignals in a topology-agnostic manner, overlooking their physiological organization. We introduce Omni-Sleep, a sleep foundation model that uses the CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained representation learning. Omni-Sleep learns structured representations through three objectives: intra-system consistency, which captures shared subsystem-level factors within neural and cardio-respiratory signals; inter-system synchronization, which aligns subsystem trajectories to model brain--body dynamics; and latent-space masked temporal modeling, which captures long-horizon sleep dynamics. Pre-trained on over 100,000 hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data, Omni-Sleep is evaluated on sleep staging and multi-disease classification. Across datasets and modality-ablation settings, Omni-Sleep outperforms strong foundation-model baselines, showing improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities. These results highlight the value of physiological hierarchy for generalizable sleep representation learning. Code is available at https://github.com/AutoBrain-sleep/OmniSleep.

</details>


### [124] [Uncertainty-gated selection for block-sparse attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07724)
*Thomas Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a value-of-information router that mitigates the myopic cutoff problem in block-sparse attention by adaptively doubling the kept block budget for queries where the top-k selection is least decisive, achieving significant recall and accuracy gains over standard top-k selection across multiple models and architectures while maintaining competitive throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block-sparse attention methods like Quest use a per-query top-k selection over key blocks to reduce complexity from O(N²) to sub-quadratic. However, this cutoff is "myopic": when the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks have nearly identical attention scores, the selector rigidly drops the (k+1)-th block without allocating extra budget. If that dropped block contains critical evidence for answering the query, the information is unrecoverable downstream, leading to degraded accuracy on long-context tasks.

Method: The authors introduce a value-of-information (VoI) router that operates on top of any existing block-scoring mechanism (e.g., Quest). For each query, the router measures the decisiveness of the top-k cutoff by computing the score gap between the k-th and (k+1)-th ranked key blocks. It then sorts queries by this gap (smallest gap = least decisive cutoff) and doubles the kept block budget (from k to 2k) for the queries where the gap is smallest, up to a total token budget constraint. The approach is backbone-agnostic, requires no retraining, and stacks with existing block-scoring methods. A fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline is also provided for efficient execution.

Result: On LongBench-v2 medium (n=215), router-on-Quest achieves paired recall of 0.75 vs. top-k baseline 0.47 (+28 pp, McNemar p<0.01), landing within 2 pp of dense attention on RULER NIAH multikey at the same context length. The lift generalizes across four models from three architectures (Qwen2.5, Mistral-Nemo, Qwen3.6). At 128K context, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 respectively, compared to SSA-style top-k at just 0.09 on the former. The fused pipeline achieves 0.62x and 0.80x of dense wall time.

Conclusion: The value-of-information router effectively addresses the myopic cutoff limitation in block-sparse attention by adaptively allocating additional budget to ambiguous selections, yielding substantial accuracy improvements over fixed top-k baselines while maintaining practical throughput. Its backbone-agnostic design enables broad applicability across models and architectures.

Abstract: Block-sparse attention scales long-context language models by replacing the O(N^2) softmax with a per-query top-k selection over key blocks. This cutoff is myopic: when the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks are nearly tied in score, the selector commits without spending extra budget, and a dropped block carrying answer evidence is unrecoverable downstream. We propose a value-of-information router that measures, for each query, how decisively the top-k cut was made, and doubles the kept set for the queries where that gap is smallest; the rule is backbone-agnostic and stacks with existing block-scoring methods such as Quest. On LongBench-v2 medium at n=215 (the entire dataset subset), router-on-Quest reaches paired recall 0.75 vs. top-k 0.47 -- +28 pp over the SSA-style baseline (McNemar p<0.01) -- and lands within 2 pp of dense on RULER NIAH multikey at the same context. The lift reproduces on four models from three architectures (Qwen2.5, Mistral-Nemo, Qwen3.6). At 128K, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09 on the former) while the fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline runs at 0.62x and 0.80x dense wall time.

</details>


### [125] [SHIFT: Survival Prediction from Incomplete and Heterogeneous Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07725)
*Muhammet Sami Yavuz,Ayhan Can Erdur,Sabri Mustafa Kahya,Benedikt Wiestler,Jana Lipkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SHIFT is a transformer-based survival model that handles incomplete genomic features across institutions without test-time imputation, using masked self-attention and variable-rate feature masking during training to achieve robust cross-cohort generalization in glioblastoma and lung cancer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Genomic prediction models fail to transfer across institutions because different sequencing panels create structural feature missingness. Current solutions — restricting to shared genes, excluding incomplete patients, or test-time imputation — reduce robustness and waste multi-center data.

Method: SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and uses masked self-attention with a feature-availability mask, ensuring predictions depend only on observed inputs. Variable-rate feature masking during training improves robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns. A single model handles differing feature sets across cohorts.

Result: SHIFT demonstrated strong generalization on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma across multiple external validation cohorts, including settings with severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. It compared favorably against standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches. Incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development improved external performance.

Conclusion: Missingness-aware modeling is a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology, enabling the use of partially observed cohorts without exclusion and supporting robust deployment across institutions with heterogeneous genomic panels.

Abstract: Genomic prediction models often fail to transfer across institutions because sequencing panels differ across sites, creating structural feature missingness at deployment. Existing approaches to this challenge typically restrict analysis to genes shared across cohorts, exclude patients with incomplete profiles, or rely on test-time imputation, all of which can reduce robustness and limit the use of multi-center data. We propose Survival prediction Handling Incomplete Features using Transformer (SHIFT), a missingness-aware survival model that directly predicts from incomplete genomic inputs without test-time imputation. SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and uses masked self-attention, along with a feature-availability mask, so that predictions are based only on observed inputs. Further, we introduce variable-rate feature masking during training to improve robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns. We evaluate the approach on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, including a challenging setting with severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. Across these settings, SHIFT shows strong generalization and compares favorably with standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches, while using a single model across differing feature sets. We also find that incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development can improve performance on external data, suggesting that partially observed cohorts need not be excluded from model building. These results support missingness-aware modeling as a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology.

</details>


### [126] [Jet-Long: Efficient Long-Context Extension with Dynamic Bifocal RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07740)
*Haozhan Tang,Zerui Wang,Yuxian Gu,Song Han,Han Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jet-Long is a tuning-free zero-shot context extension method that uses a bifocal attention mechanism — combining a local RoPE-faithful window with a dynamic long-range rescaling window — to preserve short-context fidelity while enabling clean long-context extrapolation. Fused into a single CUDA kernel, it achieves near-zero inference overhead and outperforms baselines on RULER, HELMET-RAG, and PG-19 across Qwen3 models up to 128K context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zero-shot RoPE rescaling methods use a single fixed rescaling factor, creating an unavoidable trade-off: aggressive factors degrade short-context performance, while conservative factors fail at long contexts. With modern LLMs deployed in long-context scenarios far exceeding pretraining window sizes, there is a pressing need for a method that maintains exact short-context behavior while extrapolating reliably to long sequences — all without retraining or fine-tuning.

Method: Jet-Long employs a bifocal attention design: (1) a local window uses RoPE-faithful attention to exactly recover the base model on short inputs; (2) a long-range window applies a dynamic rescaling factor that adapts to the current sequence length. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge combines both windows without double-counting. An on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation compensates for positional discrepancies. The entire mechanism is fused into a single CuTe kernel, achieving efficient prefill (up to 1.39× FA2 throughput on H100) and ≤4% generation overhead.

Result: On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B at 128K context: Jet-Long outperforms the strongest baseline on RULER by +4.79/+2.18/+2.03 percentage points. It achieves best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG and lowest perplexity on PG-19. The method also generalizes to hybrid attention architectures (Jet-Nemotron) for further improvement and demonstrates hyperparameter resilience.

Conclusion: Jet-Long demonstrates that dynamic, length-adaptive rescaling within a bifocal attention framework effectively resolves the short-vs-long context trade-off in zero-shot context extension. Its efficiency and strong empirical results across model scales and benchmarks position it as a practical, deployable solution for extending open-weight LLMs to long contexts without retraining.

Abstract: Modern LLMs are increasingly deployed in long-context applications such as retrieval-augmented generation, repository-level coding, and agentic workflows whose accumulated reasoning and tool traces routinely push the input an order of magnitude past the pretraining window, making zero-shot context extension the dominant deployment path for open-weight checkpoints. Most existing zero-shot methods fix a single rescaling factor up front, so an aggressive factor sacrifices short-context fidelity while a conservative one breaks down at long contexts. We propose Jet-Long, a tuning-free zero-shot method that pairs a local RoPE-faithful window with a long-range window whose rescaling factor adapts dynamically to the current sequence length, recovering the base model exactly at short inputs while extrapolating cleanly at long ones. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge and an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation make the bifocal construction essentially free at inference; fused into a single CuTe kernel, long-context prefill reaches up to $1.39\times$ FA2 throughput on H100 (approaching the Hopper-only FA4), and single-batch generation incurs $\le 4\%$ overhead at every length. On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B up to 128K context, Jet-Long leads RULER by $+4.79$/$+2.18$/$+2.03$~pp over the strongest baseline at 1.7B/4B/8B, achieves the best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG (a benchmark identified by HELMET as the most efficient predictor of downstream long-context performance) and attains the lowest PG-19 perplexity. Jet-Long also generalizes to hybrid attention architectures such as Jet-Nemotron for further long-context improvement without retraining, and remains hyperparameter-resilient for ease of deployment.

</details>


### [127] [Architecture Generalization with MetaNCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07743)
*Meet Barot,Daniel Berenberg,Sina Khajehabdollahi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaNCA introduces a framework that uses neural cellular automata with a Weight Transformer architecture to self-organize neural network weights through local interactions, generating weights for diverse architectures (MLPs, CNNs, ResNets) without backpropagation, scaling to 2M parameters and generalizing to unseen architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological neurons learn efficiently through local synaptic interactions and adapt over time. Existing NCA models capture morphogenesis via local rules but have not been applied to self-organizing neural network weights. The authors aim to leverage these principles—local information, adaptability, and robustness—to generate trained network weights without gradient-based optimization.

Method: The MetaNCA framework consists of a learned rule network that iteratively updates a task network's weights using only local interactions on the computation graph. The key innovation is a Weight Transformer architecture employing linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. Meta-training exposes the rule network to diverse architectures; at inference, it generates weights for novel architectures without backpropagation.

Result: MetaNCA successfully generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks with 2 million parameters. It generalizes to architectures unseen during meta-training, and architectural diversity during training improves this generalization capability.

Conclusion: MetaNCA demonstrates that local, self-organizing rules can effectively generate neural network weights across diverse architectures, offering a compelling alternative to backpropagation with strong generalization properties. Architectural diversity during meta-training is critical for robust generalization to novel architectures.

Abstract: Self-organization is an emergent property of life, driven by the collective behavior of individual components acting on local information. Biological neurons, through local interactions transmitted through synapses, are able to learn efficiently and can adapt their connections over an organism's lifespan. Motivated by these desirable properties of adaptability and local interaction, neural cellular automata (NCA) models have been successful at learning morphogenesis solely through local update rules, demonstrating stability over many updates and robustness to perturbations. In this work, we introduce Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA), a framework that learns local rules which self-organize the weights of artificial neural networks. A learned rule network iteratively updates the weights of a task network using only local interactions on the computation graph. We propose a novel Weight Transformer architecture for the local rule network, which uses linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. Once trained, the rule network generates task networks of diverse architectures without backpropagation. We show that MetaNCA generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks of 2 million parameters. We further show that MetaNCA generalizes to architectures not seen during meta-training, and that architectural diversity in the training phase strengthens this generalization.

</details>


### [128] [LiST: Lipschitz Scaling Training for Robust and Calibrated Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07745)
*Arthur Chiron,Franck Mamalet,Thomas Massena,Thomas Deltort,Mathieu Serrurier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes a theoretical link between Lipschitz constraints and Temperature Scaling for neural network calibration, then introduces LiST—a training paradigm that iteratively adjusts the Lipschitz constant to achieve out-of-the-box calibrated networks while navigating the accuracy-robustness Pareto front.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks require accuracy, robustness, and calibration simultaneously for reliability, yet these are typically studied in isolation. Lipschitz-constrained models offer certified robustness but suffer from: (1) manually chosen Lipschitz constants that arbitrarily set the accuracy-robustness trade-off, and (2) largely unexplored calibration properties. A principled method to jointly optimize all three desiderata is missing.

Method: The authors first prove a theoretical equivalence between the enforced Lipschitz constant L and the temperature parameter in Temperature Scaling. They identify an optimal L* that yields calibrated networks out-of-the-box for a given training scheme. Building on this, they propose LiST (Lipschitz Scaling Training), which iteratively tunes the global Lipschitz constant during training to converge to the calibrated operating point. A margin parameter in the loss enables constructing a fully calibrated Pareto front across the accuracy-robustness spectrum. At convergence, calibration data can be reintegrated into training to improve sample efficiency without degrading calibration.

Result: LiST achieves competitive accuracy and robustness against both constrained and unconstrained baselines on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet while remaining calibrated out of the box—eliminating the need for post-hoc calibration methods like Temperature Scaling.

Conclusion: Calibration serves as a principled criterion for selecting the Lipschitz constraint, unifying accuracy, robustness, and calibration within a single training framework. LiST enables practitioners to navigate the accuracy-robustness Pareto front with guaranteed calibration, advancing the deployment of reliable neural networks.

Abstract: While accuracy, robustness, and calibration are all essential for reliable neural networks, they are often studied separately; developing models that satisfy all three simultaneously remains a central challenge. Lipschitz-constrained models guarantee robustness by design, yet the manual selection of the Lipschitz constraint L governs the resulting accuracy-robustness trade-off, and their calibration properties remain largely underexplored. In this work, we highlight a theoretical and empirical link between the enforced Lipschitz constraint and Temperature Scaling, a state-of-the-art calibration method. Specifically, we find that for a given training scheme, there exists a non-trivial value L* that yields an out-of-the-box calibrated network, and that calibration acts as a principled criterion to select a well-defined operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front. Leveraging these insights, we introduce Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST), a novel training paradigm that iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach this operating point. Through a margin parameter in the training loss, LiST further enables the construction of a fully calibrated Pareto front, allowing users to navigate the accuracy-robustness trade-off while remaining calibrated throughout. At convergence, LiST also enables the reintegration of calibration data into training, improving sample efficiency without sacrificing calibration. We validate LiST on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet, demonstrating competitive accuracy and robustness against constrained and unconstrained baselines, while remaining calibrated out of the box. Code is available at GitHub.

</details>


### [129] [Selective Left-Shift: Turning Test-Time Compute and Difficulty-based Curation into Training Data for Low-Resource Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07748)
*Didula Samaraweera,Anjana Supun,Srinath Perera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-phase pipeline (offline data synthesis with compiler/test feedback → SFT → RLVR with I/O tests) decouples syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning, enabling Small Language Models to achieve strong code generation on Low-Resource Programming Languages like Julia and Ballerina, with up to +14.2 point gains at a fraction of the cost of prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Language Models perform poorly on Low-Resource Programming Languages (LRPLs). Improving Small Language Models (SLMs) for LRPLs faces a trilemma: SFT is data-scarce, inference-time scaling is prohibitively expensive, and RL from scratch yields negligible gains. A new approach is needed to resolve this trilemma.

Method: The authors propose a three-phase pipeline: (1) Left-shifting inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that generates verified training examples via iterative compiler and test feedback; (2) SFT on the synthetic verified data to instill strong syntactic priors; (3) RLVR using language-agnostic Input/Output tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration to avoid syntax errors. The approach is evaluated on Qwen3-8B for Julia and Ballerina.

Result: On Qwen3-8B, the pipeline improves pass@1 by up to +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia over prior SOTA. It uses only 1/3 the data and 1/6 the cost. On Ballerina (near-zero pretraining representation), it achieves 49.7% MultiPL-E Pass@1. Ablations confirm both the SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

Conclusion: Decoupling syntax learning (via offline verified data synthesis + SFT) from reasoning (via RLVR with execution-grounded rewards) effectively resolves the SLM trilemma for LRPLs. The pipeline is both data- and cost-efficient, and generalizes even to languages with minimal pretraining representation.

Abstract: Large Language Models achieve strong code generation for high resource languages like Python and Java but suffer sharp performance drops on Low-Resource Programming Languages~(LRPLs) such as Julia. Improving Small Language Models~(SLMs) for these languages faces a trilemma: Supervised Fine-Tuning~(SFT) is bottlenecked by data scarcity, inference-time scaling is too expensive for deployment, and Reinforcement Learning from scratch yields near zero advantages. We propose a three-phase pipeline that resolves this trilemma by decoupling syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning. First, we \emph{left-shift} inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that uses iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. Second, we fine-tune an SLM on this synthetic, verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. Third, we apply Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward~(RLVR) grounded by language-agnostic Input/Output tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors. Applied to Qwen3-8B, our pipeline improves pass@1 by up to +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on the Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia compared to SOTA results. Furthermore, the pipeline only used $\frac{1}{3}$ data and $\frac{1}{6}$ cost over the previous state-of-the-art. We further demonstrate that the pipeline generalizes to Ballerina achieving 49.7\% MultiPL-E Pass@1, a language with near-zero pretraining representation. Ablations confirm that both the SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

</details>


### [130] [A Transdiagnostic Space of Disorder Like Phenotypes in Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07753)
*Hari Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a framework for modelling seven psychological disorders in reinforcement learning agents by manipulating cognitive appraisal signals via tunable "knobs," demonstrating graded dose-response effects, emergent disorder clustering into a 2D affective space, differential recovery patterns, and nonadditive comorbidity interactions across thousands of controlled runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior computational psychiatry work models only one or two disorders at a time using hand-tuned reward shaping with single-run reporting, lacking systematic dose-controllable manipulation grounded in cognitive appraisal theory. The authors aim to unify disorder induction under a principled, reproducible framework.

Method: The authors use an appraisal-guided PPO agent where seven disorders — anxiety, mania, obsessive-compulsive checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and PTSD — are each induced by adjusting a single cognitive appraisal weight (a "knob"). Each disorder is grounded in a computational psychiatry account and measured by preregistered behavioral assays. Over 1,000 runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% CIs) test dose-response, recovery (knob removal vs. exposure curriculum), and two-knob comorbidity interactions. Transfer is validated in MiniWorld with a standard convolutional agent lacking an appraisal critic.

Result: Every disorder exhibits a graded, monotone dose-response not reproduced by any control. Disorders self-organize into a 2D affective space where mania mirrors anxiety. Removing a knob remits reward-distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under graded exposure. Simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, generating testable comorbidity predictions. Three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer successfully to MiniWorld with cross-assay dissociation confirmed.

Conclusion: Appraisal weights parameterize a controllable space of affective phenotypes wherein the same knobs inducing disorders can model their treatment. The framework is not specific to grid worlds or PPO's appraisal critic, suggesting broader applicability in computational psychiatry for both understanding disorder mechanisms and exploring therapeutic interventions.

Abstract: Modelling psychological disorders in artificial agents offers both a testbed for computational psychiatry and a lens on the failure modes of affective control. Prior work induces one or two disorders in a reinforcement learning (RL) agent by hand-tuned reward shaping, labels the behaviour post hoc, and reports single runs. We recast disorder modelling as dose-controllable manipulation of cognitive appraisal signals in an appraisal-guided PPO agent, expressing seven disorders (anxiety, mania, obsessive-compulsive checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and post-traumatic stress) each as a single knob grounded in a computational psychiatry account, with each symptom measured by a preregistered assay mapped to a recognised paradigm. Across more than a thousand runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% confidence intervals) every disorder shows a graded, monotone dose-response that no control reproduces. Beyond these induced effects, three findings emerge that were not written into the reward: the disorders self-organise into a two-dimensional affective space in which mania mirrors anxiety; removing a knob remits reward distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under a graded exposure curriculum; and two simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Appraisal weights thus parameterise a controllable space of affective phenotypes in which the same knobs that induce a disorder can model its treatment. We also show that three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer to a three-dimensional pixel environment (MiniWorld) with a standard convolutional agent and no appraisal critic, with cross-assay dissociation confirmed across both domains, indicating the framework is not specific to grid worlds or to PPO's appraisal critic.

</details>


### [131] [Image classification via a quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07754)
*Kumari Jyoti,Rohith Babu,Apoorva D. Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A quantum-inspired hybrid classical-quantum CNN framework using a mixture of experts with amplitude encoding, local unitary convolutions, and stabiliser code feature extraction, which reduces image classification failure rates by ~2× on MNIST/Fashion-MNIST with moderate GPU overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical CNNs rely on diffusion-based downsampling and pooling, which may be suboptimal for capturing structural features. The authors seek a more efficient strategy inspired by quantum computing principles to improve feature extraction and classification performance.

Method: A hybrid classical-quantum framework: the quantum part uses amplitude encoding of images, convolution via local unitary operations, multiple parallel experts with different parameters, and feature extraction through quantum stabiliser codes. The classical part aggregates features from all experts using a fully connected neural network for final classification.

Result: The joint multi-expert approach outperforms individual experts and reduces classification failure rate by approximately a factor of two on both MNIST and Fashion-MNIST benchmarks, with only moderate computational overhead on GPU workstations.

Conclusion: The proposed quantum-inspired mixture-of-experts strategy is a practical, efficient alternative to classical CNN schemes, offering significant accuracy gains with manageable overhead, and is executable on actual quantum processors.

Abstract: Pattern recognition problems arise in a variety of physical image processing situations, and convolutional neural networks are a popular scheme for the required feature extraction and classification tasks. The classical networks use diffusion-based smearing and block-wise pooling to downsample the image data and capture important structural features. In this work, we propose and demonstrate a more efficient quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts. It is a hybrid classical-quantum framework. The quantum part consists of amplitude encoding of the images, convolution using local unitary operations, multiple experts processing the same image with different parameters, and feature extraction using quantum stabiliser codes. The classical part then jointly processes the features extracted by different experts using a standard fully connected neural network for image class prediction. Using MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets as benchmarks, we demonstrate that the joint expert analysis outperforms the individual expert one, as well as reduces the failure rate of image class prediction by around a factor of two. The overhead of our quantum-inspired strategy is only moderate on GPU workstations, which makes our proposal a practical alternative to existing classical schemes. We also point out how the quantum part of our framework can be executed on a quantum processor.

</details>


### [132] [Federated Deep Learning for Privacy-Preserving Cardiovascular Disease Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08595)
*Hyunho Mo,Djura Smits,Mahlet A. Birhanu,Maarten J. G. Leening,Daniel Bos,Pim van der Harst,Esther E. Bron*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A federated deep learning approach for cardiovascular disease risk prediction was developed across two heterogeneous population cohorts (Lifelines: 148,230 participants; Rotterdam Study: 10,155 participants), demonstrating improved C-statistics over locally trained models while preserving patient data privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CVD risk prediction models are typically limited to single-institution or pooled datasets. Cross-institutional model development is constrained by privacy regulations and restrictions on sharing patient-level data. Federated learning offers a solution, but its healthcare application remains challenging due to heterogeneity in dataset sizes, population characteristics, and outcome definitions across institutions.

Method: A federated deep learning framework was applied to deep survival models, integrating two population-based cohorts: the large Lifelines cohort (148,230 participants with self-reported outcomes) and the smaller Rotterdam Study cohort (10,155 participants with digitally linked clinical outcomes). Model performance was primarily evaluated on the Rotterdam Study due to its complete follow-up data. Federated training was compared against locally trained models (without federation).

Result: Federated learning outperformed local training in both cohorts. For the Rotterdam Study, C-statistic improved from 0.728 (95% CI: 0.717–0.739) to 0.739 (95% CI: 0.728–0.749). For Lifelines, C-statistic improved from 0.783 (95% CI: 0.775–0.791) to 0.787 (95% CI: 0.780–0.792).

Conclusion: Federated deep learning across heterogeneous cohorts can improve CVD risk prediction performance while maintaining individual-level data privacy, supporting its feasibility for multi-institutional collaborative model development in healthcare.

Abstract: Cardiovascular disease risk prediction models often rely on data from a single institution or centrally pooled datasets. Extending these models across institutions could be limited by privacy regulations and constraints on sharing patient-level data. Federated learning enables collaborative model development without transferring sensitive patient data, but its application in healthcare remains challenging because datasets often differ in size, population characteristics, and outcome definitions. In this study, we present a federated deep learning approach for privacy-preserving cardiovascular disease risk prediction that integrates two population-based cohorts with different characteristics: Lifelines, including 148,230 participants meeting the study inclusion criteria with self-reported outcomes, and the Rotterdam Study, including a smaller cohort of 10,155 participants with digitally linked clinical outcomes. Model performance was primarily evaluated on the Rotterdam Study because of its complete follow-up. Deep survival models trained using federated learning achieved higher predictive performance than models trained locally without federation. For the Rotterdam Study, the C-statistic increased from 0.728 (95% CI: 0.717-0.739) to 0.739 (95% CI: 0.728-0.749). For Lifelines, the C-statistic increased from 0.783 (95% CI: 0.775-0.791) to 0.787 (95% CI: 0.780-0.792). These findings suggest that federated deep learning across heterogeneous cohorts can improve cardiovascular disease risk prediction while preserving the privacy of individual-level patient data.

</details>


### [133] [The Importance of Encoder Choice:A Tabular-Image Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07756)
*Ilia Koloiarov,Diego Coello de Portugal Mecke,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi,Tom Hanika,Lars Schmidt-Thieme*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates using state-of-the-art tabular models (including In-Context Learning models) as encoders in multimodal image-tabular learning, addressing the challenge that ICL models require labels to process instances, and demonstrates the importance of the encoder choice in multimodal settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal learning with tabular data typically uses plain MLPs as encoders, yet tabular deep learning remains an unsolved challenge ("the last unconquered castle"). The authors aim to explore whether modern, stronger tabular models can improve image-tabular multimodal learning, filling a gap since no prior work has systematically evaluated state-of-the-art tabular encoders in this context.

Method: The study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in an image-tabular multimodal setting. A key technical challenge addressed is that In-Context Learning (ICL) models — among the best tabular performers — require labels during instance processing, which complicates consistent embedding of both training and test instances. The authors propose solutions to handle this across multiple ICL-family models.

Result: The work enables state-of-the-art tabular models, including ICL-based ones, to function as encoders in multimodal pipelines. The study highlights that the choice of tabular encoder significantly impacts multimodal learning performance.

Conclusion: The encoder factor matters substantially in multimodal learning involving tabular data. Stronger tabular encoders, once adapted for multimodal use (particularly overcoming the ICL label-dependency issue), can improve upon the conventional plain MLP approach, urging the community to pay closer attention to encoder selection.

Abstract: Multimodal learning usually requires a dedicated encoder per modality. When a tabular modality is involved, prior work has been mostly using a \emph{plain MLP} as the encoder. Yet if it were a strong encoder, the tabular domain would not be ``the last unconquered castle for deep learning''. This study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in the image-tabular setting for the first time. An obstacle stands out. In-Context Learning models, among the best performing methods in the tabular domain, require labels to process instances, making it non-trivial to embed training and test instances the same way. We addressed this problem across multiple models of this family. With this study, we would like to highlight the importance of encoder factor in the multimodal learning.

</details>


### [134] [Dimensionality Reduction Meets Network Science: Sensemaking on UMAP's kNN Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08746)
*Duen Horng Chau,Donghao Ren,Fred Hohman,Dominik Moritz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UMAP's internal kNN graph, built before 2D projection, encodes the high-dimensional data manifold. Standard graph algorithms (PageRank, k-core decomposition, clustering coefficient) applied to this graph yield effective data sensemaking — representative point selection, core-periphery detection, and tight neighborhood identification — competitive with purpose-built methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typical UMAP workflows focus exclusively on the 2D embedding, ignoring the internally constructed kNN graph that preserves the true high-dimensional manifold structure without 2D distortion. This graph is an underutilized resource for data analysis.

Method: The authors extract the high-dimensional kNN graph from UMAP's internal construction and apply three standard graph algorithms: (1) PageRank for identifying representative exemplars, (2) k-core decomposition for revealing dense core vs. sparse periphery structure, and (3) clustering coefficient for detecting tightly connected neighborhoods. Evaluation is performed on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, comparing against k-medoids and HDBSCAN.

Result: Graph-based analyses on UMAP's internal kNN graph are practical and achieve competitive or complementary performance relative to purpose-built methods — k-medoids for exemplar selection and HDBSCAN for density-based clustering — on both quantitative and qualitative metrics across MNIST and Fashion MNIST.

Conclusion: UMAP's internal kNN graph is a powerful, previously overlooked representation that enables effective high-dimensional data sensemaking through simple graph algorithms, offering a unified alternative or complement to specialized methods.

Abstract: While UMAP is widely used for exploring high-dimensional data, typical workflows focus on its lower-dimensional embedding, largely overlooking the rich k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) graph that UMAP constructs internally. This graph encodes the data manifold in its original high-dimensional space, before the distortion that UMAP's 2D projection introduces. We demonstrate the untapped potential of this internal representation, showing how standard graph algorithms applied to this graph enhance data sensemaking: (1) PageRank identifies representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition reveals dense core regions versus sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient detects tight-knit neighborhoods with highly-similar data points. Through quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, we show that these graph-based analyses are not only practical but also competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

</details>


### [135] [Scalable and Trustworthy Earth Observation Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07758)
*Syed Usama Imtiaz,Mitra Nasr Azadani,Nasrin Alamdari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) require domain-specific adaptation due to the unique physics and operational constraints of Earth observation data. This chapter reviews design principles, benchmarks, and trustworthiness requirements for RSFMs, and presents two case studies demonstrating domain-guided principles in practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While foundation models have revolutionized general-purpose machine learning, directly transferring them to Earth observation tasks is unreliable without domain-specific adaptation. EO data are governed by measurement physics (sensor characteristics, radiative transfer) and operational constraints (revisit schedules, acquisition modes), making naive transfer suboptimal. The paper is motivated by the need to systematically understand and codify the design principles that make RSFMs effective under these constraints.

Method: This is a review/synthesis chapter.
