The authors (1) define the foundation model paradigm in remote sensing, (2) survey the current RSFM landscape including pretraining objectives, architecture designs, and downstream adaptation strategies, (3) synthesize recent benchmark evidence on model performance, (4) identify trustworthiness requirements for EO decisions, and (5) present two case studies: physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction, and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection.

Result: Key findings include: (1) no single geospatial foundation model is universally best across all tasks; (2) inconsistent evaluation protocols remain a major barrier to fair comparison and reliable deployment; (3) physics-informed pretraining strategies (e.g., spectral masking) and adaptive selection methods (e.g., RL-based station selection) demonstrate practical value in environmental monitoring.

Conclusion: The chapter argues that next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated beyond benchmark accuracy alone, incorporating modality-aware transfer capability and physically plausible representations to support trustworthy Earth observation decisions.

Abstract: Foundation models (FMs) have transformed machine learning from isolated task-specific model development toward general-purpose models pretrained on broad data and adapted to multiple downstream tasks. Earth observation (EO) is an important domain for this paradigm because satellite and airborne archives are large, high-revisit, and increasingly multimodal, while reliable field labels are often sparse. Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) cannot be transferred reliably/optimally without domain-specific adaptation. This is because EO data are governed by measurement physics and operational decision constraints. This chapter reviews the design principles arising from these domain-specific constraints. It first defines the FMs paradigm in remote sensing (RS), then synthesizes the current model landscape, pretraining objectives, architecture designs, downstream adaptation and trustworthiness requirements. The chapter also incorporates recent benchmark evidence showing that no single geospatial foundation model is universally best and that inconsistent evaluation remains a major issue to fair comparison and reliable deployment. In addition, two brief environmental monitoring case studies; physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection to illustrate the FMs domain-guided principles in practice. This chapter posits that next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations for trustworthy EO decisions.

</details>


### [136] [Trustworthy Machine Learning through the Lens of Combinatorial Optimization: Survey and Research Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07762)
*Thibaut Vidal,Julien Ferry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey explores how combinatorial optimization (CO) techniques can enhance trustworthiness in machine learning by providing formal guarantees for interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and other desiderata, offering capabilities beyond purely heuristic or gradient-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern ML models with similar empirical performance can diverge significantly in trustworthiness dimensions (transparency, robustness, fairness, privacy). Purely heuristic approaches lack formal guarantees, motivating the use of combinatorial optimization to provide global certificates, explicit trade-off management, and certifiable properties.

Method: The paper surveys CO-based formulations applied to trustworthy ML across both training and post-training stages, covering: interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks/protections. It contrasts CO approaches with gradient-based heuristics, emphasizing global guarantees and formal certificates.

Result: CO formulations across diverse trustworthiness domains offer formal guarantees, global optimality certificates, and principled trade-off handling that heuristic methods cannot provide. Scalability remains a key challenge, but advances in solvers and hybrid algorithms (combining CO with ML) are progressively expanding applicability.

Conclusion: Combinatorial optimization is poised to play a growing role in trustworthy ML systems. Continued progress in solver technology and hybrid CO-ML methods will help overcome scalability limitations, enabling wider adoption for model auditing, certification, and deployment in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Modern machine learning (ML) increasingly relies on complex models whose behavior is difficult to characterize beyond empirical performance metrics. Across a wide range of tasks, including prediction, generation, and decision-making, models with similar empirical performance can exhibit markedly different properties in terms of their transparency, interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability. This survey highlights how optimization- and certification-oriented reasoning can provide a useful framework for reasoning about such differences, supporting tasks ranging from model training and selection to auditing and certification. We review and synthesize recent advances at the intersection of combinatorial optimization (CO) and trustworthy ML, covering both training and post-training tasks, including interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks and protections. Across these domains, CO formulations offer additional capabilities over purely heuristic approaches, e.g., gradient-based ones, notably global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit treatment of trade-offs. While scalability remains an important challenge, continued progress in solvers and hybrid algorithms suggests a growing role for CO in the design and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

</details>


### [137] [Unlocking Temporal Generalization in Hamiltonian Video Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07763)
*Eli Laird,Corey Clark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HGNs fail at temporal generalization under non-conservative (forced, dissipative) dynamics due to latent magnitude growth and integrator truncation errors; the authors identify targeted fixes enabling stable prediction at unseen step sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models are locked to a fixed discrete-time step size, preventing flexible multi-timescale prediction needed for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and applications requiring dynamics queries at variable temporal resolutions. HGNs promise continuous-time grounding via an energy function independent of frame rate, but their temporal generalization collapses in non-conservative settings.

Method: The authors analyze failure modes of HGNs under externally forced, dissipative dynamics: (1) unconstrained latent magnitude growth from the action-force mapping, (2) global truncation error accumulation from an under-resolved integrator. They propose targeted fixes for each mechanism and evaluate rollouts at step sizes far outside the training distribution.

Result: The identified failure modes are confirmed through analysis; applying the targeted fixes yields stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions well beyond the training regime.

Conclusion: Temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation is achievable by addressing latent magnitude blow-up and integrator under-resolution. The authors recommend strategies to enable robust multi-timescale prediction in HGN-based world models.

Abstract: World models are typically trained to predict discrete-time physical dynamics with a fixed step size baked into the model weights, preventing prediction at variable temporal resolutions. This matters for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and scientific or game-engine applications that must query the same dynamics at multiple timescales. Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) offer a principled path forward, grounding predictions in a continuous-time energy function that is, in principle, independent of the observation frame rate. In practice, however, their temporal generalization breaks down in non-conservative settings. We show that in externally forced, dissipative environments, HGN rollouts at step sizes beyond the training regime fail due to distinct failure modes, including latent magnitude growth driven by an unconstrained action-force map, and global truncation error accumulation from an under-resolved integrator. We identify a targeted fix for each mechanism and demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions well outside the training distribution. In a detailed analysis, we recommend several strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation.

</details>


### [138] [Principled Analysis of Deep Reinforcement Learning Evaluation and Design Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07769)
*Ezgi Korkmaz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper critically examines evaluation and design paradigms in deep reinforcement learning, introduces theoretical foundations of scaling laws, and demonstrates through large-scale experiments that canonical approaches have led to incorrect conclusions — revealing that asymptotic performance rankings are non-monotonic across data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite a decade of remarkable progress in deep RL (from DQN-based game playing to algorithm-driven advances), the authors identify a fundamental problem: the canonical evaluation and design paradigms used to compare and develop RL algorithms may be systematically flawed. They are motivated to rigorously investigate whether performance conclusions drawn under standard evaluation protocols remain valid when scaling laws and data regimes are properly accounted for.

Method: The authors (1) introduce theoretical foundations of scaling laws in RL, analyzing the relationship between algorithmic performance and data regimes; (2) demonstrate theoretically that asymptotic performance rankings are not monotonic with respect to data-regime changes; and (3) conduct large-scale experiments across multiple algorithms and environments to empirically validate that standard evaluation/design paradigms yield incorrect conclusions when scaling behavior is considered.

Result: The large-scale experiments reveal that a significant line of RL research, conducted under canonical design and evaluation paradigms, has produced incorrect conclusions. The key finding is that performance rankings among algorithms shift non-monotonically as data regimes change, meaning that algorithms that appear superior under limited-data evaluation may not maintain their advantage asymptotically, and vice versa.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the RL community's standard evaluation and design paradigms are insufficient and potentially misleading. Proper assessment of deep RL algorithms must account for scaling behavior, capacity, and complexity considerations. The authors call for revised evaluation protocols that consider the non-monotonic relationship between performance rankings and data regimes to ensure robust and reliable algorithmic conclusions.

Abstract: Starting from the utilization of deep neural networks to approximate the state-action value function that led to winning one of the most challenging games, to algorithmic advancements that allowed solving problems without even explicitly stating the rules of the challenge at hand, reinforcement learning research has been the center of remarkable scientific progress for the past decade. In this paper, we focus on the key ingredients of this research progress and we analyze the canonical evaluation and design paradigms in reinforcement learning. We introduce the theoretical foundations of scaling laws in reinforcement learning and show that the asymptotic performance of reinforcement learning algorithms does not have a monotone relationship between performance rankings and data-regimes. We conduct large-scale experiments and our results demonstrate that a line of reinforcement learning research under the canonical design and evaluation paradigms resulted in incorrect conclusions. Our analysis and results provide a core analysis on scaling, capacity and complexity of deep reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [139] [Graph-Regularized Deep Learning for EEG-Based Emotion Recognition with Psychologically-Grounded Label Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07773)
*Dongyang Kuang,Zizheng Ma,Yushan Zhang,Xiaocong Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a graph-regularized learning framework for EEG emotion recognition that encodes psychological interdependencies among emotion classes via graph-based penalties (Graph Label Smoothing, Graph Laplacian Commuting Distance, Sliced Wasserstein Distance), achieving up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in implausible misclassifications across multiple architectures on SEED-IV/V datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning methods for EEG emotion recognition treat emotion classes as independent, ignoring the psychological relationships (e.g., dimensional valence-arousal proximity) among emotions. This leads to psychologically implausible predictions and suboptimal performance.

Method: A graph-regularized framework that models emotions as graph nodes with edges encoding dimensional emotion proximity. Three regularization strategies of increasing complexity are adapted: (1) Graph Label Smoothing — soft labeling based on graph proximity; (2) Commuting Distance via Graph Laplacian — spectral graph theory penalty; (3) Sliced Wasserstein Distance — optimal transport on the emotion graph. These penalize predictions deviating from the emotion topology. Evaluated on three architectures: AudioTransformer, Conformer, and DCGNN.

Result: Consistent improvements on SEED-IV (4-class) and SEED-V (5-class) datasets across all backbone architectures. Best-case: +5.42% accuracy improvement and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications.

Conclusion: The graph-regularized framework effectively raises the upper bound of performance achievable with standard EEG emotion recognition approaches by leveraging psychological structure, and is architecture-agnostic.

Abstract: EEG-based emotion recognition is critical for mental health monitoring and affective brain-computer interfaces, yet existing deep learning approaches often treat emotion classes as isolated labels, ignoring their psychological interdependencies. We propose a graph-regularized learning framework that conceptualizes emotions as nodes in a graph where edges encode proximity based on dimensional emotion theories. We adapt three complementary regularization strategies--Graph Label Smoothing (intuitive soft labeling), Commuting distance on graph via Graph Laplacian (spectral graph theory), and Sliced Wasserstein Distance (optimal transport on graph)--ordered by increasing computational complexity. These strategies penalize model predictions that deviate from the established emotion topology. Our framework is evaluated across three representative backbone architectures: AudioTransformer (pure transformer), Conformer (CNN-transformer hybrid), and DCGNN (causal graph neural network), demonstrating architecture-agnostic benefits. Experiments on SEED-IV (4 classes) and SEED-V (5 classes) datasets show consistent improvements: best case up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications. Ultimately, our framework help raise the upper bound of performance achievable with standard approaches. Code will be released.

</details>


### [140] [A law of robustness for two-layer neural networks with arbitrary weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07778)
*Yitzchak Shmalo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For two-layer unbounded-weight neural networks with piecewise-linear activations, the paper proves that fitting noisy labels below the noise floor forces the Lipschitz constant to be at least of order √(n/m) (up to a log factor), confirming a conjecture of Bubeck, Li, and Nagaraj. The proof uses a novel function-space covering argument and a rigidity lemma showing kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel generically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work by Bubeck and Sellke proved a universal Lipschitz lower bound for Lipschitz-parameterized classes, but required a polynomial bound on parameters — an assumption that is genuinely necessary at depth three. The two-layer unbounded-weight case, however, was conjectured to obey the same √(n/m) law without parameter bounds. Proving this requires a fundamentally different approach since standard parameter-space covering arguments fail when weights are unbounded.

Method: The authors replace parameter-space covering with a function-space covering strategy. The central deterministic ingredient is a "rigidity lemma": on the unit ball (and on the sphere for d≥3), the coefficient of each canonical kink in a two-layer piecewise-linear function is controlled by the function's Lipschitz constant, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. This rigidity fails at d=2, where an explicit counterexample with O(1) Lipschitz constant at width 2n matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint. The result also extends to a "realized-kink-count" version where the bound depends on the number of distinct kink hyperplanes actually realized, not the number of redundant hidden units.

Result: For data drawn uniformly from the sphere S^{d-1} (d≥3) or from N(0, I_d/d), with labels in [-1,1] and noise level σ²>0, any width-m two-layer network (with arbitrary real weights, biases, and affine skip connection) that fits the data ε below the noise floor must satisfy Lip(f) ≥ c ε √(n / (m̄ log(C m̄ n d/ε))), where m̄ = (K-1)m+1 and K is the number of linear pieces of the activation. A realized-kink-count version holds: any realized two-layer piecewise-linear function with k(f) ≤ n distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the same bound with m̄ replaced by k(f)+1.

Conclusion: The conjectured √(n/m) law holds universally for two-layer networks with continuous piecewise-linear activations, up to a logarithmic factor, with no restriction on weight magnitudes. The function-space covering technique and rigidity lemma provide a new lens for understanding generalization in overparameterized networks. The d=2 counterexample and construction at width 2n show the result is tight at the overparameterized endpoint.

Abstract: Bubeck, Li and Nagaraj conjectured that, for generic data, any two-layer neural network with $m$ neurons that fits $n$ noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant at least of order $\sqrt{n/m}$, with no restriction on the size of the weights. Bubeck and Sellke proved a universal version of this law for Lipschitz-parameterized classes, but under a polynomial bound on the parameters; at depth three that boundedness hypothesis is genuinely necessary. The two-layer unbounded-weight case requires a different argument. We prove the conjectured law, up to one logarithmic factor, for every continuous piecewise-linear activation, in particular for ReLU networks. For data drawn uniformly from $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$, $d\ge3$, or from $N(0,I_d/d)$, labels in $[-1,1]$ with noise level $σ^2>0$, and any width-$m$ two-layer network with arbitrary real weights, biases and affine skip connection, fitting the data $\varepsilon$ below the noise floor forces $\mathrm{Lip}(f)\ge c\,\varepsilon\sqrt{n/(\bar m\log(C\bar m nd/\varepsilon))}$, $\bar m=(K-1)m+1$, with high probability. A realized-kink-count version holds on the same event: every realized two-layer piecewise-linear function with $k(f)\le n$ distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the bound with $\bar m$ replaced by $k(f)+1$, irrespective of how many redundant hidden units parameterize it. The proof replaces parameter-space covering, impossible for unbounded weights, by a function-space covering. The central deterministic ingredient is a rigidity lemma: on $B_2$, and on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ for $d\ge3$, the coefficient of each canonical kink is controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. Rigidity genuinely fails at $d=2$, and an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with $O(1)$ Lipschitz constant at width $2n$ matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint.

</details>


### [141] [Predicting Pseudo-nitzschia harmful algal blooms along the Portuguese Coast using satellite-derived predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07834)
*Ayman Bnoussaad,El Khalil Cherif,Ligia Pinto,Ramiro Neves,Alexandra D. Silva,Alexandre Bernardino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine-learning framework using satellite-derived predictors was developed to forecast harmful algal blooms (HABs) of Pseudo-nitzschia along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Ensemble tree-based methods (Random Forest, Extra Trees) achieved moderate predictive skill (AUC up to 0.77) under stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation mimicking real-world forecasting. Seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions were the dominant predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. There is a need for operationally relevant early-warning systems that rely exclusively on satellite-derived predictors under realistic forecasting constraints, without in-situ measurements at prediction time.

Method: A decade-long dataset (2013–2023, 1,440 observations, >1,000 satellite-based predictors) was used for two production zones (L1–L2). Predictors included SST, upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types. Sampling locations were partitioned using a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy simultaneously withheld entire years and spatial clusters to prevent leakage. Ensemble tree-based classifiers (Random Forest, Extra Trees) were evaluated with environmental-only and environment-plus-biological feature configurations.

Result: HAB occurrence was moderately predictable. Random Forest achieved AUC 0.74±0.05 with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached AUC 0.77±0.06 with biological variables added. Feature importance analyses showed seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions, with biological indicators refining bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts. The approach validates that exclusively satellite-derived predictors under realistic cross-validation can yield useful predictive performance for coastal HAB monitoring.

Abstract: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Here we develop and evaluate a spatio-temporal machine-learning framework to predict harmful algal bloom (HAB) occurrence using exclusively satellite-derived predictors under realistic forecasting constraints. We characterised environmental and biological variability across shellfish production zones (L1-L9) using 5,882 observations, providing system-wide context. Predictive models were developed for zones L1-L2, a hotspot for Pseudo-nitzschia and domoic acid events, using a decade-long dataset (2013-2023; 1,440 observations; more than 1,000 satellite-based predictors including sea surface temperature, an upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types). Sampling locations were partitioned into ecologically meaningful sub-regions using a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy that simultaneously withholds entire years and spatial clusters prevents leakage and closely mimics real-world forecasting conditions. HAB occurrence proved moderately predictable across model classes and feature configurations. Ensemble tree-based methods achieved the strongest discrimination: Random Forest reached 0.74 +/- 0.05 with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached 0.77 +/- 0.06 with biological variables added. Feature-importance analyses revealed that seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions, while biological indicators refine bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods. The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts.

</details>


### [142] [Explaining Near-Zero Hessian Eigenvalues Through Approximate Symmetries in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07845)
*Marcel Kühn,Bernd Rosenow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Hessian's bulk of near-zero eigenvalues arises from weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes corresponding to broken continuous symmetries in network parametrization. In deep linear networks, exact symmetries yield zero modes; ReLU nonlinearities weakly break them, and the bulk spectrum lies almost entirely within the symmetry subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While explanations exist for the largest Hessian eigenvalues in neural network training loss, the origin of the vast number of vanishingly small eigenvalues (the "bulk") remains unexplained.

Method: Analytically construct exact zero-mode eigenvectors from continuous symmetries in deep linear networks. Introduce ReLU as a weak explicit symmetry-breaking perturbation. Resolve Hessian spectrum at the eigenvector level, decomposing directions into symmetry-subspace and orthogonal components. Validate on a two-layer ReLU student-teacher model, a CIFAR-10 trained network, and a convolutional architecture.

Result: High-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace; the Hessian bulk lies almost entirely within it. The diagnostic generalizes beyond fully connected layers to convolutional networks.

Conclusion: The Hessian bulk originates from weakly broken continuous symmetries of network parametrization, linking near-zero modes to pseudo-Goldstone modes and clarifying a previously elusive aspect of loss-landscape geometry.

Abstract: The Hessian of the training loss governs the local geometry of the loss landscape, yet despite existing explanations for its largest eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains elusive. We argue that the bulk consists of the weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. In deep linear networks these symmetries are exact: they generate flat directions and hence exact zero modes, whose eigenvectors we construct explicitly. Introducing a ReLU nonlinearity as a perturbation, we show that it breaks these symmetries weakly and explicitly. Resolving the spectrum at the level of eigenvectors, we find that the high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk lies almost entirely within it. We demonstrate the mechanism in a two-layer ReLU student--teacher model and in a network trained on CIFAR-10. A convolutional example demonstrates that the same diagnostic extends beyond fully connected layers. Together, these results link the Hessian bulk to weakly broken symmetries and clarify the origin of near-zero modes.

</details>


### [143] [When Does Continual Learning Require Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07847)
*Anne Harrington,Nayan Saxena,Michael Murphy,Anastasia Borovykh,Zeyu Yun,Sridhar Kamath,Ara Eindra Kyi,Trevor Darrell,Jitendra Malik,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reframes continual learning for LLMs as a problem of adapting to environmental change along spatial (new domains) and temporal (data drift) axes, then introduces a unified evaluation protocol comparing prompt-based, supervised, RL, and compression methods, finding that no single approach excels across all change patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current continual learning research for LLMs focuses narrowly on context management and forgetting mitigation. The authors argue this is incomplete: continual learning should be about increasing model competence as the world changes, encompassing realistic scenarios where new domains arrive over time, facts become outdated, and agentic interactions accumulate state.

Method: The authors disentangle environmental change into two axes: space (new domains) and time (data drift under fixed tasks). They recast standard LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and propose a mechanism-agnostic evaluation protocol that compares four method families: (1) prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), (2) supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), (3) reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and (4) context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT).

Result: Prompt-based methods fit quickly but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge stably but fail to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially improving new-task learning. Online RL adapts best to knowledge updates but is sensitive to noisy rewards. No single method handles all change patterns effectively.

Conclusion: Continual learning is not a single capability; different environmental change patterns require fundamentally different update behaviors. The key design question is determining when adaptation must be learned inside model weights versus achieved through external scaffolding. Understanding method-specific strengths and weaknesses should guide future system design.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly capable, the next question is how can we enable models to continually learn? Today, the field largely frames this as a problem of context management and mitigating forgetting. We argue this framing is incomplete: continual learning is fundamentally about increasing model competence as the world changes. We disentangle this change along two axes -- space, where the model encounters new domains, and time, where the underlying data drifts under a fixed task. This framing lets us study continual learning under realistic conditions: new domains arrive over time, facts drift past their training cutoff, and agentic interactions accumulate state across episodes. To evaluate methods under this setting, we recast widely used LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduce a single mechanism-agnostic protocol that compares prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT). Prompt-based methods fit each new stage quickly but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge stably but struggle to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially improving the ability to learn new tasks. Online reinforcement learning adapts most effectively to knowledge updates but remains sensitive to noisy reward signals. Overall, our results suggest that continual learning is not a single capability: different patterns of environmental change require fundamentally different update behaviors, determining when adaptation must be learned inside model weights and when it can be achieved through external scaffolding. We hope that understanding where each method succeeds and fails will guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

</details>


### [144] [NFTR: From Provable Mode-Averaging to Geodesic Subgoal Selection in Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07855)
*Erdemt Bao,Xing Lei,Jun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NFTR proposes two improvements to offline goal-conditioned RL: (1) replacing Gaussian subgoal policies with Conditional Normalizing Flows to avoid mode collapse, and (2) a triangle-slack reweighting mechanism that downweights subgoals with excessive detour costs, provably mitigating optimistic bias while preserving monotonic improvement guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HIQL selects subgoals via value-function advantages but suffers from two coupled failures: optimistic bias (mistaking lucky outcomes for skill) and mode collapse (collapsing a multi-modal subgoal distribution into a single Gaussian mean that often lands in unreachable areas). These failures degrade subgoal quality in offline goal-conditioned RL.

Method: NFTR introduces two components: (1) a Conditional Normalizing Flow policy that replaces the Gaussian, identified via closed-form mode-averaging analysis as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection; (2) a triangle-slack score based on the architectural triangle inequality that multiplicatively corrects AWR weights, downweighting subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. The RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition.

Result: Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The combined approach provably avoids Gaussian collapse and remains stable under stochastic dynamics.

Conclusion: NFTR provides a theoretically grounded solution to HIQL's failure modes, enabling robust subgoal selection in offline goal-conditioned RL through normalizing flow policies and triangle-slack reweighting, with formal guarantees on avoiding mode collapse and maintaining stability.

Abstract: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL), an offline goal-conditioned RL method, selects subgoals by value-function advantages alone. This rule has two coupled failure modes. Optimistic bias treats lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful choices, and mode collapse reduces a multi-modal subgoal distribution to a single Gaussian mean that often falls in unreachable regions. We propose NFTR (Normalizing Flows subgoal policies with Triangle-slack Reweighting). A conditional Normalizing Flow replaces the Gaussian policy, and a closed-form mode-averaging result identifies NFs as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. A triangle slack score, built on the architectural triangle inequality without relying on distance accuracy, multiplicatively corrects the AWR weight to downweight subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition. Together, these two ingredients yield subgoal selection that provably avoids the Gaussian collapse described above and remains stable under stochastic dynamics. GitHub page: https://github.com/erdemtbao/NFTR

</details>


### [145] [Physics-Informed Machine Learning Under Small-Data Constraints: Lessons from Abrasive Waterjet Milling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07863)
*Sarah Grewe,Jörg Frochte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: On a small abrasive waterjet milling dataset (Inconel 718, n=155), the paper demonstrates that data curation, robust evaluation, and physics integration strategy matter more than algorithm choice. Key findings: treating curation as a modelling hypothesis, instability of single-split validation, competitiveness of GP residual learning with a physics baseline, and calibrated GP uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In physically dominated machining processes, datasets are small, expensive, and material-specific. In this low-data regime, factors like data cleaning/curation, experimental design, and how physics is integrated into models can be as important as the learning algorithm itself — yet these methodological aspects are often underexamined.

Method: Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset (n=155, Inconel 718), the authors: (1) explicitly separate physics-based data cleaning from statistical curation, treating the latter as competing modelling hypotheses; (2) compare model rankings under single hold-out vs. 10-fold cross-validation to assess ranking stability; (3) evaluate a spectrum of physics integration levels — from pure data-driven to residual learning on a compact physics baseline — across multiple model classes (GPs, tree-based models, SVR, gradient boosting); and (4) assess GP uncertainty calibration via empirical coverage vs. nominal confidence.

Result: Single-split hold-out rankings are unstable: the winner under one split drops from rank 1 to rank 7 under 10-fold CV, while GP variants dominate top ranks under robust evaluation. Residual learning on a physics baseline is competitive for GPs (lower variance, interpretable decomposition) but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian hyperparameter tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines (gradient boosting, SVR) but harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated (86% empirical coverage at nominal 90%).

Conclusion: For small, expensive process datasets, reliable model comparison requires explicit curation hypotheses, robust cross-validation (not single splits), and deliberate choices about how physics enters the model. The work is primarily methodological, offering practical guidance rather than advocating a single best model.

Abstract: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental datasets are small, expensive, and material-specific; in this regime, data curation, evaluation design, and the form of physics integration can matter as much as the learning algorithm. Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset ($n{=}155$, Inconel\,718), we make three methodological contributions. First, we separate physics-based data \emph{cleaning} from statistical \emph{curation} and treat the latter as competing modelling hypotheses rather than silent preprocessing. Second, we find that model rankings from a 15-point hold-out set can be unstable: the single-split winner drops from rank~1 to rank~7 under 10-fold cross-validation, while Gaussian Process (GP) variants occupy the top ranks. Third, we study a spectrum of physics integration levels and find that residual learning on a compact physics baseline is competitive for GP, yielding lower variance and an interpretable decomposition, but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian hyper parameter tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines such as gradient boosting and SVR, yet harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated ($86\%$ empirical coverage at nominal $90\%$). The resulting picture is methodological: for small, expensive process datasets, our results suggest that, in this setting, reliable model comparison benefits from explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful choices about how physics enters the model.

</details>


### [146] [Optimal Learning Rate Scaling Depends on Data in Deep Scalar Linear Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07884)
*Yedi Zhang,Peter E. Latham,Leena Chennuru Vankadara,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes gradient descent dynamics in deep scalar linear networks, showing that optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling is data-dependent rather than universal, and that under proper scaling, convergence rate becomes depth-independent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the conventional wisdom that depth-wise learning rate scaling rules are universal/architecture-dependent only, and to demonstrate that even in the simplest possible deep network (scalar linear), the optimal scaling depends on the training data itself.

Method: The authors derive exact time-course solutions for gradient descent in scalar linear networks of the form f(x) = ∏w_l·x for arbitrary depth L, analyze convergence rates analytically, and determine the optimal learning rate scaling that maximizes convergence speed. They also extend the analysis to networks with residual connections.

Result: The optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling is data-dependent — it depends on the data second moment. Data-agnostic scaling rules fail to generalize across depths. Under the data-dependent optimal scaling, the learning dynamics become independent of data and weakly depth-dependent, yielding a constant linear convergence rate for all depths, including infinite depth.

Conclusion: Even in the simplest possible deep learning model, data plays a crucial role in determining optimal hyperparameters like learning rate scaling. This suggests that purely architecture-based scaling rules may be fundamentally insufficient for practical deep learning.

Abstract: In this short note we consider the gradient descent dynamics of deep scalar linear networks, $f(x) = \prod_{l=1}^L w_l x$, which enjoy exact time-course solutions for any integer depth. We show that even in this minimal model, the optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling depends on data, whereas data-agnostic scaling rules fail to transfer across depths. Under the data-dependent optimal scaling, the learning dynamics is independent of data and weakly dependent on depth, resulting in a constant linear convergence rate across all depths including infinity. We further show similar data-dependent effects in deep scalar linear networks with residual connections.

</details>


### [147] [Distributed Sketching on Data Partitions for OLS Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07888)
*Luyuan Yang,Brayden Garner,Shayan Shafaei,Chao Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes distributed sketching for OLS regression, where sketches are applied to partitioned data subsets (rather than the entire dataset) and then averaged. It characterizes the exact excess loss under fixed design and shows performance is comparable to full-dataset sketching when subset covariances are similar.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on distributed sketching for OLS applies sketching to the entire dataset before distribution. This paper aims to further reduce computational cost by instead sketching on pre-partitioned subsets. The key question is whether this "sketch-after-partition" approach incurs significant degradation compared to "sketch-before-partition."

Method: The authors work under a fixed design setting and derive an exact characterization of the excess loss for the averaged OLS estimator constructed from sketched subsets. The analysis quantifies how the excess loss depends on the divergence among subset covariance matrices.

Result: The exact excess loss of the averaged estimator is characterized. The loss is shown to be comparable to the established loss for sketching on the whole dataset when the divergence among subset covariances is small, i.e., when data partitions are relatively homogeneous in covariance structure.

Conclusion: Sketching on partitioned subsets is a viable strategy for distributed OLS, achieving similar statistical performance to whole-dataset sketching while offering additional computational savings, provided that the covariances across subsets do not diverge substantially.

Abstract: This paper studies distributed sketching for ordinary least squares (OLS) regression, an approach that distributes small sketches of a large data set over multiple machines to separately construct OLS estimators and average them. Unlike prior studies that consider sketching on the whole data set, we consider sketching on partitioned subsets to further reduce computational cost. Under the fixed design setting, we characterize the exact excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator. Results show that this loss is comparable to the established loss for sketching on the whole data set when the divergence among subset covariances is small.

</details>


### [148] [Multimodal Unlearning Across Vision, Language, Video, and Audio: Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07907)
*Nobin Sarwar,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Zheyuan Liu,Vaidehi Patil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey proposing a unified, system-oriented taxonomy for multimodal unlearning—selectively removing sensitive, copyrighted, or unsafe cross-modal associations from vision, language, audio, and video foundation models—while balancing deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal foundation models (VLMs, DMs, LLMs, AFMs) inadvertently encode undesirable cross-modal associations from training data. Retraining is impractical, and targeted forgetting is difficult due to distributed shared representations. A systematic framework for multimodal unlearning is needed.

Method: The authors conduct a comprehensive survey, proposing a unified taxonomy that systematically compares multimodal unlearning approaches across model architectures and modalities (vision, language, audio, video). They analyze trade-offs along five axes: deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness.

Result: The survey provides a structured taxonomy enabling systematic comparison of multimodal unlearning methods, clarifies key trade-offs, identifies open problems, and offers practical deployment considerations. A curated repository accompanies the work.

Conclusion: Multimodal unlearning is an emerging, critical research area. The proposed taxonomy and identified open problems aim to guide future research and practical deployment, with the accompanying repository serving as a community resource.

Abstract: With the growing adoption of VLMs, DMs, LLMs, and AFMs, these multimodal foundation models can inadvertently encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal associations that originate from their training data. Retraining after deletion requests or policy updates is often impractical, and targeted forgetting remains difficult because knowledge is distributed across shared representations. Multimodal unlearning addresses this challenge by enabling selective removal across modalities while retaining overall utility. This survey offers a unified, system-oriented view of multimodal unlearning across vision, language, audio, and video, grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and open problems. Our taxonomy enables systematic comparison across model architectures and modalities, clarifying trade-offs among deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness. This survey highlights open problems and practical considerations to support future research and deployment of multimodal unlearning. We release a curated repository: https://smsnobin77.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Unlearning/

</details>


### [149] [Efficient Safety Alignment of Language Models via Latent Personality Traits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07918)
*Mohamed Amine Merzouk,Nolan Smyth,Damiano Fornasiere,Linh Le,David Williams-King,Adam Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LPA aligns LLMs using 66 psychometric personality statements instead of harmful prompts, achieving near-zero jailbreak attack success rates with minimal training cost and no utility degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety methods like LAT degrade utility, require large harmful-prompt datasets, and remain vulnerable. The authors seek a harm-agnostic, lightweight alternative that implicitly constrains the adversarial subspace.

Method: LPA replaces explicit harm refusal training with adversarial training on 66 harm-agnostic personality statements from psychometric literature (e.g., Big Five). It uses a min-max optimization: adversarially perturbing embeddings to stabilize personality-anchored representations, relying on the hypothesis that these share latent structure with harm avoidance.

Result: LPA achieves near-zero ASR on HarmBench against direct requests and five jailbreak methods (GCG, PAIR, TAP, AutoDAN, PAP), uses 75× fewer examples than LAT, trains in minutes on a single GPU, and maintains full performance on standard benchmarks (no utility loss).

Conclusion: Personality-anchored adversarial alignment is a highly efficient, robust safety paradigm that generalizes across diverse attacks without exposure to harmful training data, offering a promising direction for scalable LLM safety.

Abstract: Current safety methods for large language models are known to be vulnerable to adversarial attacks, motivating research into robust alternatives. Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) is among the most effective defenses, but can degrade utility and requires training on large datasets of harmful prompts. We introduce Latent Personality Alignment (LPA), which replaces explicit harm refusal with adversarial training on just 66 harm-agnostic statements drawn from psychometric personality literature. We hypothesize that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so adversarially stabilizing them implicitly constrains the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks. LPA achieves near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, despite never seeing harmful content during training and no loss of performance on standard benchmarks. Moreover, the training process is lightweight; the entire procedure completes in minutes on a single GPU and uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT. Extensive ablations demonstrate the robustness, efficiency, and generalization of our method.

</details>


### [150] [path_boost: A Python Package for Interpretable Graph-Level Prediction using Path-Based Gradient Boosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07935)
*Claudio Meggio,Johan Pensar,Riccardo De Bin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PathBoost is a Python package implementing a gradient boosting algorithm that discovers interpretable, predictive labeled paths in graph-structured data. It produces additive models revealing which substructures drive predictions, avoiding exhaustive path enumeration through iterative selection. Compatible with scikit-learn, it supports regression/classification and is demonstrated on molecular property prediction tasks, benchmarking competitively against GNNs and graph kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks (GNNs) achieve strong predictive performance on graph-structured data but are notoriously difficult to interpret. There is a need for supervised learning methods that maintain competitive accuracy while explicitly revealing which substructures (paths) in a graph drive predictions, without the computational burden of exhaustively enumerating all possible paths.

Method: PathBoost is a gradient boosting algorithm that iteratively selects and extends labeled paths within graphs based on their predictive power. It builds an additive prediction model over path-based features by combining weak learners (each tied to a specific path) into a strong ensemble via boosting. The algorithm avoids exhaustive path enumeration through greedy, predictive-power-driven search. The package includes automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, custom base learner/selector support, and built-in variable importance computation, all within a scikit-learn-compatible interface.

Result: PathBoost is demonstrated on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds (atoms as nodes, bonds as edges) and benchmarked against an established GNN and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets, showing competitive performance while providing interpretable path-based explanations.

Conclusion: PathBoost offers a viable, interpretable alternative to GNNs for graph-structured supervised learning. Its boosting-based path discovery yields transparent additive models that explicitly reveal predictive substructures, with practical utility demonstrated in cheminformatics. The package is openly available on PyPI and GitHub.

Abstract: We present path_boost, a Python package for interpretable supervised learning on graph-structured input data. The package implements PathBoost, a gradient boosting algorithm that automatically discovers predictive labeled paths within graphs during the learning process. Unlike graph neural networks, which are generally difficult to interpret, PathBoost produces an additive prediction model over path-based features that explicitly reveals which substructures drive predictions. To avoid an exhaustive enumeration of all possible paths, the algorithm iteratively selects and extends paths during learning based on their predictive power, using boosting to combine weak learners into a strong ensemble. The package supports both regression and binary classification. Key features include compatibility with scikit-learn workflows, support for custom base learners and selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, and built-in variable importance computation. We demonstrate PathBoost on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds, where atoms serve as nodes and bonds as edges, and further benchmark PathBoost against an established graph neural network and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets. The package is available on PyPI and GitHub under an open-source license.

</details>


### [151] [Evaluating the Generalizability of Foundation Models for Extreme Environmental Events: Case Study of California Wildfire PM2.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07951)
*Yongcan Huang,Li Jiang,Ze Yu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks six time series foundation model configurations against traditional baselines for forecasting extreme PM2.5 during wildfire smoke events. The BiLSTM consistently outperforms all foundation models—even LoRA-fine-tuned ones—on every metric, including hazardous-level exceedance F1 (0.63 vs. ≤0.54). Zero-shot models improve only modestly over persistence, and Chronos-2 exhibits severe tail instability. The findings challenge the assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate in environmental forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wildfire smoke events produce extreme PM2.5 concentrations posing severe public health risks, yet forecasting rare, hazardous-level spikes remains fundamentally challenging. While time series foundation models (TSFMs) perform well on general benchmarks, their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions—such as wildfire-driven pollution episodes—is poorly understood. A rigorous, systematic evaluation is needed to guide deployment decisions for air quality prediction during these high-stakes events.

Method: The authors construct a 12-year (2013–2025) hourly PM2.5 dataset covering 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California monitoring sites. They benchmark six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, Time-MoE; plus LoRA-fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained baselines (LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer) and naive persistence. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization to unseen fires. Metrics include MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour forecast horizons.

Result: A consistent hierarchy emerges: the BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE (5.16 μg/m³) and the highest exceedance F1 at all EPA thresholds, including the Hazardous band (>225.5 μg/m³), reaching 0.63 vs. at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve on persistence only modestly. Zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE tail instability (23.4 μg/m³, negative R²) due to sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves both adapted families and largely repairs Chronos-2's instability, yet no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric.

Conclusion: These findings challenge the prevailing assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting. For wildfire air quality prediction, simpler, task-specifically trained recurrent architectures (particularly BiLSTM) remain state-of-the-art. The results provide actionable deployment guidance: practitioners should not default to foundation models for extreme-event forecasting without rigorous domain-specific benchmarking.

Abstract: Wildfire smoke events produce extreme PM$_{2.5}$ concentrations that pose severe public health risks, yet forecasting rare, hazardous-level spikes remains a fundamental challenge. Time series foundation models (TSFMs), pretrained models offering zero-shot inference and efficient adaptation, perform strongly on general benchmarks, but their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions is poorly understood. We present the first systematic benchmark comparing six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, and Time-MoE, plus LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained baselines (LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer) and naive persistence on a 12-year (2013--2025) hourly PM$_{2.5}$ dataset covering 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California monitoring sites. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization to unseen fires, using MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour horizons. Results reveal a consistent hierarchy. The BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE ($5.16\,μg/m^3$) and the highest exceedance F1 at every threshold, including the Hazardous band ($>225.5\,μg/m^3$), reaching 0.63 versus at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve on persistence only modestly, and zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE tail instability ($23.4\,μg/m^3$, negative $R^2$) from sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves both adapted families and largely repairs this instability, yet no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric. These findings challenge the assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting and provide actionable deployment guidance for wildfire air quality prediction.

</details>


### [152] [Linear Attention Architectures: Mechanisms, Trade-offs, and Cross-Layer Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07953)
*Tommaso Cerruti,Tim Rieder,George Rowlands,Lingfeng Jin,Imanol Schlag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative study of softmax attention and four recurrent linear-attention variants (DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, Gated DeltaNet-2) using a unified recurrent-memory notation. Experiments at 350M–3B scale show Kimi Delta Attention + Muon optimizer achieves lowest validation loss, pure Gated DeltaNet + AdamW yields highest throughput, and a proposed Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) mechanism modestly improves DeltaNet-style models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Softmax attention incurs quadratic cost in sequence length, limiting long-context training and inference. While recurrent linear-attention architectures promise linear complexity, the design space is fragmented and lacks systematic comparison under a common framework. The authors aim to unify and benchmark recent DeltaNet-family architectures to clarify trade-offs in expressivity, throughput, and implementation complexity.

Method: The authors express DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2 in a shared recurrent-memory notation, highlighting differences in memory decay, erase/write control, and expressivity. They conduct controlled experiments at 350M parameters (15B training tokens), with additional runs at 1.3B and 3B. Comparisons span optimizer choice (AdamW vs. Muon), pure vs. hybrid stacks, sequence-length runtime scaling, and downstream evaluations. They also propose and test cross-layer routing mechanisms, including a delta-rule error forwarding scheme and Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR), which routes the write value into the next layer's hidden stream.

Result: At 350M/15B scale: Kimi Delta Attention with Muon achieves lowest final validation loss; pure Gated DeltaNet with AdamW yields highest normalized training throughput; hybrid stacks improve loss at throughput cost; Muon consistently outperforms AdamW in matched settings. The delta-rule write-error routing fails to improve baselines, but CLVR yields modest validation loss reductions for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet. Larger-scale DeltaNet runs (1.3B, 3B) and downstream evals are reported but not highlighted in the abstract. No inference-speed benchmark is included.

Conclusion: The study provides a systematic empirical comparison of recent recurrent linear-attention architectures, identifying Pareto frontiers between loss quality and training throughput. Muon is recommended over AdamW for final loss; hybrid attention-linear stacks offer a tunable quality–throughput trade-off. Cross-Layer Value Routing offers a lightweight architectural improvement for DeltaNet-family models, while naive delta-rule error forwarding is ineffective.

Abstract: Self-attention lets each token retrieve information from the full context, but its quadratic cost in sequence length limits training and inference at long context. This paper presents a comparative study of softmax attention and four recent recurrent linear-attention architectures: DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2. We express these mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation, making explicit how they differ in expressivity, memory decay, erase and write control, training throughput, and implementation complexity. Our experiments center on 350M-parameter models trained for 15B tokens, and include optimizer and learning-rate comparisons, hybrid-versus-pure stack comparisons, sequence-length runtime measurements, larger DeltaNet runs at 1.3B and 3B parameters, and a small set of downstream evaluations. The reported speed results measure training throughput and iteration time; we do not provide an empirical inference-speed benchmark. Within the reported 350M-parameter, 15B-token sweep, Kimi Delta Attention with Muon reaches the lowest final validation loss, a pure Gated DeltaNet stack trained with AdamW has the highest normalized training throughput, hybrid stacks generally improve loss at a throughput cost, and Muon consistently lowers final validation loss relative to AdamW in the matched architecture settings we evaluate. We introduce and evaluate lightweight cross-layer routing mechanisms for DeltaNet-style memories. The most natural DeltaNet-inspired formulation, forwarding a lower layer's delta-rule write error into the next layer's value target, does not improve over matched baselines. Routing into the aligned hidden stream and forwarding the write value instead yields a modest improvement in the matched runs we report: Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) lowers final validation loss for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

</details>


### [153] [KronQ: LLM Quantization via Kronecker-Factored Hessian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07964)
*Donghyun Lee,Yuhang Li,Ruokai Yin,Priyadarshini Panda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KronQ is a post-training quantization framework that incorporates gradient covariance (via Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation) into the quantization objective, challenging the GPTQ assumption of equal output-channel importance. It introduces bidirectional incoherence processing and a new sensitivity metric for mixed-precision allocation, achieving stable 2-bit quantization (7.93 perplexity) on LLaMA-3-70B where GPTQ and GPTAQ fail completely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing second-order PTQ methods like GPTQ assume all output channels contribute equally to layer-wise reconstruction error, using only input activation statistics. This paper challenges that assumption, arguing that gradient covariance provides crucial information about output-channel sensitivity that can substantially improve quantization quality, especially at very low bit-widths.

Method: KronQ operates on two levels: (1) Bidirectional incoherence processing — extends random rotation (previously applied only on the input side) to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output axes. (2) A new inter-layer sensitivity metric derived from traces of the gradient and activation Hessians, used to guide mixed-precision bit allocation across layers. Both innovations stem from the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, where quantization loss depends jointly on activation and gradient covariances.

Result: On 2-bit weight-only quantization of LLaMA-3-70B, GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate results (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), while KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity — demonstrating robust low-bit quantization at scale.

Conclusion: Incorporating gradient covariance into the PTQ pipeline via Kronecker-factored Hessian approximations yields significant improvements over activation-only approaches, enabling stable 2-bit quantization of large models where prior methods collapse.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a widely adopted technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) without retraining. Existing second-order PTQ methods, including GPTQ, construct quantization objectives exclusively from input activation statistics, effectively assuming that all output channels contribute equally to the layer-wise reconstruction objective. We propose KronQ, a PTQ framework that challenges this assumption by introducing the gradient covariance into the quantization pipeline. Under the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, the quantization loss depends jointly on both the activation and gradient covariances, and KronQ exploits this at two complementary levels. (1) KronQ introduces bidirectional incoherence processing, extending the existing input-side random rotation to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions. (2) KronQ derives a new sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation, driven by the gradient and activation Hessian traces. Notably, in the case of 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, while GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity.

</details>


### [154] [Provably Optimal Learning Algorithms for Assistance Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08012)
*Nivasini Ananthakrishnan,Mark Bedaywi,Michael I. Jordan,Stuart Russell,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games, where an informed human and an uninformed assistant interact over T timesteps. The authors define "assistance regret" and present decentralized algorithms achieving O(T^{3/4}) regret with a (1-1/e)-approximation factor, prove this factor is tight, and show a pseudo-decentralized variant using shared randomness that achieves the optimal O(T^{1/2}) rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assistance games model AI alignment problems where a human knows the true goal but an AI assistant must infer it from the human's actions. Prior work lacked provably efficient online learning algorithms for this repeated interaction setting. The authors aim to bridge this gap by developing algorithms with formal regret guarantees.

Method: The authors formalize repeated assistance games with a latent state observed only by the human. They introduce "assistance regret" as the gap relative to the optimal hindsight joint policy mapping states to action pairs. They develop decentralized no-regret algorithms where the human uses a contextual bandit approach over latent states and the assistant deploys any no-regret learner over the human's action history. For the lower bound, they reduce from the maximum coverage problem to prove (1-1/e)-approximation hardness. For the improved O(T^{1/2}) rate, they leverage a shared random string for coordination.

Result: The decentralized algorithms achieve (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret of O(T^{3/4}) with polynomial runtime. They prove that no polynomial-time algorithm can achieve better than (1-1/e) approximation unless P=NP. In the pseudo-decentralized setting with shared randomness, they achieve O(T^{1/2}) regret, which is optimal up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: This work establishes the first regret bounds for repeated assistance games, demonstrating that efficient decentralized learning is possible with a tight (1-1/e) approximation. The gap between the fully decentralized rate (O(T^{3/4})) and the pseudo-decentralized rate (O(T^{1/2})) highlights the cost of decentralization, and the hardness result shows this approximation factor is fundamental.

Abstract: This paper studies an online variant of the assistance games framework, where an informed agent and an uninformed agent repeatedly interact over $T$ timesteps to optimize a common reward function. While the informed agent (the human) observes a latent state of the world, the uninformed agent (the assistant) observes only the human's actions. We provide the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games. We introduce the notion of assistance regret: the gap between the cumulative utility of interactions and that of the optimal joint policies in hindsight, which map latent states to action pairs. We present decentralized algorithms for both the human and the assistant that achieve a $(1-1/e)$-approximate assistance regret rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{3/4})$, with runtime polynomial in the size of the action and state spaces. These algorithms are general; in particular, they accommodate any no-regret algorithm for the assistant. We prove that achieving a regret approximation factor better than $(1-1/e)$ is computationally intractable. Furthermore, we demonstrate how these generic no-regret algorithms can be tailored to a pseudo-decentralized setting -- using a shared random string -- to achieve a rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/2})$, optimal up to logarithmic factors.

</details>


### [155] [Collate: Collaborative Neural Network Learning for Latency-Critical Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08013)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Collate is a federated learning framework that collaboratively trains heterogeneous models to satisfy diverse latency constraints across edge systems, using dynamic zeroizing-recovering for architecture adaptation and proto-corrected aggregation, achieving up to 3.09% accuracy gains with negligible overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FL optimizations target training efficiency but neglect inference latency, particularly under heterogeneous device constraints in real-time edge systems. Different edge devices have varying latency budgets, yet standard FL produces uniform models that cannot simultaneously satisfy these constraints while maintaining high accuracy.

Method: Two core techniques: (1) Dynamic zeroizing-recovering — adaptively prunes (zeroizes) and restores (recovers) parts of each local model to meet per-device latency constraints while preserving accuracy. (2) Proto-corrected federated aggregation — aggregates heterogeneous local models (differing architectures/sizes) into a unified training process, using prototype-based corrections to align representations across heterogeneous models, ensuring a single training run serves all latency constraints.

Result: Compared to state-of-the-art methods under latency constraints: extended models achieve +1.96% average accuracy; shrunk models achieve +3.09% average accuracy. Near-zero additional training overhead.

Conclusion: Collate effectively bridges the gap between FL training and latency-constrained edge inference by enabling collaborative learning of heterogeneous models in a single training process, achieving superior accuracy-latency trade-offs without extra training cost.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) empowers multiple clients to collaboratively learn a model, enlarging the training data of each client for high accuracy while protecting data privacy. However, when deploying FL in real-time edge systems, the heterogeneity of devices among systems has a severe impact on the performance of the inferred model. Existing optimizations on FL focus on improving the training efficiency but fail to speed up inference, especially when there is a latency constraint. In this work, we propose Collate, a novel training framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet the latency constraints of multiple edge systems simultaneously. We design a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint. A proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme is also introduced to aggregate all heterogeneous local models, satisfying the latency constraint of different systems with only one training process and maintaining high accuracy. Extensive experiments indicate that, compared to state-of-the-art methods and under a latency constraint, our extended models can improve the accuracy by 1.96% on average, and our shrunk models can also obtain a 3.09% accuracy improvement on average, with almost no extra training overhead. The related codes and data will be available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/Collate

</details>


### [156] [PGD-NO: A Neural Operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition for 3D Million-scale Physics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08025)
*Weiheng Zhong,Jing Bi,Victor Oancea,Hadi Meidani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGD-NO introduces a neural operator that decouples geometry encoding into a precomputation phase via iterative geometry decomposition, enabling linear memory scalability and training on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes — a scale prohibitive for existing architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural PDE solvers suffer from high memory consumption and a single-node VRAM bottleneck that strictly limits the maximum processable mesh resolution, preventing their application to large-scale, high-fidelity industrial simulations.

Method: PGD-NO employs Precomputed Geometry Decomposition: an iterative algorithm extracts geometry tokens in a deterministic offline phase, decoupling geometric feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture achieves linear memory scalability and uses attention mechanisms for both prediction and intrinsic interpretability.

Result: PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy on diverse industrial benchmarks and successfully handles meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion.

Conclusion: By overcoming mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO provides a robust, efficient solution for next-generation large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

Abstract: While neural PDE solvers have demonstrated significant potential for accelerating engineering simulations, existing architectures remain constrained by high memory consumption and the single node bottleneck, where the maximum processable mesh resolution is strictly limited by the VRAM of a single compute unit. To address these challenges, we propose PGD-NO, a neural operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition, that relocates the computational overhead of geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase. By utilizing an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, our model decouples feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture enables linear memory scalability, allowing high fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, a scale where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion. PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms. By effectively overcoming traditional mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO offers a robust and efficient solution for the next generation of large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

</details>


### [157] [Rethinking Small VLM Quantization: From Component-Wise Analysis to Hardware-Aware Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08029)
*Hyeju Shin,Chorwon Kim,Ryangsoo Kim,Hark Yoo,Jaein Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic evaluation of component-wise quantization in small vision-language models (<3B params) on Jetson edge hardware, revealing five key findings about quantization sensitivity, encoder-kernel-hardware interactions, dequantization overhead, error additivity, and energy efficiency across platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid emergence of sub-3B vision-language models has enabled on-device multimodal AI, but a detailed, component-level understanding of how quantization affects each architectural submodule (vision encoder, projector, LLM backbone) remains lacking, hindering optimal deployment on edge hardware.

Method: Systematic evaluation framework testing five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX and AGX platforms. The framework decouples the vision encoder, projector, and LLM backbone to isolate component-wise quantization effects, covering both INT8 and INT4 precision levels with MoE and dense backbones.

Result: (1) MoE backbones tolerate INT4 quantization far better than dense ones, indicating structural paradigm governs sensitivity more than model scale. (2) SigLIP vision encoders exhibit disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere GPUs due to a specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not an inherent SigLIP inefficiency. (3) INT4 LLM quantization reduces VRAM but slows token generation from dequantization overhead. (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive except along the modality-alignment projector path, where architecture matters. (5) Intelligence-per-joule varies substantially between Orin NX and AGX due to memory bandwidth differences.

Conclusion: Optimal deployment of small VLMs on edge hardware requires component-aware quantization strategies rather than uniform treatment. Key practical insights include preferring MoE backbones for INT4, avoiding INT8 for SigLIP on Jetson Ampere, and accounting for platform-specific memory bandwidth when optimizing for energy efficiency.

Abstract: The emergence of vision language models with fewer than 3 billion parameters has accelerated the implementation of on-device multimodal intelligence. However, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization remains a bottleneck for optimal deployment. This paper presents a systematic evaluation framework for empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on the Jetson Orin NX and AGX.
