By separating the vision encoder, projector, and large language model backbone yields the following results: (1) Quantization sensitivity is governed by the structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) rather than scale alone, with MoE backbones mitigating INT4 noise where dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere--a deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not a SigLIP flaw; (3) Although INT4 quantization of LLMs greatly reduces VRAM consumption, it also causes slower token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) The intelligence-per-joule profile varies significantly across platforms owing to memory bandwidth constraints.

</details>


### [158] [What to Keep, What to Forget: A Rate--Distortion View of Memory Compaction in LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08032)
*Ashwin Gerard Colaco,Nada Lahjouji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper unifies four disparate research areas (KV cache eviction, prompt pruning, architectural state bounding, and agent memory consolidation) under a single rate-distortion framework for context compaction. It proposes a taxonomy, identifies shared failure modes (premature discarding based on attention magnitude/recency), highlights the lack of multi-layer compaction benchmarks, and offers design principles and a benchmark proposal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory in LLM-based systems comes at a growing computational cost across multiple layers of the stack — KV caches, prompts, recurrent states, and agent session memory. Four separate communities work on compacting these in isolation, but the authors argue this fragmentation obscures a deeper unity: all are making the same rate-distortion tradeoff about what context-derived information to retain under a resource budget to preserve task utility.

Method: The paper formulates a unified compaction objective and derives a layer-agnostic lower bound. It constructs a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies compaction methods uniformly across KV cache management, prompt compression, architectural bottlenecks, and agent memory. It also demonstrates mechanism transfer between previously disconnected layers (e.g., from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory) and proposes a benchmark and small reference experiment exposing the multi-layer compaction problem.

Result: Two consistent patterns emerge: (1) at every layer, the retention signal is attention magnitude or recency, which fails uniformly by discarding information before the query is known, irreversibly; (2) while single-turn long-context compression is measured carefully, the repeated compaction agents perform across turns is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds a budget axis across all layers simultaneously.

Conclusion: The fragmentation of compaction research across four communities has masked shared structure and shared failure modes. The paper calls for a holistic rate-distortion lens, multi-layer compaction benchmarks, and compaction-aware design principles. It maps open problems including undo mechanisms, query-adaptive retention, and cross-layer budget allocation.

Abstract: Large language models, and the agents built on them, spend an ever-growing share of their compute and memory on remembering: caching attention keys and values, carrying long prompts, maintaining recurrent state, and storing what happened in previous turns and sessions. Because none of this memory is free, four largely separate research communities have each learned to compact it. They evict or quantize the KV cache, prune or distill prompts, bound architectural state, and consolidate agent memory. We argue that these are instances of one problem: a rate--distortion decision about what context-derived information to retain versus discard, at what fidelity, under a resource budget, so as to preserve downstream task utility. We make this lens precise with a single compaction objective and a layer-agnostic lower bound, use it to build a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies methods from across the stack uniformly, and use it to transfer mechanisms between layers that have never been connected, from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory. Two patterns hold across the survey. At every layer the signal that decides what to keep is attention magnitude or recency, and it fails in the same way everywhere, by discarding, before the query is known and with no way to undo it, information the query later needs. And while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once. We turn both observations into a benchmark proposal, a small reference experiment, and a set of compaction-aware design principles, and we map the open problems.

</details>


### [159] [An exact information theory of generalization phase transitions in Bayesian diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08041)
*Henry Hunt,Mason Kamb,Surya Ganguli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces BIRD models, analytically tractable diffusion models with restricted information per pixel, revealing an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization. The key finding: generation proceeds near the edge of memorization, and information restriction is fundamental to circumventing the curse of dimensionality in diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the fundamental mystery of how diffusion models learn complex high-dimensional distributions from finite training data without simply memorizing, and to understand what mechanism enables generalization despite the curse of dimensionality.

Method: The authors introduce Bayesian Information Restricted Diffusion (BIRD) models, where each pixel observes only restricted information about noisy data. The reverse process uses Bayesian posterior inference to determine which training sample produced the current restricted observation. They derive an information-theoretic phase boundary (memorization when mutual information between restricted observations and training data exceeds log N) and validate it experimentally across architectures (UNets, DiTs) and datasets.

Result: Spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models early in training. An information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization is identified in the joint space of training data amount, reverse process time, and information restriction level. Experiments confirm the predicted transition location. Both BIRD and early-training diffusion models track this phase boundary during generation.

Conclusion: Information restriction plays a fundamental role in enabling generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality. Diffusion models operate near the memorization-generalization phase boundary by progressively restricting information over time.

Abstract: How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [160] [Who Analyses the Analyser? Self-Validating LLM Hazard Analysis with Constitutional Meta-STPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08054)
*Samuel Tetteh,Udip Shrestha,Joshua R. Waite,Cody Fleming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents Constitutional Meta-STPA, a tool that applies STPA safety analysis recursively to LLM-assisted safety analysis tools themselves. It derives a constitution of 29 principles from the analysis chain, demonstrates that frontier LLMs can self-derive these principles, and shows that recovery quality depends on model capability rather than constitutional design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to draft safety analysis artifacts (losses, hazards, UCAs, constraints) in rigorous processes like STPA, but the LLM-assisted analysis tools themselves remain unanalyzed — creating a critical blind spot. These tools are safety-relevant systems that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail. The paper addresses the neglected question: "who analyses the analyser?"

Method: The authors build an LLM-assisted STPA tool with a closed-loop architecture. The tool performs a meta-STPA (STPA applied to the class of AI-assisted safety tools), then derives its governance constitution from the resulting loss→hazard→UCA→constraint chain rather than merely asserting principles. This yields 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles (29 total), each bound to a code enforcement point. They formalize a constitution-marginal coverage operator with a soundness lemma isolating coverage from model and scanner. They evaluate self-derivation capability by testing whether frontier models (claude-opus-4.8 + claude-sonnet-4) can recover the canonical principles from the tool's own design, comparing against a weaker model pair and cross-validating with an independently authored tool.

Result: The frontier ensemble recovers 18/21 canonical Tool Principles and all 8/8 governance principles. A weaker model pair recovers only 12/21 and 3/8, demonstrating that the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited. The same 8/8 meta-safety principles re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool, suggesting robustness of the derived constitution.

Conclusion: STPA can and should be reflexively applied to LLM-assisted safety analysis tools themselves. A derived constitution (rather than an asserted one) provides traceable governance with code-level enforcement points. The quality of self-derivation is bounded by model capability, not by the constitutional framework, and the principles generalize across independently authored tools.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted to draft the artifacts of safety analysis such as, losses, hazards, Unsafe Control Actions (UCAs), and safety constraints, inside rigorous processes such as Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA). Yet a blind spot runs through this fast-growing literature: every system gets analysed except the LLM-assisted tool doing the analysing, which is itself a safety-relevant system that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail from prompt to artifact. We take seriously the question the field has skipped -- {who analyses the analyser?} and answer it by turning STPA on the tool itself. We present \{Constitutional Meta-STPA}, an LLM-assisted STPA tool built around a closed loop: the tool runs a {meta-STPA} of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and {derives} rather than asserts, its governance constitution from the resulting loss$\to$hazard$\to$UCA$\to$constraint chain, yielding a published constitution of $21$ Tool Principles and $8$ Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to a code enforcement point. We formalise the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set $P$ ($|P|{=}29$) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner, and report four findings. {(i)~Self-derivation:} a frontier ensemble ({claude-opus-4.8}${+}${claude-sonnet-4}) recovers $18/21$ canonical and all $8/8$ governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers $12/21$ and $3/8$, so the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited, and the same $8/8$ re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

</details>


### [161] [Reinforcing the Generation Order of Multimodal Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08056)
*Yidong Ouyang,Zhe Wang,Sourav Bhabesh,Dmitriy Bespalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a learnable control module trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine optimal token generation order in Diffusion Language Models, demonstrating improvements in both text-to-image alignment (+4.08% on GenEval) and multimodal understanding (+4.85% on VLMEvalKit).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlike structured language tasks (e.g., Sudoku puzzles) where model logits can determine optimal generation order, the authors establish that logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. This gap motivates a learned approach to generation order optimization.

Method: The authors introduce a learnable control module that determines adaptive token generation ordering. This module is trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning-based approach. The control block operates on top of Diffusion Language Models (DLMs).

Result: The proposed method achieves a 4.08% relative improvement on GenEval (object-focused text-to-image alignment benchmark) and a 4.85% relative improvement on VLMEvalKit (multimodal understanding benchmark). It notably enhances fine-grained spatial relationship capture in generated images and strengthens multimodal reasoning performance.

Conclusion: Learning adaptive generation ordering via GRPO-trained control modules is broadly effective for DLMs, yielding consistent gains across both text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding tasks, beyond the previously demonstrated benefits in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have recently achieved substantial progress in natural language generation tasks. Recent research demonstrates that adaptive token generation ordering can significantly improve performance in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis applications. In this work, we investigate the optimization of generation order for both text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. We first establish that, unlike structured problems in language generation such as Sudoku puzzles, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding. To address this challenge, we introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order. Our results demonstrate that learning this control block substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding in DLMs. In particular, it enhances the model's ability to capture fine-grained spatial relationships in generated images while also strengthening performance on multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks. We evaluate our framework on GenEval, an object-focused benchmark for text-to-image alignment, where it achieves 4.08% relative improvements. In addition, experiments on VLMEvalKit confirm 4.85% relative improvements in multimodal understanding, highlighting the broad effectiveness of our approach.

</details>


### [162] [Towards Efficient Large Language Model Serving: A Survey on System-Aware KV Cache Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08057)
*Jiantong Jiang,Peiyu Yang,Rui Zhang,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey that systematizes system-aware KV cache infrastructure (sKis) for LLM serving, organizing work into three dimensions—temporal (execution/scheduling), spatial (placement/migration), and structural (representation/retention)—and identifying cross-behavior co-design opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM serving systems are memory-intensive and costly, with the KV cache being the critical bottleneck. Despite rapid model advancements, the infrastructure for managing KV caches in serving systems lacks systematic organization, motivating a structured survey to consolidate and guide future research.

Method: The authors survey recent work on KV cache infrastructure from a system behavior perspective, taxonomizing efforts into three orthogonal dimensions: (1) execution and scheduling (temporal), (2) placement and migration (spatial), and (3) representation and retention (structural). They further analyze cross-behavior co-design affinities and behavior-objective links.

Result: The survey provides a structured taxonomy of the KV cache design space for LLM serving, mapping existing techniques across temporal, spatial, and structural dimensions, and reveals underexplored cross-dimension co-design opportunities and behavior-objective relationships.

Conclusion: The work systematizes a rapidly evolving research area, establishing a conceptual foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure, with future opportunities in cross-behavior co-design.

Abstract: Despite the rapid advancements of large language models (LLMs), LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly. The key-value (KV) cache, which stores KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, is crucial for enabling low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference serving. In this survey, we focus on system-aware KV infrastructure for serving LLMs (abbreviated as sKis). We revisit recent work from a system behavior perspective, organizing existing efforts into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). Furthermore, we analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links, highlighting future opportunities. Our work systematizes a rapidly evolving area, providing a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure.

</details>


### [163] [When Thinking Hurts: Epistemic Signals in the Reasoning Chains of Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08059)
*Mayank Singal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First empirical characterization of answer entropy behavior in thinking-mode VLMs reveals three distinct collapse patterns across models. Thinking chain entropy consistently outperforms answer entropy for uncertainty quantification, and a practical abstention gate can boost accuracy from 71% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no added inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional uncertainty quantification for VLMs targets only the answer token distribution, ignoring the rich signal in the thinking chain. The authors aim to empirically characterize whether and how thinking-mode entropy behaves differently from answer entropy, and whether chain entropy provides a more reliable uncertainty signal — especially under adversarial distribution shifts.

Method: Four thinking-mode VLMs (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking, GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking, InternVL3-8B, and a non-thinking baseline) are evaluated on identical POPE adversarial samples. Entropy is computed over both answer tokens and thinking chain tokens. AUROC is used to measure how well each entropy signal discriminates correct vs. incorrect responses. A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot and HallusionBench evaluation extend the analysis. A practical abstention gate based on chain entropy thresholds is proposed and evaluated.

Result: Three distinct answer-entropy collapse patterns: Qwen3-VL (AUROC=0.492, complete collapse), GLM-4.1V (0.716, no collapse), InternVL3 (selective thinking on 50% of samples; full AUROC=0.675, thinking-only=0.602). Chain entropy outperforms answer entropy across all thinking models when chains are present (0.647/0.759/0.608 vs. 0.492/0.716/0.602). VQAv2 confirms chain entropy superiority (0.680 vs. 0.595), especially for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). HallusionBench shows moderate signal (~0.64) for both Qwen models. Structured abstention affects 12–22% of queries, asymmetrically favoring absent-object queries. The abstention gate raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage.

Conclusion: Thinking chain entropy is a more reliable uncertainty signal than answer entropy whenever chains are available, though model-specific collapse patterns vary dramatically. Chain-based abstention offers a zero-cost mechanism for substantially improving VLM reliability on adversarial distributions, with the caveat that gains are model-dependent and may be marginal for models exhibiting selective thinking behavior like InternVL3.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for visual language models (VLMs) conventionally targets the answer token distribution. We provide the first three-family empirical characterisation of answer entropy behaviour in thinking-mode VLMs. Running four models on identical POPE adversarial samples, we find three qualitatively distinct patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC = 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); and InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on only 50% of samples, ans H = 0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Across all three thinking-mode models, thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on the subset where chains are generated (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602 respectively), suggesting chain signals are the more reliable predictor whenever chains are present. This holds strongly for Qwen and GLM, but with only marginal and statistically unreliable advantage for InternVL3 (n_FP = 17). A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) outperforms answer entropy (0.595) on VQAv2 questions, with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). On harder reasoning tasks (HallusionBench) both Qwen models show moderate signal (approx. 0.64), consistent with incomplete pre-commitment on difficult questions. We additionally document structured abstention affecting 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a practical abstention gate raising accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no additional inference cost.

</details>


### [164] [Cross-Modal Generative Framework for Signal Translation from Fetal-Maternal Electrocardiograms to Fetal Doppler Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08073)
*Tongli Su,Alireza Rafiei,Marly van Assen,Reza Sameni,Gari D. Clifford,Faezeh Marzbanrad,Nasim Katebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cross-modal generative model using dilated convolutions and cross-modal attention synthesizes fetal Doppler ultrasound envelopes from maternal-fetal dual-lead ECG, quantifying the mechanical contributions to fetal hemodynamics not recoverable from electrical signals alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fetal ECG and Doppler ultrasound offer complementary but separate views of fetal cardiovascular function (electrical vs. mechanical). Understanding which Doppler components are recoverable from ECG—and which are not—can help disentangle electrical versus mechanical (e.g., placental resistance, vascular compliance) contributions to fetal circulation. Additionally, evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling motivates incorporating maternal signals.

Method: A cross-modal generative framework that combines dilated convolutions for temporal receptive fields, cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG information, and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies.

Result: PSD MSE of 49.9 ± 15.8 dB² (51% lower than two-channel baseline); heart-rate error of 4.71 ± 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible vs. 110–160 bpm physiological range); cross-modal attention yields 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation.

Conclusion: The framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling Doppler envelope synthesis from dual-lead ECG and quantifying purely mechanical Doppler contributions—those unrecoverable from electrical recordings—facilitating more comprehensive fetal assessment.

Abstract: Fetal electrocardiogram (fECG) and Doppler ultrasound provide complementary views of fetal cardiovascular function: fECG captures electrical activity while Doppler reflects mechanical hemodynamics shaped by factors such as placental resistance and vascular compliance. Understanding the recoverable and unrecoverable Doppler components through reconstruction from fECG offers insight into the relative contributions of electrical versus mechanical factors in fetal circulation, thereby informing clinical decisions. In addition, clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling suggests that maternal cardiovascular dynamics may also inform fetal hemodynamics. To computationally model these relationships, we propose a cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies, our model synthesizes Doppler envelopes with power spectral density mean squared error (PSD MSE) of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible relative to the 110-160 bpm physiological range). Cross-modal attention yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying the contribution of maternal-fetal coupling. Our proposed framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling the synthesis of Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG. By analysis of both recoverable and residual Doppler components, this approach enables quantification of the purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms -- those not recoverable from electrical recordings -- ultimately facilitating a more comprehensive fetal assessment.

</details>


### [165] [Modular Pretraining Enables Access Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08077)
*Ethan Roland,Murat Cubuktepe,Erick Martinez,Stijn Servaes,Keenan Pepper,Mike Vaiana,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena,Judd Rosenblatt,Addie Foote,Cem Anil,Alex Cloud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAM introduces auxiliary modules into a pre-trained network that can be ablated at inference to selectively remove dual-use capabilities, approximating data-filtered models at a fraction of the cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma where the same capability can be used for beneficial or harmful purposes. The ideal solution—serving separate capability-restricted models to different users—is prohibitively expensive. A method is needed to selectively disable dangerous capabilities without training multiple full models.

Method: GRAM (Gradient-Routed Auxiliary Modules) is a pre-training method that inserts auxiliary modules into a neural network. During training, gradient routing selectively updates specific modules to induce specialization in targeted capabilities. At inference, ablating (removing) a module disables the associated capability, approximating a model trained on filtered data. The approach is validated via Chinchilla-optimal scaling from 50M to 5B parameters.

Result: GRAM effectively disables targeted capabilities (across virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and code) while preserving general performance. It resists capability recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Scaling analysis shows the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones; GRAM closely tracks data filtering. With 5 capability profiles, GRAM achieves a 5x cost reduction over data filtering.

Conclusion: GRAM provides a scalable, cost-effective mechanism for access control over dual-use AI capabilities, enabling a single trained model to serve multiple capability-restricted profiles without the prohibitive expense of training and deploying separate models per profile.

Abstract: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma. An AI capability that helps one user cure a disease can help another synthesize one. This dilemma could be resolved with access control, limiting dual-use AI capabilities to trusted deployments with a legitimate need. A gold standard for access control would be to serve separate models with different capabilities to different users. However, training and deploying multiple models is prohibitively expensive. To address this challenge, we propose gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM), a pre-training method that adds modules to a neural network and selectively updates them to induce specialization. Ablating a module at inference time removes its capability from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered data. We evaluate GRAM on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use data spanning virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and specialized code. These experiments show that GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving the rest, and resists their recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Most importantly, a Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters shows that the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, and that GRAM closely tracks data filtering. GRAM's training cost is independent of the number of supported capability profiles, yielding a 5x reduction over data filtering in our 5-profile setting.

</details>


### [166] [Deep Learning Method for Stationary Distribution of Reflected Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08091)
*Jim Dai,Zhanhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning framework is proposed to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional Reflected Brownian Motion (RBM) using the Basic Adjoint Relationship (BAR), enabling accurate tail probability estimation where analytical solutions are intractable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stationary distribution of RBM is critical for analyzing high-dimensional stochastic systems (e.g., queueing networks), yet closed-form solutions exist only in a few special cases. Computing performance metrics like tail probabilities is even more intractable despite their practical importance.

Method: The authors design a deep learning pipeline that learns the Laplace transform (moment generating function) of high-dimensional RBMs. The approach leverages the Basic Adjoint Relationship (BAR) — a fundamental identity characterizing the stationary distribution — as the foundation for constructing the loss function. Careful design choices are made regarding the loss formulation, training data sampling strategy, and neural network architecture to ensure accuracy and efficiency.

Result: The method is evaluated on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrates near-perfect prediction performance in high-dimensional settings, significantly outperforming the limits of analytical tractability.

Conclusion: The proposed deep learning approach offers a general and powerful tool for analyzing stochastic systems in regimes beyond the reach of analytical methods, with strong potential for practical applications. Code is publicly available on GitHub.

Abstract: The stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) plays an important role in the analysis of high-dimensional stochastic systems, yet closed-form solutions are known only for a few special cases. Computing important performance metrics, such as tail probabilities, is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance. In this paper, we develop a deep learning approach that accurately and efficiently learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Our framework combines a careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture. We evaluate the proposed method on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrate near-perfect prediction in high-dimensional settings, highlighting its potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes. Our code can be found at https://github.com/zhangz73/NN4MGF.

</details>


### [167] [Stochastic Order Learning: An Approach to Rank Estimation Using Noisy Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08103)
*Chaewon Lee,Seon-Ho Lee,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SOL reformulates noisy ordinal label learning as a stochastic ordering problem, using dual objectives—discriminative loss and stochastic order loss—to capture label uncertainty and enforce probabilistic ordering, achieving robust rank estimation under various noise types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty (e.g., annotator disagreement, ambiguous boundaries) rather than simple random label corruption, yet existing rank estimation methods typically assume deterministic labels. This mismatch degrades performance under realistic label noise.

Method: SOL treats each instance as associated with multiple plausible ranks (a distribution over ranks). It learns an embedding space via two complementary losses: (1) a discriminative loss that organizes instance-centroid interactions in the embedding space, and (2) a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances, directly modeling the stochastic dominance structure inherent in ordinal data with uncertainty.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse datasets show that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise, outperforming baselines that assume deterministic ordinal labels.

Conclusion: Framing noisy ordinal label learning as stochastic ordering is an effective paradigm. SOL's dual-objective approach successfully captures label uncertainty, yielding robust rank estimation and providing a principled alternative to deterministic ordinal learning frameworks.

Abstract: Rank estimation under label noise poses a fundamental challenge, as ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption. In this paper, we reformulate rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem, in which each instance is inherently associated with multiple plausible ranks instead of a single deterministic label. Based on this view, we propose stochastic order learning (SOL), a learning framework that captures ordinal label uncertainty and learns an embedding space through two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss that structures instance--centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

</details>


### [168] [Vanilla SGD with Momentum Survives Heavy-Tailed Noise: Convergence Analysis without Gradient Clipping or Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08104)
*Ryusei Yamada,Naoki Sato,Hideaki Iiduka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed noise, covering strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex settings. It shows that without gradient clipping or normalization, vanilla methods achieve strictly inferior convergence rates compared to their clipped/normalized counterparts, revealing inherent limitations of momentum SGD in heavy-tailed regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While existing work addresses heavy-tailed noise in SGD through gradient clipping or normalization, there is a fundamental gap in understanding how vanilla SGD — especially with momentum — performs under such noise without any gradient control mechanisms. The paper aims to characterize the inherent limitations of these vanilla methods.

Method: The authors refine existing convergence bounds for vanilla SGD and derive new convergence analyses for vanilla SGD with momentum. The analysis spans three objective classes: strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex functions. All results are obtained without employing any gradient clipping, normalization, or other control mechanisms, relying purely on standard SGD and momentum SGD updates.

Result: The derived convergence rates for vanilla SGD and momentum SGD under heavy-tailed noise are provably inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized SGD variants. This gap holds across all three function classes, demonstrating that momentum alone cannot compensate for the lack of gradient control in heavy-tailed settings.

Conclusion: Vanilla SGD with momentum suffers from inherent limitations under heavy-tailed noise, achieving suboptimal convergence rates compared to methods with gradient clipping or normalization. These theoretical findings are validated through experiments on synthetic functions.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a cornerstone of modern optimization. While its performance under heavy-tailed noise is often addressed through specialized modifications such as gradient clipping or normalization, we investigate a more fundamental question: how does vanilla SGD, particularly with momentum, perform in the presence of heavy-tailed noise? In this paper, we refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD and, more importantly, provide the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, without employing any gradient control mechanisms. Our results demonstrate that the obtained convergence rates are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, thereby revealing inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise. The theoretical findings are supported by experiments on synthetic functions.

</details>


### [169] [Contrastive Order Learning: A General Framework for Ordinal Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08109)
*Chaewon Lee,BeomJun Shim,Kwang Pyo Choi,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConOrd introduces contrastive order learning, a framework combining contrastive learning with ordinal regression via soft affinity/disparity weights based on rank differences, achieving state-of-the-art results on age estimation, blind image/video quality assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contrastive learning methods ignore label ordering, while order learning methods rely on local margin-based comparisons and fail to capture global ordinal structure. A unified approach is needed to leverage both full-batch sample interactions and fine-grained ordinal relationships.

Method: ConOrd proposes a contrastive order loss that uses soft affinity and disparity weights derived from rank differences between sample pairs. This allows all pairs in a batch to contribute to the loss with varying strengths, enabling simultaneous global contrastive learning and fine-grained ordinal modeling without relying solely on hard margins.

Result: Extensive experiments across facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment show ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and demonstrates strong generalization across diverse ordinal regression tasks.

Conclusion: ConOrd successfully bridges contrastive learning and order learning, overcoming their individual weaknesses. The framework provides a simple yet effective solution for ordinal regression with broad applicability, as evidenced by superior performance on multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: We propose contrastive order learning (ConOrd), a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that integrates the strengths of contrastive learning and order learning. While contrastive learning effectively leverages all samples in a batch, it typically ignores the inherent ordering among rank labels. Conversely, order learning explicitly models label ordinality but often relies on local, margin-based comparisons, limiting its ability to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses these limitations by introducing a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch. Extensive experiments on a range of ordinal regression tasks, including facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment, demonstrate that ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/ConOrd.

</details>


### [170] [Workload-Preserving Differentially Private Synthetic Data for Causal Inference via Maximum-Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08122)
*Amir Asiaee,Kaveh Aryan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "causal workloads" — differentially private query sets designed around doubly robust orthogonal moments — to generate DP synthetic data that preserves causal estimands (ATE, ATT). It also proposes Causal-AIM for adaptive workload selection and a noise-aware multiple-imputation procedure for valid confidence intervals, demonstrating a tradeoff between distributional fidelity (better for point accuracy at relaxed privacy) and causal moment preservation (better for valid inference at strict privacy budgets).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing workload-based DP synthetic data methods focus on generic marginal queries that achieve strong distributional fidelity but fail to preserve causal estimands like ATE, which depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments. There is a gap between DP synthetic data generation and valid causal inference, motivating a method that directly targets the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust estimators.

Method: The authors design "causal workloads" — DP query sets built around doubly robust orthogonal moments. The noisy answers can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or post-processed via maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data. They decompose ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. They also introduce Causal-AIM (adaptive workload selector) and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for constructing confidence intervals from DP synthetic data.

Result: Causal workloads prove most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty quantification. Generic workloads often retain an advantage in point RMSE as privacy budgets relax. The NA+MI procedure yields valid confidence intervals by properly propagating DP noise, whereas treating synthetic rows as real data leads to miscalibrated inference. A single DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental tradeoff: distributional fidelity aids point accuracy under relaxed privacy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and explicitly propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic data as ground truth. The proposed causal workload framework bridges DP synthetic data and causal inference, enabling valid, multi-estimand analyses under differential privacy.

Abstract: Workload-based differentially private (DP) synthetic data methods privately measure aggregate queries and post-process the noisy answers into synthetic records. Generic workloads can achieve strong distributional fidelity, but causal estimands such as the average treatment effect (ATE) depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals need not preserve. We propose causal workloads: DP query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. The released workload can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed by maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; our theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. We also introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector, and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. Because the workload is released once, the same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending. Empirically, causal workloads are most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE as privacy relaxes. The broader lesson is a tradeoff: distributional fidelity can help point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

</details>


### [171] [DeepPySR -- A Symbolic Regression Framework with Dynamic Pruning, Pareto Selection, and Hierarchical Composition for Real-World Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08150)
*Fuling Chen,Kevin Vinsen,Phillip Melton,Rae-Chi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepPySR extends symbolic regression with dynamic variable pruning, an exponential Pareto selection criterion, and a multi-layer hierarchical architecture to handle high-dimensional inputs, principled formula selection, and data irregularities, outperforming PySR and baselines on physics and biomedical/social-science benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression produces interpretable glass-box formulas critical for clinical and social science applications, but existing methods struggle with (1) high-dimensional input spaces, (2) principled selection among Pareto-front formulas trading accuracy vs. complexity, and (3) data irregularities like multicollinearity and class imbalance.

Method: DeepPySR introduces three innovations: (1) a dynamic variable-pruning schedule that progressively removes irrelevant features during the search process; (2) an exponential Pareto selection criterion that resolves accuracy–complexity trade-offs without manual intervention; and (3) a multi-layer architecture enabling hierarchical symbolic composition of formulas.

Result: On 4 Feynman physics benchmarks and 7 biomedical/social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR: body fat R² 0.794 vs 0.702, heart disease F1 0.898 vs 0.787, student performance R² 0.964 vs 0.948, and Raine BMI R² 0.525 vs 0.370, while producing formulas aligned with known domain risk factors.

Conclusion: DeepPySR effectively addresses key limitations of symbolic regression through dynamic pruning, principled Pareto selection, and hierarchical composition, achieving superior predictive performance and interpretability across diverse domains where transparent, glass-box models are essential.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers analytical equations from data, yielding glass-box models with directly interpretable formulas, unlike black-box methods that rely on unstable post-hoc tools such as SHAP or LIME. This transparency is crucial in clinical medicine and social science, but SR faces three challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled selection of Pareto-front formulae, and data irregularities such as multicollinearity and class imbalance. We introduce DeepPySR, which addresses these issues with a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition. On four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical and social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines on body fat (R$^2$: 0.794 vs.\ 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs.\ 0.787), student performance (R$^2$: 0.964 vs.\ 0.948), and Raine BMI (R$^2$: 0.525 vs.\ 0.370), producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

</details>


### [172] [Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)
*Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies how to select student layers for replacement with teacher layers during model size interpolation. The authors frame optimal layer selection as a shortest-path problem on a DAG, evaluate simple sequential patching strategies, and introduce KLPatch—a greedy KL-divergence-based algorithm that approximates the optimal solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (boomerang distillation) showed that patching teacher layers into a distilled student model enables zero-shot model size interpolation, but how to optimally select which student layers to replace remained unexplored. The authors aim to provide a principled, systematic understanding of layer selection and its impact on interpolation behavior.

Method: The problem is formalized as: given a distilled student and its teacher, find the subset of student layers to replace with contiguous teacher-layer blocks to maximize performance at each target size. This is proven equivalent to a shortest-path problem on a directed acyclic graph. Empirically, simple sequential strategies (first-to-last, last-to-first patching) are evaluated. The authors also propose KLPatch, a greedy algorithm that iteratively selects the student layer with the highest KL divergence from its teacher counterpart for replacement, thereby approximately solving the optimization problem.

Result: Patching order significantly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects varying substantially across model families. Simple sequential strategies (particularly last-to-first) perform surprisingly well. KLPatch often outperforms last-to-first patching and provides a near-optimal solution to the layer selection problem, offering practical guidance for constructing interpolated models without additional training.

Conclusion: Layer selection is a critical factor in model size interpolation via patching. The shortest-path formulation provides a principled framework, and practical strategies like last-to-first patching and KLPatch offer effective, readily applicable solutions. The work bridges theory and practice for constructing near-optimal interpolated models at arbitrary target sizes.

Abstract: Zero-shot model size interpolation aims to create new models of intermediate target sizes by combining existing models without additional training. Recent work on boomerang distillation [Kangaslahti et al., 2026] shows that a student language model distilled from a larger teacher can be expanded by iteratively patching its layers, replacing student layers with contiguous blocks of teacher layers to obtain models whose size and performance interpolate between the student and the teacher. In this work, we provide the first systematic study of student-layer selection for model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies--patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first--often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models.

</details>


### [173] [Open-ended Multi-agent Autocurricula via Visual Inspection of Policies with Multi-modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08193)
*Lorenzo Pantè,Andrea Fanti,Roberto Capobianco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VIP (Visual Inspection of Policies) uses a Video Language Model to analyze recorded episode videos of RL agents and generate curriculum recommendations. Tested on SMAC, even a lightweight VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B) outperforms text-only and scalar-score baselines for open-ended curriculum learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing open-ended curricula for RL is challenging because assessing task difficulty relative to an agent's learning progress is hard. Prior approaches rely on scalar task scores or textual behavior summaries, which may miss nuanced behavioral information. The authors propose leveraging video recordings of policy execution as a richer, more direct signal for curriculum design.

Method: VIP (Visual Inspection of Policies): an approach that uses a Video Language Model (VLM) to process recorded episode videos of agent policies and produce curriculum recommendations. The VLM directly inspects visual behavior from videos to assess task difficulty and suggest next tasks. Implemented with VideoLLaMa2-7B, a lightweight open-source VLM. Compared against a text-only ablation and scalar task score baselines on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC).

Result: VIP with VideoLLaMa2-7B generates more effective curricula than both the text-only ablation and methods relying on scalar task scores, demonstrating that video-based policy inspection provides a superior signal for curriculum learning.

Conclusion: Direct visual inspection of policy behavior via VLMs is a promising direction for open-ended curriculum design in RL, outperforming text-based and scalar-score alternatives even with lightweight, openly accessible models. The approach naturally scales to multi-agent settings as demonstrated on SMAC.

Abstract: Open-ended curricula in Reinforcement Learning (RL) aim to train generally-capable agents by identifying tasks that facilitate learning increasingly complex skills. A major challenge when designing such curricula is assessing task difficulty relative to the agent's current learning progress. While previous work has explored using scalar task scores or textual summaries of the agent's behavior, here we study a different approach: directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos. We introduce a simple yet effective instantiation of this approach which leverages a Video Language Model (VLM) to both process these videos and provide curriculum recommendations, which we call Visual Inspection of Policies (VIP). Since videos can naturally contain any number of controllable agents, we empirically study VIP on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC). We show that even with a lightweight and openly accessible VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), VIP can use policy videos to generate more effective curricula than both its text-only ablation and methods that rely on scalar task scores.

</details>


### [174] [PIT-SUN: A Deployable Empirical Marginal Transform Framework with Expectation-Consistent Recovery for Regression in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08202)
*Mingyu Zhao,Zhaohan Li,Zhenxiong Miao,Xu Zhang,Dewei Leng,Yanan Niu,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIT-SUN proposes a probability-integral-transformed unbiased recovery framework for estimating original-space conditional expectations in recommender systems, using an empirical marginal table with multiplicative SUN recovery to address mean collapse and tail shrinkage on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated targets while preserving expectation consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MSE estimation on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, multimodal targets suffers from gradient instability causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation helps but breaks expectation consistency under direct inverse-transform (which holds only for affine transforms, incompatible with bounded tail compression). Existing conditionally linear methods restore consistency but leave open design choices: which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor to use for sparse complex marginals.

Method: PIT-SUN framework uses one empirical marginal table to define: (1) a bounded normal-score coordinate via probability integral transform, (2) an inverse-quantile lookup, (3) a variance-controlled recovery base, and (4) drift monitoring. Multiplicative SUN (Standardized Unbiased) recovery then estimates original-space expectations rather than directly inverting transformed predictions.

Result: Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment demonstrate robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

Conclusion: PIT-SUN provides a principled, deployable solution for unbiased conditional expectation estimation under heavy-tailed sparse marginals, unifying coordinate choice, inverse lookup, recovery base, and monitoring within a single empirical marginal framework.

Abstract: Estimating original-space conditional expectations is central to value-driven recommender systems, including dwell time, GMV, and LTV forecasting. Standard MSE is expectation-consistent in principle, but its gradients become unstable on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets, causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation alleviates this scale conflict, yet any useful nonlinear marginal transform loses expectation consistency under direct inversion. This is not an implementation oversight: a direct inverse-transform estimator is universally expectation-consistent only when the inverse transform is affine, which cannot simultaneously provide bounded tail compression. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods restore expectation consistency, but still leave open which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor should be selected for sparse complex marginals. We propose \textbf{P}robability-\textbf{I}ntegral-\textbf{TranS}formed \textbf{Un}biased recovery (\textbf{PIT-SUN}), a deployable empirical marginal recovery framework. PIT-SUN uses one empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, its inverse-quantile lookup, a variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of directly inverting transformed predictions. Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

</details>


### [175] [RhyMix: A Lightweight Adaptive Multi-Rhythm Network for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08234)
*Sumit Satishrao Shevtekar,Chandresh Kumar Maurya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RhyMix is a lightweight hybrid neural architecture for time series forecasting that uses a parallel dual-path design (Cyclic Path + Multi-Scale Temporal CNN) with adaptive gating, achieving SOTA on 10/12 long-term forecasting benchmarks while maintaining linear complexity, ~40K parameters, and <5ms inference latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing forecasting architectures suffer from a fundamental trade-off: transformers capture long-range dependencies but smooth local variations; convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields; linear models are efficient but cannot capture nonlinear dynamics. Real-world time series require simultaneous modeling of short-term fluctuations, seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and abrupt changes — a capability that single-path architectures cannot provide.

Method: RhyMix employs a parallel dual-path paradigm: (1) a Cyclic Path with learnable cyclic embeddings providing explicit seasonal inductive bias; (2) a Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention (MSTCN-CA) Path using multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions for varied receptive fields. Adaptive gating operates at two levels: a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel; a hybrid gate adaptively balances the Cyclic and MSTCN-CA paths based on input characteristics. The architecture maintains linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon.

Result: RhyMix achieved state-of-the-art forecasting performance on 10 out of 12 real-world datasets in long-term forecasting benchmarks. The model remains extremely lightweight (~40K parameters) with linear computational complexity and sub-5ms inference latency, making it suitable for edge deployment.

Conclusion: The dual-path architecture with adaptive gating successfully resolves the single-path limitations of prior methods, demonstrating that explicit rhythmic/seasonal modeling combined with multi-scale convolution can match or exceed transformer-based approaches while being dramatically more efficient. RhyMix is well-suited for real-time deployment on resource-constrained edge devices.

Abstract: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics characterized by multiple simultaneous temporal patterns: short-term fluctuations, periodic seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and irregular abrupt changes. However, many existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling--transformers capture long-range dependencies but smooth local variations, convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields, and linear models are efficient but cannot capture nonlinear dynamics. To address this, we introduce RhyMix (RHYthm MIXture), a hybrid neural architecture designed around a parallel dual-path modeling paradigm with adaptive gating mechanisms. RhyMix integrates two complementary encoding branches: (i) a Cyclic Path that incorporates explicit seasonal inductive bias through learnable cyclic embeddings, capturing predictable rhythmic patterns; and (ii) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention Path that employs multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions to capture temporal dependencies across different receptive fields. A key innovation is the use of adaptive gating at multiple levels: a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, and Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel, while a hybrid gate adaptively balances the Cyclic and MSTCN-CA Paths based on input characteristics. This design ensures the model adapts to specific temporal patterns while maintaining linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon. Across extensive benchmarks on 12 real-world datasets for long-term forecasting, RhyMix achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 of 12 datasets. The model remains lightweight (~40K params) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms),suitable for resource-constrained edge devices and real-time deployment.

</details>


### [176] [Structure Learning on Clustered Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08238)
*Ryan Thompson,Matt P. Wand,Veerabhadran Baladandayuthapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a mixed-effects DAG structure learning method that estimates a global (fixed-effects) causal graph while accommodating cluster-specific (random-effects) variations, using a differentiable coupling mechanism to ensure global acyclicity, with provable convergence, identifiability guarantees, and superior empirical performance on clustered data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing scalable DAG structure learning methods assume a homogeneous population, which is unrealistic for clustered data (e.g., multi-patient, multi-site studies) where cluster-specific causal variations exist. There is a need to bridge classical mixed-effects modeling with causal graph discovery.

Method: The authors propose a mixed-effects DAG model combining fixed-effects (global) and random-effects (cluster-specific) graphs. A differentiable graph coupling mechanism ensures the union of both graph components remains acyclic. Optimization uses a provably convergent first-order method with efficient batched updates across clusters. Model identifiability is formally established.

Result: The method provably recovers the true structure asymptotically under established identifiability conditions. In experiments on both real and synthetic data, the approach detects dependencies missed by alternative (homogeneity-assuming) estimators, demonstrating practical value for clustered causal discovery.

Conclusion: The proposed mixed-effects framework successfully extends DAG structure learning to clustered settings, combining the flexibility of random-effects modeling with rigorous acyclicity constraints. This fills a critical gap between classical mixed models and modern causal discovery.

Abstract: Recent algorithmic advances have made directed acyclic graph (DAG) structure learning scalable for causal discovery. Yet, the currently available techniques assume a completely homogeneous population, precluding their application to clustered data where cluster-specific variations (e.g., patient-specific effects) are common. We address this issue by introducing a new approach that estimates a global structure while accounting for local cluster-level effects. The key idea is to extend the fixed- and random-effects framework of classical mixed models to the structure learning setting. Towards this end, we present a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that guarantees the union of the fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic. Computationally, we provide a provably convergent first-order method and leverage efficient batched updates across clusters. Statistically, we establish identifiability of the model and show that our approach recovers the true structure asymptotically. In experiments on real and synthetic data, our proposal detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators, underscoring its value for structure learning in clustered settings.

</details>


### [177] [An interpretable Good--Turing restart criterion for k-means++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08243)
*Renato Cordeiro de Amorim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTRC introduces a principled restart criterion for k-means++ that uses Good-Turing estimation and confidence bounds to stop restarts adaptively based on dataset difficulty, governed by a user-specified tolerance parameter rather than an arbitrary fixed restart count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The number of k-means++ restarts is typically chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly across all datasets, which wastes computation on easy datasets, potentially under-serves hard ones, and undermines reproducibility and fair method comparison. A principled, data-adaptive stopping criterion is needed.

Method: GTRC combines three components: (1) a Good-Turing estimator to estimate the probability mass of unseen (better) clusterings from observed outcomes, (2) a proven unconditional upper bound on the probability that another restart yields improvement, and (3) a confidence-based bound that stops restarts once the probability of improvement drops below a user-specified tolerance ε.

Result: Evaluated across 36 datasets, GTRC achieved clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts while adaptively varying the number of restarts according to dataset difficulty. The stopping behavior is governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal.

Conclusion: GTRC provides a principled, reportable alternative to fixing restart counts in advance, enabling fair comparisons and efficient use of computation by adapting to each dataset's inherent difficulty.

Abstract: The k-means++ algorithm is commonly restarted multiple times to avoid poor local optima, yet the number of restarts is almost always chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly regardless of data set difficulty. This undermines any comparison relying on such a choice and wastes computation on easy data sets while potentially under-serving hard ones. We introduce GTRC, a restart criterion combining a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that a further restart would improve on the current result, stopping once this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance $\varepsilon$. Across 36 data sets, GTRC reached clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts, while the number of restarts used varied considerably and appropriately with data set difficulty, governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal rather than a fixed rule. GTRC offers a principled and reportable alternative to fixing the number of $k$-means++ restarts in advance. Software:https://github.com/RCdeAmorim/Good-Turing-Restart-Criterion.

</details>


### [178] [CASL-VAE: Learning Structured Latent Variables from Unpaired Data for Semi-supervised Clustering and Paired Sample Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08254)
*Sai Spandana Chintapalli,Pratik Chaudhari,Christos Davatzikos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASL-VAE is a deep contrastive variational autoencoder that disentangles shared and target-specific variation from unpaired data across two populations, modeling target heterogeneity as discrete subtypes with continuous within-subtype variation via hierarchical latent factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods cannot effectively separate multiple modes of target-specific variation when comparing a target population (e.g., diseased) to a reference population (e.g., healthy) using unpaired data. There is a need to model heterogeneous target variation as discrete subtypes with continuous within-subtype variation.

Method: CASL-VAE uses a contrastive variational autoencoder framework with structured latent factors: (1) continuous common factors shared across both populations, and (2) hierarchical salient factors that capture target-specific variation as discrete subtypes (categorical latent variables) and continuous within-subtype variation. The model is trained via approximate joint likelihood optimization over both domains using only unpaired data, with variational inference.

Result: On semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, CASL-VAE demonstrated improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation compared to baseline clustering and generative models. On real Alzheimer's disease data, it revealed biologically plausible heterogeneity patterns.

Conclusion: CASL-VAE provides a principled framework for cross-domain analysis with unpaired data, enabling effective separation of shared and target-specific variation, subtype discovery, and paired-sample generation without requiring paired observations.

Abstract: Quantifying variability in a target population relative to a reference population is central to many scientific and clinical problems (e.g., diseased vs. healthy). Yet, without paired data and in the presence of heterogeneous target variation, existing methods struggle to separate multiple modes of target-specific variation. We propose \textit{CASL-VAE}, a deep contrastive latent variable model that learns structured latent generative factors from unpaired data. CASL-VAE factorizes variation into continuous common latent factors shared across populations and hierarchical salient latent factors that model target-specific heterogeneity as discrete subtypes and continuous within-subtype variation. Using variational inference, we show how approximate joint likelihood optimization over reference and target domains can be performed using unpaired data, providing a principled basis for paired-sample generation and cross-domain analysis. We validate CASL-VAE on semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, demonstrating improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation compared to baseline clustering and generative models. We also validate its ability to reveal biologically plausible heterogeneity in Alzheimer's disease.

</details>


### [179] [Ensemble Diversity Optimization for Subjective Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08493)
*Xia Cui,Ziyi Huang,N. R. Abeynayake*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EDO is a prediction-space framework that jointly optimizes ensemble composition, size, and calibration via a unified differentiable objective with a signed diversity regularizer, enabling controlled modeling of annotator disagreement in subjective NLP tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subjective NLP tasks exhibit systematic annotator disagreement that should be modeled as genuine uncertainty rather than collapsed into a single ground truth. Existing methods either ignore this uncertainty or cannot flexibly control the trade-off between preserving disagreement (utility) and achieving calibrated probabilities.

Method: EDO operates in prediction space, jointly learning ensemble weights and effective cardinality via Gumbel-Softmax relaxation. It employs a unified differentiable objective combining: (1) a soft F1 surrogate and class-weighted cross-entropy for class imbalance, (2) a signed diversity regularizer (tuned on validation data) that can either promote or suppress disagreement to navigate the utility-calibration trade-off, and (3) reliability-weighted diversity to regulate intra-ensemble variability. The entire framework is optimized end-to-end.

Result: On four subjective text-classification benchmarks (ArMIS, ConvAbuse, HS-Brexit, MD-Agreement), EDO reduced cross-entropy by 40-78% and lowered Brier scores compared to Soft-CE, Soft-MD, Top-5 Voting, and WEL, while maintaining competitive F1 and better alignment with annotator distributions.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing ensemble structure with a signed diversity regularizer offers an efficient, model-agnostic approach for modeling human subjectivity in supervised learning, achieving superior probabilistic calibration without sacrificing predictive performance.

Abstract: Subjective NLP tasks often exhibit systematic annotator disagreement, requiring models that represent uncertainty rather than collapse it. We introduce Ensemble Diversity Optimization (EDO), a prediction-space framework that jointly optimizes ensemble weights, effective cardinality, and calibration through a unified differentiable objective. EDO learns ensemble composition and size end-to-end via Gumbel-Softmax relaxation and incorporates a signed diversity regularizer, tuned on validation data, to steer optimization toward either preserving or suppressing disagreement. This regularization prevents ensemble collapse and enables controlled navigation of the utility-calibration trade-off. The framework integrates a soft F1 surrogate, class-weighted cross-entropy to address imbalance, and reliability-weighted diversity to regulate intra-ensemble variability. Experiments on four subjective text-classification benchmarks (ArMIS, ConvAbuse, HS-Brexit, MD-Agreement) show that EDO substantially improves probabilistic calibration, reducing cross-entropy (40-78% depending on baseline) and lowering Brier scores relative to Soft-CE, Soft-MD, Top-5 Voting, and WEL, while maintaining competitive F1 and better alignment with annotator distributions. These results demonstrate that jointly optimizing ensemble structure with a signed diversity regularizer provides an efficient, model-agnostic approach for modeling human subjectivity in supervised learning.

</details>


### [180] [Classifier Chain-based Pathological Test Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08299)
*Abu Rafe Md Jamil,Nayan Malakar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning system using Classifier Chains for multi-label pathological test recommendation achieves 98.83% accuracy with Logistic Regression, enhanced by SHAP-based explainable AI for clinical interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnostic test selection is often delayed or subject to physicians' subjective interpretation, hindering timely and effective patient care. The study aims to accelerate test recommendation using patient symptoms before physician consultation.

Method: The task is framed as multi-label classification using Classifier Chains (CC) to model inter-test dependencies. A custom dataset was curated from SOUTHERN.IML pathology data with expert input. Models compared include Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, Random Forest, and Majority Voting ensemble. Explainable AI via SHAP is applied for clinical interpretability.

Result: Logistic Regression with CC achieved the highest overall accuracy (98.83%). The Majority Voting ensemble offered the best balance across precision (0.93), recall (0.85), and F1-score (0.89). SHAP explanations aligned with established medical knowledge, reinforcing model reliability.

Conclusion: Classifier Chains improve traditional algorithms for diagnostic test recommendation. The SHAP-verified, medically consistent reasoning supports physician decision-making in critical scenarios, demonstrating the system's practical clinical utility.

Abstract: Accurate and timely diagnoses are essential for quality patient care. However, delayed recommendation of diagnostic tests and physicians' subjective interpretations can hinder effective care. This study introduces a pathological test recommendation system that speeds up the test selection process using patient symptoms before physician consultation. The recommendation task is framed as a multi-label classification problem utilising the Classifier Chain (CC) technique to consider dependencies between tests. We collected data from the SOUTHERN.IML pathology and then created a custom dataset with the help of the expertise.
