Multiple machine learning algorithms, including Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, and Random Forest, were applied to compare models and identify the best fit for our study context. The Logistic Regression with CC model had the highest overall accuracy at 98.83%, while the Majority Voting ensemble model provided the best balance with a precision of 0.93, recall of 0.85, and F1-score of 0.89. To ensure transparency of the models and clinical interpretability, we used Explainable AI (XAI) techniques utilising SHAP (SHapley Additive Explanations), which identifies how each symptom is contributing to a test recommendation. The diagnostic reasoning revealed by the model was consistent with established medical knowledge of symptoms for the recommended tests, which further adds confidence to the model's reliability for diagnostic purposes. The reasoning could help physicians make logical decisions in critical scenarios. Overall, our findings suggest that CC can improve the efficiency of the traditional algorithms in diagnostic process providing accurate test recommendations.

</details>


### [181] [Learning $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under Locally Sampleable Graphical Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08303)
*Weiming Feng,Xiongxin Yang,Yixiao Yu,Yiyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a quasipolynomial-time learner for AC⁰ circuits under Gibbs distributions on bounded-degree graphical models that admit efficient local samplers, removing the polynomial-growth requirement from prior work. The key technique is a new low-degree approximation via truncated Glauber dynamics, with applications to two-spin systems (hard-core, Ising) near their sampling thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending quasipolynomial-time learning guarantees for AC⁰ circuits beyond the uniform distribution to broader classes of correlated distributions has been a longstanding challenge. Prior work (Chandrasekaran et al., 2026) achieved this for Gibbs distributions with both strong spatial mixing and polynomial growth, but the polynomial-growth condition is restrictive and excludes many important models. This paper aims to remove that barrier.

Method: The authors develop a new low-degree approximation for Gibbs distributions by simulating and suitably truncating classical Glauber dynamics. This provides a structural result that any Gibbs distribution admitting an efficient local sampler can be approximated by a low-degree polynomial, which then plugs into the low-degree algorithm framework of Linial-Mansour-Nisan to yield a quasipolynomial-time AC⁰ learner.

Result: A quasipolynomial-time learner for AC⁰ under graphical models requiring only efficient local samplers (no polynomial growth). Concrete applications: learners for the hard-core model and Ising model on arbitrary bounded-degree graphs, operational in parameter regimes approaching their respective sampling thresholds.

Conclusion: The polynomial-growth barrier for learning AC⁰ under Gibbs distributions is circumvented via Glauber-dynamics-based low-degree approximation. This significantly broadens the class of correlated distributions for which AC⁰ is learnable, linking computational learning theory with the theory of approximate sampling and mixing times of Markov chains.

Abstract: The problem of learning constant-depth circuits holds profound implications for computational learning theory. In a seminal result, by introducing the low-degree algorithm, Linial, Mansour, and Nisan (J. ACM 1993) presented a quasipolynomial-time learner for $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under the uniform distribution. However, obtaining comparable learning guarantees for broader classes of correlated distributions has remained a longstanding challenge. Recently, Chandrasekaran, Gaitonde, Moitra, and Vasilyan (arXiv 2026) extended these guarantees to Gibbs distributions on bounded-degree graphical models with both strong spatial mixing and polynomial growth.
  In this paper, we give a quasipolynomial-time learner for $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under graphical models that admit efficient local samplers, circumventing the polynomial-growth requirement in prior work. The key ingredient is a new low-degree approximation for Gibbs distributions, established by simulating and suitably truncating the classical Glauber dynamics. As applications, this framework yields learners for two-spin systems, including the hard-core model and Ising model, on arbitrary bounded-degree graphs, in regimes approaching their respective sampling thresholds.

</details>


### [182] [Write-Protected Discrete Bottlenecks for Language-Grounded World Models: A Structural Limitation and Sufficient Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08312)
*Jiayi Fang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language gradients cannot safely flow through Gumbel-softmax discrete symbol bottlenecks in world models — they cause catastrophic symbol collapse. The authors characterize this structural failure, show that no GumbelBottleneck variant solves it, and propose three constraints (gradient detachment, gradient-free Memory Table with co-occurrence counting, and DP-Means clustering for collision resolution) that achieve 97.2% grounding accuracy with zero collapse across all seeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether the dominant paradigm of end-to-end injection of language features (via LLMs/VLMs) into robot world models with discrete symbol systems is safe, and if not, characterize the minimal architectural constraints needed to prevent failure.

Method: The authors analyze Gumbel-softmax-based discrete symbol bottlenecks under language gradient flow, demonstrating a structural collapse phenomenon (vanilla estimator collapses to ~2.2/64 symbols). They test five anti-collapse strategies from the literature, then propose a three-layer solution: (1) gradient chain cutting via z.detach(), (2) a gradient-free non-parametric Memory Table (Dict[symbol → Counter[label]]) using co-occurrence counting instead of gradient-based binding, and (3) DP-Means streaming clustering for automatic sub-cluster splitting to handle symbol collisions. Evaluation spans 74 runs across three encoder architectures (CNN, V-JEPA 300M, CLIP ViT-L), two environments, and three texture conditions.

Result: The vanilla Gumbel-softmax bottleneck collapses to 2.2/64 symbols in 4/5 seeds. All five anti-collapse strategies maintain diversity but fail semantic learning (all ≤ 9.2% accuracy). The proposed three-layer system achieves 97.2% grounding accuracy (vs. 22.2% without Layer 3), zero symbol collapse across all 32 seeds, and 79–100% semantic binding. The fix trains under 2M parameters with no LLM fine-tuning required.

Conclusion: The failure of language gradients in Gumbel-softmax discrete bottlenecks is structural, not an optimization issue. The three proposed constraints (gradient detachment, gradient-free semantic channel via co-occurrence counting, and DP-Means collision handling) form a sufficient set to prevent collapse and enable robust language grounding in discrete symbol systems.

Abstract: How should language interface with a world model's discrete symbol system? The dominant paradigm -- end-to-end injection of LLM/VLM features into robot world models (RT-2, Octo, PaLM-E) -- implicitly assumes that language gradients can directly shape physical symbol representations. We ask whether this assumption is safe, find that it is not, and characterize the minimal architectural constraint that prevents the failure. Any language gradient entering a Gumbel-softmax-based discrete symbol bottleneck forces a structural trade-off: the vanilla estimator collapses to 2.2/64 symbols (4/5 seeds), while five anti-collapse strategies maintain diversity but fail to learn semantic labels (all <= 9.2% accuracy). No tested GumbelBottleneck variant achieves both objectives simultaneously. Within this family of discrete bottlenecks, the failure is structural rather than a matter of optimization. We characterize a sufficient set of three constraints that prevent the failure: (1) cut the gradient chain (z.detach()), preventing language signals from reaching the symbol bottleneck; (2) provide a gradient-free semantic channel -- a non-parametric Memory Table (Dict[symbol -> Counter[label]], zero parameters, zero gradients) where co-occurrence counting replaces gradient-based binding; (3) handle symbol collisions via DP-Means streaming clustering for automatic sub-cluster splitting. All three layers together achieve 97.2% grounding accuracy vs. 22.2% without Layer 3. Across two experiments spanning 74 independent runs, we demonstrate zero symbol collapse in all 32 seeds, with the blackboard achieving 79-100% semantic binding across three encoder architectures (CNN, V-JEPA 300M, CLIP ViT-L), two environments, and three texture conditions. The fix trains fewer than 2M parameters and requires no LLM fine-tuning.

</details>


### [183] [ArtMine: Discovering and Formalizing Artistic Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08331)
*Kaustubh Kumar,Ashutosh Ranjan,Vivek Srivastava,Blessin Varkey,Shirish Karande*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ArtMine is a framework that reconstructs artistic creation processes from fragmented historical evidence using Peircean abductive reasoning, compositional graphs, and self-reflective optimization, enabling process-level rather than artifact-level modeling of artistic production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative AI systems model distributions over finished artworks but fail to capture the iterative decisions, material operations, and contextual influences that constitute the creative process. Historical documentation of artistic workflows is fragmented across archival records, preparatory studies, and correspondence, making computational formalization of process-level understanding challenging. The authors aim to bridge this gap.

Method: The ArtMine framework operates in four stages: (1) synthesizing heterogeneous artwork evidence (archival records, sketches, correspondence) into a structured repository; (2) employing a Peircean abductive agent to infer evidence-grounded production steps from this repository; (3) converting inferred steps into a compositional graph and corresponding rendering prompt; (4) applying self-reflective optimization by comparing generated outputs against reference artworks to iteratively refine the process representation.

Result: A proof-of-concept case study using open-domain historical sources across multiple artists and artistic movements demonstrates that fragmented documentary evidence can yield coherent, interpretable, and auditable representations of artistic workflows. The framework successfully reconstructs plausible production sequences from partial historical records.

Conclusion: By shifting focus from final artifacts to creative processes, ArtMine advances process-centered human-AI co-creativity, with potential applications in artistic interpretation, creative education, reflective collaboration, and computational studies of cultural production.

Abstract: Understanding how artworks are created requires reasoning about the iterative decisions, material operations, and contextual influences that shape artistic production. While recent generative AI systems can synthesize artworks with high fidelity, they primarily model distributions over finished artifacts rather than the creative processes underlying their creation. In practice, artistic workflows are only partially documented through fragmented sources such as archival records, preparatory studies, correspondence, etc., making process-level understanding difficult to formalize computationally. In this work, we introduce ArtMine, a framework for discovering and formalizing artistic processes from heterogeneous historical evidence. Our approach synthesizes heterogeneous artwork evidence into a structured repository, from which a Peircean abductive agent infers evidence-grounded production steps. These steps are converted into a compositional graph and rendering prompt, then optimized through self-reflection over deviations between the generated and reference artworks. We provide a preliminary proof-of-concept case study using open-domain historical sources across multiple artists and artistic movements, demonstrating that fragmented documentary evidence can support coherent, interpretable, and auditable representations of artistic workflows. By modeling creative processes rather than only final artifacts, our work moves toward process-centred human-AI co-creativity systems that can support artistic interpretation, creative education, reflective collaboration, and computational studies of cultural production.

</details>


### [184] [AutoAnchor: Stable Diffusion Unlearning Using Cross-Attention as a Manifold Surrogate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08337)
*Siyuan Wen,Jiahao Zeng,Ningning Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses instability in diffusion unlearning, theoretically proving that anchor-free methods suffer from normal-space drift under the manifold hypothesis. It proposes a two-stage framework that automatically synthesizes manifold-proximal anchors via a cross-attention consistency loss, achieving robust and unbiased unlearning with significant improvements over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion unlearning methods are either anchor-based (relying on manually chosen semantic alternatives, risking biased unlearning) or anchor-free (using empty prompts, suffering from unconstrained latent updates and unrobust unlearning). The authors theoretically identify that lacking a manifold-proximal anchor causes normal-space drift, degrading performance.

Method: The paper formalizes unstable diffusion unlearning under the manifold hypothesis and proves normal-space drift arises without manifold-proximal anchors. The proposed two-stage framework automatically synthesizes such anchors. Since direct geometric manifold optimization is intractable, a novel cross-attention consistency loss is introduced as an efficient surrogate for manifold proximity.

Result: The method achieves up to 31.04% improvement in CLIP score for targeted concept removal and up to 4.18% for non-target utility. When integrated into existing methods, it boosts concept removal by 6.30% and utility by 6.65% on average.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables stable, robust, and unbiased diffusion unlearning by automatically synthesizing manifold-proximal anchors, significantly outperforming both anchor-based and anchor-free baselines, and can be readily integrated into existing methods to enhance their performance.

Abstract: Diffusion unlearning is essential for mitigating the generation of harmful or copyrighted content in text-to-image models. Current diffusion unlearning techniques determine the model update direction by either using alternatives of the target concept as an anchor or using empty prompts. The anchor-based method relies on manually and semantically-chosen anchors that risk biased unlearning, while the anchor-free method inherently suffers from unrobust unlearning due to unconstrained latent updates. In this work, we theoretically formalize such unstable diffusion unlearning issues under the manifold hypothesis and prove that lacking a manifold-proximal anchor inevitably induces significant normal-space drift that degrades unlearning performance. To achieve stable unlearning, we propose \mysysn, a two-stage framework that automatically synthesizes manifold-proximal anchors. However, direct geometric manifold optimization is computationally intractable. To address this challenge, \mysys introduces a novel cross-attention consistency loss which serves as a highly efficient surrogate of manifold proximity. Experimental results demonstrate that \mysys effectively achieves robust and unbiased unlearning across various state-of-the-art baselines, significantly improving targeted concept removal (by up to 31.04\% in CLIP score) and non-target utility (by up to 4.18\% in CLIP score). Moreover, \mysys can also be easily integrated into existing diffusion unlearning methods to enhance their unlearning performance (by 6.30\% for concept removal and 6.65\% for utility on average).

</details>


### [185] [Spectral Analysis of Dueling Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08340)
*Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical analysis of unregularized, unprojected dueling Q-learning with constant step-size, deriving an exact switching linear system representation for the deterministic case and a finite-time error bound for the stochastic case, clarifying how value and advantage updates differentially affect action-common and action-differential Q-function components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dueling Q-learning decomposes the Q-function into value and advantage functions, improving empirical efficiency, but theoretical understanding remains limited. Prior work only analyzed a regularized formulation; the pure tabular update without regularization or projection lacks convergence guarantees, motivating this deeper theoretical investigation.

Method: The authors analyze the centered tabular dueling Q-learning decomposition directly. They derive an exact switching linear system representation for deterministic dueling Q-learning and establish a finite-time error bound in expectation for the sampled stochastic version with constant step-size, without regularization or projection.

Result: An exact switching linear system representation for deterministic dueling Q-learning is derived. A finite-time convergence error bound in expectation is established for the stochastic variant. The analysis reveals that value and advantage updates function as distinct gains on the action-common (value function) and action-differential (advantage function) components of the Q-function.

Conclusion: The paper strengthens the theoretical foundation of dueling Q-learning by providing the first convergence guarantees for the unregularized, unprojected constant step-size recursion, and illuminates the distinct roles of value and advantage updates in the learning dynamics.

Abstract: Q-learning is a fundamental algorithm in reinforcement learning (RL) for solving discounted Markov decision processes (MDPs) when the transition kernel is unknown. The deep Q-network (DQN) extends Q-learning by using a deep neural network for Q-function approximation, which makes Q-learning applicable to more practical high-dimensional problems. Dueling Q-learning decomposes the Q-function into a value function and an advantage function and learns the two components jointly, which can improve learning efficiency. However, the theoretical understanding of dueling Q-learning is still limited. Recent work has initiated an analysis of tabular dueling Q-learning, but existing guarantees focus on a regularized formulation and leave the pure tabular update less completely understood. This paper strengthens that line of analysis by adding a direct interpretation of the centered tabular decomposition and by establishing convergence guarantees for the unregularized, unprojected constant step-size recursion. In particular, we derive an exact switching linear system representation for deterministic dueling Q-learning and a finite-time error bound in expectation for the sampled stochastic version. The analysis clarifies how the value and advantage updates act as different gains on the action-common (value function) and action-differential (advantage function) components of the Q-function.

</details>


### [186] [Certified Interventional Fidelity: Anytime-Valid, Adaptive Evaluation of Causal Claims in Mechanistic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08349)
*Amir Asiaee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CIF provides rigorous statistical confidence intervals and anytime-valid confidence sequences for mechanistic interpretability evaluations, replacing unreliable point estimates with statistically certified causal claims about model interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interventional interpretability evaluations rely on point estimates that obscure whether reported fidelity or patching effects reflect genuine causal phenomena or merely artifacts of finite sampling and arbitrary evaluation choices. There is no standard statistical framework for quantifying uncertainty in these claims.

Method: CIF formalizes interventional evaluations as causal estimands (expectations of bounded scores over input and intervention distributions), then constructs confidence intervals and anytime-valid confidence sequences using Hoeffding-style and variance-adaptive betting approaches. It handles adaptive intervention sampling via bounded mixture importance weighting, allowing valid inference even when interventions are chosen adaptively during evaluation.

Result: Variance-adaptive betting sequences reduce certification cost by 10-30x versus Hoeffding-style bounds. On MNIST abstractions and GPT-2 Small IOI circuits, CIF certifies high-fidelity claims, reveals when apparent method differences lack statistical support, and makes sensitivity to the intervention distribution explicit.

Conclusion: CIF transforms interventional interpretability from point-estimate reporting to statistically rigorous certification, enabling practitioners to distinguish stable causal claims from sampling noise and make principled decisions about when sufficient evidence has been collected.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability often evaluates explanations by intervening on a model: swapping hidden states, patching activations, ablating components, or comparing a compressed model to the original one. These experiments are usually summarized by a point estimate, even though the evaluation may be monitored while it runs or adapted toward suspected failures. This makes it hard to tell whether a reported fidelity or patching effect is a stable causal claim or a consequence of finite sampling and evaluation choices. We introduce Certified Interventional Fidelity (CIF), a statistical layer for interventional interpretability evaluations. CIF first writes the quantity being reported as a causal estimand: an expectation of a bounded score over a stated input distribution and a stated intervention distribution. It then provides confidence intervals and anytime-valid confidence sequences for this estimand, including under adaptive intervention sampling via bounded mixture importance weighting. We instantiate CIF with Hoeffding-style sequences and variance-adaptive betting sequences, the latter reducing certification cost by 10-30x in our experiments. On MNIST abstractions and GPT-2 Small IOI circuits, CIF certifies high-fidelity claims, shows when apparent method differences are not statistically supported, and makes sensitivity to the intervention distribution explicit.

</details>


### [187] [Self-Adaptive Anomaly Detection with Reinforcement Learning and Human Feedback in Connected Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08373)
*Matthias Weiß,Athreya Hosahalli Prakash,Maurice Artelt,Falk Dettinger,Nasser Jazdi,Michael Weyrich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-component online anomaly detection framework for connected vehicles uses an attention-augmented deep Q-network for detector selection, ensemble-based drift detection, and human-in-the-loop retraining. The system achieves F1=0.69 vs ≤0.11 for single detectors, recovers from concept drift (0.52→0.65) after operator-triggered retraining without catastrophic forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Connected vehicles as autonomous CPS require continuous behavior monitoring, but over-the-air updates, configuration changes, and shifting workloads cause the definition of normal behavior to evolve. Static diagnostic methods silently degrade under concept drift, and existing solutions address model adaptation and operator integration separately rather than as a unified supervisory loop.

Method: The framework integrates three coordinated mechanisms: (1) a factorized deep Q-network with self-attention that selects the best anomaly detector per microservice from a candidate pool, leveraging inter-service dependencies; (2) an ensemble of three statistical drift detectors that trigger only on unanimous agreement to prioritize precision; and (3) a human-in-the-loop retraining mechanism using a pending transition buffer with 60/40 prioritized replay to incorporate expert knowledge while preserving prior learned distributions.

Result: On a connected-vehicle testbed running automated valet parking across seven microservices: the attention-augmented DQN achieves F1=0.69 versus ≤0.11 for any single uniform detector. After a real software update induces concept drift, F1 drops to 0.52; operator-triggered retraining restores F1 to 0.65 on the new distribution while maintaining 0.69 on the prior distribution.

Conclusion: The coordinated integration of adaptive detector selection, conservative drift detection, and operator-guided retraining enables sustained anomaly detection performance in evolving CPS environments. The approach successfully balances adaptation to new data distributions with retention of prior knowledge, addressing the silent degradation problem inherent in static diagnostic methods.

Abstract: Connected vehicles are autonomous cyber-physical systems whose behavior must be continuously monitored during operation to detect deviations from normal operation before they propagate into failures. Such evaluation is challenging because the systems themselves evolve: over-the-air updates, configuration changes, and shifting workloads alter the definition of normal behavior, causing static diagnostic methods to degrade silently over time. Existing approaches typically address either automated model adaptation or operator integration in isolation, rather than as a single coordinated supervisory loop.
  This paper presents an online anomaly detection framework for autonomous CPS that integrates three coordinated mechanisms. A factorized deep Q-network with self-attention selects the most suitable detector from a candidate pool for each monitored service, exploiting inter-service dependencies in the microservice topology. An ensemble of three statistical drift detectors monitors the input distribution and raises an alarm only when all three concur, prioritizing precision over recall. A human-in-the-loop retraining mechanism, built around a pending transition buffer and a 60/40 prioritized replay strategy, allows the operator to incorporate expert knowledge while preserving the system's learned response to prior data distributions.
  The framework is evaluated on a connected-vehicle testbed running an automated valet parking application across seven backend microservices. The attention-augmented agent achieves an F1 score of 0.69, compared to at most 0.11 for any single detector applied uniformly. Following a real software update that induces measurable concept drift, F1 drops to 0.52; after operator-triggered retraining, performance recovers to 0.65 on the new distribution while remaining at 0.69 on the prior one, demonstrating sustained adaptation without catastrophic forgetting.

</details>


### [188] [Eigenvalue Calibration for Semantic Embeddings of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08377)
*Sebastian G. Gruber,Nassim Walha,Francis Bach,Florian Buettner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a temperature-scaling calibration framework for eigenvalues of semantic embeddings in LLMs, establishing theoretical guarantees (entropy-risk equivalence, calibration inequality) and empirically demonstrating that LLMs are systematically overconfident.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification is critical for reliable LLM deployment. Eigenvalues of semantic embeddings are increasingly used in state-of-the-art uncertainty methods, but conventional calibration techniques for classification probabilities do not transfer to eigenvalue-based settings. This gap motivates a dedicated calibration framework for embedding eigenvalues.

Method: The authors reinterpret LLMs with semantic embeddings as density matrix predictors. They then introduce temperature scaling applied directly to eigenvalues of these density matrices. The theoretical contribution includes proving entropy-risk equivalence under calibration, deriving a central calibration inequality for eigenvalues, and showing that temperature-scaled eigenvalues minimize proper score risks.

Result: Experiments across diverse real-world settings reveal that current LLMs exhibit systematic overconfidence. The temperature-scaled eigenvalue calibration corrects this miscalibration and validates the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The work establishes both theoretical foundations and practical methods for uncertainty quantification in semantic embeddings, advancing reliable LLM deployment through principled eigenvalue calibration.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification is central to the reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs), and eigenvalues of semantic embeddings have recently emerged as a key tool in state-of-the-art methods. However, conventional calibration results developed for classification probabilities cannot be directly transferred to eigenvalues. We address this gap by proposing a novel framework for calibrating the eigenvalues of semantic embeddings. We interpret LLMs combined with semantic embeddings of their generated answers as density matrix predictors, and we propose a novel approach to calibrate density matrix predictors by applying temperature scaling to their eigenvalues. We establish entropy-risk equivalence under calibration, derive a central calibration inequality specific to eigenvalues, and prove that temperature-scaled eigenvalues optimize calibration when minimizing proper score risks. Experiments on a variety of real-world settings show that current LLMs are systematically overconfident, and validate our theoretical findings. Together, these results advance the foundations and practice of uncertainty quantification for semantic embeddings.

</details>


### [189] [Dynamics of Gradient Descent with Large Step Size Near a Manifold of Flat Minima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08380)
*Lachlan Ewen MacDonald,René Vidal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper extends the theory of large-step gradient descent from scalar-output overparametrized least-squares to vector-valued outputs and from isolated flat minima to manifolds of flat minima, generalizing a normal form and convergence results. It applies the framework to deep matrix factorization, showing the set of flat minima forms a fiber bundle over a product of spheres and that sharpness is Morse-Bott along this manifold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical GD analyses require step size uniformly smaller than 2/sharpness, a condition routinely violated in deep network training. Prior work addressed this gap only for scalar-output least-squares near isolated flat minima. This paper aims to extend the theory to the practically relevant settings of vector-valued outputs and continua (manifolds) of flat minima, as encountered in matrix factorization.

Method: The authors generalize the normal form for large-step GD and all three convergence theorems from prior work to vector-valued outputs and manifold settings. They solve a singular PDE via a novel method to handle technical challenges. They apply the framework to deep matrix factorization, deriving structural results using fiber bundle theory and Morse-Bott analysis.

Result: The normal form and three convergence theorems are successfully extended. For deep matrix factorization under mild assumptions: (1) the set of flat minima is a fiber bundle over a product of spheres, and (2) the sharpness is Morse-Bott along this manifold.

Conclusion: The extended theory provides a rigorous understanding of large-step GD dynamics near manifolds of flat minima for vector-valued overparametrized least-squares, with concrete applications to deep matrix factorization. The novel PDE solution method may have independent mathematical interest.

Abstract: An important quantity in the theory of gradient descent (GD) is the \emph{sharpness}, defined as the largest eigenvalue of the objective Hessian. Classical analyses typically require the step size to be uniformly smaller than twice the reciprocal of the sharpness, but this condition is frequently violated in the training of deep neural networks. Recent work bridges this gap in the setting of overparametrised least-squares with a \emph{single scalar output}, providing a normal form for large-step GD in a neighbourhood of an \emph{isolated} flat minimum and establishing three corresponding convergence results. In this paper, we extend this theory in two directions: (1) to overparametrised least-squares with \emph{vector-valued outputs} (including regression with arbitrarily many observations), and (2) to a neighbourhood of a \emph{manifold} of flat minima (which we show is essential for applications such as matrix factorisation). We generalise both the normal form and all three convergence theorems of \cite{macdonaldeos} to this broader setting, overcoming several technical challenges, including the solution of a singular partial differential equation via a novel method that may be of independent interest. We further show that our framework applies to deep matrix factorisation under mild assumptions, yielding several new structural results. In particular, we prove that the set of flat minima forms a fibre bundle over a product of spheres, and that the sharpness is Morse-Bott along this manifold.

</details>


### [190] [Beyond Backpropagation: Monte Carlo Method Can Train Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08406)
*Hong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A simple random-mutation Monte Carlo algorithm — keep mutations that reduce loss, retry otherwise — can train deep networks on a single GPU without gradients, batch normalization, or residual connections, and flexibly supports pruning, discrete weights, and unconventional activation functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backpropagation suffers from vanishing/exploding gradients. The authors aim to demonstrate that a gradient-free alternative exists that is both simple and practically viable for training deep networks, challenging the presumed necessity of gradient-based methods.

Method: A Monte Carlo mutation algorithm: randomly perturb a parameter, accept the change if loss decreases, otherwise revert and retry. Run on a single GPU. No batch normalization, residual connections, or gradient computation required. Extended to pure pruning training, discrete weights, and unconventional transfer functions like Gaussian.

Result: Successfully trained networks with 20+ layers, wide networks with up to 16,384 hidden neurons, and a simple Transformer on MNIST and Tiny Shakespeare (character-level language modeling). Demonstrated flexibility across pruning, discrete weights, and non-standard activation functions, while revealing substantial redundancy in deep networks.

Conclusion: This gradient-free method offers a complementary perspective on neural network self-organization and learning, and provides an alternative route for building physically inspired deep learning systems, suggesting gradient-based optimization may not be strictly necessary.

Abstract: Backpropagation (BP) dominates deep learning training, but its reliance on gradients brings inherent troubles -- vanishing and exploding gradients. The pursuit of gradient-free methods has long been a goal in the field of artificial intelligence. This paper shows that indeed the simplest Monte Carlo algorithm implemented on a single GPU -- randomly mutate a parameter, keep it if the loss decreases, otherwise retry -- can practically train deep networks. This gradient-free method does not even need common techniques such as batch normalization or residual connections to directly train sufficiently deep networks. More remarkably, its flexibility extends to several nontrivial scenarios: it enables pure pruning training, supports discrete weights, accommodates unconventional transfer functions such as Gaussian, and reveals the substantial redundancy of deep networks. We have demonstrated its feasibility on deep networks with more than 20 layers, single-hidden-layer wide networks with up to 16,384 hidden neurons, and even a simple Transformer architecture trained on both image classification (MNIST) and character-level language modeling (Tiny Shakespeare). This simple gradient-free method may offer a complementary perspective for understanding the self-organization and learning mechanisms of neural networks, and also provides an alternative route for building physically inspired deep learning systems.

</details>


### [191] [Predicting Male Fertility Using Machine Learning: A Semen Parameters Based Analysis with the VISEM Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08429)
*Shahnawaz Qureshi,Raja Khurram Shahzad,Muhammad Fozan,Emal Kawal,Syed Aziz Shah,Sattam Al-Anazi,Syed MuhammadZeeshan Iqbal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using the VISEM dataset (85 participants), this study applied LazyPredict to benchmark 40+ ML classifiers on semen parameters (concentration, motility, morphology) for 3-class fertility classification. Nearest Centroid achieved 94.2% accuracy, outperforming SVM and QDA, with robustness confirmed via 5-fold CV and multiclass ROC-AUC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Male infertility is often underdiagnosed, and traditional semen analysis relies on manual, subjective evaluation. The study aims to provide a fast, accurate, and objective ML-based alternative for classifying fertility status to support clinical decision-making in andrology and assisted reproductive technologies.

Method: The VISEM dataset (85 participants) with three semen parameters (sperm concentration, motility, morphology) was used. Samples were labeled as Fertile, Sub-Fertile, or Infertile per WHO criteria. After preprocessing and feature engineering, the LazyPredict framework was employed to train and compare over 40 classification algorithms. The best model (Nearest Centroid) was validated using 5-fold cross-validation and multiclass ROC-AUC analysis.

Result: The Nearest Centroid classifier achieved the highest accuracy of 94.2%, surpassing Support Vector Machines and Quadratic Discriminant Analysis among the 40+ algorithms tested. 5-fold cross-validation and multiclass ROC-AUC confirmed model robustness.

Conclusion: ML models can deliver rapid, accurate, and objective semen quality assessments, showing strong potential to enhance fertility diagnostics and inform patient-specific treatment strategies in clinical andrology and ART.

Abstract: Male infertility is a significant yet often underdiagnosed aspect of reproductive health, with semen analysis serving as the cornerstone of clinical evaluation. To address this problem, this study investigates the use of machine learning algorithms to classify male fertility status based on key semen parameters, i.e., sperm concentration, motility, and morphology, using the VISEM dataset. This dataset includes semen samples from 85 participants, classified into three categories, i.e., Fertile, Sub-Fertile, and Infertile, according to the World Health Organization's criteria. After pre-processing and feature engineering, the dataset was used to train and assess multiple classification models using the LazyPredict framework. Among the more than 40 algorithms tested, the Nearest Centroid classifier achieved an accuracy of 94.2%, outperforming other models such as Support Vector Machines and Quadratic Discriminant Analysis. The model's robustness was validated using 5-fold cross-validation and multiclass ROC-AUC analysis. This study illustrates that machine learning models can provide fast, accurate, and objective assessments of semen quality, potentially supporting clinical decision-making in andrology and assisted reproductive technologies. These findings emphasize the growing potential of machine learning to enhance fertility diagnostics and inform patient-specific treatment strategies.

</details>


### [192] [MatBind: A Shared Embedding Space for Multimodal Materials Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08470)
*Le Yang,Anoop K. Chandran,Jona Östreicher,Evgenii Sovetkin,Adrian Mirza,Sebastien Bompas,Bashir Kazimi,Pascal Friederich,Stefan Kesselheim,Kevin Maik Jablonka,Stefan Sandfeld*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MatBind is a contrastive learning framework that aligns four materials modalities — crystal structure, simulated pXRD, DOS, and text — into a unified embedding space using structure as the central anchor, enabling emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval and physically meaningful organization without explicit supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crystalline material characterization involves heterogeneous data modalities (atomic structures, diffraction, electronic properties, text) that are typically stored and analyzed in isolation, hindering cross-modal querying and integrated understanding. The authors argue these modalities are complementary projections of the same physical reality and should be treated as such.

Method: MatBind employs contrastive learning with crystal structure as the central physical anchor to align four modalities — crystal structure, simulated powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD), density of states (DOS), and natural language text — into a shared embedding space. Crucially, the framework induces alignment between modality pairs never explicitly paired during training.

Result: The learned embedding space organizes materials according to physically meaningful properties without explicit supervision. The framework achieves emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval between unpaired modalities. Retrieval performance improves systematically when multiple modalities are combined at query time.

Conclusion: Treating heterogeneous materials data as complementary projections of a single physical reality — rather than isolated data sources — is not merely a practical convenience but is consistent with the underlying physics, as demonstrated by the emergent alignment and retrieval capabilities of MatBind.

Abstract: Fully characterizing a crystalline material requires integrating heterogeneous data sources -- atomic structures, diffraction patterns, electronic density of states, and natural language -- each of which captures a different facet of the same physical object. In practice, however, these modalities are stored and analyzed in isolation, making it difficult to relate or query materials across representational boundaries. We present MatBind, a contrastive learning framework that aligns four materials modalities -- crystal structure, powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD) simulated from structures, density of states (DOS), and text -- into a unified embedding space using crystal structure as the central physical anchor. The framework induces alignment between modalities never explicitly paired during training, enabling emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval as a direct consequence of the shared representation. The learned embedding space organizes materials according to physically meaningful properties without explicit supervision, and retrieval performance improves systematically when modalities are combined at query time. These results demonstrate that treating heterogeneous materials data as complementary projections of a single physical reality, rather than as isolated data sources, is not a practical choice but is consistent with the underlying physics.

</details>


### [193] [Frequency-Domain Multi-Modality Transportation Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08475)
*Jiewen Deng,Hangchen Liu,Junchen Li,Boyuan Zhang,Renhe Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FreMo is a lightweight frequency-domain framework for multi-modality transportation forecasting that disentangles per-modality spectral refinement from selective cross-modality synergy, achieving SOTA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-modality transportation forecasting is challenging because different modalities (e.g., traffic flow, public transit) exhibit distinct spectral characteristics and interact unevenly across frequencies. Existing methods operate mostly in the time domain or use coarse feature fusion, failing to capture these frequency-specific dynamics and risking negative transfer between modalities.

Method: FreMo operates in the frequency domain with two key components: (1) Modality-Wise Frequency Filter (MFF), which adaptively refines spectral components within each modality by emphasizing informative frequencies and suppressing noise; (2) Frequency-Guided Synergy Integrator (FSI), which selectively aggregates cross-modality information based on each modality's relative contribution at each frequency. The framework supports plug-and-play integration with general time series backbones.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that FreMo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving superior forecasting performance and generalization across diverse scenarios.

Conclusion: Explicit frequency-domain modeling with adaptive, frequency-selective cross-modality synergy effectively addresses the heterogeneity and uneven interactions in multi-modality transportation forecasting, enabling lightweight yet powerful forecasting.

Abstract: Multi-modality transportation refers to urban systems composed of multiple transportation modes, such as traffic flow and public transit, whose dynamics are coupled by shared temporal patterns. Accurate multi-modality transportation forecasting remains challenging because (1) different modalities exhibit distinct spectral characteristics and (2) interact unevenly across frequencies, whereas most existing methods operate primarily in the time domain or rely on coarse feature fusion. To address these limitations, we propose a lightweight yet effective Frequency-Domain Multi-Modality modeling (FreMo) that explicitly exploits the frequency domain to enable adaptive and selective cross-modality synergy. FreMo disentangles modality-wise spectral refinement from cross-modality synergy and supports plug-and-play integration with general time series backbones. Specifically, FreMo introduces a Modality-Wise Frequency Filter (MFF) to adaptively refine spectral components within each modality, emphasizing informative frequencies while suppressing noise. FreMo further incorporates a Frequency-Guided Synergy Integrator (FSI) that selectively aggregates information across modalities based on their relative contribution at each frequency, facilitating effective cross-modality knowledge sharing while mitigating negative transfer. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets show that FreMo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with superior performance and generalization across diverse forecasting scenarios. The code is available at https://github.com/beginner-sketch/FreMo.

</details>


### [194] [Systematic Evaluation of Learning Rate Scheduling Strategies Across Heterogeneous Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08511)
*Hafsa Mateen,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic evaluation of 25 learning rate scheduler configurations across 30 neural network architectures (convolutional and transformer) on CIFAR-10, finding that CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperform basic decay strategies, with scheduler effectiveness being highly architecture-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning rate scheduling strategy selection is critical yet costly and rarely exhaustive in practice. Classical AutoML approaches typically treat the scheduler as a secondary hyperparameter, leaving a gap in systematic understanding of how scheduler choice interacts with architecture choice for classification accuracy.

Method: The authors leveraged the LEMUR neural network dataset, evaluating 30 representative architectures from convolutional and transformer families. Using automated source-code injection, they applied 25 scheduler configurations spanning nine PyTorch scheduler families, resulting in 3,938 total model variants evaluated on the CIFAR-10 dataset.

Result: The best configuration reached 86.45% top-1 accuracy, with 237 variants exceeding 80%. CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperformed basic decay strategies (e.g., step, exponential decay). Scheduler effectiveness was found to be heavily architecture-dependent. The resulting accuracy landscape was contributed to the LEMUR nn-dataset.

Conclusion: Scheduler selection should not be treated as a secondary concern; it significantly impacts accuracy in an architecture-dependent manner. CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR emerge as robust default choices. The contributed accuracy landscape provides a practical reference for principled scheduler selection in future work.

Abstract: Choosing a learning rate scheduling strategy is critical to neural network training, but manual selection is costly and rarely exhaustive. While classical AutoML approaches often treat the scheduler as a secondary hyperparameter, we systematically investigate its impact on classification accuracy across a diverse pool of architectures. We evaluated 30 representative architectures from convolutional and transformer families within the LEMUR neural network dataset. Through automated source-code injection, we applied 25 scheduler configurations across nine PyTorch families, evaluating a total of 3,938 model variants on CIFAR-10. Our best configuration achieved a top-1 accuracy of 86.45%, with 237 variants exceeding 80%. The results show that the choice of scheduler depends heavily on the architecture: CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperform basic decay strategies. The resulting accuracy landscape, contributed to the LEMUR nn-dataset, provides a practical reference for principled scheduler selection.

</details>


### [195] [Stop Guessing When to Stop Testing: Efficient Model Evaluation with Just Enough Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08522)
*Ofir Arviv,Kristjan Greenewald,Yotam Perlitz,Hadar Mulian,Michal Shmueli-Scheuer,Leshem Choshen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes adopting sequential testing for model evaluation, introducing an adaptive framework with tailored stopping criteria (diminishing returns detection, minimum detectable effect size) to balance efficiency and reliability. Demonstrated on the Open VLM Leaderboard, it achieves up to 80% reduction in computational cost with minimal CI width compromise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-size benchmarks are rigid and inefficient: different evaluation objectives (ranking, model selection, iterative testing) require different statistical power, but fixed sample sizes either waste computation or sacrifice reliability. This mismatch is a critical problem for model evaluation.

Method: An adaptive evaluation framework based on sequential testing principles, with stopping criteria customized for evaluation-specific needs — specifically, diminishing returns detection and minimum detectable effect size. The framework dynamically determines when sufficient evidence has been gathered to terminate evaluation.

Result: Applied to the Open VLM Leaderboard, the framework achieves an 80% reduction in computational cost compared to fixed-size evaluation, allowing only a 2.5-point confidence interval width allowance while preserving statistical significance.

Conclusion: Sequential testing offers a principled, efficient, and reliable alternative to fixed-size benchmarks for model evaluation. The field should move toward adaptive evaluation frameworks to better navigate the efficiency–reliability trade-off.

Abstract: The inherent rigidity of fixed-size benchmarks makes them an inefficient tool for model evaluation. Diverse evaluation objectives, including model ranking, model selection and testing throughout development, demand varying levels of statistical power. The mismatch between fixed sample sizes and these diverse needs results in either excessive computational cost or compromised reliability - a critical concern for model evaluation. To overcome these limitations, we call for adoption of sequential testing in our field. We provide an adaptive evaluation framework, that provides a principled way to navigate the trade-off between efficiency and reliability in model evaluation. Our framework combines the established statistical paradigm of sequential testing with stopping criteria tailored to common evaluation needs such as diminishing returns detection, and minimum detectable effect size. We demonstrate its ability to adaptively manage the efficiency-reliability trade-off on the Open VLM Leaderboard, including, for example, a 80% reduction in computational cost compared to fixed-size evaluation (with a 2.5-point CI width allowance) while maintaining statistical significance.

</details>


### [196] [CAAD: Causality-Aware Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection via Multi-Scale Alignment and Structural Causal Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08555)
*Xin Wang,Yunshi Wen,Yanan He,Haotian Xu,Youlan Zhao,Michel Ferreira Cardia Haddad,Tengfei Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes CAAD, a framework that reformulates industrial anomaly detection as continuous verification of Granger causality consistency, using exogenous variables as residuals to detect anomalies through causal relationship breakdowns rather than mere temporal similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing anomaly detection methods focus primarily on capturing temporal similarities in representations but overlook disruptions in internal causal relationships that characterize system failures. There is a need to detect subtle causal shifts caused by external interventions that temporal methods miss.

Method: CAAD models exogenous time-series variables as residuals, identifying anomalies as deviations from Granger causality consistency caused by external interventions. It employs multi-scale alignment to internalize system dynamics and uses a gradient-based matrix to monitor internal causal relationship breakdowns, quantifying causal deviations in both dynamic evolution and relational topology.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world industrial datasets show CAAD achieves high-precision anomaly detection, outperforming most state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: By shifting focus from temporal similarity to causal consistency verification through exogenous variables, CAAD effectively captures subtle causal shifts and provides superior anomaly detection in complex industrial systems.

Abstract: The operational integrity of complex industrial systems relies on precise anomaly detection and diagnosis. The vast majority of existing methods narrowly focus on capturing temporal similarities of representations, often overlooking the disruption of internal causal relationships, which characterizes system failures and latent anomalies. In this paper, we propose a novel framework (CAAD) that reframes anomaly detection as the continuous verification of Granger causality consistency through exogenous variables.
  Specifically, the CAAD framework models exogenous time-series variables as residuals, identifying anomalies as significant deviations caused by external interventions. The proposed framework leverages multi-scale alignment to internalize system dynamics and utilizes a gradient-based matrix to monitor internal causal relationship breakdowns. By quantifying causal deviations of both dynamic evolution and relational topology, the CAAD is able to capture subtle causal shifts to achieve precise anomaly detection. Extensive experiments on real-world industrial datasets demonstrate that the CAAD achieves high-precision anomaly detection, outperforming most state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [197] [Contravariance Theory: Strong Alignment for Minimal Solutions to Hard Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08561)
*Dan Yamins,Aran Nayebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For sufficiently hard tasks, any two minimal DNN solutions exhibit weak representational alignment (via affine mappings) that guarantees strong alignment of privileged axes, and this alignment "zippers" up the network hierarchy, making convergent evolution between artificial and biological networks likely inevitable and the choice of comparison metric less sensitive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fundamental questions in NeuroAI about how to compare DNNs to brains and whether convergent evolution between artificial and real neural networks should be expected, formalizing the conditions under which different networks trained on the same task develop aligned representations.

Method: Theoretical analysis proving that for two minimal DNN solutions to a sufficiently hard task: (1) weak alignment via affine mappings implies strong alignment of privileged axes, and (2) alignment propagates ("zippers") upward through the network hierarchy due to end-to-end task optimization. The work formalizes the notion of contravariance from Cao and Yamins (2024).

Result: Two key theoretical findings: weak representational alignment (affine-mapping-based) guarantees strong alignment of privileged axes, and alignment zippers up the network hierarchy, causing privileged axes to emerge naturally from end-to-end optimization on sufficiently hard tasks.

Conclusion: With sufficiently strong tasks, the specific metric used for inter-network comparison is not highly sensitive, and convergent evolution between DNNs and biological brains is probably inevitable — offering important constraints for NeuroAI theory.

Abstract: A series of results from the NeuroAI over the past fifteen years have raised core questions both about how to compare Deep Neural Network (DNN) models to the brain, and about how much convergent evolution to expect between artificial networks and real brain networks. Here, we show that for any two minimal DNN solutions to a sufficiently hard task: (i) "weak" alignment of network representations based on affine mappings guarantees "strong" alignment of privileged axes, and (ii) alignment "zippers" up the network hierarchy, causing the emergence of privileged axes from end-to-end task optimization. These results formalize the notion of contravariance from Cao and Yamins [2024], and illustrate important consequences for the theory of NeuroAI: with sufficiently strong tasks, choice of metric for inter-network comparison is not all that sensitive, and that convergent evolution is probably inevitable.

</details>


### [198] [Spectral Stability of Pseudoinverse-Based Extreme Learning Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08581)
*Bich Van Nguyen,Ngoc Anh Khong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ELM's numerical stability is governed by the singular value spectrum of the hidden layer matrix; the smallest singular value controls perturbation amplification, the condition number quantifies instability, and SVD-based pseudoinverse methods prove more reliable than iterative alternatives under ill-conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although ELM trains rapidly via analytical Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, its numerical stability is heavily dependent on the conditioning of the hidden layer matrix — a relationship not well understood from a spectral perspective, motivating this rigorous spectral analysis.

Method: The authors adopt a spectral analysis framework: they theoretically characterize how the smallest singular value governs perturbation amplification in output weights, use the condition number as a quantitative instability measure, derive width-dependent conditioning via random feature theory, and empirically compare SVD-based pseudoinverse against iterative hyperpower methods on synthetic matrices and ELM benchmarks.

Result: SVD-based pseudoinverse computation consistently outperforms iterative hyperpower methods in reliability under ill-conditioned settings. Iterative methods exhibit greater sensitivity to the spectral properties (singular value decay, condition number) of the hidden layer matrix. Width-dependent conditioning trends align with predictions from random feature theory.

Conclusion: ELM stability is fundamentally determined by the singular value structure of the hidden layer matrix. SVD-based pseudoinverse methods are recommended for robust deployment, especially when hidden layer conditioning is poor.

Abstract: Extreme Learning Machine (ELM) computes output weights analytically using the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse. Although this leads to fast training, its numerical stability depends strongly on the conditioning of the hidden layer matrix. This paper studies pseudoinverse-based ELM from a spectral perspective. We show that the smallest singular value governs perturbation amplification in the output weights, while the condition number provides a quantitative measure of hidden-layer instability. We compare SVD-based pseudoinverse computation with iterative hyperpower methods and discuss width-dependent conditioning through a random feature interpretation. Experiments on synthetic matrices and ELM benchmarks show that SVD-based methods remain the most reliable under ill conditioning, while iterative methods are more sensitive to spectral properties. The results suggest that ELM stability is fundamentally governed by the singular value structure of the hidden layer matrix.

</details>


### [199] [Robust Bayesian Decision Making under Adversarial Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08590)
*Haripriya Harikumar,Sammie Katt,Yasir Zubayr Barlas,Samuel Kaski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a sequential adversarially robust decision-aware experimental design framework that accounts for worst-case hidden effects during data acquisition, yielding more stable and reliable downstream decisions compared to conventional methods that converge to fragile high-confidence solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard decision-aware experimental design methods assume well-specified outcome models and rely on decision stability under perturbations. In practice, hidden or weakly modeled effects can substantially alter decision optimality, leading to misleading conclusions. There is a need for experimental design criteria that explicitly target decision robustness against adversarial variation rather than nominal optimality.

Method: The authors build on Bayesian decision theory to formalize an adversarially robust optimal decision, modeling unexpected effects as variation in adversarial variables. They derive a principled Bayesian experimental design criterion that explicitly targets decision stability. The approach is sequential, where each data acquisition step considers information gain against plausible worst-case adversarial perturbations.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world scientific datasets demonstrate that conventional decision-aware design converges rapidly to high-confidence but fragile decisions, while the proposed robustness-aware approach produces decisions that are significantly more stable and reliable under adversarial variation.

Conclusion: Incorporating adversarial robustness into decision-aware experimental design fundamentally shifts the objective from nominal optimality to decision stability, yielding more trustworthy outcomes for real-world scientific applications where hidden effects are pervasive.

Abstract: Scientific experiments are often designed to maximize information gain, yet in many applications the primary objective is to support reliable downstream decision-making. Existing decision-aware experimental design and active learning methods typically assume well-specified outcome models and implicitly rely on the stability of the optimal decision under real-world perturbations. In practice, however, experimental outcomes are frequently influenced by hidden or weakly modeled effects, which can substantially alter decision optimality and lead to misleading conclusions. We study sequential adversarially robust decision-aware experimental design, where data acquisition has to take into account information gain against plausible worst-case unexpected effects, modeled here as variation in adversarial variables. Building on Bayesian decision theory, we formalize an adversarially robust optimal decision under this setting and derive a principled Bayesian experimental design criterion. The criterion explicitly targets decision stability rather than nominal optimality. Experiments on synthetic and real-world scientific datasets show that conventional decision-aware design can converge rapidly to high confidence yet fragile decisions, while our robustness-aware approach yields decisions that are significantly more stable and reliable under adversarial variation.

</details>


### [200] [Steering Neural Network Training through Interpretable Constraints Based on Partial Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08641)
*Yann Claes,Pierre Geurts,Vân Anh Huynh-Thu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces an explanation-guided learning method for regression problems that uses partial dependence to steer neural network training, ensuring model behavior aligns with domain knowledge. The approach yields better performance, improved data efficiency, and more faithful explanations compared to unconstrained models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While interpretability techniques are widely studied, few methods assess explanation quality or actively guide model training to produce explanations faithful to prior knowledge — a process called explanation-guided learning. Existing approaches predominantly target classification and assume prior knowledge about important input features/regions, leaving regression problems underexplored.

Method: The authors propose a novel explanation-guided learning approach for neural networks based on partial dependence. The method constrains training so that the model's average response to specific features aligns with user-supplied functional domain knowledge, effectively steering the learned function to match known relationships.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple regression tasks, including dynamical systems forecasting, shows that the proposed method produces models that outperform unconstrained baselines, achieve greater data efficiency, and yield interpretations that genuinely align with user-provided domain knowledge.

Conclusion: Steering neural network training via partial dependence-based explanation-guided learning is an effective strategy for regression problems — it simultaneously improves predictive performance, data efficiency, and interpretive faithfulness to domain knowledge.

Abstract: Over the last few years, there has been an increased interest in making machine learning models more interpretable. Although a great deal of effort goes into developing techniques for interpreting the interactions learned by a given model, fewer studies focus on assessing the quality of such explanations. Even fewer focus on how to adjust the model to produce explanations faithful to prior knowledge, a process known as explanation-guided learning. Furthermore, most approaches in this area focus on classification problems and usually assume prior knowledge about which input features or regions are most important. In this work, we introduce a new approach to steering neural networks based on partial dependence, such that their average response to certain features aligns with specific functional domain knowledge about the problem. We empirically demonstrate on a range of regression problems, including dynamical systems forecasting, that models whose training has been controlled using our method perform better than unconstrained models and are more data-efficient. Moreover, we highlight that interpretations obtained from the former actually align with the user-provided knowledge, whereas those obtained from the latter do not.

</details>


### [201] [BiSCo-LLM: Lookup-Free Binary Spherical Coding for Extreme Low-Bit Large Language Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08643)
*Yuantian Shao,Peisong Wang,Zhilei Liu,Chuangyi Li,Yuanteng Chen,Pengcheng Xie,Yiwu Yao,Zhihui Wei,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BiSCo-LLM proposes a codebook-free binary spherical coding method for compressing LLM weights to extreme low-bit regimes (~2 bits). It maps weight chunks to a unit hypersphere, binarizes them into compact spherical codes, adds a residual coding stage for reconstruction error, and uses category-wise recovery distillation to align local reconstruction with global model behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face deployment bottlenecks in memory, bandwidth, and storage. Existing low-bit methods fall short: scalar/group-wise quantization loses representation capacity near 2 bits per weight, while vector quantization introduces explicit codebooks and index lookup overhead. The goal is extreme low-bit compression without codebook storage overhead.

Method: BiSCo-LLM employs three stages: (1) Binary Spherical Quantization (BSQ) — local weight chunks are projected onto a unit hypersphere and binarized into sign streams, yielding bit-packed codes without explicit centroids. (2) Residual BSQ — a second-stage codec encodes the reconstruction residual from the base stage, providing rate-distortion control without stored codebooks. (3) Category-wise Recovery Distillation — after replacing each Transformer module type (attention, MLP, etc.), distillation is applied category-by-category to mitigate the mismatch between local weight reconstruction and end-to-end model outputs. An 8-bit protected-channel path serves as auxiliary stabilization for sensitive channels, counted separately in the total storage budget (which also includes binary codes, neural decoders, LoRA adapters, and metadata).

Result: The paper reports storage budgets that comprehensively account for all components: binary codes, neural decoders, protected-channel payloads, LoRA adapters, and metadata. The spherical coding framework achieves extreme low-bit compression (approaching 2 bits per weight) while maintaining model quality through the residual coding and category-wise distillation pipeline.

Conclusion: BiSCo-LLM demonstrates that codebook-free binary spherical coding is a viable path for extreme LLM weight compression, combining the representational richness of vector-level coding with the simplicity of bit-packed storage, and that category-wise recovery distillation effectively bridges local and global reconstruction objectives.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly constrained by memory capacity, weight bandwidth, and checkpoint storage during deployment. Existing low-bit compression methods mainly follow two directions. Scalar or group-wise quantization is simple and compatible with efficient low-precision kernels, but its representation capacity becomes limited when the target budget approaches 2 bits per weight. Vector-quantized weight compression provides a richer block-level representation, but usually introduces explicit codebooks, index lookup, and additional storage accounting. This paper presents BiSCo-LLM, a codebook-free binary spherical coding framework for extreme low-bit LLM weight compression. The core pipeline is built on three components. First, local weight chunks are mapped onto a unit hypersphere and binarized into compact spherical codes, so that the main payload is a bit-packed sign stream rather than explicit VQ centroids. Second, a residual BSQ stage encodes the reconstruction error left by the base spherical codec, providing an explicit rate-distortion path without stored codebooks. Third, category-wise recovery distillation is performed after replacing each Transformer module category, reducing the mismatch between local weight reconstruction and assembled model behavior. A small 8-bit protected-channel path is used as an auxiliary stabilization mechanism for sensitive channels and is counted separately from the BSQ payload. The reported storage budget includes binary codes, neural decoders, protected-channel payloads, LoRA adapters, and metadata.

</details>


### [202] [Multi-Modal, Multi-Environment Machine Teaching for Robust Reward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08647)
*Ali Larian,Qian Lin,Chang Zong Wu,Daniel S. Brown*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses reward learning for agents deployed across multiple environments. It first theoretically proves that comparisons provide strictly stronger constraints than other feedback modalities in unlimited-data regimes. It then introduces a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm that greedily selects informative environments and strategically queries low-cost feedback within them. The method outperforms uniform teaching baselines in regret and generalization under identical feedback budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IRL analyses focus on single-environment, demonstration-only settings. Rewards inferred from demonstrations in one MDP entangle with that environment's specific structure, causing generalization failures in new deployment settings. The interplay between heterogeneous feedback modalities and multi-environment dynamics for learning generalizable rewards remains underexplored.

Method: The authors first conduct a theoretical analysis comparing how different feedback modalities (demonstrations, comparisons, etc.) constrain reward functions, proving comparisons impose strictly stronger global constraints in the unlimited-data regime. They then propose a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm that operates across multiple MDPs: it greedily selects informative environments to expose complementary reward constraints, then strategically queries low-cost feedback within those selected environments.

Result: Theoretically, comparisons are shown to be the most constraining feedback modality. Empirically, the proposed hierarchical teaching method achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments compared to uniform teaching baselines, all under identical feedback budgets.

Conclusion: Multi-environment, multi-modal teaching is essential for learning dynamics-robust reward functions. Strategic environment selection combined with cost-aware feedback querying within environments yields significantly better generalization than uniform approaches.

Abstract: As autonomous agents are increasingly deployed across diverse operational contexts, aligning their behavior with human intent demands reward functions that remain robust to such changes rather than overfitting to any single environment. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a principled way to infer such objectives from human feedback. However, existing analyses of optimal teaching approaches for IRL focus on single-environment, demonstration-only settings, leaving underexplored how heterogeneous feedback modalities and environment dynamics jointly constrain reward functions that generalize across multiple environments. Because demonstrations in one MDP entangle reward information with that environments specific structure, the resulting rewards frequently fail to generalize when the agent is deployed in a new setting. We first analyze how different feedback modalities constrain rewards, showing that, in the unlimited-data regime, comparisons impose strictly stronger global constraints than other modalities. Beyond this theoretical analysis, we introduce a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm for reward learning that operates across multiple MDPs. The algorithm first greedily selects informative environments that expose complementary reward constraints, then strategically queries low-cost feedback within those environments. Empirically, our method achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments than uniform teaching baselines under identical feedback budgets, demonstrating the importance of multi-environment, multi-modal teaching for learning dynamics-robust reward functions.

</details>


### [203] [Secure Decentralized Federated Learning via Gossip and Virtual Voting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08651)
*Amirhossein Taherpour,Xiaodong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: gspDAG-FL proposes a decentralized federated learning framework that uses a gossip-derived DAG and Hashgraph-style virtual voting to achieve provenance finality without global coordination, combining payload validation and semantic auditing to resist Byzantine and lazy participants while maintaining learning quality comparable to ledger-based FL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DFL gossip methods lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine/lazy nodes. Ledger-assisted FL improves auditability but reintroduces global coordination costs (blockchains, shards, settlement committees) that conflict with DFL's locality principle. A solution is needed that achieves consensus and auditability without sacrificing decentralization.

Method: Nodes exchange model payloads only with neighbors. Full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, then reconstruct a compact Topology DAG. Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates achieves finality over unique model-origin tuples (not identical parameter states). Resilience is enhanced via payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. Formal proofs of safety and conditional liveness are provided, along with a convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing.

Result: Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling (N up to 100) show gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

Conclusion: gspDAG-FL successfully combines the locality benefits of gossip-based DFL with the auditability of ledger-assisted FL, deriving consensus directly from the gossip history. It provides a provably safe and convergent decentralized learning framework that is resilient to adversarial participation without reintroducing global coordination overhead.

Abstract: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) removes the central server by letting nodes exchange model updates through peer-to-peer gossip, but existing gossip-based methods often lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine or lazy participants. Ledger-assisted federated learning (FL) improves auditability, yet blockchains, shards, or settlement committees can reintroduce global coordination costs that conflict with DFL locality. This paper proposes \emph{gspDAG-FL}, a secure DFL framework that derives consensus from the same gossip history used to disseminate models.
