Nodes exchange model payloads only with neighbors, while full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a compact Topology directed acyclic graph (DAG), and run Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates. Finality is over unique model-origin tuples, not identical local parameter states. To improve resilience, gspDAG-FL combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. We formalize the adversarial setting, prove safety and conditional liveness of the control plane, and give a convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing. Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling, using fair held-out validation/audit data and networks up to \(N=100\), show that gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

</details>


### [204] [EdgeRefine: Privacy-Utility Balance for Graphs via Jaccard Sampling under Edge Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08659)
*Wenxiu Ding,Muzhi Liu,Zheng Yan,Mingjun Wang,Yifan Zhao,Qiao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EdgeRefine is a local differential privacy framework for GNNs that improves the privacy-utility tradeoff by adaptively refining graph edges using Jaccard similarity-based probability estimation, ranked edge removal, and privacy-budget-guided sampling of true/false edges. It achieves near-noise-free accuracy and substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods on node and graph classification while demonstrating strong resilience to graph reconstruction attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs face a critical privacy-utility tradeoff in sensitive applications: edge-level differential privacy requires injecting noise into adjacency matrices, but stronger privacy guarantees demand more noise, which severely degrades model utility. Existing methods lack effective mechanisms to preserve graph sparsity and structural reliability under privacy constraints.

Method: EdgeRefine operates in three stages: (1) it estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity between node pairs; (2) it ranks edges by these probabilities and removes noisy/low-probability edges; (3) it uses the privacy budget ε to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on the probability ranking, and controls total edge count via a sampling rate k. This yields a sparse, reliable graph under local differential privacy.

Result: At ε=2.5, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines by 17.8% on ACM (GAT) and 19.7% on Cora (GCN). Graph classification sees only ~5% average accuracy degradation versus the noise-free baseline. Under graph reconstruction attacks, relative absolute error remains above 1 across all ε, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP, indicating strong privacy protection.

Conclusion: EdgeRefine effectively balances privacy and utility in graph learning by using adaptive edge refinement guided by Jaccard similarity and a principled sampling strategy tied to the privacy budget. It sets a new state-of-the-art for differentially private GNN training, achieving near-baseline accuracy while resisting reconstruction attacks.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have shown considerable success in learning from graph-structured data, but their use in privacy-sensitive areas remains difficult because graph structure can leak sensitive link information. To satisfy edge-level differential privacy, a common approach is to inject noise into all elements of the graph's adjacency matrix, thereby obfuscating the existence of any single edge. However, stronger privacy requires more noise, and excessive noise reduces utility, making the privacy-utility balance a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.
  To address this issue, we propose EdgeRefine, a local differential privacy framework that improves this trade-off through adaptive edge refinement. EdgeRefine first estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity and ranks edges for noisy edge removal. To ensure the sparsity and reliability of the final graph, it uses the privacy budget $ε$ to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on this probability ranking, and controls the total number of edges with a separate sampling rate $k$. Extensive experiments show that EdgeRefine achieves accuracy comparable to the noise-free baseline and substantially outperforms other privacy-preserving methods across datasets and GNN architectures. Under privacy budget $ε= 2.5$, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines by 17.8\% on ACM under GAT and 19.7\% on Cora under GCN. In graph classification, it achieves an average accuracy degradation of around 5\% compared to the noise-free baseline. Under graph reconstruction attacks, EdgeRefine maintains relative absolute error levels above 1 across all privacy budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP, indicating strong resilience against privacy leakage.

</details>


### [205] [Resample or Reroute? Budget-Aware Test-Time Model Selection for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08665)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query cost budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate spending between resampling the current model and rerouting to another model to maximize expected correctness. The proposed online Resample-or-Reroute (RoR) policy, driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost, achieves a favorable cost-quality Pareto front over multiple baselines across four benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures, but this guarantee only holds under an idealized oracle with correctness labels and unconstrained budget. No existing work treats resampling a committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. This gap motivates a unified budget-aware framework.

Method: The authors formulate budget-aware test-time model selection as an online allocation problem: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each budget unit between resampling (repeatedly querying the same model) and rerouting (switching to a different model) to maximize expected correctness. They propose an online Resample-or-Reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost, grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Experiments use newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty.

Result: The RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines. The largest gains appear on the most heterogeneous benchmark. An ablation shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades. Robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where conclusions carry over.

Conclusion: Budget-aware test-time model selection combining resampling and rerouting under a unified cost constraint yields Pareto improvements over methods that treat them separately. The approach is most effective with strong verifiers and heterogeneous model pools, establishing a principled framework for cost-quality trade-offs in deployed LLM routing systems.

Abstract: Routing among large language models (LLMs) trades response quality against serving cost, motivated by the reported gap between deployed routers and a per-instance oracle. Recent analysis shows that test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures; however, that guarantee holds only under an idealized oracle equipped with correctness labels and an unconstrained budget, neither of which a deployed system has. To the best of our knowledge, no previous work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Therefore, this work formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each unit of budget between resampling and rerouting so that expected correctness is maximized. An online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost is proposed, and its behavior is grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments on newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty show that the proposed RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines for the tested pools, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark; an ablation further shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades, and robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where the conclusions carry over.

</details>


### [206] [A Practical Investigation of Training-free Relaxed Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08690)
*Guoxuan Xia,Luka Ribar,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Investigates training-free relaxed speculative decoding, unifying existing approaches in a shared framework, benchmarking them, and providing practical empirical findings—notably that relaxation requires careful capability evaluation and many relaxed methods depend on strong draft models, making them incompatible with lightweight multi-token-prediction drafters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While standard speculative decoding is lossless (exactly preserving the target LLM's distribution), recent work suggests that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controllable capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability improvements. The authors aim to systematically investigate these relaxed approaches to provide practical guidance for practitioners.

Method: The authors conduct a practical investigation of training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques. They unify existing approaches within a shared conceptual framework, benchmark them on contemporary hardware and model settings, and distill empirical takeaways through systematic evaluation.

Result: Key findings: (1) relaxation requires considerable capability evaluation, unlike lossless speculative decoding where quality is guaranteed; (2) many relaxed approaches rely on the drafter being a good language model, making them unsuitable for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters that excel primarily at drafting rather than language modeling quality.

Conclusion: Practitioners should carefully evaluate capability degradation when using relaxed speculative decoding. The field needs relaxed methods compatible with specialized lightweight drafters, as current approaches overly depend on strong language model drafters.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates sampling from an autoregressive LLM by using a faster auxiliary model to draft tokens which are then verified in parallel by the LLM. Standard speculative decoding is lossless: its rejection and resampling steps exactly preserve the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work argues that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. We practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distil takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners. Important takeaways include: relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

</details>


### [207] [MPFlow: Learning Budgeted Max-Flow Optimization on the Lightning Network with Deep Graph Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08703)
*Harrison Rush,Vincent Davis,Simone Antonelli,Vikash Singh,Jesse Shrader,Emanuele Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph reinforcement learning approach for optimal liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network, using PPO with a hub-exclusion curriculum to maximize max-flow routing capacity, outperforming heuristics and deployed in production with $16M+ in channel allocations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightning Network nodes face the strategic problem of selecting which channels to open under a fixed budget to maximize routing capacity and fee revenue. Existing approaches rely on heuristic hub attachment rather than principled capacity-aware placement, leading to suboptimal liquidity allocation.

Method: The problem is formulated as budget-constrained combinatorial optimization on graphs: selecting k edge additions that maximize s-t max-flow. A lightweight agent using a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum, where top hubs are systematically removed from training subgraphs to force the policy to learn capacity-aware placement strategies rather than defaulting to hub attachment.

Result: The method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on max-flow objectives across multiple seeds and unseen real Lightning Network snapshots. In production deployment across 30 managed nodes, the agent executed 4,640 channel-open decisions, cumulatively allocating 267.3 BTC (over $16 million).

Conclusion: Graph RL with a hub-exclusion curriculum provides a principled, effective solution for liquidity placement in payment channel networks, successfully bridging theory to large-scale production deployment.

Abstract: We address liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network (LN): given a fixed budget, which channels should a node open to maximize its routing capacity? We cast this as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem on graphs, selecting $k$ edge additions that maximize $s$--$t$ max-flow, a theory-grounded measure of routing capacity, and solve it with graph reinforcement learning. Our lightweight agent combines a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking, and is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum: the network's top hubs are removed from training subgraphs, forcing the policy to learn capacity-aware placement rather than hub attachment. In extensive experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots, our method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs. The agent has been deployed in production for peer recommendations, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that cumulatively allocate 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

</details>


### [208] [Deep Learning for Joint Narrowband Interference Cancellation and Soft Demodulation in OFDM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08717)
*Emmanouil Kavvousanos,Francky Catthoor,Vassilis Paliouras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified deep learning framework combining NBI-CNet (physics-informed CNN for interference cancellation) and LLR-CNet (structural whitener for robust soft demodulation) eliminates error floors in OFDM systems under narrowband interference, achieving near-optimal performance with reduced complexity and FFT-size generalization without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical compressed-sensing NBI mitigation suffers from high latency, leaves structured non-Gaussian residuals, and causes LLR unreliability/decoder saturation when paired with Gaussian demappers, resulting in severe error floors. The pipeline mismatch between NBI cancellation and soft demodulation needs a unified solution.

Method: A two-stage deep learning framework: (1) NBI-CNet — a physics-informed convolutional neural network that estimates NBI parameters and removes multi-tone interference in a single forward pass without prior knowledge of interferer count; (2) LLR-CNet — a structural whitener that maps non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated log-likelihood ratios for robust soft demodulation. The architecture is scale-invariant, enabling generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

Result: Under severe interference (SIR=−10 dB), the framework operates within 0.2–0.5 dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at BLER=10⁻⁴. Under mild interference (SIR=10 dB, Q=12) with heavy spectral overlap, it delivers >3 dB coding gain by avoiding signal-peak confusion that plagues greedy algorithms. It eliminates the 2×10⁻⁴ error floor from interferer-estimation errors, and reduces complexity by up to 60% versus EOMP-IDS (N=2048, Q=64).

Conclusion: The joint NBI-CNet + LLR-CNet framework successfully resolves the pipeline mismatch between interference cancellation and soft demodulation, eliminating error floors, approaching optimal iterative performance, and offering robust generalization across FFT sizes — establishing deep learning as a viable unified approach for NBI-resilient OFDM reception.

Abstract: Narrowband interference (NBI) severely degrades orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional compressed-sensing (CS) mitigation exhibits high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals that cause log-likelihood ratio (LLR) unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when employing classical Gaussian demappers. We resolve this pipeline mismatch using a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation. First, NBI-CNet employs a physics-informed convolutional architecture to estimate NBI parameters and remove multi-tone interference in a single forward pass. Without requiring prior knowledge of the active interferer count, NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% ($N{=}2048, Q{=}64$) compared to the state-of-the-art EOMP-IDS algorithm. Second, LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener by mapping non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Simulations demonstrate that this joint framework eliminates the error floors inherent to traditional baselines across dense grids. Under severe interference ($\text{SIR}{=}{-}10$ dB), the pipeline operates within a $0.2$ to $0.5$ dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at a target block error rate (BLER) of $10^{-4}$. Under mild interference ($\text{SIR}{=}10$ dB) with heavy spectral overlap ($Q{=}12$), where classical greedy algorithms erroneously subtract valid data components and corrupt the payload, NBI-CNet avoids signal-peak confusion to deliver a coding gain exceeding $3$ dB. Finally, the architecture circumvents the $2{\times}10^{-4}$ error floor triggered by interferer-estimation errors, while its scale-invariant design enables robust generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

</details>


### [209] [Latent Memory Palace: Reasoning for Control as Autoregressive Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08724)
*Chuning Zhu,Eva Xu,Jose Barreiros,Krishnan Srinivasan,Paarth Shah,Abhishek Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LMP formulates reasoning for continuous control as variational inference over an autoregressive latent space, enabling adaptive test-time compute allocation. It produces both a performant control policy (LMP-π) and an improved action tokenizer (LMP-tok).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While language models can adaptively allocate compute via chain-of-thought reasoning, transferring this capability to continuous control policies is difficult because reasoning in language space lacks the granularity needed for spatial understanding and precise motor actions. The authors seek a latent reasoning mechanism suited for control.

Method: The authors introduce Latent Memory Palace (LMP), which organizes information in an autoregressive latent space — analogous to a memory palace — where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Reasoning is cast as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution, and a latent-space reinforcement learning technique is derived to tractably optimize the variational lower bound. Two instantiations are presented: LMP-π (a control policy) and LMP-tok (a variable-length action tokenizer).

Result: LMP-π achieves strong empirical performance in both simulation and real-world domains, with interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. LMP-tok significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies.

Conclusion: The results establish latent reasoning for control through variational inference as a promising paradigm, unifying adaptive computation, interpretability, and improved policy performance within a single probabilistic framework.

Abstract: Human decision-making is highly flexible -- some actions are taken immediately; others require longer deliberation. Language models have exhibited a similar capacity for adaptive "reasoning." However, transferring this capability to continuous control policies has been challenging, as directly reasoning in language space may lack the granularity for spatial understanding and precise motions. In this work, we show that reasoning for control policies can emerge by organizing information in an autoregressive latent space reminiscent of a memory palace, where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Our method, Latent Memory Palace (LMP), formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. We derive a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to tractably optimize its variational lower bound. The resulting policy, LMP-$π$, achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains while exhibiting interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. We further show that the same framework yields a variable-length action tokenizer, LMP-$\texttt{tok}$, which significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies. Together, these results present a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through the lens of variational inference.

</details>


### [210] [Super Weights in LLMs and the Failure of Selective Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08733)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Adewale Akinfaderin,Akarsha Sehwag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the notion that Super Weights — individually critical parameters — are effective targets for isolated fine-tuning. The authors demonstrate that training Super Weights in isolation collapses model accuracy to random-guessing levels, while low-rank structured updates (LoRA) applied across entire layers succeed with far fewer parameters. Parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work has identified "Super Weights" as individual parameters whose removal catastrophically degrades LLM performance, suggesting these parameters are uniquely important. The natural follow-up question is whether targeting these important parameters for fine-tuning would be especially effective. This paper investigates that hypothesis and finds it to be false, motivating a deeper understanding of the relationship between parameter importance (for inference) and parameter trainability (for fine-tuning).

Method: The authors conduct systematic experiments on OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B. They: (1) verify that Super Weight pruning degradation is not universal across all LLMs; (2) attempt to fine-tune only Super Weights (ranging from 100 to 8,192 parameters) in isolation; (3) expand training to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters around Super Weights; (4) compare against training an equal number of randomly chosen parameters in the same layers; (5) benchmark against vanilla LoRA applied to attention matrices and down_proj layers; and (6) run a 10-seed ablation constraining LoRA updates at Super Weight coordinates to isolate the effect of position.

Result: Training Super Weights in isolation drops accuracy to random-guessing levels. Expanding to local neighborhoods provides no improvement. Training randomly chosen parameters in the same layers instead improves over the baseline, confirming the failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates rather than sparsity. Vanilla LoRA succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters. Constraining LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results from the full LoRA, showing that the structured, layer-wide decomposition — not targeting important weights — is what enables effective fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Parameter importance (as measured by pruning sensitivity) does not imply parameter trainability in isolation. Effective fine-tuning relies on structured, low-rank decompositions applied across entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights. This decouples the concepts of weight criticality for inference from weight suitability for gradient-based adaptation.

Abstract: Recent work identified Super Weights, individual parameters whose removal degrades model performance by orders of magnitude. We show that this degradation due to pruning Super Weights does not universally apply to all LLMs. Furthermore, if these parameters are so important, Super Weight-aware training should be effective. We show the opposite. Training Super Weights in isolation (100 to 8,192 parameters) drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B, and expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters provides no improvement. The failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates: training an equal number of randomly chosen positions in the same down_proj layers instead improves over the baseline, so the collapse comes from targeting Super Weights, not from sparsity itself. Vanilla LoRA, updating every position in attention weight matrices through low-rank structure, succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters, and applying the same low-rank update to down_proj succeeds as well. A 10-seed ablation confirms that constraining LoRA updates at positions corresponding to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results. These findings establish that parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation, and that effective fine-tuning relies on structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

</details>


### [211] [SLORR: Simple and Efficient In-Training Low-Rank Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08754)
*David González-Martínez,Shiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLORR is a stateless, architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization that uses GPU-friendly approximations to the Hoyer sparsity metric and nuclear norm. It induces compressibility in models (CNNs, ViTs, LLMs) with minimal training overhead (<1-8%) while preserving accuracy better than unregularized training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern neural networks are not naturally amenable to aggressive low-rank factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers suffer from drawbacks: requiring expensive SVDs of large weight matrices, modifying model architecture with extra trainable parameters, or relying on stateful cached quantities. A simple, stateless, architecture-preserving alternative is needed.

Method: SLORR (Simple, Stateless, Architecture-Preserving Low-Rank Regularization) directly regularizes original weight matrices using two variants: (1) SLORR-Hoyer based on the Hoyer sparsity metric applied to singular values, and (2) SLORR-Nuclear based on the nuclear norm. Both use GPU-friendly approximations for forward and backward passes, with formal approximation guarantees provided. The method avoids SVD during training, adds no extra parameters, and requires no cached state.

Result: On ImageNet-1K: SLORR induces compressibility in short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, with <8% training overhead. On LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-Hoyer-trained compressed models substantially outperform unregularized baselines with <1% average training overhead.

Conclusion: SLORR provides an effective and lightweight approach to in-training low-rank regularization that works across diverse model architectures (CNNs, ViTs, LLMs) and scales, enabling better post-training compression without sacrificing training efficiency or requiring architectural modifications.

Abstract: Low-rank factorization is widely used to compress neural networks, but modern models are often not naturally amenable to aggressive factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers can improve compressibility, but they often require SVDs of large weight matrices, modify the model architecture (introducing additional trainable parameters), or rely on stateful cached quantities. To address these limitations, we introduce SLORR, a simple, stateless, and architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization, instantiated with two main variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm. SLORR directly regularizes the original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for the forward and backward passes of the regularizers, for which we provide approximation guarantees. We first evaluate SLORR on ImageNet-1K across short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, where SLORR induces compressibility while introducing less than 8% training overhead. We further evaluate SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-trained compressed models preserve performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [212] [BACH: A Bayesian Admixture of Contrastive Heads for Multi-Interest Two-Tower Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08107)
*Quoc Phong Nguyen,Paul Albert,Long Vuong,Vuong Le,Julien Monteil*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: BACH introduces a Bayesian variational framework for multi-interest two-tower retrieval, modeling users as soft mixtures over interest heads to mitigate routing collapse and produce per-user interest weights reusable at serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-vector two-tower retrievers compress diverse user interests into one embedding, limiting expressiveness. Existing multi-interest models with hard routing suffer from routing collapse (under-utilized heads) and lack per-user estimates of how much each interest matters during serving.

Method: BACH frames multi-interest retrieval as a per-user mixture model over contrastive heads, trained via variational inference (ELBO maximization). A learned prior and amortized posterior produce soft assignments, ensuring every head receives gradient signal. The model supports a shared global-codebook variant enabling precomputable retrieval via maximum inner-product search over discrete codes.

Result: On MovieLens-20M, Taobao, and Netflix benchmarks, BACH outperforms hard-routing multi-interest and single-vector baselines at every head count. Scoring each candidate by its best head (consistent with serving) outperforms target-routed training; BACH yields further gains.

Conclusion: Soft mixture modeling via variational inference effectively addresses routing collapse in multi-interest two-tower retrieval, providing both improved ranking quality and interpretable per-user interest weights for serving.

Abstract: Two-tower retrievers compress each user into a single embedding, limiting their ability to serve diverse interests. Multi-interest models give each user several heads scored by a maximum inner product, but their hard-routing training under-utilizes heads (routing collapse) and gives no per-user estimate of how much each interest matters for serving. We present \textbf{BACH} (\emph{Bayesian Admixture of Contrastive Heads}), which casts multi-interest two-tower retrieval as a per-user mixture over the heads, fit by variational inference. The soft mixture trains every head (mitigating collapse), produces a per-user weighting of the interests that is reused at serving, and admits a shared global-codebook variant with precomputable retrieval. On three large-scale benchmarks, MovieLens-20M, Taobao, and Netflix, BACH improves top-of-ranking retrieval over hard-routing multi-interest and single-vector baselines at every head count; we further find that scoring every candidate by its best head, consistent with serving, outperforms the usual target-routed training, and that BACH improves further still.

</details>


### [213] [DaV-Gen: End-to-End Generative Retrieval via Draft-and-Verify](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08365)
*Meng Zhao,Chunmei Liu,Qinyong Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: DaV-Gen proposes a unified "Draft-and-Verify" framework for search and recommendation that replaces multi-stage cascade architectures. It trains a single model with dual capabilities — efficient vector-based drafting followed by fused generative+vector verification — achieving both speed and precision in an end-to-end architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-Stage Cascade Architectures (MCAs) suffer from inconsistent optimization objectives across stages, causing early-stage errors to propagate and degrade final results. While end-to-end generative models could unify the pipeline, their auto-regressive decoding is too slow for online serving.

Method: DaV-Gen uses a "Draft-and-Verify" mechanism inspired by speculative decoding. A single model is jointly trained with: (1) a contrastive loss for structuring the embedding space to enable efficient vector-based candidate drafting, and (2) a fusion loss combining generative likelihood with vector similarity for fine-grained verification. At inference, fast vector drafting generates candidates, which are then re-scored by the fused verification function.

Result: The paper claims the framework achieves both the speed of sparse (vector-based) drafting and the precision of advanced generative models within a unified, end-to-end architecture, though specific quantitative results are not detailed in this abstract.

Conclusion: DaV-Gen refactors the search/recommendation paradigm by unifying drafting and verification in a single model, resolving the inconsistency problem of MCAs while avoiding the inference latency of standard auto-regressive generative models.

Abstract: Mainstream industrial information retrieval systems (e.g., search and recommendation) are usually built upon Multi-Stage Cascade Architectures (MCAs), which balance effectiveness and efficiency through a coarse-to-fine ``retrieval-ranking'' pipeline. However, the optimization objectives across different stages are substantially inconsistent, propagating or even amplifying the early-stage errors that ultimately degrade the quality of final results. While emerging end-to-end generative models offer a potential solution by unifying the pipeline, their online serving performance is severely hindered by the auto-regressive process inherited from the standard decoder-only structure. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textbf{DaV-Gen}, a novel unified solution designed to fundamentally refactor the paradigm for both search and recommendation via a ``Draft-and-Verify'' mechanism. Inspired by the process used by speculative decoding, our framework redesigns the generation task into two synergistic operations within a single model. During training, the model is concurrently optimized for both candidate drafting and fine-grained verification. This is achieved by a composite loss function that jointly trains the model on two distinct but related objectives: 1) a contrastive loss that structures the embedding space for efficient drafting, and 2) a fusion loss that combines generative likelihood with vector similarity to produce a superior verification score. This integrated training strategy equips the model with dual capabilities. At inference time, it first performs highly efficient vector-based drafting to generate a candidate set, and then verifies these candidates using the more powerful fused scoring function, thereby achieving both the speed of sparse drafting and the precision of advanced generative models within a unified, end-to-end architecture.

</details>


### [214] [H3D: Benchmarking Unsupervised Text Hashing for Fine-Grained Document Deduplication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08382)
*Qianren Mao,Jiaxun Lyu,Junnan Liu,Zhijun Chen,Jingzheng Li,Hanwen Hao,Bo Li*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: H3D is an unsupervised text hashing benchmark that systematically compares non-learning hashing methods (MinHash, SimHash, etc.) with semantic-sensitive methods (BGE-based) on fine-grained scientific document deduplication, evaluating ranking quality, efficiency, and robustness across two complementary datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing document hashing studies lack a unified evaluation protocol for fine-grained scientific document deduplication. Prior work either focuses on coarse web document similarity or evaluates methods in isolation, making fair comparisons difficult. The authors aim to fill this gap by establishing a standardized benchmark spanning both lexical/structural and semantic-sensitive hashing approaches.

Method: H3D evaluates five non-learning hashing methods (MinHash, SimHash, Winnowing, FuzzyHash, FlyHash) and two semantic-sensitive methods (BGE-BIHash, BGE-LSHash) built from frozen BGE embeddings with quantization strategies. Benchmarking is conducted on CSFCube (facet-level scientific similarity) and RELISH (biomedical split-level similarity search), using metrics: MAP, NDCG@20, efficiency, and robustness under controlled text compression.

Result: A consistent trade-off emerges: lexical/structural fingerprints (non-learning methods) perform competitively for near-duplicate matching, while semantic-sensitive representations better preserve similarity under content rewriting but incur higher computational cost. The paper also delineates conditions under which different similarity measures become rank-equivalent for specific hash representations.

Conclusion: No single hashing method dominates all scenarios; method selection should depend on the deduplication goal (near-duplicate vs. semantic paraphrase). The benchmark improves interpretability and reproducibility of hashing comparisons for scientific documents, and the rank-equivalence analysis aids in understanding when different evaluation measures yield consistent rankings.

Abstract: Document hashing provides compact representations for efficient similarity search and document deduplication, but existing studies rarely compare hashing pipelines under a unified protocol for fine-grained scientific documents. H3D is an unsupervised text hashing benchmark for fine-grained document deduplication. It evaluates representative unsupervised non-learning hashing approaches (MinHash, SimHash, Winnowing, FuzzyHash, FlyHash) together with semantic-sensitive methods built from frozen BGE embeddings and two quantization strategies (BGE-BIHash and BGE-LSHash). The non-learning methods generate hash fingerprints through manually designed mathematical rules without training or labeled similarity pairs, which distinguishes them from neural semantic hashing models. We benchmark all methods on CSFCube and RELISH, two datasets that provide complementary evaluation settings: facet-level analysis for scientific-document similarity and larger-scale split-level evaluation for biomedical similarity search. H3D jointly reports ranking quality (MAP, NDCG@20), efficiency, and robustness under controlled text compression. The results show a consistent trade-off: lexical and structural fingerprints are competitive for near-duplicate matching, while semantic-sensitive representations better preserve similarity under content rewriting, at higher computational cost. We further analyze when different similarity measures become rank-equivalent for specific hash representations, improving the interpretability and reproducibility of method comparisons.

</details>


### [215] [Conversational Retrieval and On-the-Fly Knowledge Modeling of Historical Penitentiary Repression Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08459)
*Paula Font Solà,Adrià Molina Rodríguez,Josep Lladós*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A document analysis system for historical digital libraries that combines RAG with a graph-based knowledge model, enabling dynamic integration of expert knowledge and supporting complex queries across documents with long-term dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG-based approaches for digital libraries excel at extractive tasks on individual records but fail to (1) interpret document collections holistically and (2) dynamically incorporate expert knowledge. There is a need for systems that can reason across documents, discover links, and leverage both primary sources and previously modeled expert knowledge.

Method: The authors propose a document analysis system featuring on-the-fly knowledge modeling with a graph-based storage structure. Facts produced by expert archivists or derived from document retrieval are stored as a graph. This graph-based index functions as a memory for the language model, allowing retrieval from both primary documents and previously modeled knowledge. The system supports continuous professional interaction for incremental knowledge enrichment.

Result: The system enables increasingly complex queries involving long-term dependencies across documents, link discovery, and integration of expert knowledge not explicitly present in original sources. It facilitates the generation of richer and more comprehensive information compared to standard RAG approaches.

Conclusion: Integrating graph-based knowledge modeling with RAG allows digital library systems to move beyond single-document extraction toward holistic, expert-informed document analysis. The approach effectively uses the graph as a persistent memory layer for the language model, enabling more sophisticated reasoning over historical collections.

Abstract: Recent developments in digital libraries increasingly favor conversational and natural language access to information through Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Although these approaches are effective for extractive tasks grounded in individual records, they remain limited in their ability to interpret document collections holistically and to incorporate expert knowledge dynamically. In this article, we present a document analysis system designed for the management of historical digital libraries that supports on-the-fly knowledge modeling. The system is equipped with the capability to store facts produced either by expert archivists or derived from document retrieval processes within a graph-based structure. Through continuous professional interaction, the system can retrieve information not only from primary sources such as documents, but also from previously modeled knowledge, with the graph-based index acting as a memory for the language model to access. This enables increasingly complex queries involving long-term dependencies across documents, link discovery, and the integration of expert knowledge that may not be explicitly present in the original sources. As a result, the proposed approach facilitates the generation of richer and more comprehensive information.

</details>


### [216] [Log-Insight: Automating Microservice Incident Diagnosis via Neuro-Symbolic Log Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08529)
*Carlos Garcia-Hernandez,Aymane Abdali,Guangyu Wu,Mingxue Wang,Fei Shen,Zhaoyu Pang,Yanbin Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Log-Insight is a production-deployed automated incident diagnosis system at Huawei that compresses millions of log lines into a compact evidence dossier for LLM-based root cause analysis, achieving MRR=0.790 with >90% top-3 accuracy in under one minute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct LLM-based RCA is infeasible for large-scale microservice incidents due to massive log volumes (2M+ lines, 1.2B characters) exceeding context windows. Existing methods fail because template parsers lack semantic reasoning, deep-learning detectors are black-box, and naive LLM pipelines suffer context overflow and domain hallucination.

Method: A six-stage symbolic pipeline that automates SRE triage workflow: sampling, schema understanding, pattern clustering, and statistical anomaly ranking. This reduces raw events by 1,000–7,000×, producing a compact, pre-ranked evidence dossier which an LLM then synthesizes into a hypothesis report. The system explicitly outputs forensic evidence (log templates and skew statistics) to support operator trust.

Result: Evaluated on 11 historical production incidents (110 runs) with SRE-validated ground truth: MRR = 0.790, correct root cause returned in top-3 hypotheses in >90% of runs, with sub-minute latency. Operators cited the forensic evidence output as a key adoption factor.

Conclusion: Log-Insight demonstrates that a structured symbolic pre-processing pipeline paired with LLM synthesis effectively bridges the gap between massive telemetry and limited context windows, transforming the system from an opaque oracle into an investigative assistant. The authors also report systematic failure modes and open research directions.

Abstract: Diagnosing production incidents in large-scale microservice systems is time-critical for Site Reliability Engineers (SREs). A single 30-minute incident window in our deployment can generate over two million log lines--approximately 1.2 billion characters, far exceeding standard LLM context windows--making direct LLM-based Root Cause Analysis (RCA) infeasible. Existing approaches leave gaps: template-based parsers lack semantic anomaly reasoning, deep-learning detectors emit black-box binary signals, and LLM pipelines suffer context overflow and domain hallucination on raw telemetry.
  We present Log-Insight, an automated incident-diagnosis system deployed in production at Huawei. The core design principle automates the SRE's manual triage workflow: symbolic stages replicate the structured investigation a skilled SRE would perform--sampling, schema understanding, pattern clustering, and statistical anomaly ranking. This hands the LLM a compact, pre-ranked evidence dossier to synthesise into a hypothesis report. Our six-stage pipeline reduces millions of raw events by 1,000-7,000x while preserving statistically significant failure signals.
  Evaluated on 11 historical production incidents (110 runs, SRE-validated ground truth), Log-Insight achieves MRR = 0.790, returning the correct root cause within the top-3 hypotheses in over 90% of runs in under a minute of latency. We report systematic failure modes, active mitigations, and open research directions. The Forensic Evidence section--listing exact log templates and skew statistics--was consistently identified by operators as a key adoption factor, shifting the system's perceived role from opaque oracle to investigative assistant.

</details>


### [217] [Improving Ad-hoc Search Effectiveness for Conversational Information Retrieval via Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08540)
*Ahmed Rayane Kebir,Jose G. Moreno,Lynda Tamine*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper proposes model merging as a training-free approach to create a single retrieval model that performs well on both ad-hoc and conversational search, avoiding costly fine-tuning and catastrophic forgetting. Experiments with Model Soup and Slerp merging strategies show up to 15% NDCG@3 improvement under zero-shot conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conversational IR methods rely on fine-tuning ad-hoc retrievers on conversational data or multi-task learning, which is computationally expensive and suffers from catastrophic forgetting — where the model loses its original ad-hoc retrieval capabilities. The authors aim to address this gap with a training-free solution.

Method: The authors introduce parameter-wise model merging as a training-free strategy. They experiment with two merging techniques: linear merging (Model Soup, which averages parameters) and non-linear merging (Slerp, or Spherical Linear Interpolation). These are applied to combine ad-hoc and conversational retrieval models into a single unified model without additional fine-tuning.

Result: Model merging significantly enhances the ad-hoc search capabilities of conversational retrievers while improving cross-task generalizability. Under zero-shot conditions, the merged models achieve up to 15% higher NDCG@3 on standard benchmarks spanning both ad-hoc search and conversational retrieval tasks.

Conclusion: Model merging is an effective, training-free alternative to fine-tuning for building unified retrieval models that handle both ad-hoc and conversational queries. It mitigates catastrophic forgetting and reduces computational costs while maintaining or improving performance across settings.

Abstract: Conversational information retrieval is challenging since it requires the consideration of the conversation history which potentially gives rise to topic shifts and coreference resolution across previous turns. To address these challenges, previous work mainly rely on traditional fine-tuning of ad-hoc retrievers on conversational datasets or extrapolates their generalizability through multi-tasking. However, this mainstream approach is costly - since it requires model re-training - and exhibits catastrophic forgetting, where the model loses its foundational ad-hoc retrieval performance. In this paper, we fill this gap by introducing model merging as a training-free strategy enabling the design of a single retrieval model that operates across both ad-hoc and conversational settings with no additional fine-tuning. We conduct experiments using linear and non-linear parameter-wise merging strategies - namely Model Soup and Slerp - on standard ad-hoc search and conversational retrieval datasets. Our results demonstrate that model merging significantly enhances the ad-hoc search capabilities of conversational retrievers while improving generalizability across task-specific datasets, achieving up to 15% higher NDCG@3 under zero-shot conditions.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [218] [Collective Intelligence with Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07729)
*J. de Curtò,I. de Zarzà*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework employs solver, critic, and aggregator agents with a scoring module for cooperative reasoning. Ablation studies reveal that model heterogeneity—not framework architecture or redundant sampling—is the critical driver of performance gains, achieving 0.64 step-wise accuracy (vs. 0.54 baseline) and 2.3× improvement over homogeneous configurations, with reduced variance across STEM benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As foundation models scale, coordinating multiple models into cooperative reasoning systems can yield safer, more reliable AI. The authors aim to disentangle whether performance improvements arise from the multi-agent framework structure itself, redundant sampling, or model diversity—a question critical for designing effective cooperative reasoning architectures.

Method: A multi-agent framework with three roles: solver agents generate independent drafts, a critic agent performs structured critique and revision, and an aggregator synthesizes a final consensus solution. A scoring module evaluates semantic, numerical, and procedural quality across all agents. Four configurations are ablated: (1) Individual Baseline, (2) Homogeneous Framework (single shared model), (3) Redundant Homogeneous Solvers (multiple instances of same model), (4) Heterogeneous Framework (diverse specialized models). Evaluation spans calculus, physics, chemistry, biology, economics, optimization, statistics, and mathematics, measuring both final-answer and step-wise reasoning accuracy.

Result: Framework structure and redundant sampling provide only modest gains. Model heterogeneity is the decisive factor: the heterogeneous configuration achieves step-wise accuracy of 0.64 versus 0.54 for individual baselines and a 2.3× improvement over homogeneous configurations. Variance is reduced across categories and difficulty levels. Step-wise reasoning quality improves dramatically only with model diversity, indicating heterogeneous agents supply complementary error detection and reasoning refinement.

Conclusion: Model heterogeneity is essential for substantial cooperative reasoning gains; homogeneous architectures cannot replicate this through structure or sampling alone. Heterogeneous multi-agent coordination enables transparent, auditable, high-confidence decision-making for scientific and industrial applications under the Global Applied AI paradigm.

Abstract: As foundation models grow in scale and diversity, coordinating multiple models into cooperative reasoning systems offers a path toward safer, more reliable AI. This chapter presents a multi-agent framework where solver models generate independent drafts, each undergoes structured critique and revision by a critic agent, and an aggregator agent synthesizes a final consensus solution. A scoring module provides semantic, numerical, and procedural evaluation across all agents. Through ablation studies on a benchmark spanning calculus, physics, chemistry, biology, economics, optimization, statistics, and mathematics, we isolate the contributions of framework architecture versus model diversity. We compare four configurations: (1) Individual Baseline, (2) Homogeneous Framework using one shared model, (3) Redundant Homogeneous Solvers using multiple instances of the same model, and (4) Heterogeneous Framework with diverse specialized models. Results show that while framework structure and redundant sampling yield modest gains, model heterogeneity is the critical factor driving substantial performance improvements. The heterogeneous configuration achieves superior step-wise accuracy (0.64 vs. 0.54 for individual models; 2.3x improvement over homogeneous configurations) with reduced variance across categories and difficulty levels. Step-wise reasoning quality (correctness of intermediate steps, not just final answers) improves dramatically only with model diversity, showing that heterogeneous agents provide complementary error detection and reasoning refinement essential for explainability and auditability. We discuss architectural principles, evaluation methodology, and implications for Global Applied AI, showing how heterogeneous multi-agent coordination supports transparent, auditable, high-confidence decision-making across scientific and industrial domains.

</details>


### [219] [From Triggers to Emotions: A CPM-Grounded Appraisal Multi-Agent for Dynamic Emotional Evolution in Persona-Based Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07824)
*Jingyao Cai,Shuaijun Liu,Abdul Rehman,Yutong Guo,Qin Tian,Thomas Dolby,Sue Green,Chantel Cox,Xiaosong Yang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: CPM-MultiAgent introduces a psychologically-grounded multi-agent framework that models a persona's emotions as a dynamically evolving latent state—continuously reshaped by dialogue triggers via trigger extraction, CPM-based appraisal, and state updating—rather than treating emotion as a static trait, enabling more emotionally consistent role simulation in multi-turn interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing persona-based dialogue systems treat emotion as a static trait or surface-level stylistic cue, and affective dialogue research focuses on empathetic responses toward users rather than the agent's own evolving emotional state. This leaves trigger-driven emotional evolution within a character underexplored. The authors aim to fill this gap by modeling emotion as a dynamic, event-driven process.

Method: The authors propose CPM-MultiAgent, a framework grounded in the Component Process Model (CPM) from psychology. It operates through three key stages: (1) affective trigger extraction from dialogue, (2) CPM-based collaborative appraisal of those triggers by multiple agents, and (3) emotion state updating that continuously reshapes the character's latent emotional state across multi-turn interactions.

Result: Experiments—including baseline comparisons, ablation studies, human evaluation, and case analyses—demonstrate that CPM-MultiAgent effectively models dynamic emotional evolution, outperforming static-emotion baselines in emotionally sensitive role-simulation settings.

Conclusion: CPM-MultiAgent successfully bridges persona-based dialogue and affective computing by grounding a character's emotional evolution in established psychological theory (CPM), yielding more consistent and realistic emotion dynamics across multi-turn interactions.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have substantially advanced persona-based dialogue agents for emotion-sensitive role simulation in healthcare, education, counseling, customer service, and interactive storytelling. However, two related lines of work leave a key gap. Persona-based dialogue systems often encode emotions as static traits or surface-level stylistic cues, and affective dialogue research has largely focused on empathetic response generation toward users rather than modeling the agent persona's own evolving emotional state. As a result, trigger-driven emotional evolution within a character remains underexplored. To address this limitation, we draw inspiration from the Component Process Model (CPM), a psychological theory that views emotion as a dynamic process shaped by the appraisal of external events. We propose CPM-MultiAgent, a CPM-grounded emotion evolution multi-agent framework for supporting emotional changes in persona-based dialogue. Instead of treating a character's emotion as a fixed attribute, CPM-MultiAgent represents it as a latent state that is continuously reshaped by dialogue triggers. Through affective trigger extraction, CPM-based collaborative appraisal, and emotion state updating, the framework enables more emotionally consistent role simulation in multi-turn interactions.Experiments with baseline comparisons, ablation studies, human evaluation, and case analyses demonstrate that CPM-MultiAgent effectively models dynamic emotional evolution in emotionally sensitive role-simulation settings.

</details>


### [220] [Early to Share, Late to Save: Synchronisation-Driven Communication Gating in Bandwidth-Constrained Cooperative VLN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08504)
*Arav Gupta,Nivedan Yakolli,Avinash Gautam*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper introduces bandwidth-constrained cooperative VLN, proposing a hindsight gating mechanism that learns when to communicate by analyzing navigation failures post-hoc. The key counter-intuitive finding: optimal communication happens early and when agents are confident, not uncertain. This is explained by recurrent hidden-state alignment, where early communication creates persistent representational benefits that compound through GRU updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cooperative VLN methods assume unlimited communication between agents, which is unrealistic for real-world bandwidth-constrained applications. The authors aim to develop efficient communication strategies that maximize navigation performance under strict transmission budgets.

Method: The authors propose hindsight gating: a lightweight supervised gate trained post-hoc by labeling communication-critical steps from navigation failures, avoiding the high variance of REINFORCE-based approaches. They evaluate across multiple budget levels (B ∈ {1,3,5}) and analyze gating behavior to understand when and why communication is most effective.

Result: Counter-intuitively, trained gates fire predominantly in early episode steps and when agents are confident — not when uncertain. Early communication yields +0.072 cumulative alignment gain with B=3, nearing unconstrained communication (+0.078). This achieves 260% greater alignment efficiency than random gating (+0.020) and 320% greater than entropy-based gating (+0.017).

Conclusion: The paper establishes a new communication paradigm: synchronize representations early, navigate independently later. Early communication injects grounded trajectory representations that persist and compound through GRU updates, making it the optimal strategy for bandwidth-limited embodied agents.

Abstract: Most cooperative Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) methods assume unlimited communication, not considering real-world applications where bandwidth is restricted and information efficiency is critical. We introduce \textbf{bandwidth-constrained cooperative VLN} and propose \textbf{hindsight gating}: a lightweight supervised gate that labels communication-critical steps post-hoc from navigation failures, avoiding the high variance of REINFORCE. Contrary to the intuition that agents should communicate when uncertain, we observe a consistent counter-intuitive pattern: trained gates fire predominantly in early episode steps and more often when agents are confident, across all budget levels ($B \in \{1,3,5\}$). We explain this through \textbf{recurrent hidden-state alignment}: early communication injects grounded trajectory representations that persist and compound through subsequent Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) updates, achieving $+0.072$ cumulative alignment gain with $B{=}3$ transmissions, approaching unconstrained communication ($+0.078$) at 260\% greater alignment efficiency than random gating ($+0.020$) and 320\% greater efficiency than entropy-based gating ($+0.017$). Our results establish a new communication regime for bandwidth-limited embodied agents: synchronise representations early, navigate independently later. Our codebase is available at: https://github.com/AravG13/bandwidth-constrained-cooperative-vln

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [221] [Who Broke the System? Failure Localization in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07989)
*Yufei Xia,Anjun Gao,Yueyang Quan,Zhuqing Liu,Minghong Fang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AgentLocate is a framework for pinpointing which agent and which earliest step caused a failure in LLM-based multi-agent systems, using LLM judging, multi-perspective verification with confidence-aware aggregation, and lightweight fine-tuning to improve attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems suffer from opaque, distributed failure modes where long-horizon interactions and tightly coupled agent behaviors make it difficult to identify which agent is responsible and when the trajectory first goes irreversibly wrong.

Method: AgentLocate combines: (1) an LLM-based judge that attributes failure to a specific agent and the earliest decisive misstep; (2) multi-perspective verification by independent evaluators whose assessments are aggregated via a confidence-aware strategy; and (3) lightweight fine-tuning of the judge using the aggregated feedback to iteratively improve attribution quality.

Result: On two complementary benchmarks covering diverse tasks, agent configurations, and trajectory lengths, AgentLocate consistently outperforms existing failure localization methods in identifying both responsible agents and failure steps, while remaining efficient in token usage and running time.

Conclusion: AgentLocate provides an effective and efficient solution for failure localization in LLM-based multi-agent systems, addressing a critical gap in debugging and improving such distributed architectures.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) based multi-agent systems enable complex problem solving through coordinated reasoning and action, but their distributed structure also introduces new challenges in diagnosing system-level failures. When an execution fails, identifying which agent is responsible and at what point the trajectory first becomes irreversibly misdirected is difficult due to long-horizon interactions and tightly coupled agent behaviors. In this paper, we study the problem of failure localization in LLM-based multi-agent systems and present AgentLocate, a framework that attributes failures to both a specific agent and the earliest decisive step. AgentLocate combines an LLM-based judging mechanism with multi-perspective verification by independent evaluators, whose assessments are aggregated using a confidence-aware strategy. The resulting feedback is further used to adapt the judge through lightweight fine-tuning, improving attribution quality. We evaluate AgentLocate on two complementary benchmarks covering diverse tasks, agent configurations, and trajectory lengths. Experimental results show that AgentLocate consistently outperforms existing failure localization methods in identifying both responsible agents and failure steps, while remaining efficient in terms of token usage and running time.

</details>


### [222] [Controllability-Aware Adversarial Examples Against LLM-Based Network Traffic Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07739)
*Zhenpeng Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper evaluates the adversarial robustness of LLM-based network intrusion detection classifiers under a controllability-aware black-box transfer attack framework. By restricting perturbations to only directly controllable flow features, the authors find that LLMs exhibit substantial but dataset-dependent transfer vulnerability, with gradient/score-based attacks transferring more effectively than greedy ones across architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are increasingly used as network intrusion detection classifiers, their adversarial robustness under realistic attacker constraints — where only certain network flow features can be manipulated — remains poorly understood. Existing evaluations lack controllability-aware threat models that respect network communication semantics.

Method: The authors propose a controllability-aware black-box transfer framework that partitions flow features into directly controllable (DC), indirectly controllable (IC), and uncontrollable (UC) groups based on network semantics. Perturbations are restricted to DC features only. A shared XGBoost surrogate generates adversarial examples using three attack methods: finite-difference PGD, greedy coordinate-wise, and NES. These examples are transferred to 7 LLM targets and 2 conventional ML targets across 5 IDS benchmarks (1999–2022), totaling 27 LLM configurations and over 500,000 adversarial examples. Cross-surrogate validation with tree, neural, and linear surrogates is also performed.

Result: LLM transfer vulnerability is substantial but dataset- and comparator-dependent: LLMs are more vulnerable than LightGBM on RT-IoT2022 and CIC-IDS-2018, comparable on NSL-KDD and UNSW-NB15, and less vulnerable on HIKARI-2021; compared to the averaged ML baseline, LLMs show higher attack success rate (ASR) on all five datasets. A consistent cross-architecture transfer hierarchy emerges: gradient- and score-based perturbations transfer more effectively than greedy perturbations across all 27 LLM cells and 9/10 ML cells. Cross-surrogate validation yields similar LLM ASR, reducing evidence of XGBoost-specificity. Constraint violation rate is 0% by construction.

Conclusion: LLM-based network intrusion detection classifiers exhibit significant adversarial transfer vulnerability even under realistic controllability constraints, but the severity depends heavily on the dataset and the baseline comparator chosen. The consistent superiority of gradient/score-based attacks over greedy methods highlights a cross-architecture transfer hierarchy that practitioners should account for when deploying LLM-based IDS.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored as network intrusion detection classifiers, but their adversarial robustness under realistic attacker constraints remains unclear. We present a controllability-aware black-box transfer framework for LLM-based network traffic classifiers. The framework partitions flow features into directly controllable (DC), indirectly controllable (IC), and uncontrollable (UC) groups according to network communication semantics, then restricts perturbations to DC features while freezing IC/UC features. Using a shared XGBoost surrogate, we generate finite-difference PGD, greedy coordinate-wise, and NES adversarial examples and transfer them to seven LLM targets and two conventional ML targets across five IDS benchmarks from 1999 to 2022. Across 27 valid LLM configurations and over 500,000 adversarial examples, we find that LLM transfer vulnerability is substantial but dataset- and comparator-dependent. Compared with LightGBM, LLMs are more vulnerable on RT-IoT2022 and CIC-IDS-2018, comparable on NSL-KDD and UNSW-NB15, and less vulnerable on HIKARI-2021; compared with the averaged ML baseline, LLMs show higher ASR on all five datasets. We further observe a consistent cross-architecture transfer hierarchy: gradient- and score-based perturbations transfer more effectively than greedy perturbations across all 27 LLM cells and 9/10 ML cells. Cross-surrogate validation with tree, neural, and linear surrogates yields similar LLM ASR, reducing evidence that the findings are XGBoost-specific. Constraint violation rate is 0\% by construction.

</details>


### [223] [Forensic Schema for Psychological Manipulation in Cyber Fraud: LLM-Driven Victim Reports Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07751)
*Zikai Alex Wen,Corrazon Ogot,Juan Li,Yan Bai*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A forensic schema incorporating psychological manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence was developed and applied to ~11,000 victim reports via LLM annotation. Each fraud type showed a distinct manipulation profile, but victim narratives lacked actionable forensic detail and blockchain identifiers, suggesting AI-assisted intake with targeted follow-up questions as a remedy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cybercrime classification schemas focus on contact metadata and financial transactions but fail to capture the psychological manipulation techniques (e.g., grooming, gaslighting, urgency induction) that perpetrators use, limiting forensic and preventive insights.

Method: The authors constructed a forensic schema comprising four categories and 35 questions, adding 11 manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence fields to established forensic foundations. They applied it to 10,994 victim reports using LLM-driven annotation and validated performance against two human annotators (mean LLM-human κ = 0.69). A rationale-based evidence audit assessed the actionable detail in victim narratives.

Result: Each major fraud type exhibited a statistically distinct manipulation profile (Cramér's V up to 0.790). LLM-human agreement (κ = 0.69) matched inter-annotator human agreement (κ = 0.68). However, the evidence audit revealed that while manipulation detection was reliable, victim narratives varied widely in actionable detail, and blockchain-specific identifiers (e.g., wallet addresses, transaction hashes) were nearly absent.

Conclusion: AI-assisted victim intake with schema-informed follow-up questions is the most direct path to closing the forensic detail gap. The tiered annotation strategy also offers a reusable template for LLM-based information extraction in other forensic text domains.

Abstract: Existing cybercrime classification schemas capture contact metadata and financial transactions but omit the psychological manipulation techniques perpetrators employ. We present a forensic schema (four categories, 35 questions) adding 11 manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence fields to established forensic foundations. Applied to 10,994 victim reports via large language model (LLM)-driven annotation and validated against two human annotators (mean LLM-human $κ= 0.69$, matching inter-annotator $κ= 0.68$), the schema revealed a statistically distinct manipulation profile for each major fraud type (Cramer's $V$ up to $0.790$). A rationale-based evidence audit nonetheless exposed a forensic detail gap: detection of manipulation techniques was reliable, but victim narratives varied widely in the actionable detail supporting each Yes answer, and blockchain-specific identifiers were nearly absent. These findings point to AI-assisted victim intake with schema-informed follow-up questions as the most direct way to close the gap. The tiered annotation strategy also provides a reusable template for LLM-based extraction from other forensic text domains.

</details>


### [224] [Multi-Agent Firewall Architecture for Privacy Protection of Sensitive Data in Interactions with Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08282)
*Hugo García Cuesta,Pablo Mateo Torrejón,Alfonso Sánchez-Macián*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM firewall combining a browser extension and proxy for HTTP/WebSocket traffic interception, achieving up to 94.93% F1 in data leakage prevention through hybrid deterministic/semantic detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely adopted as productivity tools but lack adequate safeguards when integrated into workflows, creating significant security risks — particularly around data leakage and proprietary code exposure. Existing solutions are predominantly proprietary and lack privacy guarantees.

Method: An open-source, privacy-focused firewall architecture with two interception layers: (1) a browser extension for web-based LLM interactions and (2) a proxy for programmatic API calls, covering both HTTP(S) and WebSocket communications. The core detection pipeline uses a multi-agent hybrid approach: deterministic pattern-matching detectors combined with LLM-driven semantic analysis for data leakage prevention, plus dedicated components for proprietary code leakage prevention. The system is extensible for future threats (e.g., prompt injection). A layered architecture allows configurable trade-offs between computational cost, detection depth, and latency.

Result: The system achieves F1 scores up to 94.93% in optimal configurations for data leakage detection.

Conclusion: The proposed architecture provides a viable, open-source, privacy-preserving firewall solution for securing LLM interactions across heterogeneous deployment environments, with high detection performance and flexible cost-latency-depth trade-offs.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have become essential productivity tools, their integration into workflows without adequate safeguards creates significant risks. This paper proposes an open-source, privacy-focused, user-facing firewall designed to secure both web-based and programmatic LLM interactions. The architecture combines a browser extension and a proxy for total traffic interception across both HTTP(S) and WebSocket communications. At its core, a flexible multi-agent pipeline delivers data leakage prevention through a hybrid approach combining deterministic detectors with LLM-driven semantic analysis, proprietary code leakage prevention, and extensible components designed for future security enhancements such as prompt injection evasion. The framework's layered architecture enables deployment across heterogeneous environments, allowing organizations to balance computational cost, detection depth and latency. Evaluation results demonstrate it achieves F1 scores of up to 94.93% on optimal configurations.

</details>


### [225] [ScopeJudge: Cost-Aware Pre-Execution Gating for Offensive Security Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07774)
*Shane Caldwell,Max Harley,Ads Dawson,Michael Kouremetis,Vincent Abruzzo,Will Pearce*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ScopeJudge is a benchmark of 4,897 tool calls (7.7% violations) for evaluating pre-execution gating of LLM agents in offensive security. The study shows static policies fail for scope enforcement, and request-conditioned monitoring is necessary. Eight judge models are evaluated across five transcript strategies, with two recommended operating points balancing cost and recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offensive security LLM agents face a unique scope-enforcement challenge: whether a tool call is in-scope depends on the user's declared intent, the target, and runtime context — not merely the action itself. A single out-of-scope call can breach engagement boundaries, disrupt production, or void bug-bounty findings. Existing fixed safety policies cannot enumerate all scope boundaries in advance, creating a need for intent-aware, pre-execution gating.

Method: The authors introduce ScopeJudge, a benchmark comprising 4,897 tool calls collected from agent trajectories on tasks designed to tempt scope violations. Each call is labeled by professional penetration testers (Fleiss' kappa = 0.64, expert agreement F1 = 0.78). Eight judge models are evaluated under five transcript strategies that vary contextual information from static policy alone to full raw transcripts. Performance is measured along a cost-accuracy Pareto frontier, with separate reporting of precision, recall, and F1 due to asymmetric error costs.

Result: Static-policy-only judging collapses recall to near zero, demonstrating structural insufficiency for scope enforcement. Request-conditioned monitoring is confirmed necessary, as scope lives in the user's declared intent. The authors identify two operating points: a cost-sensitive configuration and a recall-first configuration for high-stakes deployments, reflecting the asymmetric cost where missed violations outweigh spurious rejections.

Conclusion: Pre-execution gating via a trusted LLM judge is a viable approach for scope enforcement in autonomous offensive security agents, but it requires request-conditioned context. The ScopeJudge dataset and benchmark are released to facilitate real-time monitoring and scalable oversight of security agents.

Abstract: As LLM agents take on offensive security work, a single out-of-scope tool call can breach a client's engagement boundary, disrupt production, or void a bug-bounty finding. Unlike a fixed safety policy, the boundary that matters is declared in the user's request and must be inferred from intent.
