That challenge is sharpened by the adversarial nature of offensive security: the same tool call is in or out of scope depending not on the action itself but on the target it touches and the context in which it runs, which no fixed policy can enumerate in advance. We study pre-execution gating: a cheap, trusted LLM judge inspects each call proposed by a strong, swappable agent, and accepts or rejects it before it runs. We introduce ScopeJudge, a benchmark of 4,897 tool calls (7.7% scope violations) from agent trajectories on tasks engineered to tempt agents out of scope and labeled at the call level by professional penetration testers, with substantial inter-grader agreement (Fleiss kappa = 0.64) that sets an expert agreement reference point of F1 = 0.78. We evaluate eight judge models under five transcript strategies, varying how much context the judge sees, from the static policy alone to the full raw transcript, and chart the resulting cost-accuracy Pareto frontier. We find that a static policy is structurally insufficient for scope enforcement: blind to the user's request, judge recall collapses to near zero, confirming that scope lives in the request and that request-conditioned monitoring is necessary. Because a missed violation costs more than a spurious rejection, we report precision, recall, and F1 separately and recommend two operating points: a cost-sensitive configuration and a recall-first one for high-stakes deployments. We release the ScopeJudge dataset to support real-time monitoring and scalable oversight of autonomous security agents.

</details>


### [226] [Open Models, Open Risks: Measuring Unsafe Generation in Text-to-Image Models In the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07827)
*Peilin Han,Yang Liu,Yilong Yang,Jingchun Zhang,Teng Li,Jianfeng Ma,Zhuo Ma*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A large-scale empirical study of 200+ in-the-wild T2I models on Hugging Face reveals that existing detector-based jailbreak metrics overestimate real-world risk due to semantic drift and generation artifacts. The authors propose Advanced ASR, a refined metric, and find that safety degradation is neither universal nor uniform, while also identifying high-risk models including seemingly benign ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior T2I jailbreak safety studies are limited to controlled lab settings with few canonical models, leaving the safety status of the rapidly expanding in-the-wild T2I ecosystem unclear. This gap is exacerbated by detector-based metrics designed for controlled evaluation and the fact that risks may stem not only from adversarial prompting but also from unsafe release practices and model derivatives.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale empirical study across 200+ in-the-wild T2I models from Hugging Face. They first demonstrate that existing detector-only jailbreak metrics overestimate risk and introduce Advanced ASR (Attack Success Rate) to better capture semantically valid and visually plausible unsafe generations. They then evaluate all models under three representative jailbreak attacks using this refined metric, and trace high-risk models back to their release context, reporting cases to Hugging Face.

Result: Detector-only metrics substantially overestimate practical risk due to semantic drift and generation artifacts. Many downstream models retain non-trivial safety even without explicit post-hoc safeguards, showing safety degradation in the wild is neither universal nor uniform. A set of high-risk models was identified, including explicitly NSFW-oriented releases and seemingly benign models whose unsafe behavior only surfaces under systematic evaluation.

Conclusion: In-the-wild T2I safety is more nuanced than lab studies suggest. The proposed Advanced ASR provides a more reliable metric for real-world evaluation. Safety degradation is not inevitable across downstream models, but systematic evaluation is essential to uncover hidden risks in seemingly benign releases. The findings underscore the need for better release practices and monitoring in the open T2I ecosystem.

Abstract: Existing safety studies on text-to-image (T2I) jailbreaks are largely conducted in controlled in-the-lab settings, typically on a small number of canonical models. As a result, the current safety status of the rapidly growing in-the-wild T2I ecosystem remains unclear. This uncertainty is amplified by two factors: existing detector-based metrics are designed for controlled evaluation, and in-the-wild risks may arise not only from adversarial prompting, but also from unsafe release practices and unsafe model derivatives.
  In this paper, we present a large-scale empirical study of in-the-wild T2I safety through the lens of jailbreak. We first show that detector-only jailbreak metrics substantially overestimate practical risk over in the wild due to semantic drift and generation artifacts, and we introduce Advanced ASR to better capture semantically valid and visually plausible unsafe generation. Using this refined metric, we evaluate 200+ in-the-wild T2I models from Hugging Face under three representative jailbreak attacks. Our results show that many downstream models retain a non-trivial degree of safety even without explicit post-hoc safeguards, indicating that safety degradation in the wild is neither universal nor uniform. At the same time, we identify a set of high-risk models, including explicitly NSFW-oriented releases as well as seemingly benign models whose unsafe behavior is only exposed through systematic evaluation. We further trace these models to their release context and report high-risk cases to Hugging Face.

</details>


### [227] [Closed-Loop Dynamic Validator Node Scaling in Private Substrate Blockchains Using Takagi-Sugeno Fuzzy Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07901)
*Thandile Nododile,Ayinde M. Usman,Clement N. Nyirenda*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy inference system that autonomously scales validator nodes in private blockchains by analyzing live metrics, empirically recalibrating membership functions to observed operating ranges, and demonstrating stable bidirectional convergence with fewer oscillations than threshold-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Private blockchain networks suffer from static node configurations that cannot adapt to dynamic workloads — over-provisioning wastes resources while under-provisioning degrades block production and finalisation. Determining the optimal validator count is challenging due to multiple interacting, time-varying factors.

Method: A Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy inference controller with three linguistic variables (block production time, block size, active node count), triangular membership functions, a 27-rule base with product t-norm aggregation, and empirical recalibration of membership functions anchored to observed testbed operating ranges. Evaluated on a 10-node Substrate blockchain with real smart water meter data hashes. Tested across 4, 7, and 10-node configurations with closed-loop bidirectional scaling experiments and comparison against three threshold-based baselines.

Result: Statistical analysis confirmed distinct operational profiles for each configuration. In closed-loop tests, the controller autonomously activated validators under rising load and removed them under over-provisioning, converging to the same stable equilibrium from both directions. Compared to threshold baselines, it achieved fewer scaling oscillations while maintaining comparable block production times.

Conclusion: TS fuzzy inference effectively supports autonomous validator management in private blockchain deployments, delivering stable scaling behavior that threshold-based approaches cannot match.

Abstract: Private blockchain networks run with fixed node configurations that cannot adapt to changing workload conditions. Too many nodes serving a light workload waste resources; too few nodes facing heavy demand slow block production and degrade finalisation. The right validator count is hard to determine, as it depends on overlapping factors that shift over time. This paper presents a Takagi-Sugeno (TS) fuzzy inference system that reads live blockchain parameters (block production time, block size, and active node count) and outputs a continuous efficiency score alongside a scaling recommendation: Scale Up, Maintain, or Scale Down. The controller uses triangular membership functions across three linguistic variables, evaluated through a complete 27-rule base with product t-norm aggregation. A key contribution is an empirical recalibration of the membership functions, anchoring linguistic terms to the observed operating range of the testbed rather than to theoretical extremes. The system is evaluated on a 10-node Substrate blockchain network storing real smart water meter data hashes from the Queensland Government open data portal. Statistical analysis across configurations of 4, 7, and 10 active nodes confirms that the controller produces distinct operational profiles reflecting each configuration's provisioning state. In closed-loop experiments, the controller autonomously adjusts validator participation in both directions, activating validators under rising load and removing them under over-provisioning, converging to the same stable equilibrium from both directions. Compared against three threshold-based baselines, it shows fewer scaling oscillations while maintaining comparable block production times. Results show that TS fuzzy inference can support autonomous validator management in private blockchain deployments, with stable scaling behaviour threshold approaches cannot match.

</details>


### [228] [Mechanistic Interpretability of LLM Jailbreaks via Internal Attribution Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07903)
*Anupam Wagle,Ifrat Ikhtear Uddin,Chaowei Zhang,Longwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper introduces a mechanistic framework that uses paired internal computation graphs to diagnose LLM vulnerabilities, revealing how adversarial attacks systematically transform internal reasoning through suppression, emergence, and rerouting of latent features. Causal interventions on these graphs enable diagnosis and mitigation of failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to understanding LLM vulnerabilities (input-output analysis, attribution methods) provide limited insight into how adversarial perturbations alter internal reasoning mechanisms. The mechanisms underlying unsafe or incorrect behaviors remain poorly understood, motivating a shift from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis.

Method: The authors propose a unified framework based on internal computation graphs — structured causal interactions among latent features during prompt-specific inference. Key components include: (1) constructing and aligning paired computation graphs for clean vs. attacked prompts; (2) decomposing computation into invariant, suppressed, and emergent structures; (3) identifying recurring vulnerability motifs associated with failure modes; and (4) performing causal interventions on nodes, paths, and subgraphs to evaluate their contributions to attack success.

Result: Experiments across multiple open-source LLMs and diverse adversarial/jailbreak benchmarks demonstrate that structural deviations in internal computation graphs strongly correlate with unsafe behaviors. Targeted causal interventions on identified vulnerability motifs improve model robustness.

Conclusion: Internal computation graphs serve as a principled foundation for understanding, diagnosing, and mitigating LLM vulnerabilities, enabling a transition from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis of model failures.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable capabilities but remain highly vulnerable to adversarial prompts and jailbreak attacks. Existing approaches primarily analyze these failures through input-output behaviors or attribution methods, offering limited insight into how adversarial perturbations alter the model's internal reasoning. Consequently, the mechanisms underlying unsafe or incorrect behaviors remain poorly understood. We introduce a mechanistic framework for diagnosing LLM vulnerabilities using paired internal computation graphs, which represent prompt-specific inference as structured causal interactions among latent features. By constructing and aligning computation graphs for clean and attacked prompts, we reveal that adversarial attacks induce systematic transformations of internal reasoning, including suppression of safety-relevant components, emergence of attack-specific features, and rerouting of computation paths. Building on this representation, we propose a unified framework that (i) decomposes computation into invariant, suppressed, and emergent structures, (ii) identifies recurring vulnerability motifs associated with failure modes, and (iii) performs causal interventions on nodes, paths, and subgraphs to directly evaluate their contributions to attack success. This enables a transition from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis of model failures. Experiments across multiple open-source LLMs and diverse adversarial and jailbreak benchmarks demonstrate that structural deviations in internal computation graphs strongly correlate with unsafe behaviors. Furthermore, targeted interventions on identified vulnerability motifs improve model robustness, establishing internal computation graphs as a principled foundation for understanding, diagnosing, and mitigating LLM vulnerabilities.

</details>


### [229] [KS-CFA: Control-Flow Attestation via Symbolic Replay Against Control-Flow Bending Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07926)
*Zhanyu Sha,Konstantinos Markantonakis,Carlton Shepherd,Amir Rafi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: KS-CFA is a control-flow attestation scheme that detects control-flow bending attacks by combining symbolic execution with selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables. The prover records control-flow traces and external inputs within a TEE, while the verifier replays the path through single-path symbolic execution to detect divergences, eliminating the need for path or value enumeration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CFA solutions fail to adequately detect control-flow bending (CFB) attacks — where adversaries manipulate branch flags, loop counters, and other runtime data to steer execution along legal CFG edges. Current approaches either require enumerating prohibitively large measurement spaces, cover only a limited subset of attacks, or depend on impractical low-level hardware modifications. A scheme is needed that detects CFB across all four transfer types without these burdens.

Method: KS-CFA employs two key techniques: (1) selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables — a strict subset of control-flow-relevant state whose values originate from external input; (2) single-path symbolic execution at the verifier side. The prover, running inside a Keystone TEE on RISC-V, records the control-flow trace and external inputs that determine relevant runtime variables. The verifier then replays the reported path using symbolic execution, predicting each control transfer and localising divergences that indicate an attack, without enumerating paths or values.

Result: Implemented on RISC-V Keystone TEE with Embench-IoT benchmarks, evaluated on both QEMU and a Rocket-based FPGA (NiteFury II). Prover-side overhead relative to unattested execution is 6.8–20.5× on QEMU and 6.7–32.2× on the FPGA. Verification requires no path or value enumeration.

Conclusion: KS-CFA demonstrates that full detection of CFB attacks across all four transfer types is achievable without path/value enumeration or hardware modifications, using a practical TEE-based architecture with symbolic execution-based verification. The overhead, while non-trivial, is within a tractable range for IoT-class devices.

Abstract: Control-flow attestation (CFA) enables a remote entity to verify program execution on a target device by monitoring control-flow behaviour at runtime. However, control-flow bending (CFB) attacks remain difficult to detect, where an adversary steers execution along legal edges of the program's control-flow graph by corrupting branch flags, loop counters, and other runtime data. Existing solutions impose significant drawbacks: they require enumerating vast measurement spaces, cover only a reduced subset of attacks, or rely on low-level hardware modifications. In this work, we present KS-CFA, a new CFA scheme that detects CFB attacks across four transfer types (indirect calls, conditional and indirect jumps, and returns) without those costs. To this end, we combine symbolic execution and selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables: a strict subset of control-flow-relevant state whose values are directly read from external input. The proving device records, inside a trusted execution environment (TEE), a control-flow trace and the external inputs that determine relevant run-time variables. The verifier then replays the reported path through single-path symbolic execution, predicting each transfer and localising divergences that signal an attack. We implement and evaluate KS-CFA using the RISC-V Keystone TEE and Embench-IoT on QEMU and a Rocket-based FPGA platform (NiteFury II). Prover-side overhead relative to unattested execution ranges from 6.8-20.5x on QEMU and 6.7-32.2x on the FPGA, and verification requires no path or value enumeration.

</details>


### [230] [Beware What You Autocomplete: Forensic Attribution of Backdoored Code Completions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08011)
*Anjun Gao,Yueyang Quan,Zhuqing Liu,Minghong Fang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CodeTracer is a forensic framework that traces malicious code completions back to the specific backdoor fine-tuning data that caused them, using behavioral fingerprinting and LLM-based reasoning under realistic post-deployment constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code completion models are vulnerable to backdoor attacks via poisoned fine-tuning data, and current defenses struggle against adaptive/sophisticated attacks. There is a need for forensic attribution — identifying exactly which training samples caused a malicious completion — rather than just detection or mitigation.

Method: CodeTracer operates with only the fine-tuning corpus and a reported miscompletion. It (1) extracts a structured behavioral fingerprint from the compromised output, (2) narrows the search space to semantically relevant code samples, and (3) employs LLM-based reasoning to attribute unsafe logic to specific backdoor data points.

Result: Evaluated across 3 vulnerability cases, 10 backdoor attacks, and 16 baselines, CodeTracer achieves high forensic accuracy, low false identification rates, and strong robustness against adaptive attacks.

Conclusion: CodeTracer provides an effective and practical post-deployment forensic solution for tracing backdoor-induced code miscompletions to their poisoned training sources, filling a critical gap beyond detection-focused defenses.

Abstract: Large language models have enabled powerful code completion systems that assist developers by predicting subsequent lines of code. However, these models remain vulnerable to backdoor attacks, where malicious fine-tuning data covertly implants unsafe behaviors. Despite advances in defensive techniques, adaptive and sophisticated backdoor attacks still evade detection and mitigation. We present CodeTracer, a forensic framework that traces malicious code completions back to the backdoor fine-tuning data responsible for them. Operating under realistic post-deployment constraints, CodeTracer relies solely on the fine-tuning corpus and the reported miscompletion event. It extracts a structured behavioral fingerprint from the compromised output, narrows the search to semantically relevant code samples, and employs LLM-based reasoning to attribute unsafe logic to specific backdoor data. Extensive evaluations across three representative vulnerability cases and ten backdoor attacks, along with sixteen competitive baselines, demonstrate that CodeTracer consistently achieves high forensic accuracy, low false identification rates, and strong robustness against adaptive attacks.

</details>


### [231] [Degree-Constrained Interval Optimization for Minimax Polynomial Approximation in Homomorphic Encryption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08042)
*Jiheon Woo,Donggyun Ryu,Yongjune Kim*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper formulates a distribution-aware interval optimization problem for minimax polynomial approximation in homomorphic encryption, balancing within-interval approximation error against outside-interval clipping error using domain extension polynomials (DEPs), with an analytically tractable proxy objective and implementation-error analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In homomorphic encryption (HE)-based inference, non-polynomial activations must be approximated by polynomials. Minimax (Remez) approximation is standard, but the approximation interval introduces a critical trade-off: wider intervals increase robustness to large inputs but degrade approximation quality under a fixed polynomial degree budget. There is no principled method to select this interval in a distribution-aware manner while managing extrapolation error.

Method: The authors formulate a distribution-aware interval optimization that minimizes MSE w.r.t. the pre-activation distribution. They combine minimax polynomials with domain extension functions (DEFs) and their HE-realizable polynomial counterparts (DEPs) to clip outside-interval inputs. An analytically tractable DEF-based proxy objective captures the within-interval minimax error vs. outside-interval clipping error trade-off. This idealized objective is then connected to HE-realizable DEP constructions via an implementation-error decomposition with an accompanying upper bound.

Result: The paper derives a closed-form proxy objective for the DEF-based case that explicitly trades off minimax approximation error and clipping error as a function of interval choice. It further provides an implementation-error decomposition that bounds the gap between the idealized DEF objective and practical DEP-based realizations, enabling principled interval selection for HE-compatible polynomial activation approximation.

Conclusion: Distribution-aware interval optimization with DEP-based clipping provides a systematic framework for selecting approximation intervals in HE polynomial activation design, yielding better MSE performance than naive interval choices by explicitly managing the trade-off between approximation fidelity and extrapolation robustness.

Abstract: Homomorphic encryption (HE) enables privacy-preserving inference under arithmetic constraints that restrict encrypted evaluation to additions and multiplications. As a result, non-polynomial activation functions must be replaced by polynomial approximations. Among polynomial approximation methods, minimax approximation, typically computed by the Remez algorithm, is a standard approach because it minimizes the maximum approximation error over a given design interval. For minimax polynomial design, the approximation interval is a critical hyperparameter: a wider interval improves robustness to large-magnitude inputs while increasing the minimax approximation error under a fixed degree budget. In this paper, we formulate this trade-off as a distribution-aware interval optimization problem, where the approximation interval is chosen to minimize the mean-squared error (MSE) with respect to the pre-activation distribution of interest. To effectively control outside-interval inputs, we combine minimax polynomials with domain extension functions (DEFs) and their HE-realizable polynomial counterparts, domain extension polynomials (DEPs), which approximate a clipping operation outside the design interval and thereby suppress uncontrolled polynomial extrapolation. We first derive an analytically tractable DEF-based proxy objective that captures the trade-off between within-interval minimax approximation error and outside-interval clipping error. We then connect this idealized objective to HE-realizable DEP constructions through an implementation-error decomposition with an accompanying upper bound.

</details>


### [232] [zkComposer: Decomposing Proof Construction to Scale zkML](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08095)
*Pawan Kumar Sanjaya,Christina Giannoula,Valdy Oktavian,Mehdi Saeedi,Gabor Sines,Gururaj Saileshwar,Nandita Vijaykumar*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: zkComposer introduces a modular proof-construction framework for zero-knowledge machine learning that decomposes monolithic proofs into independent, cryptographically linked sub-proofs, unlocking parallelism across model layers and input sequences. It achieves up to 6.84× speedup in prover/response time over existing systems without sacrificing security guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zkML systems suffer from prohibitive proof-generation costs due to monolithic proof computation that exhibits limited parallelism — prover time does not scale well with additional threads. A new approach is needed to enable parallelism beyond what current proof kernels offer.

Method: zkComposer decomposes the zkML proof of correct inference into independent sub-proofs, each covering a subset of computation (e.g., contiguous layers in an ML model). Adjacent sub-proofs are cryptographically linked via shared commitments to activations at boundary layers. This modular design unlocks an additional dimension of parallelism — partitioning across model layers and input sequences — without requiring additional linking proofs or changes to underlying cryptographic primitives, while preserving monolithic-proof guarantees.

Result: On CNN workloads, zkComposer reduces prover and response time by up to 3.25× relative to zkCNN. On GPT-2 with layer-wise partitioning, speedups reach 4.83× relative to zkGPT. When partitioning across both layers and input sequences in GPT-2, reductions reach 6.84×.

Conclusion: zkComposer demonstrates that modular, parallelized proof construction significantly mitigates the proof-generation bottleneck in zkML, enabling practical verifiable inference for both CNNs and large language models like GPT-2 while maintaining full security guarantees.

Abstract: Zero-knowledge machine learning (zkML) enables a server to perform verifiable inference while keeping model parameters private from the client. However, existing zkML systems incur prohibitive proof-generation costs. We observe that proof generation exhibits limited parallelism; that is, prover time does not decrease significantly as the number of threads increases. This limitation is because existing systems rely on monolithic proof computation, constructing a single proof for the entire machine learning model. We introduce zkComposer, a modular proof-construction framework that unlocks an additional dimension of parallelism, in addition to the parallelism in existing proof kernels. zkComposer decomposes the zkML proof of correct inference into independent sub-proofs, each covering a subset of the computation for inference e.g., each independent sub-proof can cover a subset of contiguous layers in the ML model. Adjacent sub-proofs are cryptographically linked through shared commitments to the activations from the boundary layer. zkComposer provides the same guarantees as the monolithic proof without requiring additional linking proofs or changes to the underlying cryptographic primitives. We implement zkComposer and evaluate it on three CNNs and GPT-2. We show that, on CNN workloads, zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 3.25x relative to zkCNN [1]. On GPT-2, zkComposer reduces these times by up to 4.83x relative to zkGPT [2], when partitioning along the model layers. When partitioning across both model layers and input sequences in GPT-2, we show that zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 6.84x relative to zkGPT [2].

</details>


### [233] [Simulating the Resident: Generating Executable Smart Home Schedules via LLM Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08231)
*Victor Jüttner,Xenia Wagner,Christoph Jahn,Erik Buchmann*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes using LLMs to generate diverse resident personas and executable device interaction schedules for simulated smart homes, enabling scalable, privacy-conscious experimentation without intrusive real-world data collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating real-world smart home datasets is slow, expensive, and raises significant privacy concerns due to long-term observation of residents in private spaces. A privacy-preserving alternative for generating authentic behavioral data is needed.

Method: The authors propose a three-component approach: (1) a design framework for configuring simulated households across five socio-technical dimensions, (2) a multi-stage LLM pipeline that generates structured, executable device interaction schedules from resident personas, and (3) a proof-of-concept implementation demonstrating feasibility on physical testbeds.

Result: The proof of concept demonstrates the feasibility of the approach. As a work-in-progress, quantitative evaluation results are not yet reported.

Conclusion: LLM-generated resident personas and interaction schedules offer a promising, scalable, and privacy-conscious alternative to real-world data collection for smart home HCI, security, and privacy research.

Abstract: Smart homes have emerged as an important domain for HCI research, including work on usable security and privacy. Ideally, studies in these areas draw on datasets collected in real homes with real residents, capturing authentic device interactions, network traffic, and daily routines. However, creating such datasets is slow, expensive, and raises significant privacy concerns, as it requires long-term observation of people in their most private spaces. We propose using LLMs to generate diverse resident personas that interact with a simulated smart home, producing behaviorally grounded interaction schedules that can be executed on physical testbeds. We present (1) a design framework configuring simulated households across five socio-technical dimensions, (2) a multi-stage LLM pipeline that produces structured, executable device interaction schedules, and (3) a proof of concept demonstrating feasibility. As a work in progress, we aim to support scalable, privacy-conscious smart-home experimentation without relying on intrusive real-world data collection.

</details>


### [234] [Securing Autonomous Vehicle Systems via Twin-Aware Federated Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08137)
*Zifan Zhang,Minghong Fang,Dianwei Chen,Zhuqing Liu,Prashant Khanduri,Xianfeng Yang,Anupam Das,Yuchen Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents SecAgg+ (Secure Aggregation with Poisoning-Prevention and Historical Reinforcement), a defensive framework for Federated Reinforcement Learning (FRL) in autonomous driving. It leverages digital twins for rehearsal-based learning and historical aggregated parameters with selected central gradients to filter out malicious updates, providing theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical validation on realistic highway simulations under adversarial conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated Reinforcement Learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative learning in vehicular environments but is highly vulnerable to poisoning attacks. In safety-critical autonomous driving, such attacks—where adversaries inject malicious system parameters—can compromise the global control model and create hazardous situations. This vulnerability remains largely unexplored, motivating the need for a robust defensive framework.

Method: The authors propose SecAgg+, a defensive aggregation framework with two key components: (1) strategic integration of digital twins for rehearsal-based learning, allowing the system to test and validate updates in a simulated environment before aggregation; and (2) leveraging historical aggregated model parameters together with a selected central gradient to identify and exclude malicious updates, ensuring only benign data contributes to the global model. Theoretical convergence guarantees under poisoning attacks are also provided.

Result: Theoretical analysis establishes convergence guarantees for SecAgg+ even in the presence of poisoning attacks. Empirical validation uses developed digital twins that model realistic highway environments, demonstrating the framework's effectiveness in maintaining robust autonomous vehicle control under adversarial conditions.

Conclusion: SecAgg+ effectively mitigates poisoning attacks in FRL-based autonomous driving systems by combining digital twin-based rehearsal learning with historical parameter reinforcement, ensuring robust and secure model aggregation without compromising the collaborative learning benefits of FRL.

Abstract: Federated reinforcement learning (FRL) is crucial for enabling collaborative learning across multiple agents without sharing raw data, thereby enhancing privacy and scalability in the decision-making process within dynamic vehicular environments. However, poisoning attacks pose a significant threat to the security and reliability of FRL-based systems, particularly in safety-critical autonomous driving, where this vulnerability remains largely unexplored. These attacks can compromise the global control model by subtly injecting malicious system parameters, leading to potential hazards. To counter these challenges, we present \alg (\underline{Sec}ure \underline{A}ggregation with \underline{p}oisoning-\underline{p}revention and historical reinforcement) as a defensive framework aimed at enhancing the robustness of FRL systems designed for safety-critical driving scenarios. \alg strategically integrates digital twins for rehearsal-based learning and leverages historical aggregated model parameters along with a selected central gradient to ensure that only benign data is aggregated, effectively mitigating the influence of malicious agents. Theoretical guarantees are provided for the convergence performance of \alg in the presence of poisoning attacks. We also validate the effectiveness of \alg using developed digital twins that model realistic highway environments to evaluate the control of autonomous vehicles under adversarial conditions.

</details>


### [235] [Prismata: Confining Cross-Site Prompt Injection in Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08147)
*Corban Villa,Alp Eren Ozdarendeli,Sijun Tan,Raluca Ada Popa*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Prismata is a defense system for autonomous web agents that enforces contextual least privilege through dynamic trust derivation and mechanical confinement, reducing prompt injection attacks without requiring developer annotations or sacrificing benign task utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous web agents interpret natural language instructions to automate browsing but are vulnerable to prompt injection from third-party and user-generated content. This revives the old danger of mixing trusted and untrusted content (analogous to XSS), where attackers can hijack agents. The key difficulty is that crafting a task-specific security policy requires reasoning over page structure entangled with attacker-controlled content.

Method: Prismata employs two mechanisms: (1) dynamic trust derivation that produces permission labels for page content, with structural confinement guarantees inspired by classical integrity models, ensuring labels can only decrease in privilege and mislabelings are bounded; (2) mechanical confinement that enforces these labels by redacting untrusted content and restricting agent capabilities. The approach operates without developer annotations, supporting arbitrary websites.

Result: Across recent published web agent attacks, including adaptive variants, Prismata substantially reduces attack success while preserving benign task utility.

Conclusion: Prismata demonstrates that contextual least privilege, enforced through trust derivation and mechanical confinement, is an effective and practical defense against prompt injection in autonomous web agents, applicable to the long tail of websites without requiring per-site developer effort.

Abstract: Autonomous web agents promise to automate everyday browsing tasks, but inherit one of the web's oldest attack surfaces. Cross-Site Scripting proved that mixing trusted and untrusted content is dangerous, even on benign pages. Agents resurface this risk by interpreting natural language as instructions, allowing third-party and user-generated content to hijack the agent via prompt injection. The core challenge is that deriving a task-specific security policy requires reasoning over page structure that is entangled with the attacker's content.
  We present Prismata, a defense enforcing contextual least privilege for web agents, constraining both what the agent sees and what it can do. Prismata's dynamic trust derivation produces permission labels for page content, with structural confinement guarantees, inspired by classical integrity models, that bound any labeling errors so that labels can only decrease in privilege and mislabelings are bounded. Prismata's mechanical confinement enforces these labels by redacting content and restricting agent capabilities. Importantly, these mechanisms require no developer annotations, so Prismata supports the long tail of websites. Across recent published web agent attacks, including adaptive variants, Prismata substantially reduces attack success while preserving benign task utility.

</details>


### [236] [Out of Sight: Compression-Aware Content Protection against Agentic Crawlers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08180)
*Xuefei Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CAPE protects high-value text from LLM-based agents by injecting invisible perturbations that cause severe information loss when agents apply context compression, achieving up to 75.8% improvement over baselines without altering human-readable content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents with reasoning, summarization, and memory capabilities create a new threat surface for online textual content. Conventional defenses like access controls are circumvented by agents mimicking browsers, and injection-based defenses degrade human readability. The authors identify context compression—routinely used by agents to fit context budgets—as an overlooked defense layer that can be exploited for content protection.

Method: CAPE extracts disruptive seed perturbations from an accessible surrogate compressor, then adapts them to black-box (query-only) target compressors via prior-guided evolution and preference-calibrated candidate prioritization. This operates under a low query budget, injecting invisible perturbations that leave the human-visible surface form unchanged while inducing information loss during agent-side compression.

Result: Experiments across three content types and four compression settings show CAPE improves information loss by up to 75.8% over the strongest baseline. Protected content remains visually indistinguishable from originals. CAPE also transfers to real-world settings including LangGraph agent workflows and GitHub Copilot.

Conclusion: Context compression is revealed as a viable new defense layer for content protection in the LLM-agent era. CAPE demonstrates practical value and generality, and the authors hope to promote further research in this direction.

Abstract: The rise of LLM-based agents with reasoning, summarization, and memory capabilities has created a new threat surface for online content that conventional defenses fail to address. Existing defenses like access controls can be circumvented by agents mimicking ordinary browsers, and injection-based defenses often degrade human readability. In this paper, we revisit the agent pipeline and identify context compression, which agents routinely invoke to fit context budgets, as a critical yet overlooked defense layer. We propose CAPE, a framework that protects high-value textual content by injecting invisible perturbations without changing its human-visible surface form, thereby inducing severe information loss during agent compression. CAPE extracts disruptive seed perturbations from an accessible surrogate compressor, then adapts them to query-only target compressors through prior-guided evolution and preference-calibrated candidate prioritization, achieving effective protection under a low query budget. Experiments on three content types and four compression settings show that CAPE improves information loss by up to 75.8% over the strongest baseline while keeping protected content visually indistinguishable from originals. CAPE also transfers to real-world settings, including the LangGraph agent workflow and GitHub Copilot, highlighting its generality and practical value. This paper aims to reveal context compression as a new defense layer, promoting content protection research in the agent era.

</details>


### [237] [MLQENABLER: Enabling Secure Machine Learning Queries over Encrypted Database in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08197)
*Xu Zhou,Haoyang Chen,Xinyu Lei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MLQENABLER introduces an index-aid approach to enable secure machine learning queries over encrypted cloud databases, addressing the tension between data encryption and ML functionality while maintaining acceptable security with only slight performance degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public cloud providers increasingly offer ML services on clients' stored data, creating a business opportunity but also raising serious security concerns since untrusted CSPs may sell sensitive client data to third parties. While encryption is the obvious countermeasure, standard encryption renders databases as pseudorandom numbers that are incompatible with ML computations.

Method: The MLQENABLER scheme uses an index-aid approach that allows ML queries to operate over encrypted databases without decrypting the underlying data, simultaneously achieving data security and ML capability.

Result: Initial experiments demonstrate that MLQENABLER achieves an acceptable security level with only slight degradation in ML performance.

Conclusion: The MLQENABLER scheme offers a viable solution for enabling secure ML queries on encrypted cloud-stored data, balancing the competing demands of data confidentiality and ML service utility in untrusted public cloud environments.

Abstract: In cloud computing, the public cloud service providers (CSPs) can provide cloud storage as the primary service while providing additional machine learning (ML)-based services by using the clients' data in storage. This business model extends the border of cloud computing services and brings in new business growth possibilities. Although it is promising, the model also brings in security concerns since the public commercial cloud cannot be fully trusted. For example, the public commercial clouds may sell clients' sensitive data to the government or other companies. To address the security concerns, an immediate solution is to require clients to encrypt their datasets before outsourcing to the cloud. However, if a database is formally encrypted, then the database contains only pseudorandom numbers, making it impossible to enable ML over it. In this project, we propose MLQENABLER (ML Queries Enabler) scheme to enable secure ML queries over encrypted database in cloud storage. MLQENABLER employs an index-aid approach to achieve security and ML capability simultaneously. Our initial experiments show that MLQENABLER achieves an acceptable security level while incurring only a slight ML performance degradation.

</details>


### [238] [Threshold Authorization Without Threshold Signatures: Signature-Agnostic MPC Custody](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08226)
*Dariia Porechna*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A dual-gate architecture decouples member authentication (via any EUF-CMA signature scheme) from threshold authorization (via Shamir-shared secrets), enabling post-quantum migration through simple key rotation rather than protocol redesign, while providing information-theoretic below-threshold secrecy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The post-quantum transition disrupts existing threshold signature schemes (TSS) for digital-asset custody: hash-based signatures resist efficient threshold signing, and lattice-based threshold protocols remain immature. A new architecture is needed that decouples the signature scheme from the threshold mechanism, allowing seamless migration from pre-quantum to post-quantum signature schemes without redesigning the authorization protocol.

Method: A dual-gate architecture separates member authentication from threshold authorization. Each member signs approval using any EUF-CMA signature scheme (gate one). The quorum jointly produces a "threshold seal" from Shamir-shared secrets bound to the operation (gate two). The seal serves as a base instance of programmable authorization computation, supporting simple quorum as minimal policy and richer policies via secret-shared state evaluation. The signature scheme is a deployment parameter, enabling migration (e.g., ECDSA to SLH-DSA/ML-DSA) via key rotation. Commodity HSMs participate through standard sign APIs, and the architecture targets programmable verification layers (smart contracts, vault modules, HSM-guarded master keys).

Result: The architecture produces enforcement-layer authorization rather than a native chain signature. Below-threshold secrecy is information-theoretic: an adversary possessing ≥t signing keys but no coefficient shares cannot produce the seal.

Conclusion: Decoupling authentication from authorization yields a future-proof custody architecture that accommodates post-quantum signature migration as a simple key rotation, maintains information-theoretic security below threshold, and integrates with existing HSM infrastructure and programmable verification environments.

Abstract: Digital-asset custody has been built on threshold multi-party approval: no operation proceeds unless $t$ of $n$ parties approve, and fewer than t compromised parties can neither authorize nor learn the authorization secret. Threshold signature schemes (TSS) have been the standard mechanism, but the post-quantum transition disrupts this model: standardized hash-based signatures resist efficient threshold signing, and lattice-based threshold protocols remain an emerging research track.
  We present a dual-gate architecture that separates member authentication from threshold authorization. Each member signs its approval with an ordinary signature under any EUF-CMA scheme; the quorum jointly produces a threshold seal from Shamir-shared secrets bound to the operation. The seal is the base instance of a programmable authorization computation: simple quorum is the minimal policy, while richer policies can evaluate secret-shared state without making the member-signature scheme part of that computation. The signature scheme is a deployment parameter: migrating from ECDSA to SLH-DSA or ML-DSA is a key rotation, not a protocol redesign, and members holding keys in commodity HSMs participate through the standard sign API. The architecture can be deployed wherever the asset-control path supports programmable verification, such as smart contracts, vault modules, or HSMs guarding a master key, and produces an enforcement-layer authorization rather than a native chain signature. Below-threshold secrecy is information-theoretic; an adversary holding $\geq t$ signing keys but no coefficient shares still cannot produce the seal.

</details>


### [239] [Mini-Programs, Mega-Problems: Unveiling OAuth-based Authentication Misuses in Mini-Programs via Dynamic Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08232)
*Zidong Zhang,Zhentao Xie,Lingyun Ying,Qinsheng Hou,Yacong Gu,Wenrui Diao,Jianliang Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper uncovers three new types of runtime OAuth-based authentication misuses in mini-programs that enable victim impersonation. It presents MINIAUTH, the first dynamic analysis framework for detecting such flaws at scale, and reveals 1,834 misuse cases across WeChat and Baidu mini-programs, including a cryptographic flaw in Baidu's APIs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies on OAuth security focused on static code analysis, missing runtime misuses that arise from improper integration of OAuth-based Authentication (OBA) flows in mini-programs. These runtime flaws can enable attacker impersonation of victims, yet no systematic, scalable approach existed to detect them.

Method: The authors design MINIAUTH, a dynamic analysis framework that automatically pinpoints each mini-program's OBA login page, executes the authentication workflow dynamically, and analyzes runtime behaviors to detect misuse patterns. This approach handles obfuscated code and uncovers vulnerabilities invisible to static analysis. The framework is evaluated across 44,273 WeChat and 2,721 Baidu mini-programs.

Result: MINIAUTH uncovered 1,834 misuse cases in total. Three novel runtime OBA misuse types were identified: client-side identity forgery via exposed credentials, authentication bypass through static/plaintext identifiers, and a cryptographic design flaw in Baidu's OBA APIs enabling brute-forcing of session keys. Cross-platform evaluation confirmed these misuses appear consistently across ecosystems. Findings were responsibly disclosed, earning CNVD/CNNVD IDs.

Conclusion: Runtime OAuth misuses in mini-programs are widespread and cross-platform, posing serious impersonation risks. The results highlight the urgent need for stronger developer guidance and platform-level safeguards to prevent flawed OBA integrations in super app ecosystems.

Abstract: Mini-programs have become a dominant paradigm for lightweight application deployment within super apps such as WeChat. To support seamless integration, super apps provide OAuth mechanisms for user login. However, improper integration of OAuth-based Authentication (OBA) flows by third-party developers can lead to critical security flaws. In this paper, we discover three new types of runtime OBA misuses that differ from prior static-code-based studies, enabling attackers to impersonate victims. To assess their real-world impact, we design and implement MINIAUTH, the first analysis framework for systematically analyzing OBA misuse at scale. MINIAUTH automatically pinpoints the OBA login page of a mini-program, executes the workflow dynamically, and analyzes its runtime behaviors. This enables it to handle obfuscated mini-programs and uncover vulnerabilities that existing approaches cannot detect. Applying MINIAUTH to 44,273 WeChat and 2,721 Baidu mini-programs, we uncover 1,834 misuse cases, including critical logic flaws that enable client-side identity forgery via exposed credentials and authentication bypass through static or plaintext identifiers. Our cross-platform evaluation further shows that such misuses are not confined to a single ecosystem but consistently appear across different mini-program platforms. We also identify a cryptographic design flaw in Baidu's OBA APIs that allows brute-forcing of session keys. We responsibly disclosed our findings to the developers and platforms, receiving acknowledgments and assigned CNVD/CNNVD IDs. These results underscore the need for more robust developer guidance and enhanced platform-level safeguards.

</details>


### [240] [From Legacy Documentation to OSCAL: An MCP-Based Agent Pipeline for Threat-Informed Continuous Compliance in Critical Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08288)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A multi-agent pipeline grounded in MCP converts natural-language OT system descriptions into knowledge graphs and NIST OSCAL compliance artifacts. It achieves high CVE/D3FEND recall on a synthetic water-utility scenario, while shifting hallucination risk from vulnerability fabrication to asset extraction — making errors visible, verifiable, and amenable to efficient manual review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical infrastructure OT environments cannot be actively scanned, yet active system feedback is required for risk assessment and compliance. Existing LLM-based approaches risk fabricating vulnerabilities and hallucinating attack paths. A non-invasive method is needed that provides audit-ready compliance artifacts without compromising operational safety.

Method: A non-invasive, MCP (Model Context Protocol)-grounded multi-agent pipeline that decouples LLM-based reasoning from deterministic knowledge retrieval against authoritative threat-intelligence sources. The pipeline: (1) extracts assets from natural-language system descriptions, (2) constructs a source-verified knowledge graph, and (3) generates schema-valid NIST OSCAL artifacts (System Security Plan and Security Assessment Report) for continuous automated compliance management.

Result: In a synthetic water-utility scenario, the pipeline achieves 0.90 CVE recall and perfect (1.0) D3FEND recall. It produces a schema-valid OSCAL System Security Plan and Security Assessment Report. A key finding: grounding via MCP does not eliminate errors but shifts them to the asset extraction phase — a single incorrectly extracted entity can propagate to genuine but irrelevant CVEs, though errors become visible, verifiable, and time-efficient for manual review.

Conclusion: MCP grounding does not eliminate hallucinations entirely but relocates errors to the asset extraction stage, making residual risk visible and efficiently reviewable. This trade-off is acceptable for OT environments where the infrastructure is typically well-known, and the approach enables continuous automated compliance in settings where active scanning is infeasible.

Abstract: In critical infrastructure, operational technology environments often cannot be actively scanned, and yet active system feedback is needed for risk assessment and compliance. This paper presents a non-invasive, MCP-grounded multi-agent pipeline that converts natural-language system descriptions into source-verified knowledge graph and audit-ready artifacts in the NIST OSCAL format for continuous automated compliance management. The architecture decouples LLM-based reasoning from deterministic knowledge retrieval against authoritative threat-intelligence sources, reducing the risk of fabricated vulnerabilities and hallucinated attack paths.
  In an evidence-based synthetic scenario of a water utility, the pipeline achieves 0.90 CVE recall and perfect D3FEND recall. It generates a schema-valid OSCAL System Security Plan and an OSCAL Security Assessment Report. Nevertheless, the core insight is not that grounding via MCP eliminates errors (e.g., hallucinations) entirely from the pipeline, but that it shifts errors into the first phase of asset extraction from the natural language description. Here, a single incorrectly extracted entity can lead to genuine but irrelevant CVEs in subsequent stages of the pipeline, which consumes time and resources. However, it makes the remaining risk visible, verifiable, and suitable for a time-efficient manual review, since the infrastructure (e.g., version numbers, OS, etc.) is typically known.

</details>


### [241] [Reverse Engineering Compliance: A Dual-Graph Verification Framework for Auditing Legacy IT Security Concepts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08292)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ASSERT framework extracts legacy IT security concepts into formal document graphs, compares them against verified reference graphs using five-class graph difference, and exports schema-valid OSCAL artifacts — revealing measurable document-infrastructure inconsistencies but exposing a trade-off between discovering undocumented entities and schema enforcement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The NIS-2 Directive drives demand for auditable, machine-readable compliance evidence. While OSCAL (adapted by BSI via Grundschutz++) enables this shift, organizations possess extensive legacy IT security concepts (IT-SCs) that risk transferring outdated assets if migrated without verification. Existing research addresses generation of new concepts but lacks a verification framework for legacy migration.

Method: The ASSERT Framework employs three components: (1) ontology-based extraction of legacy documents into formal document graphs; (2) a five-class graph difference computation against an independently constructed verified reference graph; and (3) export into schema-valid OSCAL outputs (system description and assessment evidence). Evaluation uses the BSI RecPlast dataset, comparing a local open-weight model and a commercial model across three configurations with varying reference-ontology exposure.

Result: ASSERT successfully makes document-infrastructure inconsistencies measurable. The evaluation reveals a trade-off between discovering undocumented entities (maximizing recall of real-world assets missing from documentation) and enforcing a strict schema (ensuring structural compliance), with performance varying across model types and ontology-exposure configurations.

Conclusion: ASSERT provides the first verification framework bridging legacy IT security concepts to OSCAL, enabling auditable migration with deterministic graph comparison. The demonstrated trade-off between entity discovery and schema enforcement highlights a design tension that practitioners must navigate when transitioning to machine-readable compliance artifacts.

Abstract: The NIS-2 Directive increases the need for continuous, auditable compliance evidence and motivates a shift from document-based compliance toward machine-readable compliance artifacts. The Open Security Controls Assessment Language (OSCAL) is a standard for this purpose, which the German Federal Office for Information Security (BSI) is adapting with Grundschutz++. However, companies are still managing extensive legacy IT security concepts (IT-SCs), and migrating them without verification could transfer outdated assets into the new format. While existing research primarily addresses the generation of new concepts, there is a lack of a verification framework that extracts legacy IT-SCs into an auditable intermediate representation, deterministically compares the extracted graph with an independently constructed reference state, and exports schema-valid OSCAL artifacts.
  This paper introduces the Automated Security Concept Structure Extraction and Reverse Topology-checking (ASSERT) Framework, which addresses this gap by using ontology-based extraction of legacy documents into formal document graphs, a five-class graph difference against a verified reference graph, and the export into schema-valid OSCAL outputs for system description and assessment evidence. Using the BSI's RecPlast dataset, we compare a local open-weight model and a commercial model across three configurations with different levels of reference-ontology exposure. The evaluation shows that ASSERT makes document-infrastructure inconsistencies measurable, but reveals a trade-off between discovering undocumented entities and enforcing a schema.

</details>


### [242] [Secure QR Codes: Authenticity Verification via EdDSA Signatures and CBOR Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08383)
*Wojciech Jonderko,Wojciech Wodo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes a two-phase evolution of secure QR code architectures: first, a fully offline scheme using Ed25519 EdDSA signatures with CBOR certificates and ZLIB compression that fits within QR static capacity; second, a scalable Hybrid Web PKI architecture with JWKS endpoints, a Central Trust Registry, and URL fragments for real-time validation and key revocation, maintaining backward compatibility with standard camera apps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QR codes lack inherent security features, making them vulnerable to spoofing attacks (quishing) on public infrastructure such as self-service parking machines. Existing solutions either lack cryptographic integrity or fail to scale with dynamic revocation needs in massive IoT/smart-city deployments.

Method: The authors first design and evaluate a fully offline proof-of-concept: EdDSA signatures on the Ed25519 curve, CBOR-encoded certificates, and ZLIB compression to fit within QR static capacity limits. Recognizing offline scalability shortcomings (no immediate key revocation), they then propose a Hybrid Web PKI architecture using standardized JWKS endpoints, a Central Trust Registry for dynamic validation, and URL fragments to preserve backward compatibility with native camera applications.

Result: The offline approach demonstrates that robust cryptographic integrity is achievable within QR code static capacity constraints. The Hybrid Web PKI model provides real-time, dynamic validation for compliant apps while ensuring seamless fallback compatibility for standard native cameras.

Conclusion: A dual-mode approach — combining offline cryptographic integrity with a scalable, dynamically revocable Hybrid Web PKI — offers a practical and deployable path toward eliminating QR code spoofing in real-world infrastructure.

Abstract: QR codes are a ubiquitous part of daily life, widely trusted by millions. However, their lack of inherent security features has given rise to critical attack vectors, such as spoofing (quishing) on public infrastructure like self-service parking machines. To address this, we present a comprehensive evolution of secure QR code architectures. First, we evaluate a fully offline proof-of-concept leveraging EdDSA signatures (instantiated on the Ed25519 curve), CBOR-encoded certificates, and ZLIB compression, demonstrating that robust cryptographic integrity can be achieved within the QR code's strict static capacity. However, recognizing the scalability limitations of fully offline models-specifically the inability to perform immediate key revocation in massive smart-city IoT deployments-we subsequently propose a scalable Hybrid Web PKI architecture. This forward-looking model utilizes standardized JWKS endpoints, a Central Trust Registry, and URL fragments to ensure seamless backward compatibility with standard native cameras while providing dynamic, real-time validation for compliant applications. This dual-mode approach offers a practical, deployable path toward eliminating QR spoofing.

</details>


### [243] [Token-Flow Firewall: Semantic Runtime Auditing for Persistent AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08395)
*Puji Wang,Yingchen Zhang,Ruqing Zhang,Jiafeng Guo,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TokenWall is a runtime semantic firewall for persistent AI agents that intercepts risky natural-language token flows before they reach privileged execution sinks, reducing attack success rate to 12.5% while maintaining 97.4% benign pass rate with only 0.69s added latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent AI agents differ from single-turn chatbots by maintaining state, skills, and tool interactions over long lifetimes, creating a much larger attack surface. Security-critical interactions flow through natural-language token streams (memory, tool arguments, inter-component messages), but existing defenses either audit sparsely or rely on costly remote large-model oversight, leaving gaps in coverage and incurring high latency.

Method: TokenWall operates as a semantic firewall over agent token flows with three components: (1) boundary-aware semantic auditing that constructs structured source-sink audit records from token flows; (2) lightweight local inspection performed pre-execution for full-coverage mediation; and (3) selective escalation of ambiguous high-risk cases to stronger arbitration modules, avoiding unnecessary remote calls on benign traffic.

Result: Evaluated on CIK-Bench, TokenWall reduces attack success rate to 12.5%, achieves a 97.4% benign executable pass rate without requiring human confirmation, and introduces only 0.69 seconds of additional latency on benign cases.

Conclusion: Semantic runtime containment via full-coverage pre-execution token-flow auditing is a practical approach for securing persistent AI agents, achieving a favorable security-utility trade-off without imposing prohibitive latency or requiring human-in-the-loop confirmation.

Abstract: Persistent AI agents extend large language models (LLMs) beyond single-turn interaction into long-lived software systems. Unlike traditional chat assistants, unsafe content in these agents can propagate through persistent state, reusable skills, and tool-mediated interactions, creating a substantially larger semantic attack surface. We observe that most security-critical interactions in such agents are transmitted through natural-language token flows, including memory updates, tool arguments, retrieved files, and inter-component communications. This observation enables a new security formulation: unsafe behavior can be intercepted as risky semantic flows before reaching privileged runtime sinks. Based on this insight, we propose TokenWall, a runtime defense framework that acts as a semantic firewall over agent token flows. TokenWall performs boundary-aware semantic auditing over these flows, constructing structured source-sink audit records, applying lightweight local inspection before execution, and selectively escalating ambiguous high-risk cases to stronger arbitration modules. Unlike prior approaches that rely on sparse auditing or remote large-model oversight, TokenWall enables full-coverage pre-execution mediation while reducing remote arbitration and latency. Experiments on CIK-Bench show that TokenWall reduces attack success rate to 12.5% while maintaining a 97.4% benign executable pass rate without human confirmation. TokenWall further introduces only 0.69 seconds of additional latency on benign cases, demonstrating that semantic runtime containment can achieve a practical security-utility trade-off for persistent AI agents.

</details>


### [244] [TRACE: A Two-Channel Robust Attribution Watermark via Complementary Embeddings for LLM-Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08400)
*Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Xiaoyan Feng,Jiaojiao Jiang,Yang Song,Yulei Sui,Zhenchang Xing,Liming Zhu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TRACE is a dual-channel watermark for LLM agent trajectories that survives adversarial resellers with full read/write access. It superposes a distortion-free selection channel (keyed on content, survives deletion) and a tally channel (keyed on log skeleton, survives rewriting), enabling attribution even when the evidence itself is manipulated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are often distributed through resellers who may rebrand or substitute cheaper models. When provenance is disputed, attribution relies on trajectory logs — the very evidence an adversary can read and modify. Existing agent watermarks fail because their detection signals are read directly off the log and can be trivially erased or overwritten. A watermark is needed that survives an adversary with full access to the detection evidence.

Method: TRACE superposes two orthogonal watermarks in the trajectory: (1) a **selection channel** that biases action choices using a distortion-free sampler keyed on local content (e.g., hash of preceding tool output), provably preserving the agent's original action distribution, and resynchronizing after deletions by re-deriving keys from surviving context; and (2) a **tally channel** that encodes the watermark in the count of records per decision group, keyed solely on the trajectory skeleton (sequence structure), which is invariant under any rewriting of record contents. The selection channel resists deletion (content-derived keys resync) and the tally channel resists rewriting (skeleton is immutable).

Result: On ToolBench and ALFWorld, TRACE matches the unwatermarked agent's task success rate. The selection channel achieves detection scores near z=100 on long-horizon trajectories and remains detectable under 70% step deletion. The tally channel remains exactly unchanged under LLM rewriting of any strength. The paper proves the watermark signal is purchased with decision entropy: each decision pays at least half its entropy, and deterministic decisions contribute nothing.

Conclusion: TRACE is the first agent watermark providing unconditional invariance under both deletion and rewriting by using two complementary channels. Erasing both channels forces a reseller to corrupt the trajectories they resell, establishing a practical attribution mechanism for LLM agent marketplaces.

Abstract: LLM agents reach users through resellers, who may rebrand a developer's agent or substitute a cheaper model. When provenance is disputed, attribution rests on the trajectory log (the record of tool calls, observations, and executed actions, not the model's reasoning), which the reseller stores and processes to meter usage. A watermark must therefore survive an adversary with full read/write access to the very evidence it is detected from; existing agent watermarks do not, as their attribution is read straight off that log. We present TRACE, to our knowledge the first agent watermark that is distortion-free in its action choices, self-synchronizing under deletion, and unconditionally invariant under rewriting. Deletion desynchronizes a position-derived key and rewriting alters content, so a deletion-robust key must come from content and a rewrite-robust key from position, and no single key serves both. A trajectory, however, has room for two watermarks. TRACE superposes a selection channel that sets which action is chosen, keyed on local content with a distortion-free sampler, so the agent's distribution is provably unchanged and detection resynchronizes after deletions, and a tally channel that sets how many records each decision group holds, keyed on the log's skeleton alone, which no rewriting can touch. We prove this behavioral watermark's signal is bought with decision entropy, each decision paying at least half its entropy and deterministic decisions nothing, and that erasing both channels forces the reseller to corrupt the trajectories it resells. On ToolBench and ALFWorld, TRACE matches the unwatermarked agent's success rate while its selection channel reaches detection scores near z = 100 on long-horizon trajectories, stays detectable under 70% step deletion, and keeps a tally channel exactly unchanged under LLM rewriting of any strength.

</details>


### [245] [TRM-Raft: A Byzantine-Resistant Raft Consensus via Integrated Trust and Reputation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08666)
*Jie Zhang,Xubo Fan,Xiaohong Li,Zhiyong Feng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TRM-Raft enhances standard Raft consensus with a blockchain-based trust and reputation model (B-TRM) to resist Byzantine faults. It uses multi-dimensional behavior quantification, adaptive penalties, and reputation-aware leader election with Schnorr signatures for log integrity, achieving <5% malicious leader ratio under 40% Byzantine nodes with minimal performance overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Raft assumes only crash faults and is vulnerable to Byzantine behaviors (election forgery, log tampering). Existing BFT protocols impose high overhead, while ad-hoc hardening approaches lack a unified defense framework for Internetware systems.

Method: TRM-Raft integrates a Blockchain-based Trust and Reputation Model (B-TRM) non-intrusively into Raft's consensus core. It quantifies multi-dimensional node behaviors, applies adaptive penalties to distinguish accidental faults from malicious acts, and embeds reputation scores into leader election and log replication. A reputation-aware election mechanism penalizes term/index forgery by excluding low-reputation nodes from leadership. A Schnorr-signature-based mechanism enables followers to verify log integrity, where tampering triggers reputation decay and leader replacement.

Result: Evaluated on Hyperledger Fabric in a realistic Internetware setting, TRM-Raft maintains malicious leader ratio below 5% even with 40% Byzantine nodes. Performance overhead is minimal: less than 10% throughput loss and under 5% latency increase compared to vanilla Raft.

Conclusion: TRM-Raft offers a lightweight, practical trustworthiness enhancement for Internetware systems that rely on Raft consensus, effectively bridging the gap between crash-fault-tolerant Raft and costly full BFT protocols.

Abstract: Internetware envisions autonomous software entities collaborating over the open Internet. Raft consensus is widely adopted for its simplicity and performance in distributed coordination, e.g., service registries and blockchains. However, Raft assumes crash faults only, making it vulnerable to Byzantine behaviors like election forgery and log tampering. Existing BFT protocols incur high overhead, while ad-hoc hardening lacks unified defense.
  We propose \textbf{TRM-Raft}, a Byzantine-resistant enhancement that non-intrusively integrates a Blockchain-based Trust and Reputation Model (B-TRM) into the consensus core. It quantifies multi-dimensional node behaviors, applies adaptive penalties distinguishing accidental faults from malice, and embeds reputation into leader election and log replication. A reputation-aware election penalizes term/index forgery, excluding low-reputation nodes from leadership. A Schnorr-signature-based mechanism lets followers verify log integrity; tampering triggers reputation decay and leader replacement. Evaluated on Hyperledger Fabric in a realistic Internetware setting, TRM-Raft keeps malicious leader ratio below 5\% even with 40\% Byzantine nodes, with <10\% throughput loss and <5\% latency increase over vanilla Raft. TRM-Raft offers a lightweight, practical trustworthiness path for Internetware systems relying on Raft.

</details>
